BCSP Certified Safety Professional · CSP11

CSP11 Study Book

Topic-wise notes for all 7 domains and 47 blueprint competencies: definition, simple explanation, real work scenario, exam tips, every blueprint point, and 10 CSP-style questions per topic.

47 competencies · 434 sub-topic notes · 470 practice questions with explanations · 13-week study plan · formula sheet
Sources: the CSP11 blueprint, your study guides and workbooks ("My research"), John's question bank and Yates (4th ed.). Personal study copy, not for distribution.

How to use this book

Each competency follows the same seven sections. Work through them in order: read sections 1 to 6, close the book, answer the ten questions on paper, then mark them with the answer key and write every miss in the error log at the back.

Daily routine (about 2 hours on weekdays)

TimeActivity
45 minRead one competency (sections 1 to 6). Underline numbers, standards and formulas.
20 minAnswer its 10 practice questions closed-book. Circle the key words: BEST, FIRST, MOST, EXCEPT, NOT.
15 minMark answers, read every explanation (including the ones you got right) and log your misses.
40 minIn the app: "Timed drill on this topic" (25 questions) for the same competency.

Weekends: review the week's topics, redo all logged misses, and take a mixed timed quiz in the app (50 to 100 questions). Exam pace is about 1.65 minutes per question (200 questions in 5.5 hours).

Targets: 8/10 or better on every topic set; 80%+ on domain tests; 85%+ on Domain 6 calculations. A topic is not done until you hit the target twice on different days.

25% of the exam · 7 competencies
1.1Prevention through Design (PtD)
Describe the principles of minimizing hazards using Prevention-Through-Design (e.g., avoidance, elimination, substitution, safety design criteria for workplace facilities, machines, and practices)
1.2Process Safety
Apply the principles of process safety (e.g., pressure relief systems, chemical compatibility, management of change, materials of construction, process flow diagrams)
1.3Common Workplace Hazards
Evaluate common workplace hazards (e.g., electrical, falls, confined spaces, lockout/tagout, working around water, caught in, struck by, excavation)
1.4Facility Life Safety
Evaluate facility life safety features (e.g., public space safety, floor loading, occupancy loads)
1.5Fleet Safety
Describe fleet safety principles (e.g., driver and equipment safety, maintenance, surveillance equipment, GPS monitoring, telematics, hybrid vehicles, fuel systems, driving under the influence, fatigue)
1.6Materials Handling
Evaluate materials handling methods and controls (e.g., forklifts, aerial lifts, and other powered industrial trucks; cranes, hand trucks, hoists, rigging, manual handling, drones)
1.7Tools, Machines and Equipment
Evaluate the use of tools, machines, and equipment (e.g., hand tools, power tools, ladders, grinders, hydraulics, robotics)

Domain 1 · Competency 1.1

Prevention through Design (PtD)

CSP11 blueprint: Describe the principles of minimizing hazards using Prevention-Through-Design (e.g., avoidance, elimination, substitution, safety design criteria for workplace facilities, machines, and practices)

1. Definition

Prevention through Design is the practice of anticipating hazards and designing them out of facilities, equipment, tools, processes and work methods at the earliest stage of the life cycle (concept and design), so that risk is reduced before workers are ever exposed. It is described in ANSI/ASSP Z590.3 and NIOSH's PtD initiative.

2. Explanation in simple words

Fix the hazard on the drawing board, not on the shop floor. Changing a drawing costs a few dollars; changing a built plant costs thousands, and changing nothing can cost a life. The earlier you act, the more options you have (eliminate or substitute) and the cheaper they are.

3. Key points to remember

  • Life-cycle thinking: concept → design → construction/installation → operation → maintenance → decommissioning/demolition. Consider every phase, including maintenance and demolition.
  • Hierarchy applied at design: avoidance (do not introduce the hazard), elimination, substitution, engineering controls, warnings, administrative controls, PPE.
  • Avoidance vs elimination: avoidance means the hazard is never introduced into a new design; elimination removes a hazard that already exists or was proposed.
  • Safety design criteria come from consensus and code bodies: ANSI/ASSP (Z590.3, Z10, Z244.1), NFPA codes, UL listings, FM Global data sheets, ISO 12100 (machinery risk assessment), NIOSH guidance, OSHA standards (minimum).
  • Design reviews: preliminary hazard analysis at concept, HAZOP/What-If at detailed design, pre-startup safety review (PSSR) before operation.
  • Typical PtD solutions: grade-level access for maintenance, parapets 42 in. high on roofs, tie-off anchors built in, remote lube/fill lines, interlocked guarding, ergonomic workstation heights, wider aisles to separate forklifts and pedestrians.
  • Business case: lower retrofit cost, lower injury costs, better productivity; cost of change rises steeply as the project moves from concept to operation.

4. Real work scenario

A new distribution center is being designed. The safety professional reviews the drawings and notices that HVAC units on the roof will need monthly filter changes. Instead of relying on harnesses later, she asks the architect to add a 42-inch parapet and a fixed stair to the roof. Mechanics now work without fall-arrest equipment, and the change cost less than 1% of the roof budget.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • When a question says 'design phase', 'blueprint review' or 'new facility', look for the answer that removes the hazard by design (relocate, eliminate, substitute) rather than training, signs or PPE.
  • If two answers are both engineering controls, pick the one applied earlier in the life cycle.
  • Remember the standard: ANSI/ASSP Z590.3 = Prevention through Design.
  • Trap: a 'good' engineering control (platform with guardrails) is weaker than eliminating the task at height (remote access, grade-level equipment).

6. Every blueprint point (6 blueprint items + 2 more)

AvoidanceBLUEPRINT

Avoidance means a hazard is never introduced in the first place - the safest design choice is made before anything exists, e.g., choosing a site, layout or process that has no fall, chemical or traffic exposure.

  • Applied at concept stage (cheapest point to change).
  • Example: placing rooftop equipment at grade so no roof access is ever needed.
  • Difference from elimination: elimination removes a hazard that exists or was proposed; avoidance never creates it.

Exam tip: Keywords 'concept stage', 'new facility', 'layout planning' point to avoidance/elimination.

EliminationBLUEPRINT

Remove the hazard entirely at the design stage so it no longer exists (Z590.3, NIOSH PtD). Top of the hierarchy of controls.

  • Example: pre-assembled components remove on-site cutting and welding.
  • Example: remote lube/fill points remove work at height.
  • PtD addresses the top two levels of the hierarchy: elimination and substitution.

Exam tip: If elimination or substitution is feasible, it is usually the correct answer over engineering, admin or PPE.

SubstitutionBLUEPRINT

Replace a hazardous material, process or equipment with a less hazardous one.

  • Water-based paints instead of solvent-based (removes VOC exposure).
  • Non-silica blasting media instead of sand.
  • Make sure the substitute does not introduce a new, worse hazard.

Minimization, moderation and simplification (inherently safer design)

Inherently safer design principles in the guide: minimize quantity/concentration, moderate (lower) operating conditions, and simplify systems to reduce human error.

  • Minimization: smaller containers, lower inventory, less concentrated solutions.
  • Moderation: run reactions at ambient temperature/pressure, lower voltage or speed.
  • Simplification: standardize control layouts across lines to prevent operator confusion.
  • Integration of engineering controls: design fixed guards, interlocks and fail-safe shutdowns into machines from the start.

Safety design criteria for workplace facilitiesBLUEPRINT

Facilities are designed against codes and consensus standards so that egress, fall protection, ventilation, fire protection and traffic separation are built in.

  • Sources: OSHA (minimum), NFPA (101, 13, 30), IBC/IFC, ANSI/ASSP, FM Global data sheets, UL listings, NIOSH guidance.
  • Examples: 42-in. parapets/guardrails, separated pedestrian and forklift routes, adequate exits, ventilation designed to ASHRAE/ACGIH.
  • ANSI/ASSP Z590.3 = PtD guideline; ISO 12100 = machinery design risk assessment.

Safety design criteria for machinesBLUEPRINT

Machine design should remove hazards (inherently safe design), then add safeguarding, then information for use - the ISO 12100 three-step method.

  • Fixed guards, interlocks, two-hand controls and safety-rated control circuits designed in, not added later.
  • Lockable energy-isolating devices on new equipment (1910.147 requires new or overhauled equipment to accept a lock).
  • Ergonomic heights and reach distances; noise specifications in purchase orders.

Safety design criteria for practicesBLUEPRINT

Work methods and procedures are designed so the safe way is the easy way: sequencing tasks, eliminating non-routine hazardous steps and building checks into the process.

  • Design procedures with the people who do the work.
  • Plan maintenance access during design (grade-level access, isolation points, lifting points).
  • Review procedures under MOC when equipment or materials change.

Life-cycle consideration and benefits of PtD

Hazards are evaluated across the full life cycle: concept, design, construction, operation, maintenance, decommissioning and demolition.

  • Example: modular equipment that can be dismantled and recycled safely at end of life.
  • Benefits: lower PPE/training/admin costs, injury prevention before exposure, regulatory compliance before commissioning, long-term reduction in catastrophic failures.
  • If the hazard already exists and redesign is impractical, PtD is not the answer - pick the best engineering or administrative control.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 35 Prevention through Design (PtD), p. 780

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

1.1-Q1. Which Prevention through Design (PtD) strategy aims to completely remove a hazard during the design phase?

  1. Substitution
  2. Minimization
  3. Elimination
  4. Moderation

1.1-Q2. During design review for a new packaging line, maintenance will need to grease bearings on a drive located 14 ft above the floor every week. Which design decision BEST applies Prevention through Design?

  1. Relocate the grease points with remote lube lines so the task is done from floor level
  2. Specify a permanent platform with guardrails at the drive
  3. Require a 100% tie-off procedure and provide harnesses
  4. Add the task to the weekly JHA and train the mechanics

1.1-Q3. A new conveyor system is being installed in a distribution center. During design review, engineers propose adding emergency-stop pull cords but leaving several exposed pinch points near the rollers because "guards can be added later if injuries occur." Which Prevention through Design action is MOST appropriate?

  1. Install signs warning employees to keep hands clear of the rollers
  2. Provide gloves to protect workers if their hands get too close
  3. Train workers to avoid pinch points during startup
  4. Eliminate or enclose the pinch points with fixed guards before installation

1.1-Q4. A safety manager is reviewing drawings for a new CNC machining area. The design places electrical panels directly behind the operators, requiring them to reach across the work zone to access them. What is the BEST Prevention through Design recommendation?

  1. Require operators to wear additional PPE when accessing the panels
  2. Relocate the panels to a safe, accessible location during design
  3. Train workers to de-energize machines before accessing the panels
  4. Post warning signs on the panels

1.1-Q5. Under inherently safer design, redesigning a reaction to run at near-ambient temperature and pressure instead of high temperature and pressure demonstrates which principle?

  1. Moderation (attenuation)
  2. Simplification
  3. Minimization (intensification)
  4. Substitution

1.1-Q6. A company is automating a welding workstation. The project manager skips the hazard review during initial design to "save time" and plans to do it at commissioning. Engineers later find that the robot's reach envelope intersects the operator walkway. Which step was missed?

  1. Vendor warranty review
  2. Purchasing specification verification
  3. Pre-startup safety review
  4. Early-stage hazard identification and risk assessment

1.1-Q7. Designing the control panel of a new unit with fewer, clearly labeled controls to reduce operator confusion during startup is an example of which inherently safer design principle?

  1. Minimization
  2. Simplification
  3. Substitution
  4. Moderation

1.1-Q8. During design of a new paint finishing line, the engineer specifies a water-based coating in place of the flammable solvent-based coating used at the old plant. This is BEST described as:

  1. Elimination
  2. An engineering control
  3. Substitution
  4. An administrative control

1.1-Q9. Which consensus standard provides guidance specifically on Prevention through Design - addressing occupational hazards and risks in design and redesign processes?

  1. ANSI/ASSP Z590.3
  2. ANSI/ASSP Z359.1
  3. ANSI Z16.1
  4. ANSI/ASSP Z10

1.1-Q10. ANSI/ASSP Z590.3 places one control ABOVE elimination in its hierarchy. It refers to preventing a hazard from ever entering the workplace, such as siting a new building outside a flood plain. What is it called?

  1. Attenuation
  2. Isolation
  3. Substitution
  4. Avoidance

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CElimination designs the hazard out so it no longer exists; it is the most effective control. Substitution and minimization only reduce the hazard.
2ARemote lube lines eliminate the need to work at height. A guarded platform is a good engineering control but still leaves exposure; tie-off and training are lower-order controls.
3DPtD addresses hazards at the design stage rather than after injuries. Fixed guarding designed in now beats signs, training or PPE, which are lower in the hierarchy.
4BChanging the layout on paper removes the exposure at almost no cost. PPE, training and signs accept the hazard instead of designing it out.
5AModeration uses less hazardous conditions (lower temperature, pressure or concentration) to reduce severity. Minimization reduces the quantity; substitution swaps the material.
6DPtD requires hazard identification and risk assessment at the conceptual/design stage, when changes are cheapest. A PSSR at commissioning is too late to redesign the layout cheaply.
7BSimplification removes unnecessary complexity so errors are less likely and easier to detect. The trap is minimization, which refers to reducing hazardous inventory.
8CReplacing a hazardous material with a less hazardous one is substitution. The hazard is reduced, not removed, so it is not elimination.
9AANSI/ASSP Z590.3 is the PtD standard. Z10 is the OHS management system standard, Z359 covers fall protection, and Z16.1 covers safety metrics/recordkeeping.
10DAvoidance (prevention at concept/siting) sits at the top of the Z590.3 hierarchy, followed by elimination, substitution, engineering controls, warnings, administrative controls and PPE.
Printed book page 12
Domain 1 · Competency 1.2

Process Safety

CSP11 blueprint: Apply the principles of process safety (e.g., pressure relief systems, chemical compatibility, management of change, materials of construction, process flow diagrams)

1. Definition

Process safety is the management of hazards in processes that handle highly hazardous chemicals to prevent fires, explosions and toxic releases. In the U.S. it is governed by OSHA 29 CFR 1910.119 (Process Safety Management, PSM) and EPA 40 CFR 68 (Risk Management Program, RMP).

2. Explanation in simple words

Personal safety stops slips and cuts; process safety stops the big events that kill many people at once (Bhopal, Texas City). It works by knowing your chemicals and equipment, analyzing what can go wrong, keeping equipment in good condition, and controlling every change.

3. Key points to remember

  • PSM has 14 elements: employee participation, process safety information (PSI), process hazard analysis (PHA), operating procedures, training, contractors, pre-startup safety review (PSSR), mechanical integrity, hot work permit, management of change (MOC), incident investigation, emergency planning and response, compliance audits, trade secrets.
  • PHA must be revalidated at least every 5 years; compliance audits every 3 years; operator refresher training at least every 3 years; incident investigation must start within 48 hours and reports kept 5 years.
  • PHA methods: What-If, Checklist, What-If/Checklist, HAZOP (guide words: no, more, less, reverse, other than), FMEA, Fault Tree Analysis.
  • Pressure relief systems: relief valves (reclose), safety valves (gas, pop action), rupture discs (non-reclosing). A rupture disc upstream of a relief valve protects the valve from corrosion; the space between must be monitored. Never put a block valve in a relief path without a car-seal/lock-open program.
  • Chemical compatibility: segregate incompatibles (acids/bases, oxidizers/flammables, hypochlorite/acids → chlorine, water-reactives). Use a compatibility chart, SDS Section 10 and separate secondary containment.
  • Materials of construction: choose metals, gaskets and linings compatible with the chemical, temperature and pressure (e.g., chloride stress-corrosion cracking of 304/316 stainless; PTFE gaskets creep). A material change is not 'replacement in kind' and needs MOC.
  • PFD vs P&ID: the process flow diagram shows major equipment, flow paths and operating conditions; the piping and instrumentation diagram adds every pipe, valve, instrument, interlock and relief device. Both are part of process safety information.
  • MOC covers changes to chemicals, technology, equipment, procedures and facilities (and organization/staffing in good practice), including temporary changes. Replacement in kind is excluded.

4. Real work scenario

A refinery wants to raise the throughput of a distillation column by 10%. The MOC review checks whether the existing relief valve is still sized for the new maximum flow, updates the P&ID and operating procedures, trains operators, and completes a PSSR before startup. The review finds the relief valve is undersized, and it is replaced before the change goes live.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Questions often hide an MOC trigger inside 'it's basically the same part' or 'it's only temporary'. If the specification changes, MOC applies.
  • Know which PSM element matches a scenario: missing drawings = PSI; corroded pipe left unrepaired = mechanical integrity; no PHA follow-up = PHA recommendations must be resolved.
  • HAZOP uses guide words; FMEA lists component failure modes; FTA works top-down from an event.
  • Numbers to memorize: 5-year PHA revalidation, 3-year audit, 3-year refresher training, 48-hour investigation start.

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 3 more)

PSM scope and the 14 elements (29 CFR 1910.119)

PSM applies to processes with highly hazardous chemicals (Appendix A) at or above threshold quantities, or 10,000 lb or more of flammable liquids/gases.

  • 14 elements: employee participation, PSI, PHA, operating procedures, training, contractors, PSSR, mechanical integrity, hot work permit, MOC, incident investigation, emergency planning and response, compliance audits, trade secrets.
  • Exempt: retail facilities, oil and gas well drilling/servicing, hydrocarbon fuels used only for workplace heating/cooking, atmospheric tanks not connected to a process, farms (fertilizer use), flammables below 10,000 lb, non-production labs, normally unoccupied remote facilities.
  • EPA's Risk Management Program (40 CFR 68) parallels PSM for community protection.

Exam tip: 'Operator not included in PHA team' = employee participation violation.

Process safety information (PSI)

Complete, accurate, up-to-date written information compiled BEFORE the PHA: chemical hazards, process technology and process equipment.

  • Chemicals: SDSs, toxicity/exposure limits, physical, reactivity, corrosivity, thermal stability, mixing (incompatibility) data.
  • Technology: block/process flow diagrams, safe operating limits, process chemistry, maximum intended inventory, consequences of deviation.
  • Equipment: P&IDs, relief system design basis, materials of construction, electrical classification, ventilation, design codes (ASME, API, NFPA), material/energy balances, safety systems.

Exam tip: Outdated P&IDs, missing reactivity data or unknown maximum inventory = PSI failure.

Process hazard analysis and other PSM numbers

PHA is required before startup, by a team with process and methodology expertise, and must be revalidated at least every 5 years.

  • PHA must address: process hazards, previous incidents with catastrophic potential, controls and detection, consequences of control failure, facility siting, human factors, qualitative evaluation of effects.
  • Methods: What-If, Checklist, What-If/Checklist, HAZOP, FMEA, FTA (plus LOPA in practice).
  • Timeframes: operating procedures certified annually; refresher training at least every 3 years; incident investigation starts within 48 hours, reports kept 5 years; compliance audits every 3 years (keep the two most recent reports).
  • PSSR is required before hazardous chemicals are introduced to new or modified facilities.
  • Trade secrets can never be used to withhold PSI needed for safety (confidentiality agreements allowed).

Exam tip: Facility siting is often overlooked in exam scenarios involving community impact or weather.

Pressure relief systemsBLUEPRINT

Relief devices protect equipment from overpressure by releasing to a safe location.

  • Design and sizing per ASME BPVC Section VIII and API 520/521; discharge away from people and ignition sources.
  • Inspection: visual annually (API 576); bench test/recertification typically every 5 years, sooner in corrosive or fouling service.
  • Relief valve recloses; rupture disc is non-reclosing; disc upstream of a valve protects it from corrosion (monitor the space between).
  • A rupture disc alone is not a substitute for a relief valve unless designed as part of a dual system.

Chemical compatibilityBLUEPRINT

Prevent dangerous reactions by keeping incompatible chemicals, materials and equipment apart.

  • Check SDS Section 10 (stability and reactivity) and compatibility charts for containers, piping and gaskets.
  • Segregate per NFPA 400 (e.g., oxidizers away from flammables; acids away from cyanides - produces HCN; hypochlorite away from acids - chlorine).
  • Storing incompatibles in the same containment area without separation is a violation even if containers are sealed.

Management of change in process safetyBLUEPRINT

All modifications to HHC processes are evaluated, authorized and documented before implementation.

  • Applies to equipment changes, raw material type/quality/supplier, operating conditions, control systems/software, procedures.
  • Required considerations: technical basis, impact on health and safety, modification of SOPs, time period (temporary/permanent), authorization.
  • Communicate and train affected employees and contractors before startup.
  • 'Replacement in kind' excluded - but PSSR may still apply.

Exam tip: Trigger words: new material supplier, modifying reactor temperature, upgrading control software.

Materials of constructionBLUEPRINT

Select materials that withstand the pressures, temperatures and chemicals for the life of the process.

  • Select per ASME, API 650/653 (tanks) and ASTM material standards; consider corrosion allowance and coatings.
  • Document specifications in PSI before startup.
  • A material change is not replacement in kind - it requires MOC (e.g., stainless steel in hot chloride service can stress-crack).

Exam tip: Choosing material based only on cost, without corrosion resistance, can invalidate code compliance.

Process flow diagrams and P&IDsBLUEPRINT

PFDs show major equipment, streams and operating conditions; P&IDs add every pipe, valve, instrument, interlock and relief device.

  • Both are part of PSI and must match field conditions.
  • Update P&IDs after every approved MOC.
  • Block flow diagram < PFD < P&ID in detail.

Exam tip: Which diagram shows safety interlocks and relief valves? The P&ID, not the PFD.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 4 Regulations (PSM), Ch. 38 Management of Change, p. 859; Ch. 27 Hazardous Materials Management

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

1.2-Q1. Which process safety drawing shows process piping together with instrumentation, control loops, interlocks and safety devices such as relief valves?

  1. Process Flow Diagram (PFD)
  2. Piping and Instrumentation Diagram (P&ID)
  3. Block Flow Diagram (BFD)
  4. Plot plan

1.2-Q2. A rupture disc is installed directly upstream of a spring-loaded relief valve in corrosive service. What is the PRIMARY reason for this arrangement, and what must be provided between them?

  1. To isolate the relief valve from the corrosive process fluid; a pressure gauge or telltale indicator to detect disc leakage
  2. To increase relieving capacity; a check valve
  3. To allow the valve to reseat faster; a block valve
  4. To reduce the set pressure; a second rupture disc

1.2-Q3. A chemical plant substitutes a new raw material in a batch recipe. The engineer assumes it is compatible because it is "chemically similar" and skips the hazard review. At startup, excessive heat causes the reactor relief valve to lift. Which MOC requirement should have prevented this?

  1. Training operators after the change
  2. Inspecting the relief valve after installation
  3. Reviewing safety data and performing a hazard evaluation of the change before implementation
  4. Performing a post-incident investigation

1.2-Q4. A warehouse stores drums of oxidizers next to flammable liquids with no separation. Which process safety concept is MOST directly violated?

  1. Pressure relief design
  2. Process flow design
  3. Materials of construction
  4. Chemical compatibility

1.2-Q5. Piping for a new hydrochloric acid line is selected only on lowest cost, without considering corrosion resistance. Which process safety element is being ignored?

  1. Process flow diagram review
  2. Contractor safety
  3. Emergency planning
  4. Materials of construction

1.2-Q6. Operators at a blending unit rely on tribal knowledge because the written procedures were never updated after a recipe change. During startup an operator adds an ingredient too early, causing a runaway reaction that lifts the relief system. Which PSM element was MOST directly violated?

  1. Operating procedures
  2. Mechanical integrity
  3. Contractors
  4. Incident investigation

1.2-Q7. During a turnaround, operations wants to bypass a high-level interlock on a tank for 6 hours while a transmitter is replaced. Under a PSM-compliant program, this temporary bypass should be:

  1. Allowed without paperwork because it is temporary
  2. Managed through MOC with a defined time limit, compensating measures and authorization
  3. Approved verbally by the shift supervisor
  4. Documented only after the work is complete

1.2-Q8. While reviewing a PFD for a high-pressure steam loop, you notice steam passes directly from a boiler to a reactor with no symbol for a pressure relief device. What is the BEST action?

  1. Approve the design if the piping is sized correctly
  2. Assume relief is shown on the P&ID and not relevant
  3. Flag the drawing and confirm whether overpressure protection was omitted or is provided elsewhere
  4. Confirm the pipe routing and allow commissioning

1.2-Q9. Which of the following changes would require a Management of Change (MOC) review under OSHA PSM?

  1. Replacing a valve with an identical spare part
  2. Reviewing a closed incident report
  3. Performing routine scheduled lubrication
  4. Changing a reactor's operating temperature range

1.2-Q10. A single pressure relief valve protects a pressure vessel. Under ASME pressure vessel practice, the relief valve set pressure should be no higher than the vessel's:

  1. Maximum allowable working pressure (MAWP)
  2. Hydrostatic test pressure
  3. Normal operating pressure
  4. Burst pressure

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BP&IDs show valves, instruments, interlocks and relief devices. PFDs show only major equipment, main flows and operating conditions.
2AThe disc protects the valve internals from corrosion and fouling. The space between them must be monitored, because a pinhole leak can build back-pressure and keep the disc from bursting at its set point.
3CMOC requires the technical basis and safety impact of a change, including chemical reactivity and compatibility, to be evaluated BEFORE the change is made. Everything else in the list happens after the hazard has been introduced.
4DOxidizers greatly intensify the burning of flammables. Incompatible materials must be separated by distance, barriers or cabinets.
5DMaterials of construction must be compatible with the process fluid, temperature and pressure. Under-specified materials corrode and lead to loss of containment.
6A1910.119(f) requires written, current operating procedures, certified annually. An MOC failure contributed, but the most direct gap is outdated procedures.
7BTemporary changes, including interlock bypasses, fall under MOC. They need a technical review, a time limit, authorization and compensating measures such as a dedicated operator watching the level.
8CNever assume overpressure protection exists. Verify it against the P&ID and relief design basis before approving; a missing relief device is a potentially catastrophic gap.
9DMOC applies to changes in chemicals, technology, equipment, procedures and operating limits. A "replacement in kind" (identical part) is specifically excluded.
10AThe set pressure of a single relief device must not exceed MAWP; allowable accumulation above that (for example 10%) occurs only while relieving. Setting it at operating pressure would cause nuisance lifting.
Printed book page 17
Domain 1 · Competency 1.3

Common Workplace Hazards

CSP11 blueprint: Evaluate common workplace hazards (e.g., electrical, falls, confined spaces, lockout/tagout, working around water, caught in, struck by, excavation)

1. Definition

Common workplace hazards are the recurring energy sources and conditions that cause most serious injuries and fatalities: electricity, falls from height, hazardous atmospheres and engulfment in confined spaces, unexpected release of energy during servicing, drowning, caught-in/between and struck-by events, and trench cave-ins. OSHA's 'Focus Four' in construction are falls, struck-by, caught-in/between and electrocution.

2. Explanation in simple words

Every one of these hazards is an energy that can hurt you: electrical, gravity, mechanical, chemical or soil weight. The safety professional finds the energy, keeps people away from it or controls it, and checks that the control is working before anyone is exposed.

3. Key points to remember

  • Electrical (NFPA 70E, OSHA 1910 Subpart S, 1926 Subpart K): de-energize first (electrically safe work condition); energized work only if de-energizing creates greater hazard or is infeasible, with an energized work permit, shock and arc-flash risk assessment and PPE. GFCIs trip at about 4-6 mA imbalance; let-go threshold ~10 mA; ventricular fibrillation ~50-100+ mA.
  • Falls: general industry protection at 4 ft (1910.28), construction at 6 ft (1926.501), scaffolds at 10 ft, steel erection 15 ft. Guardrail top rail 42 in. ± 3 in., withstand 200 lb. PFAS: anchor 5,000 lb per worker, free fall ≤ 6 ft, maximum arresting force 1,800 lb with harness, deceleration distance ≤ 3.5 ft. Body belts are prohibited for fall arrest.
  • Confined spaces (1910.146 / 1926 Subpart AA): large enough to enter, limited entry/exit, not designed for continuous occupancy. A permit space also has a hazardous atmosphere, engulfment, inwardly converging walls or another serious hazard. Acceptable oxygen 19.5-23.5%; flammables < 10% LFL. Test in order oxygen → flammables → toxics. Attendant never enters to rescue. Cancelled permits kept 1 year.
  • Lockout/tagout (1910.147): written energy control program, machine-specific procedures, authorized vs affected employees, periodic inspection at least annually, verify zero energy, release stored energy (springs, hydraulics, capacitors, gravity). Group lockout uses a lockbox; shift change requires continuity (the incoming lock goes on before the outgoing lock comes off).
  • Working around water (1926.106): USCG-approved PFDs, ring buoys with at least 90 ft of line no more than 200 ft apart, and a lifesaving skiff immediately available.
  • Caught-in/between: unguarded rotating parts, in-running nip points, shifting loads, trench collapse, equipment rollover. Struck-by: falling/flying objects, vehicles and mobile equipment, swinging loads. Controls: guarding, tool tethers, toe boards, exclusion zones, high-visibility clothing, spotters, backup alarms.
  • Excavation (1926 Subpart P): protective system required at 5 ft or deeper (unless stable rock); designed by a registered professional engineer over 20 ft. Ladder/ramp within 25 ft of lateral travel at 4 ft or deeper; test atmosphere at > 4 ft where hazardous atmosphere is possible; spoils at least 2 ft back. Soil: Stable rock (vertical), Type A ¾:1, Type B 1:1, Type C 1½:1. A competent person inspects daily and after rain.

4. Real work scenario

A utility crew is replacing a water main in a 7-ft trench in Type B soil next to a busy road. The safety professional requires a trench box rated for the depth, spoils 2 ft back from the edge, a ladder within 25 ft, atmospheric testing because a sewer line runs nearby, a daily competent person inspection, and a flagger and barriers for traffic. When it rains overnight, the competent person re-inspects before anyone enters.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Numbers are heavily tested: 4 ft / 6 ft / 10 ft fall thresholds, 5 ft and 20 ft trench thresholds, 19.5-23.5% oxygen, 10% LFL, 25 ft to a ladder.
  • For electrical scenarios, the BEST answer is almost always 'de-energize and lock out' before any PPE answer.
  • For confined space rescue questions, the attendant summons rescue and never enters.
  • LOTO shift change: watch for answers that remove the old lock first; continuity of protection is required.
  • Fall clearance = free fall + deceleration (3.5 ft) + D-ring height/worker height + safety factor (often 2-3 ft).

6. Every blueprint point (8 blueprint items + 3 more)

Electrical hazardsBLUEPRINT

Shock, arc flash, arc blast, burns and fires from contact with energized parts or faults. Governed by OSHA 1910 Subpart S, 1926 Subpart K and NFPA 70E.

  • Establish an electrically safe work condition first; energized work only when de-energizing creates greater hazard or is infeasible (energized work permit, shock and arc-flash risk assessment, PPE).
  • Current, not voltage, injures: ~1 mA perception, ~10 mA let-go, 50-100+ mA ventricular fibrillation.
  • GFCI trips on a hot/neutral imbalance of about 4-6 mA within ~1/40 s; required for temporary wiring/construction (or assured equipment grounding program).
  • Approach boundaries (70E): limited (unqualified may enter only with qualified escort), restricted (qualified only, with shock PPE). Arc flash boundary = 1.2 cal/cm² onset of second-degree burn.
  • Insulating gloves: Class 00 500 V, 0 1,000 V, 1 7,500 V, 2 17,000 V, 3 26,500 V, 4 36,000 V AC; dielectric test every 6 months, inspect before each use, wear leather protectors.

Exam tip: Electrical scenario: 'de-energize and lock out' beats any PPE answer.

Falls (working at heights, slips and trips)BLUEPRINT

Falls from height and same-level slips/trips are leading causes of serious injury.

  • Thresholds: general industry 4 ft (1910.28), construction 6 ft (1926.501), scaffolds 10 ft, fixed ladders > 24 ft need ladder safety system or PFAS (cages being phased out by 2036).
  • Hierarchy of fall protection: elimination > passive (guardrails, covers, nets) > fall restraint > fall arrest > administrative (warning lines, safety monitor).
  • Guardrail: top rail 42 in. ± 3 in., withstand 200 lb; mid-rail ~21 in.; toe board ≥ 3.5 in.; floor holes > 2 in. covered.
  • PFAS (ABC: Anchorage 5,000 lb per worker, Body harness, Connector): free fall ≤ 6 ft, deceleration ≤ 3.5 ft, arresting force ≤ 1,800 lb. Positioning devices: free fall ≤ 2 ft. Safety nets ≤ 30 ft below, drop-tested with 400-lb bag.
  • Warning lines ≥ 6 ft from roof edge; safety monitor on same level with no other duties.
  • Suspension trauma: serious effects in 5-15 min; rescue promptly, ideally < 5 min (ANSI Z359.2); trauma relief straps.
  • Stair rail required with ≥ 4 risers or > 30 in. rise; handrail 30-38 in. (construction).

Exam tip: 'Works automatically without worker input' = passive protection; 'prevents reaching the edge' = restraint.

Confined spacesBLUEPRINT

A confined space is large enough to enter, has limited entry/exit and is not designed for continuous occupancy. A permit-required space also has a serious hazard.

  • PRCS hazards: hazardous atmosphere (O₂ < 19.5% or > 23.5%, flammables ≥ 10% LFL, toxics above PEL), engulfment, inwardly converging walls/sloping floor, other serious hazards.
  • Test order: oxygen → flammables → toxics.
  • Roles: entrant; attendant (stays outside, monitors, summons rescue, never enters); entry supervisor (authorizes/cancels entry, signs permit).
  • Rescue: in-house or third party, trained and equipped; non-entry retrieval (tripod, lifeline, full-body harness) for vertical spaces > 5 ft deep where feasible.
  • Cancelled permits kept 1 year; program reviewed annually. Construction: 1926 Subpart AA.

Exam tip: 'Who authorizes entry?' = entry supervisor.

Lockout/tagout (hazardous energy control)BLUEPRINT

1910.147 protects workers from unexpected start-up or release of stored energy during servicing and maintenance.

  • Energy types: electrical, hydraulic, pneumatic, mechanical (springs, flywheels, gravity), thermal, chemical, magnetic.
  • Written program + machine-specific energy control procedures; lockout devices durable, standardized, substantial, identifiable.
  • Lock if the device can be locked; tagout only if it cannot, with additional measures (remove handle, block switch).
  • 8 steps: prepare, notify affected employees, shut down, isolate, apply locks/tags, release stored energy, verify isolation (try start, return controls to off), return to service (only the person who applied the lock removes it).
  • Authorized vs affected vs other employees; retraining when assignments, machines or procedures change.
  • Periodic inspection at least annually per procedure, by an authorized employee other than the one using it; certify machine, date, employees, inspector.
  • Shift change: documented transfer so protection is continuous.

Exam tip: Stored energy is the most-missed LOTO concept: bleed hydraulics, discharge capacitors, block raised parts.

Working around waterBLUEPRINT

Drowning hazards on bridges, docks, barges and work over or near water (1926.106).

  • USCG-approved PFDs (life jackets) where there is a danger of drowning.
  • Ring buoys with at least 90 ft of line, no more than 200 ft apart.
  • At least one lifesaving skiff immediately available.
  • Also consider cold-water shock, currents and electrical hazards near water.

Caught-in/between hazardsBLUEPRINT

Being squeezed, crushed or pinched between objects - machinery, collapsing materials, trench walls, vehicles.

  • Sources: unguarded rotating parts, in-running nip points, shear points, trench collapse, equipment rollover, materials shifting.
  • Controls: guarding (point of operation, power transmission < 7 ft above floor), LOTO, trench protective systems, ROPS + seat belts, staying out of swing radius.

Struck-by hazardsBLUEPRINT

Being hit by falling, flying, swinging or rolling objects or vehicles.

  • Sources: falling tools/materials, flying particles from grinding, swinging crane loads, backing vehicles, mobile equipment.
  • Controls: toe boards, tool tethers, debris nets, exclusion zones, barricades below overhead work, high-visibility apparel, backup alarms, spotters, eye/face protection (Z87+), hard hats (Type II for lateral impact).

Excavation and trenchingBLUEPRINT

1926 Subpart P. Cave-ins are the most common and fatal hazard; also falling loads, hazardous atmospheres, water accumulation, mobile equipment.

  • Trench: narrow excavation deeper than wide, bottom width ≤ 15 ft.
  • 4-5-20 rule: ≥ 4 ft access/egress (within 25 ft lateral travel) and atmosphere testing; ≥ 5 ft protective system unless stable rock; > 20 ft protective system designed by a registered PE.
  • Soil types (by competent person; visual + manual tests): Stable rock 90°; Type A > 1.5 tsf, ¾:1 (53°); Type B 0.5-1.5 tsf, 1:1 (45°); Type C ≤ 0.5 tsf, 1½:1 (34°). Fissured, previously disturbed or vibrated soil cannot be Type A.
  • Protective systems: sloping/benching, shoring, shielding (trench boxes).
  • Spoil piles ≥ 2 ft from edge; no work in accumulated water without precautions; daily inspection by a competent person and after rain.

Exam tip: Even < 5 ft may need protection if the competent person finds unstable soil.

Machine guarding (caught-in/struck-by control)

Guards protect against point-of-operation, power transmission and other moving parts; covered in detail under 1.7.

  • Guard types: fixed, interlocked, adjustable, self-adjusting; devices: light curtains, pressure mats, pullback, restraint, two-hand controls.
  • Any moving power transmission part < 7 ft above floor/platform must be guarded.

Personal protective equipment (supporting control)

PPE is the last line of defense, used when engineering and administrative controls are not enough (1910 Subpart I; hazard assessment and written certification required).

  • Eye/face: ANSI Z87.1; Z87+ = high impact; goggles for chemical splash; face shield only with glasses/goggles underneath; laser eyewear by wavelength and optical density.
  • Head: ANSI Z89.1 - Class G 2,200 V, Class E 20,000 V, Class C no electrical; Type I top impact, Type II top + lateral; replace suspension ~12 months, shell ~5 years.
  • Foot: ASTM F2413 markings I/75 C/75 (toe), Mt (metatarsal), EH (electrical hazard), PR (puncture), Cd (conductive/static).
  • HAZWOPER PPE levels: A (vapor-tight + SCBA), B (splash + SCBA - highest respiratory, lower skin), C (APR, known contaminant, O₂ ≥ 19.5%), D (work clothes).
  • Employer pays for required PPE (with limited exceptions).

Compressed gases and pressure vessels

Cylinders (DOT) and stationary pressure vessels (ASME BPVC) store large amounts of energy.

  • Cylinders: hydrostatic retest typically every 5 years; valve caps on when not in use; upright and secured; oxygen ≥ 20 ft from fuel gases or a 5-ft-high, ½-hour fire-rated barrier; identify by label/stencil, not color alone.
  • Acetylene: never used above 15 psig (unstable).
  • Pressure vessel: internal pressure > 15 psig; MAWP marked and not exceeded; ASME Section VIII (U stamp), Section I power boilers (S stamp).

Exam tip: DOT governs cylinders (transport); ASME governs pressure vessels (stationary).

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 16 Electrical Safety, p. 427; Ch. 25 Construction Safety, p. 567; Ch. 29 Walking and Working Surfaces, p. 657

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

1.3-Q1. A worker uses a personal fall arrest system with a 6-ft shock-absorbing lanyard. Because of the work position, the worker's dorsal D-ring is 2 ft ABOVE the anchorage connection. The shock absorber can extend up to 3.5 ft. What is the free fall distance before the system begins to arrest the fall?

  1. 8 ft
  2. 9.5 ft
  3. 11.5 ft
  4. 13.5 ft

1.3-Q2. A worker uses a 6-ft shock-absorbing lanyard with the anchor at the same height as the D-ring. Deceleration distance is 3.5 ft, the D-ring is 5 ft above the worker's feet, and a 2-ft safety margin is required. What is the minimum clearance needed below the anchor (ignore harness stretch)?

  1. 11.5 ft
  2. 14.5 ft
  3. 16.5 ft
  4. 18.5 ft

1.3-Q3. A crew uses a 150 gpm pump to dewater a flooded trench 16 ft long and 3 ft wide, holding 3 ft of water. Using 7.48 gal per cubic foot and assuming no inflow, about how long will pumping take?

  1. 5.2 minutes
  2. 6.2 minutes
  3. 6.5 minutes
  4. 7.2 minutes

1.3-Q4. Workers are installing pipe in a 9-ft-deep trench in Type B soil with no protective system in place. The competent person has stepped away. What corrective action is required before work can continue?

  1. Provide hard hats and high-visibility vests
  2. Get workers out and install a protective system (sloping, shoring or shielding) before re-entry
  3. Backfill the trench at the end of the shift
  4. Place a ladder at one end of the trench

1.3-Q5. In a 6-ft-deep excavation, spoil piles have been placed right at the trench edge and rain has made the soil wet and heavy. What is the MOST appropriate corrective action?

  1. Place plywood over the trench as temporary protection
  2. Have the spoil moved at least 2 ft back from the edge (or retained by a device)
  3. Allow work to continue because the trench is less than 7 ft deep
  4. Build a berm of spoil at the edge to hold the soil in place

1.3-Q6. Under OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147, when may a tagout program be used instead of lockout?

  1. When an OSHA inspector authorizes it
  2. When two or more employees are involved
  3. When the work is considered low risk
  4. When the energy-isolating device is not capable of being locked out

1.3-Q7. An attendant outside a permit-required confined space sees that the entrant has stopped moving and is not responding. What should the attendant do FIRST?

  1. Summon rescue and emergency services and begin non-entry retrieval if equipped
  2. Enter the space to help the entrant
  3. Leave to find a supervisor with rescue equipment
  4. Shut down the ventilation system

1.3-Q8. An electrician is told to replace a breaker in an energized 480-V panel to avoid a production shutdown. Under NFPA 70E, what should be the FIRST consideration?

  1. Select arc-rated PPE from the PPE category tables
  2. Use insulated tools rated for 1,000 V
  3. Establish an electrically safe work condition (de-energize, lock out, test for absence of voltage)
  4. Have the electrician sign an energized work permit

1.3-Q9. Construction workers are placing forms on a bridge deck over a river, and fall protection does not fully eliminate the drowning hazard. Under OSHA 1926.106, which combination is required?

  1. USCG-approved life jackets, ring buoys with at least 90 ft of line, and a lifesaving skiff immediately available
  2. Hard hats and a high-visibility vest only
  3. A rescue plan that calls the local fire department
  4. Swim training for all employees on the crew

1.3-Q10. At a busy construction site, laborers on foot are repeatedly found in the travel paths of backing dump trucks. Which control is MOST effective at reducing the struck-by risk?

  1. Require high-visibility Class 3 vests for all laborers
  2. Implement an internal traffic control plan that physically separates pedestrian and vehicle routes and minimizes backing
  3. Remind drivers to sound the horn before backing
  4. Hold a toolbox talk on struck-by hazards

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1AWhen the D-ring is above the anchor, free fall = lanyard length + D-ring height above the anchor = 6 + 2 = 8 ft. The deceleration distance (3.5 ft) is added only for total fall/clearance, not free fall. Note this exceeds the 6-ft free fall limit.
2CClearance = free fall (6) + deceleration (3.5) + D-ring height (5) + safety margin (2) = 16.5 ft. Leaving out the D-ring height gives the 11.5-ft trap.
3DVolume = 16 x 3 x 3 = 144 ft3; 144 x 7.48 = 1,077 gal; 1,077 / 150 = 7.18 min.
4BTrenches 5 ft or deeper require a protective system unless they are in stable rock (1926.652). PPE and a ladder do not protect against a cave-in.
5B1926.651(j)(2) requires spoil and equipment to be kept at least 2 ft from the edge, or held back by retaining devices, to reduce surcharge loading and falling material.
6DTagout alone is allowed only when the isolating device cannot accept a lock. If it can, lockout is required unless tagout is shown to give full employee protection.
7AAttendants must not enter (1910.146(i)). They call rescue, attempt non-entry retrieval and stay at their post. Untrained would-be rescuers account for many confined space deaths.
8CNFPA 70E requires de-energizing as the primary protection method. Energized work is justified only if de-energizing creates a greater hazard or is infeasible, and production convenience is not a valid reason.
9A1926.106 requires approved life jackets or buoyant work vests, ring buoys with 90 ft of line no more than 200 ft apart, and at least one lifesaving skiff immediately available.
10BSeparating people from moving equipment, and designing routes that minimize backing, removes the exposure. Vests, horns and training are lower-order controls that still leave workers in the path.
Printed book page 23
Domain 1 · Competency 1.4

Facility Life Safety

CSP11 blueprint: Evaluate facility life safety features (e.g., public space safety, floor loading, occupancy loads)

1. Definition

Facility life safety is the design, construction and operation of buildings so occupants can survive a fire or other emergency long enough to escape or be protected in place. NFPA 101 Life Safety Code and the International Building/Fire Codes set requirements for means of egress, occupant load, compartmentation, alarms, sprinklers and emergency lighting. Floor loading ensures structures are not overloaded.

2. Explanation in simple words

A safe building lets everyone get out quickly and keeps the floor from collapsing. You count how many people can be in a space, make sure there are enough wide, unlocked, well-lit exits, keep smoke out of escape routes, and never put more weight on a floor than it was designed to carry.

3. Key points to remember

  • Means of egress has three parts: exit access (path to an exit), exit (protected route, e.g., enclosed stair) and exit discharge (to the public way).
  • Occupant load = area ÷ occupant load factor (ft² per person) from the code table. Examples (NFPA 101/IBC): business ~150 ft² gross; assembly with tables and chairs ~15 ft² net; standing assembly ~5-7 ft² net; storage ~300-500 ft² gross. Always use the factor given in the question and round up.
  • Egress capacity: width = occupant load × capacity factor (typically 0.2 in./person for level components and doors, 0.3 in./person for stairs). Minimum door width about 32 in. clear; exit doors must open without keys or special knowledge.
  • Number of exits: generally 2 minimum; 3 when occupant load is 501-1,000; 4 when over 1,000. Travel distance and dead-end limits depend on occupancy and sprinklers.
  • OSHA 1910.36/.37: exit routes kept free of obstructions, exits marked with illuminated signs, doors side-hinged and swinging in the direction of travel when the room holds more than 50 people or the area is high hazard.
  • Public space safety: crowd management (NFPA 101 requires trained crowd managers for large assembly occupancies, 1 per 250 occupants), emergency lighting (90 minutes), slip resistance, CPTED (crime prevention through environmental design).
  • Floor loading: rated live load in pounds per square foot (psf). Load intensity = weight ÷ contact area. Post the rated load where required (1910.22(b)); check mezzanines and racking. Point loads (rack legs, forklift wheels) can exceed slab capacity even when average psf is acceptable.

4. Real work scenario

A company converts a 6,000 ft² warehouse bay into a training hall with tables and chairs. Using 15 ft² per person, the occupant load becomes 400. The safety professional confirms the room now needs at least 80 in. of egress width (400 × 0.2), two separate exits remotely located, doors that swing outward with panic hardware, emergency lighting and a posted occupant load sign.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Occupant load and floor load questions are straight calculations; read the units (gross vs net area, psf).
  • An increased occupant load (change of use) triggers new egress requirements - a favorite scenario.
  • Propped-open fire or smoke doors and locked exits are always serious life-safety violations.
  • Floor load trap: divide by AREA (ft²), not by one side length.

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 0 more)

Public space safetyBLUEPRINT

Places open to the public (assembly, retail, healthcare) must protect occupants who are unfamiliar with the building.

  • Clear, marked and lit means of egress; exit signs; doors with panic hardware in assembly occupancies.
  • Emergency lighting: ≥ 1 foot-candle (10 lux) average at floor along exit paths, 90-minute duration; test monthly ≥ 30 seconds and annually for 90 minutes.
  • Crowd managers for large assembly occupancies (NFPA 101: 1 per 250 occupants).
  • Slip resistance, lighting, CPTED (crime prevention through environmental design), smoke compartments kept closed (never prop smoke/fire doors).

Exam tip: Remember emergency lighting: monthly 30 s + annual 90 min.

Floor loadingBLUEPRINT

Floors, mezzanines and platforms must not be loaded beyond their design (maximum intended) load (1910.22(b)).

  • Load intensity (psf) = weight ÷ contact area (ft²).
  • Post and enforce the maximum load limit where required (warehouses, mezzanines, storage areas).
  • Point loads (rack legs, forklift wheels) can exceed slab capacity even when average psf is acceptable.

Exam tip: Heavy-storage warehouse scenario: post and enforce floor load limits.

Occupancy loadsBLUEPRINT

Occupant load = floor area ÷ occupant load factor (ft² per person) from NFPA 101/IBC tables; it drives the number and width of exits.

  • Factors in the guide (NFPA 101 table): assembly standing ~7 ft²/person net (5 in concentrated areas), assembly with chairs ~15 ft² net, business 100 ft² (current editions use 150 ft² gross), industrial 100 ft², storage 300 ft².
  • Egress width = occupant load × 0.2 in. (level/doors) or 0.3 in. (stairs).
  • Exits: 2 minimum; 3 for 501-1,000; 4 for > 1,000 occupants.
  • Use the factor given in the question and round up.

Exam tip: Change of use (storage → training room) increases occupant load and egress needs.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 12 Fire Prevention and Protection, p. 350; Ch. 29 Walking and Working Surfaces, p. 657

1.4-Q7. Under NFPA 101, battery-powered emergency lighting must be tested:

  1. Weekly for 5 minutes
  2. Quarterly for 10 minutes
  3. Monthly for at least 30 seconds and annually for 90 minutes
  4. Every 6 months for 60 minutes

1.4-Q8. In a new healthcare wing, contractors prop open smoke-barrier doors for long periods while moving equipment between patient sleeping units. Under NFPA 101, what is the GREATEST concern?

  1. Door hardware will wear out prematurely
  2. The facility may be cited for fire drill documentation
  3. Employees may trip on staged equipment
  4. The propped doors compromise smoke-compartment integrity, which defend-in-place protection depends on

1.4-Q9. A warehouse has 150,000 ft2 total area, of which 30,000 ft2 is not usable (offices, equipment, aisles). If the maximum allowable stack height is 30 ft, what is the usable storage volume?

  1. 900,000 ft3
  2. 3,600,000 ft3
  3. 4,500,000 ft3
  4. 5,400,000 ft3

1.4-Q10. To deter shoplifting, a retail store manager chains one of the two required exit doors during business hours. What is the BEST response from the safety professional?

  1. Allow it if an employee carries a key to the chain
  2. Post an "Emergency Exit - Use Other Door" sign on the chained door
  3. Have the chain removed immediately; required exits must be openable from the egress side without keys or special knowledge (consider approved delayed-egress hardware)
  4. Allow it as long as the other exit is unobstructed

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1DA change of use that raises occupant load requires a re-check of egress capacity and door width. A 24-in. door is below the minimum clear width for a means-of-egress door.
2BRequired width = 300 x 0.2 in. = 60 in. That total is then divided among the required exits, each of which must also meet the minimum door width. Using 0.3 in./person (the stair factor) gives 90 in.
3CLoad = weight / area = 2,400 lb / 16 ft2 = 150 psf, which exceeds 125 psf. The 600 psf distractor divides by one side (4 ft) instead of the area.
4AOccupant load = area / factor = 9,000 / 150 = 60 persons. Use the factor given in the stem; dividing by 100 (an older business factor) gives 90.
5B6,000 / 15 = 400 persons. The 857 distractor uses 7 ft2 per person, which applies to concentrated assembly with chairs only.
6ANFPA 101 requires an initial average of 1 ft-candle (10.8 lux) and a minimum of 0.1 ft-candle at any point, for at least 90 minutes.
7CNFPA 101 requires a 30-second functional test every month and a full 1.5-hour test every year, with written records kept.
8DHealthcare occupancies use a defend-in-place strategy that relies on smoke compartments. Doors must self-close, or be held open only by devices that release on alarm.
9BUsable area = 150,000 - 30,000 = 120,000 ft2; x 30 ft = 3,600,000 ft3. Using the gross area gives 4,500,000 ft3, which is the trap. Actual loading must still be checked against floor-load ratings and sprinkler clearance.
10CNFPA 101 and OSHA 1910.36/.37 prohibit locking required exits against egress while the building is occupied. Approved delayed-egress or alarmed hardware addresses theft without trapping occupants.
Printed book page 27
Domain 1 · Competency 1.5

Fleet Safety

CSP11 blueprint: Describe fleet safety principles (e.g., driver and equipment safety, maintenance, surveillance equipment, GPS monitoring, telematics, hybrid vehicles, fuel systems, driving under the influence, fatigue)

1. Definition

Fleet safety is the management system that controls risk from company-owned or operated vehicles: selecting and training drivers, maintaining vehicles, monitoring driving behavior, and controlling impairment and fatigue. Commercial motor vehicles in the U.S. are regulated by FMCSA (49 CFR Parts 382-396), and ANSI/ASSP Z15.1 gives a fleet safety management framework.

2. Explanation in simple words

Motor vehicle crashes are a leading cause of work deaths. A good fleet program hires good drivers, keeps vehicles in good repair, watches how people drive with technology, and makes sure nobody drives tired, drunk or distracted.

3. Key points to remember

  • ANSI/ASSP Z15.1 elements: management leadership, operational environment, driver selection and qualification (MVR checks), driver behavior/training, vehicle selection and maintenance, incident reporting and analysis.
  • FMCSA hours of service (property carriers): 11 hours driving after 10 hours off duty, 14-hour on-duty window, 30-minute break after 8 hours driving, 60/70-hour weekly limits; electronic logging devices (ELDs) record duty status.
  • Drug and alcohol testing (49 CFR 382 / Part 40): pre-employment, random, reasonable suspicion, post-accident, return-to-duty and follow-up. Alcohol 0.02-0.039 BAC = removed for 24 hours; 0.04+ = violation requiring SAP evaluation before return.
  • Maintenance: driver pre-trip/post-trip inspection (DVIR), preventive maintenance schedule, annual inspection for CMVs, records retained.
  • Telematics/GPS: data on speeding, harsh braking/acceleration, cornering, seat belt use, idling, location; inward/outward cameras. Use data for coaching and root cause (routes, schedules, fatigue), not only discipline.
  • Hybrid/electric vehicles: high-voltage systems (orange cables), follow OEM disable procedure and capacitor discharge time, HV-rated gloves for service, lithium battery thermal runaway risk.
  • Fuel systems: gasoline (flammable, vapor heavier than air), diesel (combustible), CNG/LNG/propane (pressure, leaks, ventilation in garages), hydrogen. Static bonding during fueling, no smoking, engine off.
  • Fatigue: circadian low 2-6 a.m., sleep debt, shift length; controls include scheduling limits, rest breaks, fatigue risk management systems. Distracted driving: hands-free policies, no texting (FMCSA bans hand-held phones for CMV drivers).
  • Rates: crashes per million miles = crashes × 1,000,000 ÷ miles driven.

4. Real work scenario

Telematics shows that three drivers on overnight routes generate most harsh-braking events. Instead of firing them, the safety manager reviews the routes and schedules with the drivers and finds they are working 13-hour shifts with tight delivery windows. The company adds a second driver to the longest route and moves delivery windows, and harsh-braking events drop by 60%.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • When telematics data appear in a question, the BEST answer usually analyzes and coaches before punishing.
  • Know the BAC thresholds (0.02 and 0.04) and the 11/14-hour rules.
  • For hybrids and EVs, the answer involves isolating the high-voltage system, not only the 12-V battery.
  • Fleet rate calculations use per 1,000,000 miles (sometimes 100,000 miles) - read the stem.

6. Every blueprint point (7 blueprint items + 0 more)

Driver safety, licensing and trainingBLUEPRINT

Driver selection and qualification (MVR checks, CDL where required) and training in defensive driving, hazard recognition and company policy.

  • ANSI/ASSP Z15.1 = safe practices for motor vehicle operations.
  • Defensive driving systems: Smith System (5 keys: aim high in steering, get the big picture, keep your eyes moving, leave yourself an out, make sure they see you), IPDE (identify, predict, decide, execute), SEE (search, evaluate, execute).
  • Following distance 3-4 s cars, 6-8 s trucks; reduce speed by 1/3 in rain, by 1/2 on snow/ice; seat belts required (FMCSA 392.16).
  • DOT physical every 2 years (vision 20/40 corrected, forced whisper at 5 ft, blood pressure); untreated sleep apnea can disqualify.

Equipment safety and maintenanceBLUEPRINT

Vehicles are selected for safety features and kept in safe condition.

  • Safety features: ABS, electronic stability control, backup cameras, collision mitigation, lane departure warning.
  • Driver pre-trip/post-trip inspection (DVIR); preventive maintenance schedule; defects corrected before dispatch; annual inspection for CMVs; records kept.

Surveillance equipment, GPS monitoring and telematicsBLUEPRINT

Technology to monitor and coach driver behavior.

  • GPS monitoring: routes, speeds, idle time, location.
  • Telematics: GPS + vehicle diagnostics - engine faults, seat belt use, harsh braking/acceleration, real-time data, predictive maintenance.
  • Cameras/event recorders: dash and inward-facing; used for incident investigation and coaching with recorded footage; balance with privacy.
  • Use data to find root causes (schedules, routes, fatigue) and coach, not only to discipline.

Exam tip: Engine faults/seat belt/real-time data = telematics; routes/speed/idle = GPS; training with footage = event recorder.

Hybrid and alternative-fuel vehiclesBLUEPRINT

High-voltage battery packs and alternative fuels add shock, arc flash, fire and explosion hazards.

  • Orange cables = high voltage; follow OEM disable procedure and capacitor discharge time; HV-rated gloves and arc-rated gear for service (NFPA 70E).
  • Lithium battery fires: thermal runaway, re-ignition; water alone may not be effective quickly - special responder training.
  • Emergency responders need EV/hybrid-specific procedures.

Fuel systemsBLUEPRINT

Each fuel has specific fire, pressure and static hazards.

  • Gasoline/diesel: flammable/combustible; bond and ground to control static.
  • CNG/LNG/propane: high-pressure or cryogenic storage per DOT/ASME; upright, ventilated; garages need ventilation/gas detection.
  • Hydrogen: extremely flammable, governed by NFPA 2.
  • Fueling: engine off, no smoking, no cell phone use near pumps.

Exam tip: 'Which fuel system requires NFPA 2?' = hydrogen.

Driving under the influence (DUI) and drug/alcohol testingBLUEPRINT

FMCSA (49 CFR 382, Part 40) testing for CDL drivers.

  • Tests: pre-employment, random, reasonable suspicion, post-accident, return-to-duty, follow-up.
  • BAC 0.04% or higher = violation for CDL drivers (stricter than public 0.08%); 0.02-0.039 = off duty for 24 hours.
  • Zero tolerance for illegal drugs; violation = removal from safety-sensitive work, SAP evaluation before return.
  • Signs of impairment: slurred speech, erratic driving, odor of alcohol, pinpoint pupils (opioids).

Exam tip: Impaired operator observed: remove from safety-sensitive work immediately and follow the reasonable-suspicion process.

Fatigue management (hours of service)BLUEPRINT

Fatigue is as dangerous as alcohol impairment; microsleeps at highway speed cover long distances blind.

  • Property carriers: 11 hours driving after 10 consecutive hours off; 14-hour on-duty window; 30-minute break after 8 hours driving; 60 h/7 days or 70 h/8 days.
  • Electronic logging devices record hours.
  • Controls: scheduling, route planning, rest breaks, fatigue risk management, sleep apnea screening.

Exam tip: Numbers 11/14/30/60/70 = hours-of-service question.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 25 Construction Safety (vehicles); Ch. 30 Materials Handling and Storage (powered vehicles)

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

1.5-Q1. A CDL driver's random alcohol test shows 0.03 BAC. Under FMCSA rules (49 CFR Part 382), the employer must:

  1. Remove the driver from safety-sensitive functions for at least 24 hours
  2. Allow the driver to continue because the result is below 0.04
  3. Terminate the driver immediately
  4. Retest after 8 hours and then return the driver to duty

1.5-Q2. The Smith System defensive-driving principle "Leave Yourself an Out" emphasizes:

  1. Avoiding distractions inside the cab
  2. Keeping space cushions around the vehicle so there is an escape path
  3. Obeying posted traffic laws
  4. Wearing seatbelts at all times

1.5-Q3. A logistics company's telematics show that a driver's lane-departure warnings and harsh-braking events rise sharply after 9 hours on shift. What is the MOST effective immediate action?

  1. Disable the lane-departure warnings to reduce distraction
  2. Move the driver to night routes when traffic is lighter
  3. Assign an online fatigue-awareness module after the shift
  4. Require an immediate rest break and review shift scheduling practices

1.5-Q4. During scheduled maintenance on a hybrid delivery van, a technician smells fuel vapor near the rear tank. The van also has a high-voltage battery system. What is the safest procedure?

  1. Continue working and finish the battery inspection first
  2. Apply sealant to the suspected leak before shutting down power
  3. Disconnect only the 12-V battery and proceed
  4. Ventilate the area, isolate both the fuel system and the high-voltage system, and follow the manufacturer's hybrid lockout procedure

1.5-Q5. A fleet drove 4,500,000 miles last year and had 18 preventable crashes. What is the preventable crash rate per million miles?

  1. 0.25
  2. 2.5
  3. 4.0
  4. 40

1.5-Q6. Your company drove 57,553,441 miles last year and had 42 collisions. Using Fleet incident rate = (incidents x 1,000,000) / miles driven, what is the rate?

  1. 0.42
  2. 0.57
  3. 0.73
  4. 1.37

1.5-Q7. Under FMCSA Hours-of-Service rules for property-carrying drivers, what is the maximum driving time allowed after 10 consecutive hours off duty?

  1. 8 hours
  2. 10 hours
  3. 11 hours
  4. 14 hours

1.5-Q8. A skid steer is stolen overnight from a construction site. The machine's telematics show it was moved off-site at 2:15 a.m., and the security cameras on the south fence were offline. What should be the FIRST preventive improvement?

  1. Provide reliable/redundant power to the cameras and set geofence alerts that trigger when equipment moves after hours
  2. Have operators take equipment home after each shift
  3. Leave all keys in the cab at night
  4. Remove GPS units so thieves cannot tamper with them

1.5-Q9. What is the PRIMARY safety purpose of event recorders (inward- and outward-facing cameras) in a fleet program?

  1. Monitoring vehicle emissions
  2. Capturing driving events for investigation and targeted driver coaching
  3. Replacing GPS tracking
  4. Automatically enforcing seatbelt use

1.5-Q10. A truck driver's post-trip Driver Vehicle Inspection Report (DVIR) lists defective brakes. Under 49 CFR 396.11, before the vehicle is dispatched again the motor carrier must:

  1. Let the next driver decide whether the vehicle is safe
  2. Reduce the vehicle's load by 25% until repaired
  3. Record the defect in the maintenance log for the next scheduled PM
  4. Repair the defect and certify on the DVIR that it was repaired (or that repair was unnecessary)

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1AA result of 0.02-0.039 requires removal from safety-sensitive functions for at least 24 hours. A result of 0.04 or more is a violation that triggers removal and the SAP return-to-duty process.
2B"Leave yourself an out" means managing the space around the vehicle so you can always steer away from a hazard. The other Smith keys are: aim high, get the big picture, keep your eyes moving, and make sure they see you.
3DThe data show fatigue in progress. Stopping the exposure now and fixing the scheduling (the root cause) is best. Night routes make circadian fatigue worse.
4DHybrids combine a flammable fuel hazard with a high-voltage electrical ignition source. Both energy sources must be controlled per the OEM procedure; the 12-V battery alone does not de-energize the HV system.
5CRate = crashes x 1,000,000 / miles = 18 x 1,000,000 / 4,500,000 = 4.0. Always check the normalizing factor (per million miles versus per 100,000 miles).
6C42 x 1,000,000 / 57,553,441 = 0.73 collisions per million miles. 1.37 is the inverse (miles/incidents scaled); 0.42 and 0.57 simply misread the inputs.
7CProperty carriers may drive up to 11 hours within a 14-hour on-duty window after 10 consecutive hours off. The 14-hour figure is the duty window, a classic distractor.
8ACombining surveillance with telematics geofencing gives real-time detection. The other options remove protection or increase the risk of theft.
9BEvent recorders capture video around harsh events, which supports accurate investigations and individualized coaching. Coaching is what produces the safety benefit.
10DDefects that affect safe operation must be repaired, and the repair certified on the DVIR, before the vehicle is operated again. The next driver must review that certification.
