Domain 1 · Competency 1.6

Materials Handling

CSP11 blueprint: Evaluate materials handling methods and controls (e.g., forklifts, aerial lifts, and other powered industrial trucks; cranes, hand trucks, hoists, rigging, manual handling, drones)

1. Definition

Materials handling covers the lifting, moving, storing and placing of materials by hand or by machine. Hazards include tip-over, struck-by, crush, overload, dropped loads, musculoskeletal injury and collisions with pedestrians. Key standards: OSHA 1910.178 (powered industrial trucks), 1910.179/1926 Subpart CC (cranes), 1910.184/1926.251 (slings and rigging), ANSI A92 (aerial work platforms), NIOSH lifting equation, FAA Part 107 (drones).

2. Explanation in simple words

Anything that lifts or moves a load can drop it, tip over or hit someone. You choose the right equipment, train and evaluate the operator, inspect the gear, know the weight and center of gravity, and keep people out of the path of the load.

3. Key points to remember

  • Forklifts/PITs: operator training and evaluation every 3 years (and after incidents, near misses, unsafe operation or new truck type); daily pre-use inspection; stability triangle - lateral tip-over from turning with a raised load, longitudinal tip-over from overload; travel with load low and tilted back; loaded travel up a ramp with load upgrade; no modifications affecting capacity without manufacturer's written approval.
  • Aerial lifts (boom and scissor, ANSI A92.22/.24): boom lifts require fall restraint/arrest attached to the basket anchor; operators must not climb the rails; check ground conditions, overhead power lines and wind limits.
  • Cranes: qualified/certified operators (1926.1427), load charts, critical lift plans (often > 75% of capacity, multiple cranes, personnel lifting, near power lines), anti-two-block devices, outrigger setup on firm ground, 20-ft clearance from power lines up to 350 kV (1926.1408), inspections each shift/monthly/annually. Personnel platforms: total load ≤ 50% of rated capacity, trial lift and proof test at 125%.
  • Rigging: know load weight and center of gravity. Sling tension per leg = (W ÷ number of legs) ÷ sin(angle from horizontal). Angle factors: 60° ×1.155, 45° ×1.414, 30° ×2.0. Avoid angles below 30°. Inspect slings before each use; wire rope removal criteria (e.g., broken wires, kinks, birdcaging, heat damage). Three wire rope clips minimum, U-bolts on the dead end ('never saddle a dead horse').
  • Hoists and hand trucks: rated capacity marking, hook latches, push rather than pull hand trucks, keep the load center over the wheels.
  • Manual handling: NIOSH Revised Lifting Equation RWL = LC × HM × VM × DM × AM × FM × CM (LC = 51 lb); Lifting Index = load ÷ RWL; LI > 1 increases risk, LI > 3 high risk. Controls: lift assists, adjust heights (waist level), reduce reach, team lifts as a last resort.
  • Drones (FAA Part 107): remote pilot certificate, visual line of sight, ≤ 400 ft AGL, daylight/twilight rules, restrictions over people; include flight paths and no-fly zones in the JHA.

4. Real work scenario

A plant must lift a 6,000-lb motor with a two-leg bridle. The rigger's first plan gives a 30° sling angle because the headroom is low, which would put 6,000 lb on each leg. The safety professional asks for a spreader beam to make the legs vertical, which drops the tension to 3,000 lb per leg, and confirms the crane load chart includes the weight of the hook block and rigging.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Sling-angle calculations appear often: divide by the number of legs FIRST, then by sin θ (θ from horizontal).
  • In a three- or four-leg bridle, assume only two legs carry the load unless told otherwise.
  • Total crane load = load + hook block + rigging; compare to the chart at the actual radius.
  • Forklift questions: lateral tip-over is about center of gravity leaving the stability triangle; the answer to tip-over survival is 'stay in the seat, hold on, lean away'.

6. Every blueprint point (8 blueprint items + 1 more)

Forklifts and other powered industrial trucksBLUEPRINT

1910.178 covers forklifts, pallet jacks, reach trucks, tow tractors; hazards include tip-over, struck-by, falling loads, pedestrian collisions, fuel/battery hazards.

  • Training: formal + practical + evaluation; evaluation at least every 3 years; refresher after unsafe operation, accident/near miss, poor evaluation, new truck type or workplace change.
  • Daily pre-use inspection; defective trucks removed from service; design per ANSI/ITSDF B56.1; no capacity-affecting modifications without manufacturer's written approval.
  • Safe operation: seat belt (tip-over protection), load low and tilted back, no riders, avoid turning on grades, travel loaded up a ramp with load upgrade, horn at intersections.
  • Classes: I-III electric; IV-V internal combustion (cushion/pneumatic tires); VI tractors; VII rough terrain. IC trucks indoors need CO monitoring/ventilation.
  • Stability triangle: lateral tip-over when combined CG leaves the triangle (turning with raised load).

Exam tip: Tip-over scenario: seat belt; stay in seat, hold on, lean away.

Aerial liftsBLUEPRINT

Boom lifts and scissor lifts (ANSI A92.20/.22/.24); OSHA treats scissor lifts as mobile scaffolds.

  • Boom lifts: body harness with lanyard attached to the basket anchor; stay tied off when repositioning.
  • Do not climb or stand on rails; check ground conditions, slopes, overhead power lines and wind limits.
  • Operator training and familiarization; pre-use inspection and function test.

CranesBLUEPRINT

Mobile/tower cranes (1926 Subpart CC) and overhead/gantry cranes (1910.179).

  • Operators trained and certified/qualified (1926.1427); qualified signal persons; qualified riggers.
  • Load chart for configuration and radius; total load includes hook block and rigging; critical lift plans (often > 75% capacity, tandem, personnel, near power lines).
  • Power lines up to 350 kV: maintain 20 ft (1926.1408); if contact occurs, operator stays in the cab.
  • Inspections: each shift, monthly, annual. Anti-two-block devices required.
  • Personnel platforms (1926.1431): total load ≤ 50% of rated capacity, trial lift, proof test at 125%, crane level within 1%.

Hand trucksBLUEPRINT

Two-wheel and four-wheel hand trucks reduce manual carrying but still cause strains and crush injuries.

  • Keep load center of gravity low and over the axle; tilt so weight is on wheels.
  • Push rather than pull where possible; go down ramps with the truck ahead (or backwards with load uphill as manufacturer directs).
  • Rated capacity respected; secure loads; keep hands inside handles.

HoistsBLUEPRINT

Chain hoists, wire rope hoists and monorails used for lifting within plants.

  • Rated load marked; hook safety latches; inspect hooks, chains and wire rope before use.
  • Never lift people with material hoists; keep clear of suspended loads.

RiggingBLUEPRINT

Slings and hardware connecting the load to the hook.

  • Know load weight and center of gravity before selecting slings.
  • Sling tension per leg = (W ÷ legs) ÷ sin θ (θ from horizontal); 60° ×1.155, 45° ×1.414, 30° ×2.0 (capacity reduced ~50%); avoid < 30°.
  • Wire rope slings typically 5:1 design factor; chain slings must be alloy (Grade 80+) with tags; synthetic slings protected from chemicals, heat and sharp edges.
  • Inspect slings before each shift/use; remove damaged slings from service.
  • Taglines control swinging loads; never stand under a suspended load.

Exam tip: As sling angle decreases, tension increases.

Manual handlingBLUEPRINT

Lifting, lowering, carrying, pushing and pulling by hand - a major source of MSDs.

  • NIOSH RWL = LC × HM × VM × DM × AM × FM × CM; LC = 51 lb; HM = 10/H; VM = 1 − 0.0075|V − 30|; DM = 0.82 + 1.8/D; AM = 1 − 0.0032A; FM and CM from tables (coupling good 1.0, fair 0.95, poor 0.90).
  • LI = load ÷ RWL; LI ≤ 1.0 acceptable for most workers; > 1.0 increased risk - redesign.
  • Ideal lift height ~30 in. (knuckle height); keep loads close; avoid floor and overhead lifts; use mechanical aids.

Exam tip: Most tested NIOSH fact: LC = 51 lb; twisting (AM) and frequency (FM) lower the safe weight the most.

DronesBLUEPRINT

Unmanned aircraft systems for inventory, inspection, delivery and remote monitoring.

  • FAA Part 107: Remote Pilot certificate, aircraft < 55 lb, ≤ 400 ft AGL, visual line of sight, restrictions over people.
  • Benefits: inspect high racks, tanks and roofs without ladders or scaffolds.
  • Controls: planned flight paths away from people, no-fly zones, barriers, pre-flight checks.

Exam tip: Drone compliance keywords: FAA Part 107, < 55 lb, < 400 ft.

Scaffolds (supported and suspended)

Temporary elevated platforms (1926 Subpart L).

  • Support own weight + 4× intended load; suspension ropes 6× load.
  • Fully planked, gaps < 1 in.; guardrails at ≥ 10 ft (construction), top rail 38-45 in.
  • Ties every 26 ft vertically and 30 ft horizontally (when height-to-base ratio > 4:1).
  • Competent person supervises erection and inspects before each shift and after events; never climb cross-braces.
  • Platform ≤ 14 in. from the face of work unless guarded.

Exam tip: Frequently tested: 4× load, 26 ft vertical / 30 ft horizontal ties, 10 ft fall protection.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 30 Materials Handling and Storage, p. 687; Ch. 24 Ergonomics (lifting), p. 546

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

1.6-Q1. A 500-lb pipe is lifted by a two-leg bridle sling with the load centered. Each leg makes a 60-degree angle with the horizontal. What is the tension in each leg?

  1. 250 lb
  2. 289 lb
  3. 354 lb
  4. 500 lb

1.6-Q2. A 2,000-lb load is supported by a two-leg sling, and each leg makes a 45-degree angle with the horizontal. What force is exerted in each leg?

  1. 1,000 lb
  2. 1,155 lb
  3. 1,414 lb
  4. 2,000 lb

1.6-Q3. A forklift travels at 6 mph (about 8.8 ft/s). Operator perception-reaction time is 1.5 s and braking deceleration is 4 ft/s2. What is the total stopping distance (reaction distance + braking distance, where braking = v2/2a)?

  1. 9.7 ft
  2. 13.2 ft
  3. 22.9 ft
  4. 25.0 ft

1.6-Q4. A mobile crane load chart shows 10,000 lb capacity at the planned radius. The load weighs 8,200 lb, the hook block 450 lb and the rigging 150 lb. What percentage of rated capacity is the lift?

  1. 82%
  2. 85%
  3. 88%
  4. 92%

1.6-Q5. Using the NIOSH lifting equation: LC = 51 lb and horizontal distance H = 20 in. (HM = 10/H). All other multipliers equal 1.0. The load weighs 30 lb. What is the Lifting Index?

  1. 0.59
  2. 0.85
  3. 1.18
  4. 1.70

1.6-Q6. A crane will work near a power line whose voltage has not been confirmed by the utility. Under OSHA 1926.1408(a)(2), with the line assumed to be up to 350 kV, no part of the crane, load line or load may come closer to the line than:

  1. 5 ft
  2. 10 ft
  3. 15 ft
  4. 20 ft

1.6-Q7. A team must lift a 3,000-lb component with a 5-ton bridge crane. The only available wire rope sling has a visible cut in its outer strands. What is the safest course of action?

  1. Remove the damaged sling from service and defer the lift until an inspected, compliant sling is available
  2. Proceed but reduce crane speed to compensate
  3. Have a qualified rigger certify the damaged sling for one-time use
  4. Use the sling only if the load is under 50% of its rated capacity

1.6-Q8. A warehouse is piloting drones (UAS) to move small parts between workstations. During testing, a drone flies over occupied work areas and briefly loses stability in an HVAC air current. What is the BEST control?

  1. Limit drone use to break and lunch periods
  2. Redesign flight paths to avoid flying over people, using barriers or no-fly zones
  3. Require hard hats while drones operate
  4. Continue testing and add signage

1.6-Q9. A worker is operating a boom-supported aerial lift (articulating boom). Under OSHA 1926.453, what fall protection practice is required?

  1. Wear a body harness or restraint with the lanyard attached to the boom or basket, never to an adjacent pole or structure
  2. No fall protection is needed if the guardrails are intact
  3. Tie off to a nearby structure for maximum anchorage strength
  4. Stand on the midrail to reach higher work

1.6-Q10. A sit-down counterbalanced forklift begins to tip over sideways. What should the operator do?

  1. Jump clear on the downhill side
  2. Lower the forks quickly to the ground
  3. Jump clear on the uphill side
  4. Stay in the seat with the seatbelt on, grip the wheel, brace the feet and lean away from the direction of the tip

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BTension per leg = (W/2) / sin(angle) = 250 / 0.866 = 289 lb. 250 lb ignores the angle; 354 lb uses 45 degrees.
2CEach leg = (2,000/2) / sin 45 = 1,000 / 0.707 = 1,414 lb. 1,155 lb would be the answer at 60 degrees. As the angle from horizontal decreases, leg tension increases.
3CReaction = 8.8 x 1.5 = 13.2 ft; braking = 8.8^2 / (2 x 4) = 77.4 / 8 = 9.7 ft; total = 22.9 ft. Picking only one of the two parts is the trap.
4CTotal = 8,200 + 450 + 150 = 8,800 lb; 8,800 / 10,000 = 88%. Hook block and rigging count against chart capacity, and many companies classify a lift above 75-85% of capacity as critical.
5CHM = 10/20 = 0.5; RWL = 51 x 0.5 = 25.5 lb; LI = 30 / 25.5 = 1.18. An LI above 1.0 means increased risk, so redesign the task, for example by reducing H.
6D1926.1408 sets a 20-ft minimum approach distance for lines up to 350 kV unless the utility confirms the voltage, in which case Table A applies (for example 10 ft up to 50 kV). Over 350 kV the distance is 50 ft.
7A1910.184 requires slings with damage such as cut strands to be removed from service immediately. Derating or "one-time use" is not permitted.
8BKeeping the flight path away from people removes exposure to falling drones and payloads. Hard hats and signs are lower-order controls.
9A1926.453(b)(2)(v) requires a body belt/harness with the lanyard attached to the boom or basket. Tying off to an adjacent structure can pull the worker out if the lift moves. Standing on rails is prohibited.
10DMost forklift tipover deaths occur when operators try to jump and are crushed by the overhead guard. Staying belted in the operator compartment is the trained response.
Printed book page 37
Domain 1 · Competency 1.7

Tools, Machines and Equipment

CSP11 blueprint: Evaluate the use of tools, machines, and equipment (e.g., hand tools, power tools, ladders, grinders, hydraulics, robotics)

1. Definition

This competency covers the selection, guarding, inspection and safe use of hand tools, power tools, ladders, abrasive grinders, hydraulic systems, machines and industrial/collaborative robots. Key references: OSHA 1910 Subpart O (machine guarding, 1910.212), Subpart P (hand and portable powered tools, 1910.242-.244), 1910.23 and 1926 Subpart X (ladders), ANSI B11 series, ANSI/RIA R15.06 and ISO 10218 (robots).

2. Explanation in simple words

Tools and machines hurt people through the moving parts, flying pieces, electricity and stored pressure. Guard the moving parts, inspect tools before use, use the right tool for the job, and lock out energy before maintenance.

3. Key points to remember

  • Machine hazards: point of operation, power transmission (belts, gears, shafts) and other moving parts; motions (rotating, reciprocating, transverse) and actions (cutting, punching, shearing, bending). Guard types: fixed (preferred - simple and permanent), interlocked, adjustable, self-adjusting. Devices: presence-sensing (light curtains), pullback, restraint, two-hand controls/trips, gates.
  • Guard requirements: prevent contact, secure (not easily removed), protect from falling objects, create no new hazards, allow safe lubrication.
  • Hand tools: most injuries come from misuse and poor maintenance (mushroomed chisel heads, cracked handles, wrong tool). Employer is responsible for tool condition, including employee-owned tools.
  • Power tools: double-insulated or grounded; GFCI on construction sites; never carry by the cord; disconnect before changing blades; guards in place on circular saws; powder-actuated tools require training and licensed operators; compressed air for cleaning limited to < 30 psi with chip guarding (never on the body).
  • Grinders (1910.215): work rest ≤ 1/8 in. from the wheel; tongue guard ≤ 1/4 in.; ring test wheels before mounting; never exceed rated RPM; stand aside during first minute of startup; face shield plus safety glasses.
  • Ladders: 4:1 rule (base 1 ft out for every 4 ft of height, about 75.5°); side rails extend 3 ft above the landing; Type IA = 300 lb, Type I = 250 lb, Type II = 225 lb, Type IAA = 375 lb; top step/cap of stepladder not used; inspect before use; three points of contact. New fixed ladders over 24 ft need a ladder safety system or PFAS (1910.28).
  • Hydraulics: high-pressure fluid injection injuries are surgical emergencies; never check for leaks with hands; relieve pressure and block raised parts (gravity energy) before work.
  • Robotics: perimeter guarding with interlocked gates, teach mode at reduced speed (≤ 250 mm/s) with enabling device, LOTO for maintenance; collaborative robots use power and force limiting, speed and separation monitoring, hand guiding or safety-rated monitored stop - all based on a risk assessment (ISO/TS 15066).

4. Real work scenario

Operators on a packaging line keep opening a guard to clear jams, and one suffers a finger injury. The safety professional finds that the jams happen at a fixed spot. Engineering adds an interlocked access door with a jam-clearing tool slot and adjusts the infeed guide so that jams drop by 80%. Training alone had not stopped the behavior because the guard was blocking the job.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • When workers bypass guards, the BEST answer fixes the reason (design the task so the guard is not in the way) rather than only disciplining.
  • Fixed guards are generally the preferred guard type; light curtains are devices, not guards.
  • Grinder numbers: 1/8 in. work rest, 1/4 in. tongue guard - learn them together.
  • Ladder angle calculations use the 4:1 rule and the Pythagorean theorem (ladder length = √(height² + base²) + 3 ft extension).

6. Every blueprint point (6 blueprint items + 1 more)

Hand toolsBLUEPRINT

Most hand-tool injuries come from misuse and poor maintenance.

  • Employer is responsible for safe condition of all tools, including employee-owned.
  • Hazards: mushroomed chisel heads, cracked handles, wrong tool, dull blades; inspect, dress or remove from service.
  • Use spark-resistant tools near flammables; tether tools at height.

Power toolsBLUEPRINT

Electric, pneumatic, hydraulic, powder-actuated and fuel-powered tools.

  • Electrical: double-insulated or grounded; GFCI on construction sites; never carry by the cord or yank to disconnect; disconnect before changing blades/bits.
  • Guards stay on (circular saw lower guard); anti-kickback pawls and riving knives on saws.
  • Pneumatic: secure hoses, safety clips on tools; compressed air for cleaning < 30 psi with chip guarding, never on the body.
  • Powder-actuated tools: trained operators, never point at anyone, wait 30 s after misfire.
  • Locking triggers/continuous-run features need dead-man or automatic shutoff; sequential-trip nailers reduce injuries.

LaddersBLUEPRINT

Portable and fixed ladders (1910.23, 1926 Subpart X).

  • 4:1 rule (~75°); side rails extend 3 ft above the landing; secure top and bottom; three points of contact.
  • Duty ratings: IAA 375 lb, IA 300 lb, I 250 lb, II 225 lb, III 200 lb.
  • Do not use the top step/cap of a stepladder; non-conductive side rails near electricity; inspect by a competent person; remove defective ladders.
  • Rungs 10-14 in. apart; a ladder or stairway is required at a break in elevation of 19 in. or more (construction).

Grinders (abrasive wheels)BLUEPRINT

Bench, pedestal and portable grinders can burst and throw fragments.

  • Work rest ≤ 1/8 in. from wheel; tongue guard ≤ 1/4 in.
  • Ring-test wheels before mounting; never exceed the rated RPM; stand aside during start-up.
  • Safety glasses plus face shield; side guards in place.

Exam tip: Grinder clearances 1/8 in. and 1/4 in. appear often.

HydraulicsBLUEPRINT

Hydraulic presses, jacks, lifts and lines store high pressure and gravity energy.

  • Relieve pressure (bleed lines, accumulators) and block raised parts before maintenance - part of LOTO.
  • Never search for leaks with hands; use cardboard. Fluid injection injuries are surgical emergencies.
  • Inspect hoses and fittings; rated components only.

Robotics and emerging technologyBLUEPRINT

Industrial robots, cobots and other new technologies (AI, AR, digital twins, data mining).

  • Robots: perimeter guarding with interlocked gates, light curtains/pressure mats, accessible e-stops; ANSI/RIA R15.06 and ISO 10218 require risk assessment; LOTO for maintenance; teach mode at reduced speed.
  • Cobots share workspace with humans (ISO/TS 15066): power and force limiting, speed and separation monitoring, hand guiding, safety-rated monitored stop.
  • AI: computer vision for PPE compliance, NLP of safety reports, wearables for fatigue; concerns - privacy, bias, over-reliance; AI supports, does not replace compliance.
  • Digital twin = virtual replica to simulate hazards; AR = real-time hazard overlays; predictive analytics/data mining find leading indicators (ANSI/ASSP Z16.1).

Exam tip: Shared workspace with humans = cobot; virtual replica = digital twin; real-time visual overlay = AR.

Machine guarding requirements

OSHA 1910.212 general requirements and Subpart O.

  • Guards must prevent contact, be secured, protect from falling objects, create no new hazard, allow safe lubrication and not obstruct visibility (e.g., polycarbonate).
  • Point-of-operation guarding on presses, shears, saws; power-transmission parts < 7 ft enclosed.
  • Guard types: fixed (preferred), interlocked (stops machine when opened), adjustable, self-adjusting; devices: light curtains, mats, pullback, restraint, two-hand controls.
  • Woodworking: circular saw guard covers blade above table; radial arm saw returns automatically.

Exam tip: CSP prefers engineering controls (guards, interlocks) over PPE.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 25 Construction Safety (tools, ladders), p. 567; Ch. 29 Walking and Working Surfaces, p. 657

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

1.7-Q1. James must change a hydraulic hose on a press used earlier that day. The press is powered off, but he does not bleed residual pressure before loosening the fitting, and a burst of fluid causes an injection injury to his forearm. What should he have done before loosening any fittings?

  1. Removed the drip pan from under the couplings, then powered off the press
  2. Opened the reservoir vent cap and loosened the fittings immediately
  3. Raised the hose 6 in. so gravity could drain the fluid
  4. Shut off and locked out the press, then relieved and verified dissipation of stored hydraulic pressure

1.7-Q2. On a bench grinder, what is the maximum distance allowed between the wheel periphery and the adjustable tongue guard (upper peripheral guard)?

  1. 1/16 in.
  2. 1/8 in.
  3. 1/4 in.
  4. 1/2 in.

1.7-Q3. A worker mounts a new grinding wheel that fits the arbor correctly but has not been ring tested. When the grinder is started, the wheel shatters. Which action would have MOST effectively prevented this?

  1. Inspecting and ring testing the wheel before mounting
  2. Wearing a face shield and gloves during startup
  3. Running the grinder at full speed for 10 minutes before use
  4. Reducing the tool rest clearance

1.7-Q4. A robotic welding cell is running in automatic mode. The operator powers off the teach pendant, assumes the robot cannot move, and reaches between the arm and fixture to clear a jam. The robot cycles and pinches his hand. Which procedure would MOST effectively have prevented the injury?

  1. Keep the pendant off and wear cut-resistant gloves when reaching in
  2. Lock out the robot's main power (and other energy sources) and verify zero energy before entering
  3. Use single-block mode to jog the arm away without powering off the controller
  4. Press the pendant E-stop and rely on it to de-energize the robot

1.7-Q5. A robotic cell has interlocked gates, but workers prop a gate open with a tool so they can make minor adjustments without stopping the robot. What is the BEST corrective measure?

  1. Install cameras to monitor worker behavior
  2. Install tamper-resistant interlocks and investigate why workers feel the need to bypass, retraining as needed
  3. Post signs telling workers not to enter
  4. Reduce the robot's operating speed

1.7-Q6. A roofer shoots his thumb with a pneumatic nailer set to contact (bump) actuation. He was holding the trigger down and tapping the nose on the shingles to drive multiple nails. The tool was in good working order. What is the BEST recommendation?

  1. Issue verbal discipline for the injury
  2. Require puncture-resistant gloves
  3. Tell the worker to go slower
  4. Switch to nailers with a full sequential trigger

1.7-Q7. A technician leans a straight ladder against a wall to reach a platform 15 ft high. The base is only 2 ft from the wall, and the ladder shifts during use. What is the proper correction?

  1. Reposition the ladder using the 4-to-1 rule (base about 3.75 ft out), on firm footing, and secure it
  2. Have a coworker hold the base
  3. Use a shorter ladder
  4. Tie off the top but leave the base unsecured

1.7-Q8. A ladder is used to access a roof 20 ft high. It will be set at a 75-degree angle and must extend 3 ft above the roof edge. What ladder length is needed?

  1. 20 ft
  2. 22 ft
  3. 24 ft
  4. 26 ft

1.7-Q9. Operators on a conveyor repeatedly remove an interlocked guard to clear jams. What is the BEST long-term solution?

  1. Train operators and enforce discipline for violations
  2. Install a camera to monitor compliance
  3. Post warning signs about removing guards
  4. Modify the design to provide a jam-clearing tool or access point that does not require removing the guard (or eliminate the jam cause)

1.7-Q10. During a tool crib inspection, several cold chisels and drift pins are found with mushroomed heads. What is the BEST action?

  1. Issue them only to experienced workers
  2. Require safety glasses whenever they are used
  3. Remove them from service until the heads are dressed or the tools replaced
  4. Tape the heads to contain fragments

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1DLOTO covers stored energy: residual hydraulic pressure must be relieved and verified as zero. High-pressure fluid injection injuries are surgical emergencies.
2COSHA 1910.215 sets the tongue guard at 1/4 in. or less and the work rest at 1/8 in. or less. Mixing up the two is a classic trap; remember 'rest 1/8, tongue 1/4'.
3A1910.215(d)(1) requires wheels to be inspected and sound (ring) tested before mounting to detect cracks. PPE only reduces the injury after failure. Standing aside during the 1-minute run-up is also good practice.
4BEntering the robot envelope for jam clearing requires hazardous energy control. E-stops and pendants are control-circuit devices, not energy-isolating devices.
5BDefeat-resistant interlocks restore the engineering control. Addressing the production pressure behind the bypass prevents it from recurring.
6DNIOSH found full sequential triggers cut nail gun injury risk roughly in half compared with contact triggers. This is an engineering fix; gloves, pace and discipline are weaker.
7AThe 4:1 rule (1 ft out for every 4 ft of working length, about 75 degrees) gives stability. For 15 ft that is about 3.75 ft; the side rails should also extend 3 ft above the landing.
8CLength to the eave = 20 / sin 75 = 20 / 0.966 = 20.7 ft; adding 3 ft gives 23.7 ft, so a 24-ft ladder is needed. Forgetting the 3-ft extension gives about 21 ft.
9DFixing the design removes the reason to bypass the guard. Discipline, cameras and signs treat the symptom and are weaker controls.
10COSHA 1926.301 and 1910.242 require unsafe hand tools to be removed from service. Mushroomed heads can shatter and throw fragments when struck.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.1

Benchmarking and Gap Analysis

CSP11 blueprint: Compare performance against established benchmarks (e.g., gap analysis)

1. Definition

Benchmarking is the systematic comparison of an organization's safety performance, processes and practices with a reference point - industry averages, best-in-class peers, internal sites or a standard. Gap analysis identifies the difference between the current state and the desired state and the reasons for that difference.

2. Explanation in simple words

Benchmarking tells you where you stand compared with others. Gap analysis tells you how far you are from where you want to be and why. Together they show what to fix first.

3. Key points to remember

  • Types: internal (site vs site), competitive (same industry peers, e.g., BLS rates by NAICS code), functional/generic (best practice from any industry), performance (metrics such as TRIR, DART) and process (how work is done).
  • Common benchmarks: BLS injury and illness rates by NAICS code, EMR (1.0 = industry average), industry association data, ISO 45001/ANSI Z10 requirements, OSHA VPP criteria.
  • Gap analysis steps: define the desired state (standard, benchmark, objective) → assess the current state (audit, data, interviews) → identify and quantify the gaps → analyze causes → prioritize → action plan with owners, resources and dates → monitor.
  • Compare like with like: same NAICS code, same rate formula (200,000 hours), same time period, similar size.
  • A gap is a starting point for root-cause thinking, not the conclusion. Do not 'fix' a gap by changing how cases are recorded.

4. Real work scenario

A plant's DART rate is 2.1 and the BLS rate for its NAICS code is 1.4. The gap analysis shows that 70% of DART cases are hand injuries at two press lines, where guarding is older than the rest of the plant. The action plan targets guarding upgrades at those lines first.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • The next step after finding a gap is almost always 'analyze the cause and create an action plan', not setting a slogan goal.
  • Recognize that benchmarking against BLS data must use the correct NAICS code and the 200,000-hour base.
  • An EMR above 1.0 means worse than the industry average and higher premiums.

6. Every blueprint point (2 blueprint items + 3 more)

BenchmarkingBLUEPRINT

Benchmarking compares your safety performance against a reference point so you know where you stand. The reference can be regulatory, industry, internal or best-in-class, and it must fit your risks, size, processes and safety maturity.

  • Regulatory/national: BLS injury and illness rates by NAICS code; OSHA recordkeeping formulas (TRIR, DART).
  • Industry: NSC annual averages and best-in-class statistics, trade association data.
  • Internal: your best site, best year or an aspirational company target.
  • Best-in-class/world-class: leading organizations in or outside your sector.
  • Process (functional) benchmarking compares how a task is done (e.g., how a peer runs MOC), not just the outcome number.
  • The guide states regulatory benchmarks are 'legally required' (e.g., keep TRIR at or below the BLS average) - in fact OSHA sets no required TRIR; BLS averages are comparison data only.

Exam tip: Pick the benchmark that is relevant (same NAICS, size, hazards). A small specialty shop compared to a national all-industry average is a weak benchmark.

Gap analysisBLUEPRINT

Gap analysis is a systematic comparison of current performance (or current program) against a benchmark or standard, followed by finding why the gap exists and closing it with an action plan.

  • Steps: select benchmark -> collect and validate data -> compare and quantify gap -> analyze root causes -> action plan -> engage management and workers -> communicate -> monitor and adjust.
  • Quantify clearly: 'Our TRIR is 2.5, industry average 1.6, best-in-class 1.0' -> gap to average = 0.9 (56% above).
  • Program gap analysis: compare your SMS element by element against ISO 45001 or ANSI Z10 clauses before certification.
  • Use both lagging (DART, lost-time rate) and leading (near-miss reports, observations) data.
  • Raise the benchmark as performance improves (continuous improvement).

Exam tip: A gap analysis is done BEFORE implementing a new management system - it tells you what is missing. The first step is choosing an appropriate benchmark/criteria.

Data collection and validation

Benchmarking is only as good as the data. Records must be accurate, complete and comparable (same definitions, same time period, same hours basis).

  • Sources: OSHA 300/300A logs, audit results, training records, workers' comp claims, observation data.
  • Under-reporting makes lagging data look better than reality - validate against first-aid logs, clinic visits and comp claims.
  • Normalize to exposure: rates per 200,000 hours, not raw counts.
  • Compare like with like: same NAICS code, similar size and work type.

Exam tip: A low injury rate with very few near-miss reports suggests under-reporting, not excellent performance.

Root cause of performance gaps

Numbers show a gap exists; analysis shows why. Look for system causes such as training, leadership, equipment, procedures or reporting culture.

  • Tools: interviews, 5 Whys, fishbone diagrams, audit findings.
  • ANSI/ASSP Z16.1 and Z10 support analyzing causes, not just measuring outcomes.
  • Segment the data (by site, shift, department, task) to find hot spots - Pareto helps here.

Communicating and sustaining benchmark results

Share results and the improvement plan widely and track progress over time with dashboards; technology makes benchmarking visible at all levels.

  • Communicate through dashboards, reports, toolbox talks and digital platforms - transparency builds buy-in.
  • Review gaps in management meetings and safety committees.
  • Document results; they are evidence of improvement during audits.
  • EHS software automates data collection and visualization (NSC best practice).

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 36 Measuring Safety and Health Performance, p. 797; Ch. 37 Safety Program Auditing Techniques, p. 818

2.1-Q1. Your plant's DART rate is 2.1. The industry benchmark (same NAICS) is 1.4. Management asks what to do next. What is the BEST next step?

  1. Set a goal of matching the benchmark next year and announce it
  2. Analyze the causes of the 0.7 gap (injury types, tasks, controls) and build an action plan with owners and dates
  3. Change the recordkeeping method to reduce DART cases

  1. Internal Benchmarking - Analyze safety performance within your own teams first.
  2. Functional Benchmarking - Examine safety practices in different industries.
  3. Competitive Benchmarking - Directly compare safety incident rates against similar companies.
  4. Generic Benchmarking - Benchmark against unrelated industries to find innovative safety practices.

2.1-Q3. As a safety coordinator in a logistics company, you're tasked with improving vehicle incident rates. Which benchmarking approach would provide the MOST practical insights?

  1. Performance Benchmarking - Measuring specific safety processes against industry best practices.

  4. Identify best practices and performance gaps for improvement

  2. Plant B has the highest rate (5.0); both plants exceed the 3.0 benchmark
  3. Both plants are below the industry benchmark
  4. Rates cannot be compared between plants of different sizes

  3. Last year's injury count with this year's injury count
  4. Worker perception survey results with supervisor survey results only

2.1-Q8. Senior management decides to pursue ISO 45001 certification for a site that already has an OSHA-compliant safety program. What should the safety professional do FIRST?

  1. Train all employees on ISO 45001 clauses
  2. Schedule the certification audit with a registrar
  3. Rewrite all safety procedures in ISO format
  4. Perform a gap analysis of the existing program against ISO 45001 requirements

2.1-Q9. A safety manager wants to compare her plant's DART rate with national data. Which approach gives the MOST valid benchmark comparison?

  1. Compare the plant's total number of DART cases with the national number of DART cases
  2. Compare the plant's DART rate with BLS data for the same NAICS industry code, establishment size and year
  3. Compare the plant's DART rate with the all-industry national average
  4. Compare the plant's DART rate with a competitor's TRIR

2.1-Q10. A company with 12 plants compares lockout/tagout audit scores and practices among its own sites to identify the best-performing site's methods and spread them. This is an example of:

  1. Competitive benchmarking
  2. Generic benchmarking
  3. Internal benchmarking
  4. Strategic benchmarking

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BBenchmarking shows where you stand; gap analysis must then find why the gap exists and drive an action plan with owners, dates and measures. Goals without causes, recordkeeping changes and incentives do not close the gap.
2CComparing incident rates directly against similar companies/industry leaders is competitive benchmarking. Internal compares your own units; functional/generic look at other industries.
3APerformance benchmarking compares specific measurable processes (e.g., vehicle incident rates, driver programs) against best practice, giving the most practical, actionable insight.
4DThe purpose of benchmarking is to identify best practices and performance gaps that drive improvement; it does not replace audits or management review.
5CBenchmarking is the process of comparing your safety program and performance to industry peers or recognized leaders.
6BTRIR = cases x 200,000 / hours: A = 6 x 200,000 / 300,000 = 4.0; B = 4 x 200,000 / 160,000 = 5.0. Both exceed 3.0. Trap: comparing raw counts instead of normalized rates.
7AGap analysis = current state vs. desired/required state (e.g., ISO 45001 clauses, OSHA requirements, best-in-class benchmark); the gaps become the basis of the action plan.
8DA gap analysis first shows which requirements are already met and which are missing, so effort and resources go to real gaps. Scheduling, rewriting or training before knowing the gaps is premature.
9BValid benchmarking compares like with like: the same metric, normalized to hours (rate), for the same industry, size class and period. Raw counts, all-industry averages or different metrics distort the comparison.
10CComparing performance and practices among units within the same organization is internal benchmarking; data are easy to obtain and comparable, but the view is limited to company practice.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.2

Performance Standards and Action Planning

CSP11 blueprint: Analyze performance standards to determine plan of action

1. Definition

Performance standards are the defined expectations for safety performance - regulatory requirements, consensus standards, company policies and objectives. Action planning turns gaps against those standards into specific, resourced, time-bound actions with accountable owners.

2. Explanation in simple words

First decide what 'good' looks like and how you will measure it. Then write a plan that says who will do what, by when, with what money and how you will know it worked.

3. Key points to remember

  • SMART objectives: Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound.
  • Action plan contents: problem/gap statement, objective, actions, owner (one accountable person), resources/budget, due dates, success measures, status tracking and verification of effectiveness.
  • Prioritize by risk (severity × likelihood), legal requirements, cost and feasibility; quick wins plus long-term fixes.
  • Performance standards hierarchy: law/regulation (minimum) → consensus standards (ANSI, NFPA, ISO) → company standards (often stricter) → site procedures.
  • PDCA ties it together: Plan (objectives, plans), Do (implement), Check (measure, audit), Act (correct and improve).

4. Real work scenario

After an audit shows that 30% of forklift operators have expired evaluations, the safety manager writes an objective: 'All 120 operators will have a current evaluation by March 31; monthly status report to the plant manager.' Supervisors are named as owners for their crews, and trainer time is budgeted.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Choose the answer that contains measurable criteria and a deadline.
  • 'Improve safety' or 'zero injuries' without measures are weak objectives on the exam.
  • Action plans need verification of effectiveness - closing an action is not the same as solving the problem.

6. Every blueprint point (2 blueprint items + 3 more)

Performance standardsBLUEPRINT

A performance standard defines the expected level of performance (an outcome, rate or activity level) that people and programs are measured against. Analyzing performance against the standard tells you what action plan is needed.

  • Examples: 100% of corrective actions closed within 30 days; 95% training completion; TRIR below 1.5; 4 safety observations per supervisor per month.
  • Regulatory 'performance' standards state the required outcome but let the employer choose the method (e.g., PSM, General Duty); 'specification' standards prescribe exact means (e.g., 42-in. guardrail).
  • Standards should be measurable, achievable and tied to accountability (performance reviews).
  • ANSI/ASSP Z16.1 provides consistent metric definitions for comparing performance.

Exam tip: If a question asks why a facility met all OSHA specification standards but still had a fatality, the answer usually involves missing performance/management system elements.

Action planningBLUEPRINT

An action plan converts a gap or objective into specific tasks with owners, resources, deadlines and measures, and is managed through PDCA.

  • Each action: what, who (single named owner), by when, resources, how success is verified.
  • Objectives must be SMART: Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound.
  • Prioritize actions by risk (severity x likelihood) and regulatory urgency.
  • Track in an action register; review status in management meetings; escalate overdue items.

Exam tip: 'Reduce injuries' is not SMART; 'Reduce forklift-pedestrian near misses at Plant 2 by 50% by Dec 31 through separated walkways' is.

SMART objectives

SMART is the standard test for whether an objective is usable for planning and accountability.

  • Specific - clear scope; Measurable - a metric; Achievable - realistic with resources; Relevant - tied to key risks; Time-bound - a deadline.
  • ISO 45001 clause 6.2 requires OH&S objectives that are measurable (if practicable), monitored, communicated and updated.
  • Mix leading and lagging objectives.

Plan-Do-Check-Act (PDCA) cycle

PDCA (Deming/Shewhart cycle) is the engine of continual improvement in every modern management system standard.

  • Plan: policy, objectives, hazard identification, risk assessment, actions.
  • Do: implement, train, communicate, provide resources.
  • Check: monitor, measure, audit, investigate incidents.
  • Act: management review, corrective action, improve - then repeat.
  • ISO 45001, ISO 14001 and ANSI Z10 are all structured on PDCA.

Exam tip: Audits and incident investigations are 'Check'; management review and corrective action are 'Act'.

Accountability and performance review

Performance standards only work if people are held accountable and results are reviewed regularly.

  • Tie supervisor and manager appraisals to safety activities (leading) not only injury counts.
  • Avoid incentive programs that reward 'zero injuries' - they suppress reporting (OSHA 1904.35 prohibits retaliation for reporting).
  • Management review at least annually or after serious incidents.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 31 Safety Management System, p. 717; Ch. 36 Measuring Safety and Health Performance

QAnsWhy
1CSMART = Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound. Only C states a scope (24 presses), measures (assessments, 90% closure) and deadlines; the others are vague slogans.
2AIdentifying hazards, assessing risk and selecting controls before implementation is planning (ISO 45001 clause 6). Do = implementing the controls; Check = monitoring; Act = improving.
3DReviewing data is Check; changing the system (checklists, training) based on findings is Act. If nothing ever changes, the Act phase is failing.
4AWhen activity targets are met but outcomes do not improve, the indicators may not measure what actually drives risk. Reassess and realign indicators rather than closing the review or cutting activity.
5BMeeting numeric objectives with low engagement signals a culture gap; visible leadership and two-way communication address engagement. Technical controls alone or lagging-only focus do not.
6CSafety input to the business plan integrates safety goals into the overall strategy and resource allocation, so safety is resourced like any other business objective.
7DEffective safety goals must be achievable and measurable (SMART). Broad goals cannot be tracked, and injury rates alone are lagging measures.
8BDo = implementing the plan: operational controls, training, communication and procedures.
9CAn action plan turns findings into accountable work: what will be done, by whom, by when, with what resources, and how completion/effectiveness will be verified. Lists without owners and dates rarely get closed.
10BAction plans are prioritized by risk (severity x likelihood). A daily exposure to a potentially fatal fall outranks administrative and documentation gaps.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.3

EHS Culture: Measure, Analyze and Improve

CSP11 blueprint: Determine how to measure, analyze, and improve EHS culture

1. Definition

Safety culture is the shared values, beliefs, attitudes and behaviors that determine how safety is actually managed in an organization - 'how we do things here when no one is watching'. Safety climate is a snapshot of employee perceptions of that culture at a point in time.

2. Explanation in simple words

Culture is what people really do and believe, not what the policy says. You measure it by asking and watching, look for gaps between what managers think and what workers experience, and improve it through visible leadership, involvement and trust.

3. Key points to remember

  • Measurement tools: perception surveys (anonymous, repeated over time), focus groups, interviews, observation, behavior-based safety data, near-miss reporting rates, participation in safety activities, corrective action closure.
  • Look for perception gaps between management and front-line responses; follow up surveys with focus groups to understand why.
  • Maturity models: Hudson ladder (pathological → reactive → calculative → proactive → generative); DuPont Bradley curve (reactive → dependent → independent → interdependent).
  • Characteristics of a strong culture: visible management commitment, employee involvement, just culture (fair accountability, no blame for honest errors), open reporting without retaliation, learning from incidents, stop-work authority.
  • Behavior-based safety (BBS): identify critical behaviors, observe and measure, give feedback (positive reinforcement), improve barriers. ABC model: Antecedent → Behavior → Consequence; consequences that are soon, certain and positive drive behavior most.
  • Culture killers: incentives tied to 'zero injuries' (suppress reporting), punishing reporting, production pressure, inconsistent discipline.

4. Real work scenario

A survey shows 92% of managers agree that 'production never comes before safety', but only 41% of operators agree. Focus groups reveal that supervisors praise crews for meeting quotas but rarely mention safety. Leadership starts daily safety conversations, adds safety metrics to supervisors' reviews, and repeats the survey a year later; the operator score rises to 68%.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Culture questions usually reward involvement, communication and leadership by example over discipline or posters.
  • A rise in near-miss reports after a campaign is usually a positive sign of reporting culture.
  • BBS feedback should be immediate, specific and positive; public correction or write-ups are distractors.

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 4 more)

Measuring EHS cultureBLUEPRINT

Culture is the shared values, beliefs, attitudes and behaviors about safety. It is measured with several methods together, because no single tool captures it.

  • Safety climate/perception surveys (validated, anonymous) - climate is a snapshot of culture.
  • NSC 8 safety climate factors: management commitment, leadership communication, employee involvement, peer support, hazard recognition and reporting, safety training, supervisor support, accountability.
  • Behavioral observations (PPE use, procedure compliance).
  • Leading indicator review: near-miss reports, meeting participation, corrective-action closure.
  • Focus groups and interviews; system review of committees, communications and leadership visibility.

Exam tip: Best answer for 'how to assess culture' combines surveys + observations + leading indicators + interviews, not one tool.

Analyzing EHS cultureBLUEPRINT

Analysis turns survey and indicator results into understanding of where and why culture is weak.

  • Gap analysis against ANSI/ASSP best practice and NSC benchmarks.
  • Segment data by department, shift, role - management often rates culture higher than front-line workers (perception gap).
  • Root cause tools (5 Whys, fishbone) for under-reporting or low engagement.
  • Benchmark against NSC national/sector data.

Exam tip: A large gap between management and worker survey scores signals a leadership communication/credibility problem.

Improving EHS cultureBLUEPRINT

Culture improves through visible leadership, real worker engagement, open blame-free communication, empowerment and accountability, repeated through PDCA.

  • Leaders set expectations, lead by example, are visible on the floor.
  • Involve workers in committees, hazard ID, investigations and solutions; recognize positive behavior.
  • Share survey results and action plans; remove barriers to near-miss reporting.
  • Empower stop-work authority; train on the 'why'.
  • Tie manager performance reviews to culture goals; re-measure to track progress.

Exam tip: Model answer: measure (surveys, observations, indicators) -> analyze (gap/root cause) -> leadership-driven participative improvement plan.

Safety culture vs. safety climate

Culture is deep and slow to change (values, assumptions); climate is the measurable surface perception at a point in time.

  • Climate surveys measure perceptions now; culture change takes years.
  • Schein: artifacts, espoused values, basic assumptions.
  • Repeat surveys (e.g., every 1-2 years) to trend climate.

Safety culture maturity models

Maturity models describe stages organizations move through toward a generative culture.

  • Hudson ladder: pathological -> reactive -> calculative -> proactive -> generative.
  • DuPont Bradley Curve: reactive -> dependent -> independent -> interdependent.
  • Higher maturity = workers own safety, reporting is valued, leading indicators drive decisions.

Exam tip: 'We do safety because we were caught' = reactive; 'safety is how we do business' = generative.

Just culture and reporting

A just culture separates honest error from reckless behavior so people report errors and near misses without fear, while accountability for reckless acts remains.

  • Human error -> console/fix system; at-risk behavior -> coach; reckless behavior -> discipline.
  • Blame-and-retrain cycles suppress reporting and hide system causes.
  • OSHA 1904.35: employees must have a reasonable reporting procedure and be protected from retaliation.

Behavior-based safety (BBS)

BBS uses peer observation and feedback on critical safe behaviors to reinforce safe practices and generate leading data.

  • Elements: identify critical behaviors, observe, give feedback, analyze data, remove barriers.
  • ABC model: Antecedent -> Behavior -> Consequence; consequences that are soon, certain and positive drive behavior most.
  • BBS must not replace hazard control - fix conditions first (hierarchy of controls).

Exam tip: BBS that blames workers while ignoring system hazards is a weak answer.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 21 Management Theories, p. 505; Ch. 36 Measuring Safety and Health Performance

2.3-Q10. An organization that openly shares lessons learned from incidents across departments is demonstrating:

  3. Calculative culture based on data alone

QAnsWhy
1APerception surveys of all workers, repeated over time, directly measure shared beliefs and attitudes, i.e., culture. Citation counts and guideline adoption are compliance measures, not culture measures.
2BA large manager-worker perception gap is a key culture finding. Follow up with focus groups/interviews to learn how production pressure is handled in practice; dismissing or repeating the survey ignores the signal.
3CHudson's ladder levels are Pathological, Reactive, Calculative, Proactive and Generative. 'Predictive' is not a level.
4DA just culture separates honest error from reckless behavior, emphasizing learning and system fixes with fair accountability; it neither ignores unsafe acts nor blames by default.
5BHigh near-miss reporting combined with visible management response shows trust and engagement. Low hazard reports usually mean underreporting, not safety.
6CRepeated guard bypassing despite reminders points to a system barrier (jam frequency, design, workflow). Remove the reason for the at-risk behavior; discipline and signs do not address the cause.
7ARewarding injury-free outcomes encourages underreporting. Recognizing measurable safe behaviors (pre-task planning, suggestions, PPE use) reinforces the behaviors that prevent incidents.
8DA culture shift shows up as management support, open communication and company-wide participation. Fewer injuries and citations are lagging results, and more audits or incentives are inputs.
9BBBS uses respectful one-on-one feedback that addresses risk perception ('never had a problem' is optimism bias). Write-ups or ignoring the behavior do not change beliefs; signs are weak controls.
10AOpenly sharing lessons learned across the organization is a hallmark of a generative (learning) culture; pathological cultures hide problems and reactive cultures act only after events.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.4

Incident Investigation and Root Cause Analysis

CSP11 blueprint: Determine appropriate incident investigation techniques (root causes) and apply corrective actions

1. Definition

Incident investigation is the systematic process of collecting facts about an incident, determining direct, contributing and root causes, and implementing corrective actions that prevent recurrence. Root causes are the underlying management-system failures that allowed the immediate causes to exist.

2. Explanation in simple words

Ask what happened, how it happened and why - then keep asking why until you reach something the organization can fix in its systems. Blame finds a person; investigation finds the fix.

3. Key points to remember

  • Steps: secure the scene and care for the injured → preserve evidence → gather information (people, positions, parts, paper - the 4 Ps) → interview witnesses individually and promptly → build a timeline → analyze causes → develop corrective actions → report → track to closure → verify effectiveness → share lessons.
  • Interview tips: private setting, explain the purpose is prevention not blame, open-ended questions ('Tell me what you saw'), let the witness talk, review notes with the witness.
  • Causes: direct/immediate (unsafe act or condition, energy release), contributing, root (system: training, procedures, design, supervision, management commitment).
  • Techniques: 5 Whys, fishbone/Ishikawa (people, methods, machines, materials, environment, management), fault tree analysis, events and causal factors charting, change analysis, barrier analysis, MORT, multilinear events sequencing (MES), TapRooT, SCAT (systematic cause analysis technique), Swiss cheese model, bow-tie.
  • Accident theories: Heinrich domino (unsafe acts), Bird/Loss Causation (lack of control → basic causes → immediate causes → incident → loss), Reason's Swiss cheese (aligned holes in defenses), energy transfer (Haddon).
  • Corrective actions should follow the hierarchy of controls; 'retrain' and 'discipline' alone are weak. Assign owners and dates and verify that the actions work.

4. Real work scenario

An operator's hand is caught in a conveyor while clearing a jam. A 5-Whys analysis shows the guard was removed because jams were frequent, jams were frequent because a new box size was introduced without changing the guides, and the box change was never reviewed under MOC. Corrective actions: adjust the guides, install an interlocked access door and add packaging changes to the MOC procedure.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • 'The worker didn't follow the procedure' is a symptom - the BEST answer keeps asking why.
  • First action at an incident scene: care for the injured and make the scene safe.
  • Interview witnesses separately and as soon as possible.
  • Fishbone = categorize causes; FTA = top-down logic; FMEA = bottom-up component failures.

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 6 more)

Incident investigation processBLUEPRINT

A systematic, standards-based process (OSHA Incident Investigation guide, ANSI Z10, NSC) to learn why an incident happened and prevent recurrence - learning, not blaming.

  • Step 1: secure the scene and provide aid - protect people first, stop hazardous operations, LOTO, control spills; then preserve the scene.
  • Step 2: report and notify - internally at once; OSHA: fatality within 8 h, in-patient hospitalization/amputation/eye loss within 24 h.
  • Step 3: assemble a trained multidisciplinary team led by a neutral leader not involved in the incident.
  • Step 4: collect evidence - private witness interviews ASAP with open-ended questions, photos/sketches, physical evidence with chain of custody, records (SOPs, training, maintenance), video/logs.
  • Step 5: reconstruct the timeline (sequence diagrams, flow charts), then root cause analysis, corrective actions, report, follow-up.
  • Investigate near misses too - they are free lessons.

Exam tip: FIRST action at an incident scene is always care for the injured and make the scene safe - not interviewing or photographing.

Root cause analysisBLUEPRINT

Root causes are the underlying, systemic reasons (management, design, procedures, culture) that allowed the immediate causes. Fixing them prevents recurrence; fixing only immediate causes leads to 'blame and retrain'.

  • Immediate causes = unsafe acts (bypassed guard) and unsafe conditions (wet unmarked floor).
  • Root causes = why the system allowed them (no procurement policy requiring operator input, production pressure, poor design).
  • Tools: 5 Whys, fishbone, change analysis, barrier analysis, FTA, MORT.
  • ANSI Z10: RCA expected for significant incidents and near misses.
  • Never accept 'human error' or 'worker carelessness' as the final cause.

Exam tip: If an answer choice stops at 'employee failed to follow procedure', look for the one that asks why (training, design, supervision, pressure).

Corrective actionsBLUEPRINT

Corrective and preventive actions must target root causes, follow the hierarchy of controls, be owned, tracked, verified and embedded into the system.

  • Prioritize by risk (risk matrix, bow-tie); life-threatening and regulatory items first.
  • Single named owner and due date for every action; track in an action register (NSC).
  • Verify completion AND effectiveness (re-assess risk) before closing; escalate overdue actions to management.
  • Update SOPs, training, permits, drawings; share lessons learned organization-wide.
  • Include preventive actions: 'how do we stop it happening anywhere else?'

Exam tip: 'Retrain the employee' or 'discipline the worker' is rarely the best corrective action; engineering/system fixes are preferred.

5 Whys

Ask 'why?' repeatedly, using each answer as the basis for the next question, until you reach a system, policy or management cause (often about five whys).

  • Best for routine incidents, near misses and simple recurring problems; first step before more detailed tools.
  • Example chain: bypassed guard -> slowed production -> guard hard to access -> chosen on cost -> purchasing need not consult operators -> no policy for operator input (root cause).
  • Document each why; avoid blaming questions; go beyond five if needed.

Exam tip: If you reach a policy, culture or design issue, you are near the true root cause.

Fishbone (Ishikawa) diagram

A cause-and-effect diagram with the problem at the head and main cause categories as bones; a team brainstorms causes in each category.

  • Common categories: People, Equipment (Machines), Process (Methods), Materials, Environment, Management (6 Ms variant: Man, Machine, Method, Material, Measurement, Mother nature).
  • Best for complex incidents with many possible contributors and group brainstorming.
  • Combine with 5 Whys on each branch; attach to the investigation record.

Change analysis

Compare the incident situation with the normal, incident-free situation and list every difference; then test how each change contributed.

  • Use when 'this has never happened before' or a system that normally works failed.
  • Typical changes: substitute operator, new material or pallet type, wet surface, time pressure, temporary workaround.
  • Ask: who approved the change, was risk assessed, were controls adjusted? Link findings to the MOC process.

Exam tip: Key question for change analysis: 'What was different today?'

Barrier analysis

List every barrier (engineering, administrative, PPE, alarms, physical) that should have stopped the incident and determine whether each was missing, failed, bypassed, ineffective or not updated.

  • Best in high-hazard settings with layers of defense (chemical, oil and gas, confined spaces).
  • Example: double-wall piping missing; permit bypassed; relief valve stuck; alarm ignored (alarm fatigue); face shield not worn; SOP not updated.
  • Leads to system-level fixes rather than behavior fixes; supports defense-in-depth.

Accident causation theories

Theories explain why incidents occur and shape where investigators look.

  • Heinrich domino theory: ancestry/social environment -> fault of person -> unsafe act/condition -> accident -> injury; remove the middle domino.
  • Heinrich ratio 1 major : 29 minor : 300 no-injury; Bird ratio 1 serious : 10 minor : 30 property damage : 600 near misses.
  • Reason's Swiss cheese model: latent conditions (organizational) line up with active failures to let a hazard through barriers.
  • Multiple causation: incidents have many contributing factors - system, management, culture.
  • Energy release theory (Haddon): incidents are unwanted energy transfer; control the energy or the barrier.

Exam tip: Swiss cheese = latent organizational failures + active errors; questions about 'layers of defense' point to Reason.

Interviews and evidence handling

Credible investigations depend on timely, unbiased, well-documented evidence.

  • Interview witnesses separately and privately, soon after the event, with open-ended non-judgmental questions.
  • Photograph before moving anything; tag and log physical evidence (chain of custody).
  • Compare what should have happened (procedures) with what did happen.
  • Gather environmental data (air, noise, lighting), control-system logs and video.

Exam tip: Group interviews create groupthink - interview individually.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 22 Accident Causation and Investigation Techniques, p. 515

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

  1. Interview witnesses
  2. Secure and preserve the scene
  3. Collect physical evidence
  4. Develop recommendations

  2. Fishbone Diagram
  3. 5 Whys

The team uses a structured worksheet to identify causes and corrective actions for People, Procedures, Equipment, Environment, and Management Systems. Which investigation technique is being applied?

  1. Cause-and-effect (fishbone/Ishikawa) analysis
  2. Barrier analysis
  3. Event and causal factor charting

  2. Personal failure
  3. Contributing cause

  2. Immediately after implementation
  3. After a reasonable period to confirm effectiveness
  4. Only during management review

  1. Recommendations and verified corrective actions
  2. Assignment of blame
  3. A record of injuries
  4. Witness statements

  1. Based on the evidence only because witness statements are usually biased or hearsay.
  2. Immediately, with all witnesses simultaneously to get the most accurate statements.
  3. The next working day after emotions have settled down.
  4. Individually, with all direct and indirect witnesses as soon as possible.

2.4-Q8. A supervisor is being interviewed about a forklift near-miss. The investigator asks, "Did you see the forklift operator speeding?" The supervisor answers "No." What would have been a better question to encourage useful information?

  1. "Why didn't you stop the operator if they were speeding?"
  2. "Tell me what you observed when the forklift entered the aisle."
  3. "Did the forklift almost hit the pedestrian?"
  4. "Do you think the operator is reckless?"

2.4-Q9. The Bowtie method in incident investigation is best described as:

  1. A brainstorming technique to list every possible hazard
  2. A timeline that shows human errors in sequence
  3. A method to calculate injury severity after incidents
  4. A model that links threats, preventive barriers, the top event, and consequences

2.4-Q10. An investigation finds that an operator's hand was caught while clearing jams on a conveyor because the task requires reaching into the nip point while the belt runs, and jams occur about 20 times per shift. Which corrective action is MOST effective?

  1. Retrain all operators on the hazards of nip points
  2. Discipline the operator for reaching into the conveyor
  3. Redesign the infeed to reduce jams and install an interlocked guard that stops the belt when opened
  4. Post a warning sign at the jam location

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BFirst secure the scene to prevent further harm and preserve evidence; interviews and evidence collection follow.
2CThe 5 Whys repeatedly asks 'why' to move from the immediate cause to the underlying system cause.
3ACategorizing causes into branches such as People, Procedures, Equipment, Environment and Management Systems is the cause-and-effect (fishbone/Ishikawa) method.
4DWorn, uneven ladder steps are the unsafe condition that directly produced the fall (direct/immediate cause). The root cause would be why the ladder was not inspected or removed from service.
5CCorrective actions should be verified after a reasonable period to confirm they are effective and sustained and did not create new hazards; checking on the day of installation only confirms completion.
6AAn investigation's value is in actionable, verified corrective actions that prevent recurrence, not in blame or data collection alone.
7DInterview witnesses individually and as soon as possible, while memories are fresh and before accounts are influenced by others. Group interviews contaminate testimony.
8BOpen-ended, non-leading questions ('Tell me what you observed...') produce more complete, unbiased information than yes/no or accusatory questions.
9DA bowtie links threats, preventive barriers, the top event, mitigative barriers and consequences in one diagram.
10CCorrective actions should follow the hierarchy of controls and address the root cause: reducing jams (elimination of the task) plus interlocked guarding (engineering) beats training, signs and discipline.
Printed book page 63
Domain 2 · Competency 2.5

Management of Change (MOC)

CSP11 blueprint: Describe the Management of Change process (prior, during, after)

1. Definition

Management of Change is a formal process to identify, evaluate and control the hazards and risks introduced by changes to processes, equipment, chemicals, procedures, facilities, organizations or personnel before the change is implemented. It is required by OSHA PSM (1910.119(l)) and is a key element of ISO 45001 (8.1.3) and ANSI Z10.

2. Explanation in simple words

Every change can bring new hazards. Before you change something, stop and check what could go wrong, fix it, tell and train people, and check afterwards that it worked.

3. Key points to remember

  • Before (prior): request and describe the change, technical basis, hazard identification and risk assessment, impact on procedures, training, PSI and permits, approvals by authorized people.
  • During: implement controls, update drawings (P&IDs) and procedures, train and inform affected employees and contractors, pre-startup safety review (PSSR) for PSM changes.
  • After: verify effectiveness, monitor for unintended consequences, close out and document, retain records.
  • Types of change: permanent, temporary (with an expiry date), emergency (expedited but still reviewed); organizational changes (staffing, reorganizations, mergers) and personnel changes are often missed.
  • Replacement in kind (identical specification) does not need MOC; anything that changes specifications, materials, settings or conditions does.
  • Change management (people side): communicate the reason, involve those affected, address resistance, provide resources (e.g., Kotter's 8 steps, Lewin's unfreeze-change-refreeze).

4. Real work scenario

Maintenance wants to replace a pump with a higher-pressure model. The MOC review finds that the downstream piping, flange gaskets and relief valve are rated below the new pump's shutoff pressure. The relief valve is resized, gaskets are upgraded, procedures are updated and operators are trained before the pump is started.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • The FIRST step of MOC is to evaluate the hazards and risks of the proposed change before implementation.
  • Temporary changes (bypassing an interlock) and organizational changes are MOC triggers.
  • Watch for 'it's the same thing' traps - different material, rating or supplier formulation is not replacement in kind.

6. Every blueprint point (1 blueprint items + 5 more)

MOC processBLUEPRINT

MOC ensures any change to equipment, processes, materials, personnel or organization is identified, assessed and controlled before, during and after implementation so it does not create new hazards or weaken controls.

  • Required for PSM-covered processes by 29 CFR 1910.119(l) and expected by ANSI Z10 and ISO 45001 (clause 8.1.3).
  • PSM MOC must address: technical basis, impact on safety and health, modifications to operating procedures, time period (temporary changes), authorization requirements.
  • Affected employees and contractors must be informed and trained BEFORE startup of the changed process.
  • Update PSI (P&IDs, procedures) after the change; PSSR for new or significantly modified facilities.
  • 'Replacement in kind' (same specification) is not an MOC change.

Exam tip: Most-missed step: the risk assessment of a 'small' or 'temporary' change. Every change - even temporary - goes through MOC.

Before the change (pre-implementation)

Identify and log the change, define scope and impact, assess risk, check regulations, involve stakeholders and obtain documented authorization.

  • Triggers: new equipment, revised procedures, raw materials or supplier, software/controls, layout, staffing levels, organizational changes, temporary modifications.
  • Consider direct and indirect impacts (new chemical -> waste handling hazards).
  • Risk tools: JHA, What-If, HAZOP, FMEA.
  • Check permits, notifications, fire codes, EPA requirements.
  • Formal change request + risk assessment record + management/EHS approval before work starts.

Exam tip: If the scenario says the change was implemented and then reviewed, the MOC failed at the 'before' stage.

During the change (implementation)

Communicate, train, install and verify controls, supervise closely and document everything.

  • Tell affected personnel what, why and when; give clear new instructions.
  • No change is 'safe' until everyone affected is competent.
  • Engineering, administrative and PPE controls in place and verified before the system is activated.
  • Have contingency plans to pause or reverse the change.
  • Use an MOC checklist (ANSI Z10).

After the change (post-implementation)

Verify and validate that controls work, track deviations, update documents, review outcomes and share lessons.

  • Inspect/audit the changed system; get feedback from front-line users.
  • Track incidents and near misses linked to the change; update the risk assessment.
  • Revise SOPs, training, P&IDs, permits, emergency plans.
  • Review MOC outcomes in management review; close the MOC only when documentation is complete.

Organizational and personnel change

Changes in people and structure (downsizing, reassignment, new contractors, loss of experienced staff, shift changes) can degrade safety as much as hardware changes.

  • Assess loss of knowledge, reduced staffing at critical tasks, changed responsibilities.
  • ISO 45001 8.1.3 explicitly includes changes in work organization, staffing and knowledge.
  • Temporary changes need an end date and must be reversed or re-approved.

Exam tip: A reorganization that removes an experienced operator from a critical task should trigger MOC.

Common MOC pitfalls

Most change-related incidents come from skipped steps rather than missing procedures.

  • Skipping risk assessment for 'minor' changes.
  • Failing to update procedures, drawings and training afterward.
  • Not involving the people who will work with the change daily.
  • Allowing 'temporary' changes to become permanent without review.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 38 Management of Change, p. 859
