Domain 2 · Competency 2.6

System Safety Analysis

CSP11 blueprint: Describe system safety analysis techniques (e.g., fault tree analysis, failure modes and effects analysis [FMEA], Safety Case approach, risk summation)

1. Definition

System safety applies engineering and management principles to identify hazards and control risk across the entire life cycle of a system. Analysis techniques can be inductive (bottom-up: what happens if this part fails?) or deductive (top-down: what could cause this undesired event?).

2. Explanation in simple words

Some tools start from a single part and ask what happens if it fails (FMEA). Others start from the disaster and work backwards to find every way it could happen (fault tree). A safety case puts all the evidence together to prove the system is safe enough.

3. Key points to remember

  • Preliminary Hazard Analysis (PHA): early, concept stage, broad hazard list and rough risk ranking.
  • FMEA: inductive, bottom-up; for each component list failure modes, effects and causes; rate Severity, Occurrence and Detection (1-10); RPN = S × O × D. FMECA adds criticality. Limitation: single failures, does not handle combinations or human error well.
  • Fault Tree Analysis (FTA): deductive, top-down from a top (undesired) event using Boolean logic. AND gate: all inputs must occur (multiply probabilities). OR gate: any input (P = 1 − Π(1 − Pi), or approximately add for rare events). Minimal cut sets identify the smallest combinations causing the top event.
  • Event Tree Analysis (ETA): inductive, forward from an initiating event through success/failure of safeguards to outcomes.
  • HAZOP: team-based, guide words (no, more, less, as well as, part of, reverse, other than) applied to process parameters at nodes.
  • Safety Case: a structured, evidence-based argument that a system is acceptably safe (often to ALARP) for a defined use; required in UK offshore, nuclear, rail; goal-based rather than prescriptive.
  • Risk summation: total risk = Σ (frequency × consequence) for all scenarios, used to understand overall risk exposure and compare with tolerability criteria.
  • Other: Energy Trace and Barrier Analysis (ETBA), MORT (management oversight and risk tree), Job Safety Analysis, What-If, Checklist. Design terms: fail-safe, fail-passive (shuts down safely), fail-active (alarms/defaults to safe state), fail-operational (keeps working safely).

4. Real work scenario

A chemical company models a reactor overpressure (top event) with a fault tree. Overpressure occurs if cooling fails (OR gate of pump failure 0.01 and power loss 0.02) AND the relief valve fails (0.001). Cooling failure ≈ 0.0298; top event ≈ 0.0298 × 0.001 = 3.0 × 10⁻⁵ per year, which the safety case compares with the company's tolerability criterion.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Top-down/deductive = FTA. Bottom-up/inductive = FMEA (and ETA).
  • AND gate multiplies; OR gate adds (approximately). Series systems fail if any part fails; parallel/redundant systems fail only if all fail.
  • A high RPN ranks first, but many companies act on any severity 9-10 regardless of RPN.
  • Safety Case = an argument plus evidence that risk is acceptable.

6. Every blueprint point (4 blueprint items + 6 more)

Fault Tree Analysis (FTA)BLUEPRINT

FTA is a deductive, top-down (backward) technique that starts with an undesired Top Event and uses Boolean logic gates to map all combinations of equipment failures, human errors and external events that could cause it. It can be qualitative or quantitative.

  • AND gate: output occurs only if ALL inputs occur - P = P1 x P2 (independent events).
  • OR gate: output occurs if ANY input occurs - P = 1 - (1-P1)(1-P2); approx. P1 + P2 for small probabilities.
  • Example: AND of 0.01 and 0.02 = 0.0002; OR of 0.01 and 0.02 = 1 - (0.99)(0.98) = 0.0298.
  • Minimal cut set = smallest combination of basic events that causes the top event; a single-event cut set = single point of failure.
  • Best for complex, high-risk technical systems (process, aviation, nuclear, utilities); output = fault tree diagram and cut sets.

Exam tip: FTA = deductive, top event, logic gates. Adding redundancy changes an OR into an AND and sharply lowers top-event probability.

FMEA / FMECABLUEPRINT

FMEA is an inductive, bottom-up, proactive technique that lists each component, every way it can fail (failure mode) and the effect on the system. FMECA adds a criticality ranking of severity and likelihood.

  • RPN = Severity x Occurrence (probability) x Detection, each usually rated 1-10 (RPN 1-1000).
  • Example: S = 8, O = 5, D = 4 -> RPN = 160; act on highest RPN and on any high-severity mode.
  • Detection is rated inversely: 10 = almost impossible to detect.
  • Good for design, manufacturing and equipment reliability; poor at human error and combined failures.
  • Not a root cause/investigation tool - it anticipates what could fail, not why something did fail. PEM chart summarizes ratings.

Exam tip: FMEA = component failure modes, bottom-up, single failures; FTA = top event, top-down, combinations.

Safety CaseBLUEPRINT

A structured, evidence-based argument showing that a system or facility achieves and maintains an acceptable level of safety, prepared for regulators and stakeholders.

  • Three elements: Claim (the system is safe under stated conditions), Argument (reasoning), Evidence (tests, analyses, records, audits).
  • Living document - updated after changes, incidents, audits or new regulations.
  • Must be documented, traceable, auditable and defensible.
  • Used in high-hazard regulated sectors: offshore oil and gas (UK), nuclear, rail, aviation, defense; often required for licensing.

Exam tip: If the question says 'demonstrate to a regulator that risks are reduced to ALARP with supporting evidence', the answer is a Safety Case.

Risk summation (risk aggregation)BLUEPRINT

Risk summation combines many individual risk values into a total risk profile so cumulative risk is not underestimated.

  • Risk = Probability x Consequence for each hazard; total = sum of individual risks.
  • Example: 0.1/yr x $50,000 = $5,000; 0.05/yr x $200,000 = $10,000; 0.5/yr x $4,000 = $2,000 -> total expected loss $17,000/yr.
  • Compare total to risk tolerance/ALARP; the largest contributor ($10,000) gets resources first.
  • Use consistent scales (all 1-5 or all real probabilities and dollars).

Exam tip: Several 'low' risks can sum to an unacceptable total - that is the point of risk summation.

Event Tree Analysis (ETA)

ETA is inductive and forward-looking: from one initiating event, it branches at each safety function (success/failure) to show all possible outcomes. No logic gates.

  • Outcome frequency = initiating frequency x product of branch probabilities along the path.
  • Example: fire starts 0.1/yr; alarm fails 0.05; sprinkler fails 0.02 -> worst outcome = 0.1 x 0.05 x 0.02 = 0.0001/yr.
  • Used to evaluate barrier effectiveness and redundancy and model consequences.

Exam tip: ETA = initiating event -> consequences (forward); FTA = top event -> causes (backward).

Cause-consequence analysis and bow-tie

CCA combines FTA (causes) and ETA (consequences). The bow-tie diagram shows threats and preventive barriers on the left, the hazardous top event (knot) in the middle, and consequences with mitigating barriers on the right.

  • Left side = prevention barriers; right side = mitigation/recovery barriers.
  • Fishbone often used first to brainstorm causes.
  • Excellent for communicating risk to non-technical audiences.

HAZOP

Team-based, node-by-node study of deviations from design intent using guide words combined with process parameters.

  • Guide words: No/None, More, Less, As well as, Part of, Reverse, Other than (plus High/Low).
  • Parameters: flow, pressure, temperature, level, time, composition.
  • Deviation = guide word + parameter (e.g., 'No flow', 'High pressure').
  • Led by a trained facilitator with a multidisciplinary team; output = report with recommendations; aims for ALARP. A PSM-accepted PHA method.

Exam tip: Guide words + nodes = HAZOP.

THERP (human reliability)

Technique for Human Error Rate Prediction breaks a task into steps and assigns human error probabilities to each to estimate overall task failure probability.

  • Uses task analysis and standard error probability tables, adjusted by performance shaping factors (stress, training, design).
  • Overall success = product of step success probabilities; e.g., 3 steps each 0.99 -> 0.97 success, 0.03 failure.
  • Feeds FTA/PRA; used in nuclear and process industries for routine procedural tasks.

MORT (Management Oversight and Risk Tree)

MORT is a pre-drawn logic tree used to find management and organizational causes of incidents ('oversights and omissions' and 'assumed risks').

  • Developed for the U.S. Department of Energy; very detailed checklist-style tree.
  • Focus is on management system failures, not just technical causes.
  • Used reactively (investigations) and proactively (program evaluation).

Exam tip: Management/organizational root causes + tree = MORT.

MIL-STD-882E and other hazard analyses

MIL-STD-882E is the DoD system safety standard defining risk assessment matrices and hazard analysis tasks across the system life cycle.

  • Severity: 1 Catastrophic, 2 Critical, 3 Marginal, 4 Negligible.
  • Probability: A Frequent, B Probable, C Occasional, D Remote, E Improbable, F Eliminated.
  • Preliminary Hazard List/Analysis (PHL/PHA) is done early in design; What-If and checklist analyses are simpler qualitative methods.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 22 Accident Causation and Investigation Techniques (FTA), p. 515; Ch. 26 Risk Assessment and Management, p. 604; Ch. 15 Statistics (probability)

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BRPN = S x O x D. X = 10 x 2 x 3 = 60; Y = 5 x 6 x 5 = 150, so Y ranks higher. Trap: many organizations also act on any severity 10 regardless of RPN.
2BAND gate multiplies: 0.1 x 0.2 = 0.02. OR gate: 1 - (1 - 0.02)(1 - 0.01) = 0.0298, about 0.03 (rare-event approximation: 0.02 + 0.01).
3CRisk summation adds each scenario's frequency x consequence: 5,000 + 2,000 + 1,000 = $8,000 per year. $522,000 adds consequences without frequency.
4AA Safety Case is a structured, evidence-supported argument that a system is acceptably safe for a given application and environment (used in offshore, nuclear, rail, defense).
5DEvent tree analysis is inductive: it starts with an initiating event and maps success/failure paths of safeguards to outcomes. FTA works top-down from an undesired event.
6AFault tree analysis uses Boolean AND/OR gates to show combinations of basic events that produce a top event; it is deductive (top-down).
7CFMEA is inductive (bottom-up): it starts with individual component failure modes and determines their effects on the system. FTA is the deductive opposite.
8DBreaking a process into nodes and applying guide words (more, less, none, reverse) to parameters like flow, pressure and temperature is HAZOP.
9CFail-passive design: on failure the system goes to its lowest-energy/inoperative state without creating an adverse effect (e.g., a circuit breaker opening). Fail-active/operational designs keep functioning.
10DETBA identifies energy sources in a system and evaluates the adequacy of barriers between those energies and people or property.
Printed book page 73
Domain 2 · Competency 2.7

Leading and Lagging Indicators

CSP11 blueprint: Evaluate leading and lagging indicators

1. Definition

Lagging indicators measure outcomes after harm or loss has occurred (injuries, illnesses, lost days, costs). Leading indicators measure the activities, conditions and behaviors that predict and prevent future harm (inspections completed, hazards corrected, training, near-miss reports, observations).

2. Explanation in simple words

Lagging indicators look in the rear-view mirror - they tell you how many people already got hurt. Leading indicators look through the windshield - they tell you whether you are doing the things that prevent people from getting hurt.

3. Key points to remember

  • Lagging examples: TRIR, DART rate, LTIR, severity rate, fatalities, workers' compensation costs, EMR, property damage.
  • Leading examples: % inspections completed on time, % corrective actions closed by due date, near-miss reports, safety observations, training completion, PTW/JHA quality audits, management walk-arounds, perception survey scores.
  • Formulas: Incidence rate = (number of cases × 200,000) ÷ hours worked; 200,000 = 100 full-time workers × 40 h × 50 weeks. Severity rate = (days lost × 200,000) ÷ hours.
  • Good leading indicators are linked to specific risks, actionable, measurable, timely and validated against outcomes; avoid metrics that can be 'gamed'.
  • Use both: leading to drive prevention, lagging to verify results and benchmark. TRIR alone hides low-frequency, high-severity (SIF) risks.
  • Interpreting changes: rising near-miss reports after a reporting campaign usually indicates better reporting; a sudden drop may indicate underreporting.

4. Real work scenario

A construction firm with a TRIR of 1.2 had a fatality from a trench collapse. Its TRIR had looked good for years because minor injuries were rare. It adds leading indicators focused on serious-injury potential: daily trench inspections completed, % of excavations with protective systems verified, and critical-lift plans reviewed.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Indicator classification questions are common - ask 'did harm already happen?' If yes, lagging.
  • Calculation: 14 recordables in 350,000 hours → (14 × 200,000) ÷ 350,000 = 8.0.
  • Lagging indicators are useful for benchmarking; leading indicators guide proactive action.

6. Every blueprint point (2 blueprint items + 3 more)

Leading indicatorsBLUEPRINT

Proactive, predictive measures of activities, behaviors or conditions that prevent incidents and show whether safety systems are working.

  • Examples: training sessions completed, % corrective actions closed on time, near-miss and hazard reports, safety observations/BBS checks, toolbox talk completion, inspections done, PM completed on time.
  • Strengths: early warning, encourage prevention and participation.
  • Limitations: harder to define and standardize; need a reporting culture.
  • Sources: ANSI/ASSP Z16.1, NSC leading indicator guidance, OSHA.

Exam tip: Number of near-miss reports is LEADING; number of injuries is LAGGING. Rising near-miss reporting is usually good news.

Lagging indicatorsBLUEPRINT

Reactive, outcome-based measures of harm or loss that has already occurred.

  • Examples: TRIR, DART rate, lost-time injury rate, severity rate, workers' comp claims and costs, citations, property damage.
  • TRIR = recordable cases x 200,000 / hours worked. Example: 12 cases, 480,000 h -> 5.0.
  • DART rate = DART cases x 200,000 / hours. Example: 6 cases, 480,000 h -> 2.5.
  • Severity rate = lost workdays x 200,000 / hours. Example: 90 days, 480,000 h -> 37.5.
  • 200,000 = 100 full-time workers x 40 h x 50 weeks.
  • Limitations: show only past failures, don't say what to fix, can be masked by under-reporting.

Exam tip: 'Managing by the accident book' (lagging only) is a weak program. Use both types.

Selecting and evaluating indicators

Indicators should align with key risks and be balanced between leading and lagging.

  • Choose metrics tied to your significant risks; set baselines and targets (OSHA/NSC data).
  • Track trends - do increases in leading activity precede decreases in lagging outcomes?
  • Communicate and act on results; re-assess indicators as performance improves.
  • Good indicator: valid, reliable, actionable, understood, not easily manipulated.

Experience Modification Rate (EMR)

EMR is a workers' compensation rating comparing an employer's actual losses to expected losses for its industry and payroll.

  • EMR = actual losses / expected losses (simplified); 1.0 = industry average.
  • EMR 0.80 -> premium about 20% below manual; EMR 1.25 -> 25% above.
  • Based on about 3 prior policy years (excluding the most recent year); often used to prequalify contractors.
  • It is a lagging indicator.

Exam tip: EMR < 1.0 is better than average.

Pitfalls of indicators

Poorly designed metrics drive the wrong behavior.

  • Injury-free incentive programs can suppress reporting.
  • Counting activities (e.g., number of audits) without measuring quality.
  • Using lagging data alone for small sites - too few events to be statistically meaningful.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 3 Recording and Reporting Occupational Injuries, p. 44; Ch. 36 Measuring Safety and Health Performance, p. 797

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

2.7-Q1. During a quarterly safety review, a site had 5 recordable injuries with 250,000 work hours. What is the Total Recordable Incident Rate (TRIR)?

  1. 3.2
  2. 4.0
  3. 2.5
  4. 1.6

2.7-Q2. A company records 11 DART cases in 452,000 hours worked. DART rate?

  1. 3.4
  2. 4.9
  3. 7.8
  4. 11.0

2.7-Q3. A site recorded 8 OSHA recordable cases in 400,000 hours: 3 involved days away from work, 2 involved restricted duty/job transfer, and 3 were medical treatment only. What is the DART rate?

  1. 1.5
  2. 2.5
  3. 4.0
  4. 1.0

2.7-Q4. During a monthly safety review, a supervisor presents data showing a drop in near-miss reports compared to the previous quarter. What type of indicator is this, and how should it be interpreted?

  1. Leading indicator; it shows workers are becoming less engaged in hazard reporting
  2. Lagging indicator; it confirms fewer injuries occurred
  3. Leading indicator; it may reflect underreporting or lack of hazard awareness
  4. Lagging indicator; it demonstrates completed corrective actions

2.7-Q5. A construction company uses its Total Recordable Incident Rate (TRIR) as the sole measure of safety program success. What is the main limitation of relying only on this lagging indicator?

  1. It tracks leading behaviors, not outcomes
  2. It requires extensive job hazard analysis
  3. It provides real-time predictive insight
  4. It only reflects past injuries and may ignore hidden risks

2.7-Q6. A general contractor is evaluating the safety performance of subcontractors. Which of the following is the BEST combination of leading and lagging indicators?

  1. Pre-task hazard analyses completed and number of recordable cases
  2. Lost-time injuries and workers' compensation costs
  3. Number of OSHA citations and experience modification rate
  4. Days without injury and safety posters displayed

2.7-Q7. Which of the following is the most effective leading performance indicator?

  1. Percentage of near-miss reports closed with action verification
  2. Annual OSHA recordable rate
  3. Workers' compensation claim cost
  4. Lost-time injury frequency rate

2.7-Q8. Which key question should management ask to verify KPI effectiveness?

  1. Can the KPI be compared to competitors?
  2. Is the KPI simple to calculate?
  3. Does the KPI align with organizational goals and drive desired behavior?
  4. Does the KPI reduce workload for data teams?

2.7-Q9. A construction company has experienced a rise in near-miss incidents over the past quarter, though no recordable injuries have occurred. The safety manager decides to start tracking the number of completed pre-task hazard analyses (PTHAs), the frequency of supervisor safety observations, and attendance at toolbox talks each week. Which Of the following best describes the type Of safety performance measurement being implemented?

  1. Lagging indicators that evaluate past incidents and injuries
  2. Predictive analytics that estimate total recordable incident rates (TRIR) based on prior data
  3. Financial performance metrics that track the cost of safety incidents
  4. Leading indicators that measure proactive actions taken to prevent incidents

2.7-Q10. Your company wants to get to an annual incident rate of 0.5 recordable injuries. This indicator is a:

  1. Lagging indicator
  2. Occupational indicator
  3. Leading indicator
  4. Cumulative indicator

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BTRIR = 5 x 200,000 / 250,000 = 4.0. The 200,000 represents 100 full-time workers x 2,000 hours.
2BDART = 11 x 200,000 / 452,000 = 4.87, about 4.9 (OSHA 1904 rate formula).
3BDART counts only days-away plus restricted/transfer cases: (3 + 2) x 200,000 / 400,000 = 2.5. Trap: using all 8 cases gives the TRIR (4.0), and days-away only gives 1.5.
4CNear-miss reporting is a leading indicator; a drop may mean underreporting or lower hazard awareness rather than a safer workplace, so investigate before celebrating.
5DTRIR is lagging: it reflects past injuries, is statistically unstable at small hours, and can hide high-potential hazards and underreporting.
6APre-task hazard analyses completed (leading) paired with recordable cases (lagging) gives a meaningful balanced view. The other pairs are weak, mostly one type, or not meaningful measures.
7APercentage of near-miss reports closed with verified action measures proactive hazard control and follow-through. The other options are lagging outcomes.
8CA KPI is effective only if it aligns with organizational goals and drives the desired behavior; ease of calculation or comparability are secondary.
9DPTHAs completed, supervisor observations and toolbox talk attendance measure proactive activities before incidents: leading indicators.
10AA target incident rate is still an outcome measure based on injuries that already occurred: a lagging indicator.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.8

EHS Management and Audit Systems

CSP11 blueprint: Recognize safety, health, and environmental management and audit systems (e.g., ISO 14000 series, 45001, 19011, ANSI Z10)

1. Definition

Management systems are structured frameworks that integrate EHS into business processes using the Plan-Do-Check-Act cycle. ISO 45001 (OH&S), ISO 14001 (environmental), ANSI/ASSP Z10 (OHSMS, U.S.) and ISO 19011 (guidelines for auditing management systems) are the principal standards.

2. Explanation in simple words

A management system is the company's 'operating system' for safety and environment: set policy, plan, do the work, check results, and keep improving. Audits check whether the system is in place and working.

3. Key points to remember

  • ISO 45001 (2018): Annex SL high-level structure - context, leadership and worker participation, planning (hazards, risks, opportunities, legal requirements, objectives), support (resources, competence, communication, documented information), operation (hierarchy of controls, MOC, procurement, contractors, emergency), performance evaluation (monitoring, internal audit, management review), improvement (incidents, nonconformity, corrective action, continual improvement). Requires consultation and participation of non-managerial workers.
  • ISO 14001: environmental aspects (causes, e.g., solvent use) and impacts (effects, e.g., air pollution), compliance obligations, life-cycle perspective. ISO 14000 family includes 14031 (performance evaluation), 14040/44 (life-cycle assessment), 14064 (greenhouse gases).
  • ANSI/ASSP Z10: U.S. OHSMS standard; hierarchy of controls includes 'warnings' between engineering and administrative controls; emphasizes management leadership and employee participation.
  • ISO 19011: guidelines for auditing management systems. Principles: integrity, fair presentation, due professional care, confidentiality, independence, evidence-based approach, risk-based approach. Audit program → plan → opening meeting → collect and verify evidence → findings → closing meeting → report → follow-up.
  • Other: ISO 9001 (quality), ISO 31000 (risk management), ISO 45003 (psychological health and safety at work), ISO 50001 (energy), ISO 27001 (information security), OSHA VPP, OHSAS 18001 (withdrawn, replaced by 45001).
  • Audit types: first-party (internal), second-party (customer/supplier), third-party (certification). Findings: major/minor nonconformity, observation, opportunity for improvement.

4. Real work scenario

A manufacturer pursues ISO 45001 certification. The gap assessment shows strong procedures but no formal process for non-managerial worker participation in hazard identification. The company forms worker-led JHA teams and a safety committee with elected members, and documents their input before the certification audit.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Aspect = cause; impact = effect (ISO 14001).
  • ISO 19011 is about how to audit; it is not a certifiable management system standard.
  • Auditors should not audit their own work (independence).
  • Management review is part of 'Check/Act'; internal audit is 'Check'.

6. Every blueprint point (4 blueprint items + 4 more)

ISO 14000 seriesBLUEPRINT

ISO 14001:2015 is the certifiable environmental management system (EMS) standard; the wider 14000 family supports it with guidance on performance, life-cycle and greenhouse gases.

  • ISO 14001 focuses on environmental aspects and impacts (emissions, discharges, waste, resource use, biodiversity), compliance obligations, and pollution prevention.
  • Same high-level (harmonized) structure as ISO 45001 and ISO 9001: clauses 4 Context, 5 Leadership, 6 Planning, 7 Support, 8 Operation, 9 Performance evaluation, 10 Improvement.
  • ISO 14004 = EMS implementation guidance; ISO 14031 = environmental performance evaluation; ISO 14040/14044 = life-cycle assessment; ISO 14064 = GHG accounting.
  • Certification is voluntary, by third-party registrars.

Exam tip: Aspect = the activity element (solvent use); impact = the change to the environment (air pollution).

ISO 45001BLUEPRINT

ISO 45001:2018 is the first international certifiable occupational health and safety management system standard; it replaced OHSAS 18001 (withdrawn 2021).

  • Clauses: 1 Scope, 2 Normative references, 3 Terms, 4 Context of the organization, 5 Leadership and worker participation, 6 Planning, 7 Support, 8 Operation, 9 Performance evaluation, 10 Improvement.
  • Core principles: leadership commitment, worker consultation and participation (including non-managerial workers), risk-based thinking, context, PDCA continual improvement.
  • Clause 8 covers hierarchy of controls, MOC, procurement/contractors and emergency preparedness.
  • Certification is voluntary but often required for contracts and supply chains.

Exam tip: Choose ISO 45001 when international recognition or certification is required.

ISO 19011BLUEPRINT

ISO 19011:2018 gives guidelines for auditing any management system (45001, 14001, 9001); it is guidance, not certifiable.

  • Audit principles: integrity, fair presentation, due professional care, confidentiality, independence, evidence-based approach, risk-based approach.
  • Covers managing an audit program, conducting audits and auditor competence (knowledge, skills, behavior).
  • Audit steps: initiate (objectives, scope, criteria) -> prepare (review documents, audit plan) -> conduct (opening meeting, interviews, observation, sampling, evidence) -> report (closing meeting, major/minor nonconformities, opportunities) -> follow-up (verify corrective action effectiveness).
  • Close findings only when evidence of real improvement is confirmed.

Exam tip: Auditor independence: auditors should not audit their own work.

ANSI Z10BLUEPRINT

ANSI/ASSP Z10.0-2019 is the U.S. consensus OHSMS standard developed by ANSI and ASSP (first published 2005). It is a guidance standard, not a certification scheme.

  • Structure (PDCA): management leadership and employee participation; planning; implementation and operation; evaluation and corrective action; management review.
  • Strong emphasis on prevention through design, employee participation and integration with business processes.
  • U.S. legal context (references OSHA and NIOSH); flexible language for any size organization.
  • Compared with ISO 45001: both PDCA and require worker participation; 45001 is certifiable and global, Z10 is not certifiable and U.S.-focused.

Exam tip: U.S. company wanting a practical, flexible framework without certification -> Z10; international contracts -> ISO 45001.

Safety management system (SMS) basics

An SMS is a formal, organization-wide framework of interrelated elements to identify, assess, control and review OH&S risks - an integrated, dynamic approach, not a manual on a shelf.

  • Key functions: hazard identification, risk assessment and control, regulatory compliance, promotion of safety culture.
  • Effective SMS: visible leadership, active worker participation, defined accountability, integration with business operations, measurement and continual improvement.
  • Reasons: legal compliance, risk reduction, continual improvement, reputation/competitive advantage, moral responsibility.
  • Hazard = source of potential harm; risk = likelihood x severity of harm.

Exam tip: An SMS is most effective when supported by top management and integrated into business processes; otherwise it becomes a 'paper system'.

OSHA Recommended Practices (SHMS)

OSHA's Recommended Practices for Safety and Health Programs (2016) are voluntary best-practice guidance with seven core elements.

  • 7 elements: management leadership; worker participation; hazard identification and assessment; hazard prevention and control; education and training; program evaluation and improvement; communication and coordination for host employers, contractors and staffing agencies.
  • OSHA cannot cite an employer simply for lacking a comprehensive program.
  • Specific OSHA standards remain mandatory.

Exam tip: Voluntary guidance vs mandatory standard is a common distractor.

Integrated management systems (IMS)

Because ISO 9001, 14001 and 45001 share a common high-level structure, organizations often combine them into one IMS.

  • Shared policy, unified procedures (incident reporting, audits, corrective action), combined audits, joint management reviews.
  • Benefits: saves time and resources, less duplication, simpler documentation, consistent compliance, aligned safety and environmental goals.
  • Example: one solvent-reduction objective cuts emissions and worker exposure.

Types of audits

Audits are classified by who performs them.

  • First-party (internal): organization audits itself - self-assessment, preparation.
  • Second-party: customer (or agent) audits a supplier or contractor.
  • Third-party: independent certification body (registrar) - leads to certification.
  • Compliance audit (against regulations) vs management system audit (against a standard) vs program audit.
  • Nonconformities: major (system failure/absence of requirement) vs minor (isolated lapse).

Exam tip: Customer auditing its supplier = second-party.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 31 Safety Management System, p. 717; Ch. 37 Safety Program Auditing Techniques, p. 818; Ch. 34 Environmental Management

  2. Failure to meet a specified requirement

QAnsWhy
1AISO 19011 requires audit objectives, scope and criteria to be determined before the audit plan is finalized; contact the auditee/client to confirm them. Assuming or waiting compromises the audit.
2BISO 19011 principles include independence: auditors should not audit their own work, to remain free from bias and conflict of interest.
3CThe evidence-based approach is the rational method for reaching reliable, reproducible audit conclusions from verifiable evidence.
4DISO 45001 clause 9.3 management review inputs include audit results, incidents, objectives, performance trends and opportunities for improvement.
5BA repeat nonconformity indicates that the earlier corrective action did not address the root cause or was not verified for effectiveness.
6AAspect = an element of activities, products or services that can interact with the environment (the cause); impact = the resulting change to the environment.
7CIntegrated management systems typically combine ISO 9001 (quality), ISO 14001 (environment) and ISO 45001 (OH&S), which share the same high-level structure and PDCA basis.
8DISO 45001 and ANSI/ASSP Z10.0 are the principal consensus standards for occupational health and safety management systems. ISO 9001 is quality, ISO 14001 environmental, ISO 19011 audit guidance, and Lean a production method.
9CISO 45001 policy must include commitments to eliminate hazards and reduce OH&S risks and to continual improvement; it does not prescribe specific tools or individual certification.
10BA nonconformity is non-fulfillment of a requirement (standard, procedure, legal or organizational requirement).

Domain 2 · Competency 2.9

Plans, Systems and Policies

CSP11 blueprint: Describe required components for plans, systems, and policies (e.g., safety, health, and environmental regulations and standards)

1. Definition

Written plans, programs and policies document how an organization meets regulatory and consensus requirements and controls its risks. Many OSHA and EPA standards require specific written programs with defined elements.

2. Explanation in simple words

A policy says what the company commits to; a program or plan says how it will do it, who is responsible and how it is checked. Regulators ask, 'Show me it is written, trained and working.'

3. Key points to remember

  • Safety and health policy: signed top-management commitment, scope, responsibilities, compliance, continual improvement, worker participation.
  • Core program elements (OSHA Recommended Practices for Safety and Health Programs, 2016): management leadership, worker participation, hazard identification and assessment, hazard prevention and control, education and training, program evaluation and improvement, communication and coordination for host employers, contractors and staffing agencies.
  • Examples of required written programs: Hazard Communication (1910.1200), Emergency Action Plan (1910.38 - may be oral for 10 or fewer employees), Fire Prevention Plan (1910.39), Respiratory Protection (1910.134), Energy Control/LOTO (1910.147), Permit-Required Confined Space (1910.146), Bloodborne Pathogens Exposure Control Plan (1910.1030, reviewed annually), Hearing Conservation (1910.95), PSM (1910.119); EPA SPCC (40 CFR 112), RMP, stormwater SWPPP.
  • Typical program structure: purpose, scope, definitions, responsibilities, procedures, training, inspections/audits, recordkeeping, program review.
  • Multi-employer worksites: OSHA citation policy - creating, exposing, correcting and controlling employers; host and contractor coordination.
  • Legal hierarchy: OSH Act (General Duty Clause 5(a)(1)) → OSHA standards (vertical/specific vs horizontal/general) → consensus standards incorporated by reference → company rules.

4. Real work scenario

A host employer brings in a contractor to repair a tank. The plans required are the contractor's confined-space program, the host's permit system and hazard information exchange, a site-specific safety plan defining responsibilities, and the emergency/rescue plan. Both employers sign the entry permit and coordinate rescue.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • If a scenario asks what is missing from a program, look for the required element (e.g., employee accountability in an EAP, worker participation in an SMS).
  • A policy without signed management commitment is weak on legal defensibility.
  • Know who is the controlling employer on multi-employer sites (the general contractor with authority over the site).

6. Every blueprint point (1 blueprint items + 5 more)

Plans, systems and policiesBLUEPRINT

A policy states commitment and direction; plans translate the policy into specific objectives, actions and resources; systems (procedures, programs, controls) carry the plan out day to day.

  • Hierarchy: policy (why, signed by top management) -> plans/programs (what, who, when) -> procedures/SOPs (how) -> records (proof).
  • Policy = concise statement of intent; procedure = specified step-by-step way to do an activity.
  • Plans and policies must be based on applicable regulations and standards (OSHA, EPA, NFPA, ANSI, ISO).
  • Review regularly and after significant change or incidents.

Exam tip: A strong plan is SMART, reviewed and ties policy to action; a weak plan is vague with unclear roles and no measurement.

Essential elements of an SH&E policy

A credible policy is short, signed by top management, communicated to all and reviewed.

  • Statement of intent/commitment to SH&E.
  • Assignment of roles and responsibilities (accountability).
  • Risk management approach (hazard ID, hierarchy of controls).
  • Commitment to comply with legal requirements and to continual improvement.
  • Commitment to communication and worker consultation.
  • ISO 45001 5.2 also requires commitments to eliminate hazards/reduce risks and to worker participation.

Exam tip: Policy signed by the CEO/top management shows commitment - the safety manager signing alone is weaker.

Components of an SH&E plan

The plan provides the roadmap from policy to results.

  • SMART goals and objectives.
  • Legal compliance list (OSHA, EPA, consensus standards).
  • Hazard identification methods (walkthroughs, JHA, investigations, worker reports, audits).
  • Training schedules; emergency plans; audit and inspection checklists.
  • Record retention schedule (what, how long, where).

Core SMS elements in practice

Leadership, planning, implementation, evaluation and continual improvement must all be present.

  • Leadership: signs policy, allocates budget and staff, chairs safety committee, responds to audit findings.
  • Planning: hazard ID, risk matrices, SMART objectives, legal requirements.
  • Implementation: roles, training and competence, operational controls (SOPs, contractors, safe purchasing), communication.
  • Evaluation: leading/lagging measures, incident investigation, audits, management review at least annually.
  • If incidents continue despite an 'SMS', look for the missing element: leadership, participation, risk assessment or effective corrective action.

Exam tip: Strong SMS: proactive, resourced, data-driven, management-led, employee-involved. Weak: reactive, underfunded, compliance-only.

OSHA mandatory written programs

Although overall SHMS is voluntary, many OSHA standards require written programs and training.

  • Written programs: Hazard Communication (1910.1200), energy control/LOTO (1910.147), respiratory protection (1910.134), permit-required confined spaces (1910.146), PSM (1910.119), emergency action and fire prevention plans (1910.38/.39; may be oral if 10 or fewer employees), bloodborne pathogens exposure control plan (reviewed annually).
  • PPE: written certification of hazard assessment (1910.132(d)); PPE provided at no cost.
  • Training examples: forklift operator evaluation at least every 3 years; BBP annual; LOTO for authorized, affected and other employees.
  • General Duty Clause 5(a)(1): workplace free from recognized hazards likely to cause death or serious harm (used for heat, workplace violence, etc.).

OSHA recordkeeping and reporting (29 CFR 1904)

Employers record work-related injuries and illnesses and report severe events.

  • Forms: 300 log, 300A annual summary, 301 incident report (within 7 calendar days of learning of the case).
  • Report fatality within 8 hours; in-patient hospitalization, amputation or loss of an eye within 24 hours.
  • Post 300A from February 1 to April 30; signed by a company executive.
  • Retain 5 years following the end of the calendar year covered.
  • Partial exemption: 10 or fewer employees, and certain low-hazard industries.

Exam tip: Fatality = 8 hours; hospitalization/amputation/eye = 24 hours.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 4 Regulations, p. 55; Ch. 31 Safety Management System, p. 717

2.9-Q10. A chemical processing plant stores large quantities of flammable liquids under pressure. Which of the following best describes the primary objective of a Process Hazard Analysis (PHA)?

  2. To document safety performance indicators for quarterly OSHA reporting
  3. To determine the financial impact of loss-of-containment scenarios
  4. To verify the competency of emergency response contractors

QAnsWhy
1AA safety and health policy must include a written commitment from top management; it establishes leadership accountability and supports legal defensibility.
2BThe HSG65 model (POPMAR): Policy, Organizing, Planning and implementing, Measuring performance, and Auditing and reviewing. Options missing auditing/review are incomplete.
3CThe GC had general supervisory authority over the site and the ability to correct hazards, which makes it the controlling employer under OSHA's multi-employer citation policy, even though it did not create the hazard.
4DContractor prequalification evaluates safety performance and capability (rates, EMR, programs, training) before award; insurance is only one part.
5BContractor orientation should cover site-specific hazards, emergency procedures and reporting expectations before work begins.
6AOSHA defines a competent person as one who can identify existing and predictable hazards and has authorization to take prompt corrective measures to eliminate them.
7CWorker participation (OSHA Recommended Practices; ISO 45001 clause 5.4) means employees are consulted and participate in hazard identification and program review, not just informed after the fact.
8DProject scope, site conditions and subcontractor responsibilities belong in the project overview and responsibility-assignment section of a site-specific safety plan.
9BThe host/GC should require the subcontractor's program to be revised to meet OSHA requirements before approval. Past performance does not substitute for compliance, and rewriting it yourself shifts responsibility.
10AA PHA under PSM (1910.119(e)) systematically identifies, evaluates and controls hazards of highly hazardous chemical processes.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.10

Document Retention and Management

CSP11 blueprint: Utilize document retention or management principles (e.g., incident investigation, training records, exposure records, maintenance records, environmental management system, audit results, privacy, trade secrets, personal information)

1. Definition

Document management ensures EHS records are created, controlled, protected, retrievable and kept for the required time, while respecting privacy, confidentiality and trade-secret requirements. Records provide legal evidence, support analysis and demonstrate compliance.

2. Explanation in simple words

If it is not written down, it did not happen. Keep the right records for the right number of years, protect personal and medical information, and make sure you can find the latest version quickly.

3. Key points to remember

  • OSHA 300/300A/301 injury and illness records: 5 years after the end of the calendar year; 300A posted Feb 1 - Apr 30.
  • Employee exposure and medical records (1910.1020): exposure records 30 years; medical records duration of employment + 30 years; employees and representatives have access rights (usually within 15 working days).
  • Other examples: PSM incident investigation reports 5 years; PHA for life of the process; confined-space cancelled permits 1 year; noise exposure measurements 2 years and audiograms duration of employment; respirator fit tests until the next test; bloodborne pathogens training 3 years, medical records employment + 30 years; RCRA hazardous waste manifests 3 years; crane inspection records; forklift training certification.
  • Privacy: privacy concern cases on the 300 Log (sexual assault, mental illness, HIV/hepatitis/TB, needlestick from contaminated sharps, others on request) - write 'privacy case' instead of the name. Medical records kept confidential and separate from personnel files; HIPAA (healthcare), GDPR (EU personal data), PII protection (e.g., SSN, financial data).
  • Trade secrets: HCS allows withholding a chemical's specific identity on the SDS, but it must be disclosed immediately to a treating health professional in an emergency (confidentiality agreement afterwards); non-emergency requests in writing.
  • Document control: version control, approval, distribution, obsolete document removal, backups (cloud, off-site), access control, retention schedule, legal hold during litigation. Never alter or backdate records; if data are missing, redo the measurement and document the failure.
  • Records are discoverable in litigation; audit findings that are documented must be acted on - known but uncorrected findings increase liability.

4. Real work scenario

A noise dosimeter fails during a compliance survey just before a deadline. Instead of estimating readings, the safety professional documents the equipment failure, re-samples the next day and files both records. Months later an attorney requests the records in a hearing-loss claim, and the complete, honest documentation supports the company.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • 5 years (OSHA logs) vs 30 years (exposure) vs employment + 30 years (medical) is a classic exam trio.
  • Ethics plus records: never falsify or 'adjust' data.
  • Trade secret + medical emergency = disclose immediately to the treating professional.

6. Every blueprint point (9 blueprint items + 1 more)

Incident recordsBLUEPRINT

Injury and illness records document what happened and support trend analysis and compliance.

  • OSHA 300, 300A, 301: keep 5 years following the end of the calendar year; update the 300 log during the 5 years.
  • Employee or representative may request 300 log copy - provide by end of next business day.
  • Investigation reports: keep per company policy; PSM incident investigation reports kept 5 years.
  • Electronic submission (1904.41): establishments with 20-249 employees in designated industries submit 300A; 100+ employees in high-hazard industries also submit 300 and 301 data.

Exam tip: 300 logs = 5 years; exposure records = 30 years - don't mix them up.

Training recordsBLUEPRINT

Training records prove competence and compliance; retention periods vary by standard.

  • Record: name, date, content/topic, trainer, method of evaluation.
  • Bloodborne pathogens training records: 3 years from training date.
  • Asbestos training: 1 year beyond last date of employment.
  • Forklift: certification of training and evaluation; evaluate every 3 years.
  • Many standards require 'certification' records (LOTO periodic inspection, PRCS) - keep at least as long as the standard specifies; keep longer by policy for defense.

Exposure recordsBLUEPRINT

Employee exposure records (monitoring results, biological monitoring, SDS/chemical identity data) must be kept long-term because occupational diseases have long latency (29 CFR 1910.1020).

  • Exposure records: at least 30 years.
  • Medical records: duration of employment plus 30 years (except employees employed less than 1 year who are given their records at termination).
  • Background data (lab reports, field notes) may be kept 1 year if sampling results, methods and summary are kept 30 years.
  • SDSs need not be kept if a record of chemical identity, where and when used is kept for 30 years (the guide states SDSs must be kept 30 years).
  • Noise: exposure measurements 2 years; audiograms for duration of employment (1910.95(m)).
  • Access: employees and designated representatives within 15 working days.

Exam tip: 1910.1020 = 30 years exposure; employment + 30 years medical.

Maintenance recordsBLUEPRINT

Maintenance and inspection records prove equipment is safe and support mechanical integrity, warranty and investigation.

  • PSM mechanical integrity: document each inspection and test (date, inspector, equipment ID, test performed, results).
  • Cranes: monthly inspection records of critical items (hooks, ropes) kept; certification records.
  • Fire extinguishers: annual maintenance record kept 1 year after last entry or life of shell, whichever is less (1910.157).
  • LOTO periodic inspection certified at least annually.

Environmental recordsBLUEPRINT

Environmental permits and regulations specify record contents and retention.

  • RCRA: hazardous waste manifests, biennial reports and waste analyses - at least 3 years.
  • Clean Water Act NPDES monitoring records - at least 3 years; SPCC inspection records - 3 years.
  • Clean Air Act Title V monitoring records - 5 years.
  • EPCRA Tier II reports filed annually by March 1; TRI (Form R) by July 1.
  • Retention clocks are extended during unresolved enforcement actions.

Audit resultsBLUEPRINT

Audit reports, findings and corrective-action closure records demonstrate continual improvement and due diligence.

  • PSM compliance audits at least every 3 years; keep the two most recent audit reports.
  • ISO 45001/14001 require retained documented information as evidence of audit programs and results.
  • Track each finding to closure with verification evidence.
  • Consider attorney-client privilege/self-audit policies (EPA Audit Policy encourages self-disclosure).

Exam tip: Unresolved audit findings repeated in the next audit = failed corrective-action system.

PrivacyBLUEPRINT

Injury, medical and exposure records contain sensitive information that must be protected while still meeting access and reporting rules.

  • 1904.29 privacy concern cases: write 'privacy case' instead of the name - intimate body part or reproductive system injury, sexual assault, mental illness, HIV/hepatitis/TB, contaminated needlestick or sharps injury, and other illness where the employee requests it.
  • Keep medical records separate from personnel files; restrict access to need-to-know (occupational health staff).
  • HIPAA generally applies to health-care providers and plans, not to employers acting as employers; ADA requires medical information be kept confidential.
  • Electronic data: access controls, encryption, backups.

Exam tip: Needlestick with contaminated sharp is a privacy concern case - no name on the 300 log.

Trade secretsBLUEPRINT

Employers and manufacturers may protect confidential business information, but never at the expense of worker health and safety.

  • HazCom 1910.1200(i): specific chemical identity may be withheld from the SDS if the hazards and protective measures are still disclosed.
  • In a medical emergency the identity must be disclosed immediately to the treating physician or nurse (confidentiality agreement may follow).
  • Non-emergency: written request by health professional with need stated; may require confidentiality agreement; written denial within 30 days.
  • PSM: trade secrets cannot be used to withhold PSI needed by PHA teams, procedures, investigations or emergency planning.
  • 1910.1020 allows trade-secret protection with similar conditions.

Exam tip: Trade secret protection NEVER overrides emergency medical need.

Personal informationBLUEPRINT

Personally identifiable information (PII) - names, SSNs, birth dates, medical details, addresses - must be collected only as needed, protected and disposed of securely.

  • Collect minimum needed; limit access; encrypt and password-protect; shred or securely wipe when retention ends.
  • Laws: state breach-notification laws, GDPR (EU workers), ADA/GINA confidentiality.
  • Do not post incident reports with names and medical detail on bulletin boards.
  • Breach of PII requires notification under many state laws.

Document control and retention schedules

A document management system ensures people use current versions and records are retained, retrievable and disposed of on schedule.

  • Document (procedure, plan - controlled, revised) vs record (evidence of what happened - not changed).
  • Document control: approval, version/revision numbers, distribution, removal of obsolete copies.
  • Retention schedule: record type, retention period (longest of legal, contract, company), location, owner, disposal method.
  • Litigation hold suspends destruction when litigation is anticipated.

Exam tip: Using an outdated SOP after a revision = document control failure.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 3 Recording and Reporting Occupational Injuries and Illnesses, p. 44; Ch. 4 Regulations; Ch. 40 BCSP Code of Ethics

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

2.10-Q1. A worker's injury involves a sexual assault at work. How should it be entered on the OSHA 300 Log?

  1. Enter the worker's full name and details
  2. Enter 'privacy case' instead of the name and keep a separate confidential list
  3. Do not record it
  4. Record it only on the 301 form

2.10-Q2. How long must an employer retain the OSHA 300 Log, 300A Summary and 301 forms?

  1. 1 year
  2. 3 years
  3. 5 years following the end of the calendar year they cover
  4. Duration of employment plus 30 years

2.10-Q3. Under 29 CFR 1910.1020, employee exposure records must be retained for:

  1. At least 30 years
  2. 50 years
  3. 5 years
  4. Duration of employment only

2.10-Q4. A supplier withholds a chemical's specific identity on the SDS as a trade secret. A treating physician needs the identity for an exposed worker in a medical emergency. What does the HCS require?

  1. The supplier may refuse until a lawsuit is filed
  2. Only OSHA can request the identity
  3. The employer must guess based on the hazard class
  4. The supplier must disclose the identity immediately to the treating health professional, and may later request a confidentiality agreement

2.10-Q5. A document control system should ensure:

  1. Unlimited copies of all forms are in circulation
  2. Outdated procedures are removed from use
  3. All documents remain permanently editable
  4. Old records are deleted immediately after revision

2.10-Q6. A safety professional oversees noise monitoring and discovers that the data logger failed to record any readings during the shift. The company is nearing an OSHA deadline for exposure documentation. What is the most appropriate next step?

  1. Submit the report anyway and explain the issue if audited
  2. Adjust readings using historical data and average estimates
  3. Request an exemption from OSHA due to equipment failure
  4. Redo the sampling as soon as possible and document the equipment failure

2.10-Q7. Which is an example personally identifiable information (PII)?

  1. Credit Card Number
  2. Race of the person.
  3. City of Residence.
  4. Marital status

2.10-Q8. For A Large Quantity Generator Of Hazardous Waste, How Long Must You Retain Copies Of Annual Training Provided To Those Workers Who Are Currently Employed By The Company?

  1. Three years from the date of training
  2. Duration of employment plus 30 years
  3. Until the facility closes
  4. Fifteen years from the date of training

2.10-Q9. An internal audit identifies recurring deficiencies in training records. What should management do first?

  1. Assign blame to individual trainers
  2. Review and correct the system process for training record control
  3. Delete all incomplete records
  4. Extend the audit schedule

2.10-Q10. A plant's occupational health nurse keeps employee audiograms and respirator medical evaluations. Where should these records be maintained?

  1. In a separate, confidential medical file with access limited to authorized persons
  2. In each employee's personnel file so supervisors can review fitness for duty
  3. In the supervisor's office for quick reference
  4. In the training records database with the employee's course history

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1B1904.29(b)(7): for privacy concern cases (sexual assault, mental illness, HIV/hepatitis/TB, contaminated needlestick, or on employee request for other illnesses), enter 'privacy case' and keep a separate confidential list of names.
2C29 CFR 1904.33: OSHA 300, 300A and 301 forms are retained 5 years following the end of the calendar year they cover. Trap: 30 years applies to exposure/medical records.
3A29 CFR 1910.1020: exposure records are kept at least 30 years; medical records for duration of employment plus 30 years.
4D1910.1200(i): in a medical emergency the specific chemical identity must be disclosed immediately to the treating physician or nurse; a confidentiality agreement may be requested afterward.
5BDocument control ensures only current, approved revisions are in use and obsolete versions are withdrawn (and archived/marked if retained).
6DExposure records must be accurate. Redo the sampling promptly and document the equipment failure; never estimate, fabricate or submit incomplete data.
7AA credit card number uniquely identifies a person's account and is PII. Race, city or marital status alone generally do not identify an individual.
8C40 CFR 262.17(a)(7)(v): training records for current personnel are kept until facility closure; for former employees, at least 3 years from the last date worked.
9BRecurring record deficiencies indicate a system problem; correct the record-control process and verify it is sustained rather than blaming individuals or deleting records.
10AEmployee medical records must be kept confidential and separate from general personnel files (ADA; 1910.1020 access rules). Supervisors may be told work restrictions, not diagnoses.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.11

Budgeting, Finance and Economic Analysis

CSP11 blueprint: Apply budgeting, finance, and economic analysis techniques and principles (e.g., timelines, budget development, resourcing, return on investment, cost/benefit analysis, role in procurement process)

1. Definition

Engineering economics and budgeting allow the safety professional to justify, plan and fund EHS investments in business terms: costs, benefits, time value of money, return on investment and payback, and to influence procurement so that safe equipment and services are purchased.

2. Explanation in simple words

Managers decide with money. Show what a safety project costs, what it saves (including hidden indirect costs of accidents), how fast it pays back and whether the benefit is bigger than the cost.

3. Key points to remember

  • Direct (insured) costs: medical, indemnity/compensation. Indirect (uninsured) costs: lost time of injured and coworkers, supervisor time, investigation, retraining, replacement workers, production delays, damaged equipment, reputation. Indirect costs are often several times the direct costs (iceberg).
  • Simple payback = initial cost ÷ annual savings.
  • ROI (%) = (benefit − cost) ÷ cost × 100 (some questions define ROI as annual savings ÷ investment × 100 - use the formula given).
  • Benefit-cost ratio = PV of benefits ÷ PV of costs; > 1 is favorable. Net present value (NPV) = PV of benefits − PV of costs; > 0 accept.
  • Time value of money: F = P(1 + i)ⁿ; P = F(1 + i)⁻ⁿ; uniform series P = A[(1 + i)ⁿ − 1] ÷ [i(1 + i)ⁿ]; F = A[(1 + i)ⁿ − 1] ÷ i.
  • Life-cycle cost: purchase + installation + operation + maintenance + disposal − salvage; the cheapest purchase is not always the lowest life-cycle cost.
  • Sales needed to cover a loss = loss ÷ profit margin (e.g., $137,000 ÷ 5% = $2.74 million in sales).
  • Budget types: capital vs operating; zero-based, incremental, activity-based. Budget development: needs/risk assessment, prioritization, cost estimates, timeline, approval, tracking variance.
  • Procurement: specify safety requirements in purchase specs (guarding, noise levels, CE/UL listing), prequalify contractors (EMR, TRIR, programs, training), include safety terms and indemnity in contracts.

4. Real work scenario

An $80,000 automated palletizer will eliminate manual lifting that causes about four back injuries a year at $44,000 each. Annual savings ≈ $176,000; simple payback = 80,000 ÷ 176,000 ≈ 0.45 years (5.5 months); first-year ROI = (176,000 − 80,000) ÷ 80,000 = 120%. The CFO approves the project.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Cost-benefit analysis comparing control cost with injury costs is the MOST persuasive business case on the exam.
  • Read whether the question wants payback in months or years.
  • NPV and B/C require discounting; simple ROI does not.
  • Safety's role in procurement is to build requirements in before purchase (PtD for buying).

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 4 more)

Budget developmentBLUEPRINT

A safety budget allocates money and people to planned activities and is justified with risk and financial analysis aligned to business goals.

  • Operating budget (recurring: training, PPE, salaries, consultants) vs capital budget (long-life assets: ventilation, guarding, sprinklers).
  • Incremental budgeting adjusts last year's budget; zero-based budgeting justifies every item from zero each cycle.
  • Build from the action plan/risk register: prioritize by risk and regulatory requirement.
  • Track variance = actual - budgeted; explain significant variances.

Exam tip: Capital projects are justified with ROI, payback or NPV; operating items are justified by risk and compliance need.

ResourcesBLUEPRINT

Resources include people, equipment, time, money, information and outside expertise; management commitment is shown by allocating them.

  • ISO 45001 7.1 and Z10 require management to determine and provide resources.
  • Options: in-house staff, consultants, contractors, shared services, insurance carrier loss-control services.
  • Allocate resources where risk reduction per dollar is greatest.

Return on investment (ROI)BLUEPRINT

ROI expresses the net benefit of a safety investment as a percentage of its cost.

  • ROI (%) = (benefits - cost) / cost x 100.
  • Example: $50,000 program avoids $120,000 in losses -> ROI = (120,000 - 50,000)/50,000 = 140%.
  • Benefits include direct costs avoided (medical, comp) and indirect costs avoided (lost production, overtime, investigation, hiring, damage).
  • Simple ROI ignores time value of money - use NPV for multi-year projects.

Exam tip: Read whether the question wants net ROI (subtract cost) or a simple benefit/cost ratio.

Cost/benefit analysisBLUEPRINT

Cost-benefit analysis compares the (present) value of benefits with costs to decide whether a control is worth funding.

  • Benefit-cost ratio = PV benefits / PV costs; >1 = justified.
  • Example: benefits $90,000, costs $60,000 -> B/C = 1.5.
  • Expected annual loss = probability (frequency) x consequence; benefit = reduction in expected loss.
  • Include life-cycle costs: purchase, install, maintenance, training, disposal.
  • Cost-effectiveness compares cost per unit of risk reduced when benefits are hard to price.

Payback period

Payback is how long it takes for savings to repay the initial investment.

  • Payback (years) = initial cost / annual net savings.
  • Example: $40,000 lift assist saves $16,000/yr -> 2.5 years.
  • Shorter is better; ignores savings after payback and the time value of money.

Exam tip: Payback is the simplest and most commonly tested measure.

Time value of money and NPV

Money now is worth more than money later; present value converts future savings to today's dollars.

  • FV = PV x (1 + i)^n; PV = FV / (1 + i)^n.
  • Example: $10,000 in 3 years at 10% -> PV = 10,000 / 1.331 = $7,513.
  • NPV = sum of CF_t/(1 + i)^t - initial cost; NPV > 0 = accept.
  • Example: cost $20,000; savings $10,000/yr for 3 yrs at 10% -> PV = 9,091 + 8,264 + 7,513 = 24,868; NPV = $4,868.
  • Annuity PV = A x [1 - (1 + i)^-n] / i.

Direct and indirect costs of incidents

Direct (insured) costs are medical and compensation; indirect (uninsured) costs are often several times larger.

  • Indirect: lost productivity, supervisor time, investigation, training replacements, damaged equipment, legal, morale, reputation.
  • Heinrich estimated indirect:direct about 4:1; OSHA Safety Pays uses multipliers that shrink as direct cost rises.
  • Sales needed to pay for a loss = loss cost / profit margin. Example: $30,000 loss at 5% margin -> $600,000 in sales.
  • Straight-line depreciation = (cost - salvage) / useful life.

Exam tip: The 'sales to cover loss' calculation is a favorite way to show management the value of prevention.

ProcurementBLUEPRINT

Procurement controls what hazards enter the workplace; specifying safety requirements at purchase is a prevention-through-design opportunity.

  • Write safety, ergonomic and noise specifications into purchase orders; require guarding and lockable isolation devices.
  • Review SDSs before approving new chemicals (safer substitutes).
  • Contractor prequalification: EMR, TRIR/DART, written programs, training, OSHA citation history.
  • Consider total cost of ownership (life-cycle), not lowest bid.
  • ISO 45001 8.1.4 requires controls for procurement, contractors and outsourcing.

Exam tip: Cheapest-bid purchasing that ignores safety is a root cause in many case studies.

Insurance and risk financing costs

Insurance costs connect safety performance to finance.

  • Workers' comp premium = (payroll/100) x manual rate x EMR (simplified, before other credits).
  • Example: $2,000,000 payroll, $5/$100 rate, EMR 0.8 -> 20,000 x 5 x 0.8 = $80,000.
  • Deductibles/self-insured retention keep small losses in-house (retention).

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 20 Engineering Economics, p. 495; Ch. 23 Workers' Compensation, p. 538

  1. $23,610.37
  2. $20,902.69
  3. $21,805.37
  4. $25,000.69

  1. $108,000
  2. $136,468
  3. $180,000
  4. $215,912

  1. $120,000

  3. $1,114,857

  1. 50%
  2. 150%
  3. 15%
  4. 5%

  1. 0%
  2. 66%
  3. 100%
  4. 150%

  1. $548,000,000
  2. $8,000,000
  3. $2,000,000
  4. $2,740,000

2.11-Q7. As a safety manager, which approach is MOST effective in demonstrating to senior management the value of investing in additional safety training and staff?

  1. Present an ROI figure of 15% without supporting data
  2. Compare the cost of safety equipment with the cost of training and staffing
  3. Use a cost/benefit analysis that compares these costs with the injury and incident costs they will avoid
  4. Forecast where the investment would be most visible to employees

2.11-Q8. During budget planning, a safety director must choose among ergonomics training ($15k), a noise control system ($45k) and a forklift camera system ($30k). Incident data show most losses come from back injuries and musculoskeletal claims. Which project should be prioritized?

  1. Noise control system
  2. Ergonomics program
  3. Forklift camera system
  4. Delay all projects until more funds are available

2.11-Q9. A $60,000 automated lift-assist system is expected to save $24,000 per year in injury and productivity costs. What is the simple payback period?

  1. 0.4 years
  2. 2.5 years
  3. 3.0 years
  4. 4.0 years

2.11-Q10. Purchasing is about to issue a request for proposals for new powered industrial trucks. What is the safety professional's MOST valuable role in the procurement process?

  1. Write safety, ergonomic and training requirements into the specifications and help evaluate bids before selection
  2. Inspect the trucks after delivery and reject unsafe units
  3. Recommend the lowest-cost bid so savings can fund training
  4. Leave procurement to purchasing and train operators after delivery

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CF = 20,000 x 1.025^3.5 = 20,000 x 1.0903 = about $21,805. Trap: using simple interest or rounding n to 4 or more.
2BP = A[(1+i)^n - 1]/[i(1+i)^n] = 36,000 x 3.7908 = $136,468. $180,000 ignores the time value of money.
3DP = F/(1+i)^n = 1,200,000 / 1.045^5 = 1,200,000 / 1.2462 = about $962,960. Present value is always less than the future amount.
4AROI = (benefit - cost)/cost x 100 = (15,000 - 10,000)/10,000 = 50%. Trap: 150% is benefit/cost, not net return.
5AThree years of savings = $300,000; ROI = (300,000 - 300,000)/300,000 = 0%. The program has just broken even (payback = 3 years).
6DSales needed = loss / profit margin = 137,000 / 0.05 = $2,740,000. The $4,000 guard cost is a distractor (though it makes a strong business case).
7CA cost/benefit analysis ties the spending to the losses it prevents, which is the language management decides in. Visibility and internal cost comparisons do not show value.
8BBudget priority should follow the loss data; MSDs drive the losses, so the ergonomics initiative gives the best risk reduction per dollar.
9BPayback = initial cost / annual savings = 60,000 / 24,000 = 2.5 years. Trap: 0.4 is the inverted ratio.
10ASafety is cheapest to build in at the specification stage (prevention through design). Post-delivery inspection is reactive and costly.
Printed book page 98
Domain 2 · Competency 2.12

Management and Leadership

CSP11 blueprint: Differentiate management leadership techniques (e.g., management theories, leadership theories, motivation, discipline, authority, responsibility, accountability, communication styles)

1. Definition

Management is planning, organizing, directing and controlling resources to achieve objectives; leadership is influencing people to achieve goals willingly. Safety professionals need both to drive change, motivate behavior and hold people accountable.

2. Explanation in simple words

Managers make sure the work is organized; leaders make people want to do it right. Know the main theories, match your style to the people and situation, and remember that authority can be delegated but accountability stays with you.

3. Key points to remember

  • Classical/scientific management: Taylor (time studies, one best way), Fayol (14 principles incl. unity of command, span of control), Weber (bureaucracy). Human relations: Mayo (Hawthorne effect - people perform better when observed/valued).
  • Motivation: Maslow hierarchy (physiological → safety → belonging → esteem → self-actualization); Herzberg two-factor (motivators: achievement, recognition, responsibility, growth; hygiene: pay, conditions, policy, supervision); McGregor Theory X (people dislike work, need control) vs Theory Y (people seek responsibility); Ouchi Theory Z (Japanese, long-term employment, consensus); McClelland acquired needs (achievement, affiliation, power); Vroom expectancy (effort → performance → reward valued); Adams equity theory; Skinner reinforcement.
  • Leadership: trait theories; behavioral (Blake-Mouton managerial grid); situational (Hersey-Blanchard: directing, coaching, supporting, delegating by follower readiness); contingency (Fiedler); path-goal (directive, supportive, participative, achievement-oriented); transformational (inspires, vision) vs transactional (rewards/punishment); servant leadership; Likert's four systems (exploitive-authoritative to participative).
  • Quality management: Deming (PDCA, 14 points, TQM), Juran (quality trilogy), Crosby (zero defects).
  • Authority (right to decide), responsibility (duty to perform), accountability (answerable for results). Authority should equal responsibility; responsibility and authority can be delegated, accountability cannot.
  • Discipline: progressive, consistent, fair, documented, timely (hot-stove rule: immediate, consistent, impersonal, with warning); use as a last resort after removing system barriers.
  • Communication: sender → message → channel → receiver → feedback; active listening (paraphrasing, summarizing); assertive vs passive vs aggressive styles; conflict resolution styles (Thomas-Kilmann: competing, collaborating, compromising, avoiding, accommodating); face-to-face is best for important safety messages.

4. Real work scenario

A new supervisor manages a crew of highly experienced, motivated electricians and tries to direct every step; morale drops. Using situational leadership, the safety manager coaches him to delegate planning to the crew while staying involved in risk reviews. Participation in JHAs and near-miss reporting increases.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Recognition is a Herzberg motivator; pay and working conditions are hygiene factors.
  • Hawthorne effect = performance changes because people know they are being observed.
  • 'Leading by example' is the best answer for how safety professionals should lead.
  • Accountability cannot be delegated - a frequent distractor.

6. Every blueprint point (8 blueprint items + 1 more)

Management theoriesBLUEPRINT

Management theories explain how managers plan, organize, lead and control; each fits different situations.

  • Scientific management (Taylor): time study, standardization, task specialization, monetary incentives.
  • Bureaucratic (Weber): hierarchy, formal rules, defined roles, impersonality.
  • Administrative (Fayol): plan, organize, command, coordinate, control; unity of command, division of work, discipline.
  • Human relations (Mayo, Hawthorne studies): social factors, recognition and attention raise productivity.
  • Contingency/situational: no one best way; systems theory: organization as open system with feedback; TQM (Deming): continuous improvement, all employees involved.

Exam tip: Hawthorne effect: performance changes because people know they are being observed/attended to.

Leadership theoriesBLUEPRINT

Leadership theories explain what makes leaders effective - traits, behaviors, situation fit, or the relationship with followers.

  • Trait theory: inherent characteristics (integrity, confidence, charisma).
  • Behavioral: task-oriented vs people-oriented; Ohio State (initiating structure vs consideration), Michigan (production- vs employee-centered), Blake-Mouton managerial grid.
  • Situational (Hersey-Blanchard): directing, coaching, supporting, delegating based on follower readiness; Fiedler contingency: style fit to situational control.
  • Transformational: vision, charisma, intellectual stimulation, individualized consideration - drives culture change.
  • Transactional: rewards and corrective action for performance - suits routine compliance; servant leadership: leader serves team needs.

Exam tip: Changing safety culture -> transformational; new, inexperienced worker -> directing (Hersey-Blanchard S1).

MotivationBLUEPRINT

Motivation theories explain what drives people to perform and how to design rewards and jobs.

  • Maslow hierarchy (bottom to top): physiological, safety, social/belonging, esteem, self-actualization.
  • Herzberg two-factor: hygiene factors (pay, conditions, policies) prevent dissatisfaction; motivators (achievement, recognition, responsibility, work itself) create satisfaction.
  • McGregor Theory X (dislike work, need control) vs Theory Y (self-motivated, seek responsibility); Ouchi Theory Z (long-term employment, collective decisions).
  • McClelland: needs for achievement, affiliation, power; Vroom expectancy: Motivation = Expectancy x Instrumentality x Valence; Adams equity: fairness compared to others.
  • Skinner reinforcement: positive reinforcement strengthens behavior; punishment suppresses it only temporarily.

Exam tip: Pay and working conditions are Herzberg HYGIENE factors, not motivators.

DisciplineBLUEPRINT

Discipline enforces rules to maintain order and correct conduct; it should be fair, consistent, documented and focused on improvement.

  • Progressive discipline: verbal warning -> written warning -> suspension -> termination.
  • Positive discipline: collaborative problem solving, clear expectations, coaching, support.
  • Preventive discipline: clear policies, consistent enforcement, recognition of good behavior.
  • Hot stove rule (McGregor): immediate, with warning, consistent, impersonal.
  • Discipline should follow a just-culture review - punish reckless behavior, not system-induced error.

Exam tip: Disciplining a worker when the root cause was a system failure is the wrong answer.

AuthorityBLUEPRINT

Authority is the formal right to make decisions, give orders and allocate resources.

  • Line authority: direct supervisory control over subordinates (production managers).
  • Staff authority: advisory - provides expertise (typically the safety professional's role).
  • Functional authority: right to direct specific activities in other departments (e.g., safety may stop unsafe work).
  • Authority must match responsibility; authority can be delegated.

Exam tip: A safety manager usually has staff (advisory) authority - line managers own safety in their areas.

ResponsibilityBLUEPRINT

Responsibility is the duty to perform assigned tasks and meet objectives.

  • Tasks can be delegated, but ultimate responsibility cannot be fully delegated - the manager remains responsible.
  • Must be clearly assigned and communicated (job descriptions, RACI).
  • Line management is responsible for safety of their people; the safety professional advises and supports.

AccountabilityBLUEPRINT

Accountability is the obligation to answer for results and accept consequences; it flows upward through the organization.

  • Authority is given, responsibility is accepted, accountability is enforced.
  • Authority without responsibility -> misuse; responsibility without authority -> frustration.
  • Accountability tools: performance reviews tied to safety activities, metrics, recognition and consequences.
  • The single 'A' in RACI is the accountable person.

CommunicationBLUEPRINT

Effective leaders adapt communication style to the audience, context and goal.

  • Autocratic/directive: one-way, fast - best in emergencies or with inexperienced staff.
  • Democratic/participative: two-way, builds buy-in - best for problem solving; slower.
  • Laissez-faire: minimal direction - only for highly skilled, self-motivated teams.
  • Assertive: clear, honest, respectful - generally most effective.
  • Avoid passive, aggressive and passive-aggressive styles; use active listening and feedback; nonverbal cues carry much of the message.

Exam tip: Emergency = directive; developing a new procedure with workers = participative.

Organizational structure principles

Classic principles help structure safety roles.

  • Unity of command: each person reports to one supervisor.
  • Span of control: number of direct reports a supervisor can effectively manage (narrower for complex/hazardous work).
  • Delegation: assign authority with responsibility.
  • Safety professional position: reporting to senior management gives influence and independence.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 21 Management Theories, p. 505
