QAnsWhy
1BTheory Y assumes workers are self-motivated, supporting participative management. Theory X assumes workers dislike work and need control.
2ATransactional leaders manage by exchange (rewards/discipline); transformational leaders build commitment through vision, trust and individual consideration.
3CMotivators are achievement, recognition, responsibility and growth. Pay, policy and conditions are hygiene factors that only prevent dissatisfaction.
4DFiedler's contingency model matches the leader's style to those three situational factors. Path-goal focuses on clearing obstacles to goals.
5DLow competence with high commitment calls for directing: clear instructions and close supervision. Move to coaching, supporting and delegating as competence grows.
6BAuthority and responsibility can be delegated (and should be matched), but accountability stays with the delegator.
7CExpectancy theory: motivation = expectancy (effort leads to performance) x instrumentality (performance leads to reward) x valence (reward is valued).
8CThe BCSP Code of Ethics puts protection of people first; a permit certifies conditions that have not been verified. Delegating or verbally approving is the same violation.
9AActive listening means paraphrasing and checking understanding, which builds trust and surfaces the real issue.
10DThe 'hot stove' rule: discipline should be immediate, consistent, impersonal and with prior warning. Inconsistency destroys credibility and legal defensibility.

Domain 2 · Competency 2.13

Project Management

CSP11 blueprint: Apply project management principles and techniques (e.g., RACI charts, project timelines)

1. Definition

Project management applies knowledge, tools and techniques to deliver a defined scope on time, within budget and to quality and safety requirements. Key tools include the work breakdown structure, Gantt charts, critical path method (CPM), PERT and RACI responsibility matrices.

2. Explanation in simple words

A project is a one-time job with a start and an end. Break it into tasks, put them on a timeline, find which tasks set the finish date, and make sure everyone knows who does what and who signs off.

3. Key points to remember

  • Project life cycle: initiating → planning → executing → monitoring and controlling → closing. Triple constraint: scope, time, cost (with quality and safety).
  • Work breakdown structure (WBS): decomposes deliverables into manageable work packages.
  • Gantt chart: bar chart of tasks against time; shows sequence and overlaps (useful to spot simultaneous high-risk activities).
  • Critical path: the longest path through the network; tasks on it have zero float - any delay delays the project. Float (slack) = latest start − earliest start.
  • PERT expected time = (optimistic + 4 × most likely + pessimistic) ÷ 6.
  • RACI: Responsible (does the work), Accountable (owns the outcome and signs off - only ONE per task), Consulted (two-way input), Informed (kept up to date, one-way).
  • Integrate safety: safety requirements in scope and contracts, risk register, pre-task planning, sequence high-risk tasks to avoid conflicts, stakeholder communication, lessons learned at closeout.

4. Real work scenario

For a plant shutdown, the safety professional reviews the Gantt chart and sees crane lifts scheduled directly above an area where welders will work on the same day. She asks the project manager to resequence the lifts to the night shift and records it in the RACI as 'Accountable: shutdown manager; Responsible: lift supervisor; Consulted: safety; Informed: operations'.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Exactly one 'A' per task in a RACI chart.
  • Critical path = longest duration path; tasks off the path have float.
  • Gantt charts visualize timelines; they do not calculate injury rates or risk.

6. Every blueprint point (2 blueprint items + 5 more)

RACIBLUEPRINT

A RACI chart (responsibility assignment matrix) lists tasks against people and assigns roles so everyone knows who does what.

  • R = Responsible: does the work (can be several).
  • A = Accountable: owns the outcome, final decision - only ONE per task.
  • C = Consulted: provides input (two-way).
  • I = Informed: kept up to date (one-way).
  • Prevents overlap and gaps; especially useful for cross-functional teams.

Exam tip: Two 'A's on one task is an error - only one person is accountable.

TimelinesBLUEPRINT

Project timelines (Gantt charts) show each task as a bar across a calendar with start/finish dates, dependencies, milestones and percent complete.

  • Gantt chart = visual schedule; easy progress tracking and resource adjustment.
  • Milestones mark key events with zero duration (e.g., permit approved).
  • Dependencies: finish-to-start most common.
  • Updated regularly during monitoring and controlling.

Exam tip: Gantt shows schedule over time; PERT/CPM shows network dependencies and the critical path.

Project life cycle and triple constraint

Projects deliver objectives within constraints of scope, time, cost and quality.

  • Phases: initiation (objectives, business case, approval) -> planning (tasks, schedule, budget, risks, communication) -> execution -> monitoring and controlling -> closing (acceptance, lessons learned).
  • Triple constraint: scope, time, cost (quality in the middle) - changing one affects the others.
  • Scope creep = uncontrolled scope growth; manage through formal change control.

Work Breakdown Structure (WBS)

A hierarchical decomposition of the project into deliverables and work packages.

  • Foundation for scheduling, budgeting, resource assignment and RACI.
  • Lowest level = work package that can be estimated and assigned.
  • Example: new ventilation project -> design, procurement, installation, testing, training.

PERT

Program Evaluation and Review Technique models tasks as a network and uses three time estimates to handle uncertainty.

  • Expected time te = (O + 4M + P) / 6.
  • Example: O = 4, M = 6, P = 14 days -> te = (4 + 24 + 14)/6 = 7 days.
  • Standard deviation = (P - O) / 6 -> (14 - 4)/6 = 1.67 days.
  • Nodes = activities/events; arrows = dependencies.

Exam tip: Remember the 4 multiplier on the most likely estimate.

Critical Path Method (CPM)

The critical path is the longest sequence of dependent tasks; it sets the minimum project duration and its tasks have zero slack (float).

  • Example: path A-B-D = 3 + 5 + 4 = 12 days; path A-C-D = 3 + 2 + 4 = 9 days -> critical path A-B-D, 12 days; task C has 3 days of float.
  • Delay of any critical task delays the whole project.
  • Crashing = adding resources to shorten critical tasks; fast-tracking = overlapping tasks.

Exam tip: Critical path is the LONGEST path, not the shortest.

Project risk management

Identify, assess and respond to risks that could affect project success.

  • Risk register: risk, likelihood, impact, owner, response, status.
  • Responses: avoid, mitigate, transfer, accept (and exploit/enhance for opportunities).
  • Review risks continuously; communicate with stakeholders; document lessons learned.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 21 Management Theories; Ch. 20 Engineering Economics (timelines)

Domain 2 · Competency 2.14

Data Analysis

CSP11 blueprint: Analyze and/or interpret data (e.g., exposure, release concentrations, sampling data, mean, median, mode, confidence intervals, probabilities, Pareto analysis)

1. Definition

Data analysis uses descriptive and inferential statistics to summarize safety and exposure data, test whether differences are real, estimate uncertainty and prioritize action. Industrial hygiene data are often lognormally distributed.

2. Explanation in simple words

Turn numbers into decisions: what is typical, how spread out is the data, how sure are we, and which few problems cause most of the losses.

3. Key points to remember

  • Central tendency: mean (average, sensitive to outliers), median (middle value, best for skewed data), mode (most frequent, best for categories such as injury type).
  • Spread: range (max − min), variance, standard deviation (s = √[Σ(x − x̄)² ÷ (n − 1)]), coefficient of variation.
  • Normal distribution: ±1σ ≈ 68.3%, ±2σ ≈ 95.4% (±1.96σ = 95%), ±3σ ≈ 99.7%. z = (x − μ) ÷ σ.
  • Confidence interval for a mean: x̄ ± z(s/√n) (use t for small samples); a larger sample gives a narrower interval.
  • Exposure data: geometric mean and geometric standard deviation for lognormal data; 95th percentile compared with the OEL; upper confidence limit for compliance decisions.
  • Probability: AND (independent) P(A and B) = P(A) × P(B); OR P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A)P(B); complement 1 − P; series reliability = R1 × R2; parallel failure = F1 × F2.
  • Pareto analysis (80/20): sort categories by frequency or cost; the 'vital few' causes produce most of the losses.
  • Correlation (r from −1 to +1) does not prove causation; control charts show special-cause variation; release concentrations and dispersion depend on wind speed and distance.
  • Qualitative data: interviews, focus groups, observations analyzed by thematic coding.

4. Real work scenario

Ten noise samples average 88 dBA, but two readings at 101 dBA come from a single grinding task. The median is 85 dBA. Instead of averaging the problem away, the hygienist splits the data into task-based groups and finds the grinding task needs engineering controls while the rest of the area is below the action level.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • When data are skewed or have outliers, the median is the best 'typical' value.
  • 'Most common injury type' = mode.
  • Pareto questions: add categories in descending order until you reach about 80%.
  • Read whether a probability question asks for reliability or failure.

6. Every blueprint point (9 blueprint items + 3 more)

Exposure dataBLUEPRINT

Exposure data are concentrations or doses measured on workers, used to compare against OELs and decide on controls.

  • 8-h TWA = sum(Ci x Ti) / 8. Example: 100 ppm for 2 h, 50 ppm for 4 h, 0 for 2 h -> (200 + 200)/8 = 50 ppm.
  • Mixtures (additive effects): Em = C1/L1 + C2/L2 + ...; > 1 = overexposure. Example: 40/100 + 30/50 = 1.0 -> at limit.
  • OSHA action level is usually 1/2 the PEL (e.g., noise 85 dBA vs 90 dBA PEL).
  • Exposure data are usually lognormally distributed - summarize with geometric mean (GM) and geometric standard deviation (GSD).
  • Compliance: compare result and its confidence limit (UCL/LCL using sampling and analytical error) to the PEL.

Exam tip: TWA for an 8-hour shift always divides by 8 h, even if sampling covered fewer hours (unsampled time assumed zero only if no exposure).

Release concentrationsBLUEPRINT

Release calculations estimate airborne concentration from a leak or evaporation, and convert between units.

  • ppm to mg/m3 = ppm x MW / 24.45 (25 C, 1 atm). Example: 50 ppm toluene (MW 92) -> 50 x 92/24.45 = 188 mg/m3.
  • 1% by volume = 10,000 ppm. Example: LEL 1.2% = 12,000 ppm.
  • Steady-state room concentration: C (ppm) = G / Q x 10^6, where G = vapor generation (cfm) and Q = ventilation (cfm). Example: G = 0.05 cfm, Q = 1,000 cfm -> 50 ppm.
  • Purge (decay): C = C0 x e^(-Qt/V). Example: V = 10,000 ft3, Q = 1,000 cfm, t = 10 min -> C = C0 x e^-1 = 0.37 C0.
  • Use mixing factor K (1-10) to correct for imperfect mixing: Q actual = Q x K.

Sampling dataBLUEPRINT

Sampling data turn collected mass and air volume into a concentration; quality depends on calibration, blanks and representative sampling.

  • Air volume = flow rate x time. Example: 2.0 L/min x 240 min = 480 L = 0.48 m3.
  • Concentration = mass / volume. Example: 0.12 mg / 0.48 m3 = 0.25 mg/m3.
  • 1 m3 = 1,000 L; 1 mg/m3 = 1 ug/L.
  • Calibrate pumps pre- and post-sampling (use average flow); submit field blanks.
  • Sample types: personal (breathing zone) for compliance vs area; full-shift vs short-term (STEL 15 min); grab vs integrated.

Exam tip: Convert liters to cubic meters (divide by 1,000) before computing mg/m3 - a classic unit trap.

MeanBLUEPRINT

The arithmetic mean is the sum of values divided by the number of values - the most common measure of central tendency, but sensitive to outliers.

  • Mean = sum(x) / n.
  • Example data (ppm): 2, 4, 4, 5, 7, 9, 11 -> sum 42, n = 7 -> mean = 6.
  • Geometric mean = nth root of product (or antilog of mean of logs) - preferred for lognormal exposure data. Example: GM of 2 and 8 = sqrt(16) = 4.
  • Weighted mean is used for TWA (weighted by time).

Exam tip: One very high outlier pulls the mean up but not the median.

MedianBLUEPRINT

The median is the middle value when data are ordered; with an even count, average the two middle values.

  • Example: 2, 4, 4, 5, 7, 9, 11 -> median = 5 (4th of 7 values).
  • Even count: 3, 5, 8, 10 -> (5 + 8)/2 = 6.5.
  • Robust to outliers; good for skewed data such as lost-workday counts or claim costs.

Exam tip: Always sort the data first.

ModeBLUEPRINT

The mode is the most frequently occurring value; a data set may have no mode or more than one (bimodal).

  • Example: 2, 4, 4, 5, 7, 9, 11 -> mode = 4.
  • Only measure of central tendency usable for categorical data (most common injury type, body part).
  • Range = max - min (11 - 2 = 9).

Standard deviation and variance

Dispersion measures show how spread out data are around the mean.

  • Sample SD: s = sqrt[ sum(x - mean)^2 / (n - 1) ]; variance = s^2.
  • Example: 4, 6, 8 -> mean 6; deviations^2 = 4 + 0 + 4 = 8; s^2 = 8/2 = 4; s = 2.
  • Population SD divides by N instead of n - 1.
  • Coefficient of variation = s / mean x 100 (%).
  • Normal curve: mean +/- 1 SD = 68%, +/- 2 SD = 95%, +/- 3 SD = 99.7%.

Confidence intervalsBLUEPRINT

A confidence interval gives a range likely to contain the true population mean at a chosen confidence level.

  • CI = mean +/- z x (s / sqrt(n)); s/sqrt(n) = standard error.
  • z: 90% = 1.645, 95% = 1.96, 99% = 2.576.
  • Example: mean 40 ppm, s = 8, n = 16 -> SE = 2; 95% CI = 40 +/- 3.92 = 36.08 to 43.92 ppm.
  • Small samples (n < 30) use t instead of z (wider interval).
  • Larger n or smaller s -> narrower interval; higher confidence -> wider interval.
  • If the 95% UCL of the exposure is below the OEL, compliance can be stated with 95% confidence (one-sided UCL uses z = 1.645).

Exam tip: Watch whether the question gives SD or standard error - don't divide by sqrt(n) twice.

ProbabilityBLUEPRINT

Probability (0 to 1) quantifies the chance of events; it underpins FTA, ETA, reliability and risk calculations.

  • Independent AND: P(A and B) = P(A) x P(B). Example: 0.1 x 0.2 = 0.02.
  • OR: P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A and B) = 0.1 + 0.2 - 0.02 = 0.28. Mutually exclusive events: just add.
  • At least one in n trials: 1 - (1 - p)^n. Example: p = 0.05 per day, 10 days -> 1 - 0.95^10 = 0.40.
  • Reliability in series: R = R1 x R2 (0.9 x 0.9 = 0.81); parallel (redundant): R = 1 - (1 - R1)(1 - R2) = 0.99.
  • Complement: P(not A) = 1 - P(A).

Exam tip: Redundant (parallel) components increase reliability; components in series reduce it.

Pareto analysisBLUEPRINT

Pareto analysis ranks categories by frequency or cost to show the 'vital few' that cause most of the problem (about 80/20).

  • Steps: categorize, count/cost each, sort descending, compute percent and cumulative percent, plot bars with cumulative line.
  • Example: 200 injuries - strains 90 (45%), cuts 50 (70% cumulative), falls 30 (85%), other 30 -> focus on strains and cuts first.
  • Rank by cost or severity when frequency alone could mislead.
  • Used in gap analysis, incident trending and resource allocation.

Exam tip: Pareto tells you where to focus FIRST - largest bar(s) on the left.

Incidence rates and normalizing data

Rates normalize counts to exposure hours so different-size operations can be compared.

  • Rate = N x 200,000 / hours worked (100 workers x 2,000 h).
  • Example: 3 recordables in 150,000 h -> 4.0.
  • Hours from employees: 250 workers x 2,000 h = 500,000 h.
  • Epidemiology: incidence rate = new cases / population at risk over time; prevalence = existing cases / population at a point in time.

Correlation, trend and control charts

Statistical tools show relationships and whether changes are real or random variation.

  • Correlation coefficient r ranges -1 to +1; correlation does not prove causation.
  • Regression fits a trend line (y = a + bx) for forecasting.
  • Control charts: upper/lower control limits at mean +/- 3 SD; points outside = special-cause variation.
  • Small data sets (few injuries) fluctuate randomly - avoid overreacting to one month.

Exam tip: A strong correlation between near-miss reporting and fewer injuries does not by itself prove cause and effect.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 15 Statistics for the Safety Professional, p. 408; Ch. 5 Math Review, p. 211; Ch. 8 Industrial Hygiene Air Sampling

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

2.14-Q1. Twenty-five personal samples have a mean of 2.0 mg/m3 and a standard deviation of 0.5 mg/m3. What is the approximate 95% confidence interval for the true mean (z = 1.96)?

  1. 1.02 - 2.98 mg/m3
  2. 1.50 - 2.50 mg/m3
  3. 1.80 - 2.20 mg/m3
  4. 1.90 - 2.10 mg/m3

2.14-Q2. Industrial hygiene data are usually lognormally distributed. Three samples are 1, 4 and 16 ppm. What is the geometric mean?

  1. 4 ppm
  2. 7 ppm
  3. 10 ppm
  4. 16 ppm

2.14-Q3. While plotting airborne benzene concentrations over time, a hygienist notices extreme outliers on two days. Which statistic is LEAST affected by these outliers?

  1. Mean
  2. Maximum
  3. Standard deviation
  4. Median

2.14-Q4. Reliability data for a chemical transfer system show a 0.5 probability of pump seal failure and a 0.3 probability of pipe gasket leak over 1,000,000 hours. Assuming the events are independent, what is the probability that BOTH failures occur?

  1. 0.15
  2. 0.60
  3. 0.80
  4. 1.00

2.14-Q5. A lot of 100 items contains 3% defectives. If a sample of 4 items is drawn without replacement, what is the probability that none is defective?

  1. 98.4%
  2. 88.4%
  3. 96.0%
  4. 87.0%

2.14-Q6. A safety manager wants to identify the most common type of injury at a plant last year. Which statistical measure should be used?

  1. Mean
  2. Mode
  3. Median
  4. Range

2.14-Q7. In a normal distribution, approximately what percentage of values fall within one standard deviation of the mean?

  1. 34%
  2. 50%
  3. 68%
  4. 95%

2.14-Q8. What does a 95% confidence interval for a population mean indicate?

  1. The population mean is 95% larger than the sample mean
  2. 95% of the sample data fall within the interval
  3. Any single interval has exactly 95% of the population values
  4. If sampling were repeated many times, about 95% of the intervals built this way would contain the true mean

2.14-Q9. An industrial hygienist measures respirable dust (mg/m3) at five locations: 0.45, 0.50, 0.55, 0.60 and 0.65. What is the arithmetic mean concentration?

  1. 0.50 mg/m3
  2. 0.55 mg/m3
  3. 0.60 mg/m3
  4. 0.65 mg/m3

2.14-Q10. Last year's 100 injuries were: lacerations 46, strains 30, contusions 12, burns 7, eye injuries 5. Using Pareto analysis, which categories should be targeted first?

  1. Lacerations and strains
  2. Lacerations only
  3. Contusions, burns and eye injuries, because they are easiest to fix
  4. All five categories equally

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CCI = mean +/- z(s/sqrt n) = 2.0 +/- 1.96(0.5/5) = 2.0 +/- 0.196. Trap: using s instead of the standard error gives 1.02-2.98.
2AGM = (1 x 4 x 16)^(1/3) = 64^(1/3) = 4 ppm. 7 ppm is the arithmetic mean.
3DThe median depends only on the middle rank, so extreme values barely move it; mean, maximum and SD are all pulled by outliers.
4AAND for independent events = P1 x P2 = 0.5 x 0.3 = 0.15. Trap: adding gives 0.8 (that is not even the correct OR, which is 0.65).
5BP = (97/100)(96/99)(95/98)(94/97) = 0.884. Using 0.97^4 gives 88.5%, about the same; 96% wrongly subtracts 4 x 1%.
6BThe mode is the most frequent value and is the only central-tendency measure for categorical data such as injury type.
7CEmpirical rule: 68% within 1 SD, 95% within 2 SD, 99.7% within 3 SD. 34% is only one side of the mean.
8DConfidence describes the method's long-run capture rate of the true mean, not the spread of individual data points.
9BSum = 2.75; 2.75 / 5 = 0.55 mg/m3.
10APareto (80/20) focuses on the vital few: lacerations and strains are 76% of injuries. Lacerations alone leave a large share untouched.

Printed book page 114
Domain 3 · Competency 3.1

Safety Risk Evaluation Process

CSP11 blueprint: Apply general principles of the safety risk evaluation process (i.e., identifying, analyzing, evaluating, monitoring, and communicating risk affecting an organization)

1. Definition

Risk is the combination of the likelihood of an event and the severity of its consequences. The risk evaluation process (ISO 31000, ANSI/ASSP Z690.2, ANSI/ASSP Z590.3) identifies hazards, analyzes and evaluates the resulting risk against criteria, treats it, and continuously monitors and communicates it.

2. Explanation in simple words

Find what can hurt you, judge how likely and how bad it is, decide whether that is acceptable, fix what is not, keep checking, and tell everyone who needs to know.

3. Key points to remember

  • ISO 31000 process: establish scope/context/criteria → risk assessment (identification → analysis → evaluation) → risk treatment → monitoring and review; communication and consultation throughout; recording and reporting.
  • Identification: inspections, JHAs, incident data, SDSs, process reviews, employee input, regulatory review. Hazard = source of potential harm; risk = likelihood × severity of harm from that hazard.
  • Analysis: qualitative (risk matrix: e.g., 5 × 5), semi-quantitative (scored), quantitative (frequency and consequence, QRA).
  • Evaluation: compare analyzed risk with risk criteria/tolerability - intolerable, ALARP (reduce unless cost grossly disproportionate), broadly acceptable. Decide priority and need for treatment.
  • Risk appetite = amount of risk an organization is willing to pursue; risk tolerance = acceptable variation around objectives; risk register = central list of risks, owners, controls and status.
  • Monitoring and review: verify controls are effective, re-assess residual risk after changes, track leading indicators.
  • Risk communication: tailored to audience, two-way; in crises be first, be right, be credible, show empathy (CDC CERC). Risk perception differs from technical risk (dread, unfamiliar, involuntary risks feel larger).

4. Real work scenario

A warehouse identifies forklift-pedestrian interaction as a hazard. Analysis rates it Likely × Major = High. Evaluation against the company criteria says High risks need controls within 30 days. Treatment: separate walkways with barriers and blue spot lights. Monitoring: monthly near-miss tracking. Communication: toolbox talks and signage explain the new traffic rules.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Analysis estimates the risk; evaluation compares it with criteria - a classic confusion.
  • The FIRST step is hazard identification.
  • After controls are installed, the next step is to verify effectiveness and re-rate residual risk.
  • Risk communication answers stress clarity, audience understanding and transparency.

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 4 more)

Identify (risk identification)BLUEPRINT

Find and describe hazards and risks that could affect people, assets, the environment or objectives. Identification must be proactive and continuous, covering routine and non-routine work and internal and external factors.

  • Sources: inspections, audits, incident and near-miss investigations, toolbox talks, surveys, safety observations, SDS and process data.
  • Structured tools: JHA (task level), FMEA (component failures), HAZOP (process deviations), What-If, checklists.
  • ANSI/ASSP Z690.1 (ISO 31000): identification is proactive and continuous.
  • OSHA PSM (1910.119) requires PHAs for covered highly hazardous chemical processes.

Exam tip: You cannot analyze or control a hazard that has not been identified - if a scenario skips identification, that is the gap.

Analyze (risk analysis)BLUEPRINT

Understand the nature, causes and possible consequences of each identified risk and determine its level by estimating likelihood and severity.

  • Qualitative: expert judgment, historical comparison, risk matrix (low/medium/high).
  • Quantitative: failure-rate data, statistical probabilities, loss data.
  • Analytical tools: risk matrices, Fault Tree Analysis (traces top event to causes), bow-tie diagrams (preventive barriers left, mitigative barriers right).
  • ISO 45001 requires routine AND non-routine operations to be considered; NFPA 551 covers fire risk analysis (ignition, fuel load, detection/suppression).

Exam tip: Distractors jump straight from identification to controls - analysis must come between them.

Risk estimation (Likelihood x Severity x Exposure)

Risk estimation puts a value on each risk so risks can be compared and prioritized. The guide uses Risk Score = Likelihood x Severity x Exposure, using qualitative, semi-quantitative or quantitative scales.

  • Likelihood scale (guide): rare 1%, unlikely 10%, possible 50%, likely 75%, certain 100%.
  • Severity scale: negligible (first aid), minor (medical treatment), moderate (lost workdays), severe (permanent disability/hospitalization), critical (fatality/catastrophe).
  • Exposure scale: rarely (yearly), occasionally (monthly), frequently (weekly), constantly (daily).
  • Worked example: L = 3, S = 4, E = 2 gives risk score 3 x 4 x 2 = 24; after a control drops L to 1, score = 8 (residual).
  • Same idea as the Fine-Kinney method (Probability x Exposure x Consequence).

Exam tip: Read which factors the question uses - a 2-factor matrix (L x S) and a 3-factor score (L x S x E) give different numbers.

Detectability and the FMEA Risk Priority Number

FMEA replaces exposure with detectability: a failure that is hard to detect before it causes harm is ranked higher. RPN = Severity x Occurrence x Detection.

  • Detection is reverse-scored: easily detectable = low score, undetectable = high score (typically 1-10).
  • RPN ranges 1-1,000 on 1-10 scales.
  • Example: S = 8, O = 3, D = 7 gives RPN = 168.
  • High-severity items (e.g., S = 9-10) usually need action regardless of total RPN.

Exam tip: If a question mentions 'detectability' or 'RPN', it is FMEA, not a standard risk matrix.

Evaluate (risk evaluation)BLUEPRINT

Compare analyzed risk levels against defined risk criteria (tolerance, legal requirements, risk appetite) to decide which risks need treatment and in what priority.

  • ALARP (as low as reasonably practicable): reduce risk until further reduction cost is grossly disproportionate to the benefit.
  • Rank risks by severity, frequency and detectability.
  • Corporate risk appetite is the benchmark for decisions.
  • Z690.1 (ISO 31000): decisions must be based on defined evaluation criteria; PSM: prioritize correction of high-severity process hazards.

Exam tip: Evaluation = comparing to criteria. Analysis = determining the level. Know the difference.

Monitor (monitoring and review)BLUEPRINT

Check over time that risks remain controlled, that controls work, and that changes (new processes, people, materials) are captured.

  • Methods: trend analysis of incidents and near misses, inspections, compliance audits, real-time monitoring (gas detectors, vibration sensors).
  • ISO 45001: ongoing performance evaluation and verification of hazard controls.
  • NFPA 551: continuous review of fire safety systems and inspection intervals.
  • Reassess after any change - link to Management of Change.

Communicate (risk communication and consultation)BLUEPRINT

Make sure stakeholders understand the risks and the controls. Under ISO 31000 communication is two-way - informing people and gathering feedback - and runs through every step.

  • Tools: risk registers (central tracking), dashboards (visual, real-time performance), safety briefings and training tailored to the audience.
  • Tailor content: executives (cost, exposure, decisions), supervisors (actions, owners), operators (task controls).
  • OSHA PSM requires communication and training on process hazards for all affected employees.
  • Convert rankings into a prioritized action plan with owners and due dates.

Exam tip: One-way 'tell them' answers are weaker than two-way consultation answers.

Risk register

A centralized, structured log that documents, tracks and manages identified risks through their life cycle - the core tool of an ISO 31000-aligned system.

  • Purpose: single point of truth, prioritization, ownership, monitoring of mitigation, communication.
  • Typical fields: risk ID, description, source/cause, consequences, likelihood, severity, risk score, current controls, residual risk, recommended actions, action owner, due date, status (open/in progress/closed), review date.
  • Residual risk is rated after current controls are applied.

Exam tip: A risk register with no owner or due date is the classic deficiency in scenario questions.

ISO 31000 / ANSI Z690 risk management process

ANSI/ASSP Z690.1 is the U.S. adoption of ISO 31000 (principles and guidelines); Z690.2 = vocabulary (ISO Guide 73); Z690.3 / IEC 31010 = risk assessment techniques.

  • ISO 31000 process: scope, context and criteria -> risk assessment (identification, analysis, evaluation) -> risk treatment, with communication and consultation plus monitoring and review running throughout, and recording and reporting.
  • Risk assessment = identification + analysis + evaluation.
  • The guide's teaching sequence: Identify -> Analyze -> Evaluate -> Monitor -> Communicate.

Exam tip: The guide states the sequence Identify-Analyze-Evaluate-Monitor-Communicate; in ISO 31000 monitoring and communication are continuous, not only final steps. If options show a misordered sequence, the first three must be identify, analyze, evaluate.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 26 Risk Assessment and Management, p. 604

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

  2. Monitor -> Identify -> Evaluate -> Analyze -> Communicate

  4. Identify -> Analyze -> Evaluate -> Monitor -> Communicate

  2. Risk analysis

  1. Risk evaluation
  2. Risk communication
  3. Risk control
  4. Hazard identification

  2. Risk identification
  3. Risk evaluation

  1. Risk identification
  2. Risk evaluation
  3. Risk monitoring
  4. Risk treatment

  1. One-way and management-driven
  2. Two-way, involving stakeholders, feedback and discussion
  3. Only for reporting to regulators
  4. Limited to top management

  1. Stop monitoring because likelihood is low
  2. Re-evaluate and update the residual risk rating
  3. Remove the hazard from the risk register
  4. Classify it as acceptable risk automatically

  1. Eliminate the hazard or substitute the task
  2. Accept the risk and monitor the activity
  3. Proceed with caution and add signage
  4. Document the risk and assign it to a supervisor

3.1-Q9. During a PHA, a hazard is rated severity 4 and likelihood 3 on 1-5 scales. The company matrix defines scores 1-4 as Low, 5-9 as Medium, 10-16 as High and 20-25 as Extreme. What is the risk level?

  1. Low
  2. Medium
  3. High
  4. Extreme

3.1-Q10. Risk tolerance differs from risk appetite because risk tolerance:

  1. Applies to specific objectives, projects or activities
  2. Represents the total company-wide risk
  3. Is set only by regulators
  4. Cannot vary within an organization

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1DYou must find the hazard before analyzing likelihood/consequence, then evaluate against criteria; monitoring and communication continue throughout.
2CAnalysis estimates likelihood and consequence; evaluation compares that result to criteria and prioritizes. Trap: confusing analysis with evaluation.
3DRecognizing what could go wrong and who could be hurt is identification; no likelihood or ranking has been done yet.
4ASampling quantifies the likelihood and magnitude of exposure, which is analysis. Comparing results to the OEL is the next step (evaluation).
5CMonitoring and review confirm that controls remain effective and detect new or changing risks.
6BISO 31000 treats communication and consultation as a continuous two-way process with stakeholders at every step.
7BAny change in likelihood or severity requires re-evaluating residual risk against criteria; high-severity hazards stay on the register.
8AA High rating means the risk is not tolerable; treatment should start at the top of the hierarchy of controls, not with signage or acceptance.
9CRisk = 4 x 3 = 12, which falls in the 10-16 High band. Always use the matrix the question gives you.
10ARisk appetite is the overall amount of risk the organization will pursue; tolerance is the acceptable variation for a specific objective or activity.
Printed book page 119
Domain 3 · Competency 3.2

Risk Management Strategies and Hierarchy of Controls

CSP11 blueprint: Apply risk management strategies to identify and mitigate EHS hazards (e.g., risk analysis, job hazard analysis, process hazard analysis, hierarchy of controls)

1. Definition

Risk management strategies are the tools used to identify hazards at the task and process level (JHA, PHA, HAZOP, What-If) and to choose controls in order of effectiveness using the hierarchy of controls.

2. Explanation in simple words

Break the job or the process into steps, ask what can go wrong at each step, and then pick the strongest control that is practical - remove the hazard first and rely on PPE last.

3. Key points to remember

  • Hierarchy of controls (NIOSH): elimination → substitution → engineering controls → administrative controls → PPE. ANSI Z10 adds 'warnings' between engineering and administrative controls.
  • Engineering controls: isolate people from the hazard (guards, interlocks, ventilation, enclosures, guardrails). Administrative: procedures, permits, training, rotation, signage (warnings), scheduling. PPE: last line of defense, does not remove the hazard, depends on fit and behavior.
  • PPE first is justified only as an interim measure while higher controls are installed, for emergencies, or when other controls are not feasible.
  • Job Hazard Analysis (JHA/JSA): select job (prioritize by injury history, severity, new/changed or non-routine jobs) → break into steps → identify hazards per step → determine controls → implement, review periodically and after changes. Involve workers who do the job.
  • Process Hazard Analysis (PHA, PSM): What-If, Checklist, HAZOP, FMEA, FTA; team with process, engineering and operations expertise; recommendations must be resolved and tracked.
  • Preliminary Hazard Analysis: early design screening of hazards.
  • Hazard mitigation: reduce likelihood (prevention) and/or severity (protection, emergency response); layers of protection (LOPA).

4. Real work scenario

Silica exposure during abrasive blasting is currently controlled by supplied-air respirators and toolbox talks. Applying the hierarchy, the safety professional substitutes steel grit for silica sand (substitution), adds a blast room with local exhaust (engineering) and keeps respirators as backup. Measured exposures drop below the action level.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • 'MOST effective' questions: the highest level of the hierarchy that actually addresses the hazard wins.
  • Classify correctly: two-person lift = administrative; guardrails = engineering; moving work to a safer place may be called substitution or elimination depending on the source - read the options.
  • JHA questions: break the job into steps first; involve the workers who perform it.
  • Interlocks and light curtains are engineering controls; signs and labels are warnings/administrative.

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 3 more)

Risk analysisBLUEPRINT

The process of understanding the nature of risk and determining its level: identify sources and causes, understand existing controls, estimate likelihood and consequences.

  • Qualitative: risk matrix scoring (low/medium/high), 3x3 or 5x5.
  • Quantitative: failure probability modeling, statistical loss data, FTA with failure rates.
  • OSHA PSM: PHAs updated and revalidated at least every 5 years.
  • Output is a risk level used for evaluation and treatment.

Job Hazard Analysis (JHA)BLUEPRINT

A systematic, step-by-step method to identify and control hazards of a specific job or task before they cause injury, illness or damage.

  • Steps: (1) break the job into tasks in sequence, (2) identify hazards for each step (physical, chemical, biological, ergonomic, psychosocial), (3) recommend controls using the hierarchy.
  • Prioritize jobs with highest injury rates, severe potential, new or changed jobs, and infrequent/non-routine jobs (OSHA 3071).
  • Involve the workers who perform the task.
  • Example: welding steel frames - UV, fumes, sparks -> screens, LEV, filter-lens helmet, FR clothing.
  • ISO 45001 link: JHA supports hazard identification under clause 6.1.2.

Exam tip: Too broad (whole job in one step) or too detailed steps are classic JHA errors; first step is always breaking the job into steps.

Process Hazard Analysis (PHA)BLUEPRINT

A team-based, systematic evaluation of hazards in a process involving highly hazardous chemicals, required by OSHA 1910.119(e) for covered processes.

  • Methods: HAZOP (guide words: no, more, less, reverse, other than, part of, as well as), What-If, Checklist, What-If/Checklist, FMEA, FTA.
  • Team includes someone with process engineering knowledge, an operator/employee with process experience, and a person knowing the PHA method.
  • Revalidated at least every 5 years; recommendations resolved promptly and documented.
  • NFPA 551 fire risk evaluation can be integrated for flammable processes.

Exam tip: HAZOP = guide words applied to process deviations at nodes; What-If = brainstorming; FMEA = component level.

Hierarchy of ControlsBLUEPRINT

The NIOSH/OSHA order of preference for controls: elimination, substitution, engineering, administrative, PPE. Higher levels are more effective and less dependent on human behavior.

  • Elimination: remove the hazard (discontinue the process).
  • Substitution: less hazardous material/equipment (non-flammable solvent instead of flammable).
  • Engineering: isolate people from the hazard (guards, ventilation, enclosures).
  • Administrative: procedures, training, rotation, signage, permits.
  • PPE: last line of defense.
  • ISO 45001 clause 8.1.2 requires the hierarchy when determining controls; ANSI Z10 also requires it.

Exam tip: When two controls are offered, choose the one higher in the hierarchy - substituting a non-flammable solvent beats issuing FR clothing.

Hazard mitigationBLUEPRINT

Hazard mitigation reduces the likelihood or severity of a hazard by applying preventive and protective measures, combined in layers so no single failure causes harm.

  • Preventive barriers reduce likelihood (interlocks, inherently safer design, maintenance).
  • Mitigative barriers reduce consequences (sprinklers, containment, emergency response).
  • NFPA 551: fire prevention measures (fuel load limits, detection/suppression) belong in the overall hazard mitigation plan.
  • PSM: controls for high-hazard processes must be documented, implemented and periodically reviewed.

Integrated (enterprise) risk management approach

Hazard identification, risk assessment and controls are embedded into the organization's overall risk strategy so safety, health, environmental and operational risks are managed together, not in silos.

  • Aligns mitigation with organizational objectives, risk appetite and compliance requirements.
  • Integrates safety into strategic planning, budgeting and continuous improvement.
  • ISO 31000 / Z690.1: risk management integrated into governance, strategy, planning and daily operations.

Exam tip: Combined hazards (chemical + fire) may require citing both PSM and NFPA references.

Bow-tie analysis

A diagram with the hazard/top event in the center, threats and preventive barriers on the left, and consequences and mitigative (recovery) barriers on the right.

  • Combines fault tree logic (left) and event tree logic (right).
  • Escalation factors show what can defeat a barrier.
  • Used to track barrier health (critical controls) on dashboards.
  • Good for communicating risk to non-specialists.

Exam tip: Left side = prevention (likelihood); right side = mitigation (consequence).

Layer of Protection Analysis (LOPA)

A semi-quantitative method that estimates scenario frequency by multiplying the initiating event frequency by the probability of failure on demand (PFD) of each independent protection layer (IPL).

  • Mitigated frequency = initiating event frequency x PFD1 x PFD2 x ...
  • Example: initiating event 0.1/yr, BPCS loop PFD 0.1, relief valve PFD 0.01 -> 0.1 x 0.1 x 0.01 = 1 x 10^-4 per year.
  • IPL must be independent, effective (specific to the scenario) and auditable.
  • SIL 1: PFD 10^-1 to 10^-2; SIL 2: 10^-2 to 10^-3; SIL 3: 10^-3 to 10^-4 (IEC 61511).
  • Procedures and training alone are usually not credited as IPLs.

Exam tip: Weak IPL independence is a reason to escalate to inherently safer or engineering solutions, not PPE.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 26 Risk Assessment and Management, p. 604; Ch. 14 Personal Protective Equipment, p. 385; Ch. 35 Prevention through Design

  2. Report the supervisor to the project manager
  3. Politely hold the start and gather the crew for a few minutes to review the lift and improve the JHA together
  4. Hand the supervisor a generic JHA and ask him to copy it

  1. ISO 45001
  2. OSHA 29 CFR 1910.119
  3. ANSI Z690.1
  4. NFPA 551

  1. Every year
  2. Every 3 years
  3. At least every 5 years
  4. Every 10 years

3.2-Q7. A company identifies a chemical hazard. Until a safer alternative is found, it provides special training, updates work procedures and posts hazard signs. Which control levels are being applied?

  1. Elimination and substitution
  2. Administrative controls and warnings
  3. Engineering controls and PPE
  4. Substitution and engineering controls

  1. Process-level deviations
  2. Component-level failure prediction
  3. Management decision-making

3.2-Q9. Several loud machines expose nearby workers to noise above the PEL. Which control would be MOST effective in reducing the risk of noise-induced hearing loss?

  1. Replace the noisy machines with quieter models
  2. Provide earplugs or earmuffs to all workers
  3. Start a noise monitoring program
  4. Rearrange the workspace to increase distance from the machines

3.2-Q10. The hierarchy of controls, in order of preference, is:

  1. PPE -> Administrative -> Engineering -> Substitution -> Elimination
  2. Administrative -> PPE -> Engineering -> Substitution -> Elimination
  3. Elimination -> Substitution -> Engineering -> Administrative -> PPE
  4. Substitution -> Elimination -> PPE -> Administrative -> Engineering

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1DZ10 ranks elimination, substitution, engineering, warnings and administrative controls above PPE. PPE can be interim but is not the first recommendation.
2ASubstitution removes the silica hazard at the source; the other choices are administrative or PPE measures.
3CA JHA is only effective when the workers doing the task help identify hazards. Fix it before work starts, constructively; reporting or generic forms do not.
4AOSHA 1910.132(d) requires assessing the hazards first and certifying the assessment in writing; selection follows.
5BThe Process Safety Management standard, 1910.119(e), requires PHAs for covered processes.
6C1910.119(e)(6) requires update and revalidation at least every 5 years. Trap: compliance audits are every 3 years.
7BTraining and procedures are administrative controls; signs are warnings. None change the hazard itself.
8DA JHA breaks a job into steps, identifies hazards in each and assigns controls. Process deviations are HAZOP; component failures are FMEA.
9AReplacing the source with quieter equipment removes the noise at its origin, the highest control available here. Monitoring is not a control.
10CMost effective to least: eliminate, substitute, engineer, administer, then PPE (ANSI Z10 adds warnings before administrative controls).

Domain 3 · Competency 3.3

Financial Risk Mitigation Strategies

CSP11 blueprint: Differentiate financial risk mitigation strategies as they relate to risk avoidance, risk retention, risk sharing, risk transfer, loss prevention and reduction

1. Definition

Financial risk management (risk financing) decides how an organization pays for losses: avoid the exposure, retain it (self-fund), share it, transfer it (insurance, contracts), or control it through loss prevention (lower frequency) and loss reduction (lower severity).

2. Explanation in simple words

You can stay away from a risk, keep it and pay for losses yourself, split it with a partner, pass it to an insurer or another company by contract, or make losses less likely and less severe. Most organizations use a mix.

3. Key points to remember

  • Avoidance: do not undertake the activity (e.g., decline a project, stop using a chemical). Eliminates the exposure entirely.
  • Retention: accept and pay for losses - deductibles, self-insurance, self-insured retentions, captive insurance companies (funded retention). Suits frequent, low-severity, predictable losses.
  • Sharing: losses split with others - joint ventures, pools, co-insurance. (Some texts treat insurance as sharing among a pool of insureds.)
  • Transfer: shift financial consequences - insurance (financial transfer), contractual transfer via hold-harmless/indemnity agreements, additional-insured endorsements, surety bonds, leasing, subcontracting.
  • Loss prevention: reduce frequency (inspections, training, guarding, housekeeping, hot-work permits). Loss reduction: reduce severity (sprinklers, first aid, emergency response, backups, business continuity).
  • Insurance terms: premium, deductible, EMR (experience modification rate; > 1.0 worse than average → higher premium), subrogation (insurer recovers from a responsible third party), pure risk (loss or no loss - insurable) vs speculative risk (gain or loss - not insurable), moral hazard (dishonesty), morale hazard (carelessness because insured).
  • Workers' compensation: no-fault system; benefits include medical, partial wage replacement, rehabilitation; exclusive remedy (employee generally cannot sue the employer); not pain and suffering.
  • Expected annual loss = probability × consequence; total cost of risk = retained losses + premiums + risk-control costs + administration.

4. Real work scenario

A warehouse operator has frequent small forklift-damage claims (about $3,000 each) and a small chance of a major fire. It retains the small losses with a $25,000 deductible and a strong loss-prevention program, buys property insurance for the catastrophic fire exposure, requires tenants to sign hold-harmless agreements, and installs sprinklers (loss reduction).

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Sprinklers reduce severity (loss reduction), they do not prevent fires.
  • Insurance and hold-harmless agreements = transfer; captives and deductibles = retention.
  • Video surveillance and guarding are loss control/mitigation, not transfer.
  • Know pure vs speculative risk and moral vs morale hazard.

6. Every blueprint point (6 blueprint items + 8 more)

Avoidance (risk avoidance)BLUEPRINT

Removing the hazardous activity, process or substance entirely so no exposure or loss can occur. It is the only strategy that reduces the chance of loss to zero, but it also gives up any benefit of the activity.

  • Example (guide): installing a Clean-In-Place system removes the need for manual tank entry, eliminating the confined space hazard.
  • Other examples: not entering a high-risk market, discontinuing a hazardous product line.
  • Best for catastrophic risks (e.g., handling certain explosives).

Exam tip: Avoidance (financial) parallels elimination (hierarchy of controls).

Retention (risk retention)BLUEPRINT

Accepting a risk and budgeting to pay for losses internally (self-insurance). Retention can be planned (deductibles, self-insured retention, funded reserves) or unplanned (unknown or uninsured risks).

  • Appropriate for frequent, low-severity, predictable losses.
  • Example (guide): a small firm pays minor property damage claims itself instead of buying expensive insurance.
  • Deductibles and self-insured retentions are forms of retention.
  • Retaining risk does not remove the duty to control it (General Duty Clause still applies).

Exam tip: Low severity + high frequency = retain (and prevent); high severity + low frequency = transfer (insure).

Sharing (risk sharing)BLUEPRINT

Distributing a risk among several parties so each bears part of the potential loss.

  • Example (guide): joint venture partners each assume part of the EHS compliance liability.
  • Other examples: consortiums, risk pools, co-insurance.
  • ISO 31000 uses 'sharing' to include contracts and insurance.

Transfer (risk transfer)BLUEPRINT

Shifting the financial consequences of a risk to another party through insurance, contracts, warranties, hold-harmless/indemnity clauses or outsourcing.

  • Example (guide): buying property and liability insurance for a plant.
  • Contractual transfer: hold harmless and indemnity clauses in contractor agreements.
  • Transfer moves the financial burden, not the hazard - and not the employer's legal/moral duty of care.
  • Best for low-frequency, high-severity losses.

Exam tip: If two answers seem correct, prefer the one that reduces the root cause (prevention) over one that only shifts the cost (transfer).

Loss preventionBLUEPRINT

Measures that reduce the FREQUENCY (likelihood) of losses before they occur.

  • Example (guide): interlocks on machinery to prevent unsafe operation.
  • Others: inspections, preventive maintenance, training, hot work permits, housekeeping.
  • Acts before the event.

Exam tip: Frequency = prevention; severity = reduction.

Loss reductionBLUEPRINT

Measures that reduce the SEVERITY of losses once an incident occurs.

  • Example (guide): sprinklers minimize fire damage; compartmentation and fire-rated barriers limit spread.
  • Others: emergency response, spill containment, first aid/AEDs, backup data, return-to-work programs.
  • Acts during or after the event.

Exam tip: Sprinklers do not prevent fires - they are loss reduction (and fire protection), not loss prevention.

Risk mitigation vs. risk treatment

Risk treatment is the overall selection of options to modify risk (avoid, reduce, share/transfer, retain). Risk mitigation is the subset that actually lowers likelihood, severity or impact through preventive or protective measures.

  • ISO 31000 treatment selection depends on: risk appetite and tolerance, cost-benefit analysis, and compliance obligations.
  • Strategies are usually combined: avoid catastrophic risks, insure moderate costly risks, prevent and reduce operational hazards.
  • Risk control = applying measures to eliminate or reduce risk to acceptable levels.

Risk profiling and sensible risk management

A risk profile describes the types, levels, sources and patterns of risk an organization faces. Risks are then prioritized by probability, severity and cost of control (cost-benefit) before action.

  • Sensible risk management: prioritize high probability, high severity, low-cost-to-control risks first.
  • Do not ignore low-probability, high-severity risks (e.g., boiler explosion).
  • Risk characterization is the final step of risk assessment: describes impact, likelihood and severity (with uncertainties) so decision-makers can act.

Risk appetite vs. risk tolerance

Risk appetite is the overall amount and type of risk the organization is willing to pursue or retain, set by top management. Risk tolerance is the acceptable variation for a specific risk, activity or department within that appetite.

  • Appetite: strategic, big picture; can be qualitative ('low tolerance for safety incidents') or quantitative ('no more than 1 lost-time injury per million hours').
  • Tolerance: more granular; defines limits before corrective action is triggered.
  • Example: low appetite overall, but tolerance of up to two first-aid cases per year in a high-risk maintenance division.

Exam tip: Appetite = big picture; tolerance = limits for specific risks.

Risk terms: acceptable, tolerable, inherent, residual, unacceptable

Exams test precise risk vocabulary using cue words in the scenario.

  • Acceptable: reduced to a level satisfactory to the organization, regulators or society (cue: 'meets standard', 'within policy').
  • Tolerable: allowed only because further reduction is impracticable or grossly disproportionate - managed under ALARP (e.g., work at height with fall arrest).
  • Inherent: raw risk with no controls (solvent room with no safety systems).
  • Residual: risk remaining after reasonably practicable controls (small ignition risk in a booth with explosion-proof gear).
  • Unacceptable: exceeds legal/organizational thresholds; stop or control immediately (crane with damaged load chain).

Exam tip: Inherent appears before controls in a question; residual appears after controls are implemented.

Pure vs. speculative risk

Pure risk has only the possibility of loss or no loss (no gain); speculative risk can produce gain or loss.

  • Workplace hazards and OSHA concerns are pure risks; only pure risks are normally insurable.
  • Speculative examples: gambling, stock investment, starting a business, real estate.
  • Example: investing in AI safety monitoring is speculative - it may cut losses and raise profit, or lose money.

Legal terms associated with risk

Contract and insurance terms that determine who ultimately pays for a loss.

  • Subrogation: insurer steps into the policyholder's shoes to recover what it paid from the at-fault third party.
  • Hold harmless: one party agrees not to hold the other responsible for certain risks; indemnity: promise to compensate the other for specified liabilities.
  • Limited liability: owners' personal assets protected from business debts.
  • Piercing the corporate veil: courts reach owners' personal assets when the corporate form is misused for fraud or to evade obligations.

Exam tip: Insurer recovering from a negligent contractor after paying a claim = subrogation.

Risk homeostasis (risk compensation / Peltzman effect)

People adjust behavior to perceived safety, so added protection may be offset by riskier behavior and the overall risk level stays about the same.

  • Example: drivers drive faster after ABS brakes are installed.
  • Reason why engineering controls should not depend on behavior and why PPE may increase risk-taking.
  • Associated with Wilde (risk homeostasis) and Peltzman (risk compensation).

Cost-benefit, ROI and payback for safety investments

Financial strategies are justified with simple economics: compare the cost of a control with the losses it avoids.

  • ROI = (annual savings - annual cost) / investment x 100%.
  • Payback period = initial investment / annual savings. Example: $50,000 guard system saving $20,000/yr -> 2.5 years.
  • Benefit-cost ratio = benefits / costs (>1 favorable).
  • Indirect costs of injuries are often several times the direct (insured) costs - include them in savings.

Exam tip: Expect cost tables; compute payback and ROI carefully and keep annual vs. one-time costs separate.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 26 Risk Assessment and Management, p. 604; Ch. 23 Workers' Compensation, p. 538

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

3.3-Q1. A large company forms a captive insurance subsidiary to cover its own workers' compensation losses. Which financial risk strategy is this?

  1. Avoidance
  2. Funded retention (self-insurance)
  3. Loss prevention
  4. Contractual transfer

3.3-Q2. Management decides to purchase liability insurance for potential chemical spills. Which financial strategy is being applied?

  1. Risk retention
  2. Risk sharing
  3. Risk avoidance
  4. Risk transfer

3.3-Q3. A company hires a subcontractor who agrees by contract to carry liability insurance and accept responsibility for injuries caused by its work. What is this an example of?

  1. Risk avoidance
  2. Risk retention
  3. Risk sharing
  4. Risk transfer

3.3-Q4. Which of the following is an example of risk sharing?

  1. Joint venture partners splitting EHS liabilities and costs
  2. Purchasing liability insurance
  3. Eliminating flammable materials
  4. Installing automatic sprinklers

3.3-Q5. Which statement BEST distinguishes risk mitigation from risk avoidance?

  1. Mitigation eliminates the hazard; avoidance reduces exposure
  2. Mitigation transfers cost to another party; avoidance shares the risk
  3. Mitigation reduces likelihood or impact; avoidance removes the activity or hazard entirely
  4. Mitigation retains the hazard; avoidance controls it

3.3-Q6. Which pair correctly matches loss PREVENTION and loss REDUCTION?

  1. Prevention: housekeeping and hot-work permits; Reduction: automatic sprinklers
  2. Prevention: automatic sprinklers; Reduction: housekeeping
  3. Prevention: insurance; Reduction: deductibles
  4. Prevention: first aid; Reduction: machine guarding

3.3-Q7. An insurer pays a business for damages and then seeks recovery from the third party who caused the loss. This is called:

  1. Hold harmless
  2. Limited liability
  3. Subrogation
  4. Indemnification

3.3-Q8. A company's actual primary losses are $150,000 and expected losses are $120,000. What is the experience modification rate (EMR) and its effect?

  1. 0.80; 20% premium credit
  2. 1.25; 25% premium surcharge
  3. 1.25; 25% premium credit
  4. 1.50; 50% premium surcharge

3.3-Q9. A warehouse has a 4% annual probability of a fire causing $250,000 in damage. Insurance costs $14,000 per year. What is the expected annual loss without insurance?

  1. $4,000
  2. $10,000
  3. $14,000
  4. $250,000

3.3-Q10. A small contractor chooses not to buy insurance for minor equipment damage and instead budgets for those losses. This demonstrates:

  1. Risk retention
  2. Risk transfer
  3. Risk avoidance
  4. Risk sharing

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BThe captive is owned by the company, so losses stay in the corporate family: funded retention, not true transfer.
2DInsurance transfers the financial consequence of a loss to the insurer in exchange for a premium. It does not reduce the hazard.
3DIndemnity and insurance requirements in contracts are contractual (non-insurance) risk transfer.
4ASharing spreads a risk among parties that each keep a portion. Insurance is transfer; eliminating materials is avoidance; sprinklers are loss reduction.
5CAvoidance means not doing the activity at all; mitigation (loss prevention/reduction) keeps the activity but lowers likelihood or severity.
6APrevention lowers frequency (stops the fire starting); reduction lowers severity after the event (sprinklers, first aid).
7CSubrogation lets the insurer step into the insured's shoes to recover from the responsible party. Hold harmless/indemnity are contract terms.
8BEMR = actual / expected = 150,000 / 120,000 = 1.25, so premiums are about 25% above the manual rate. EMR above 1.0 is a debit.
9BExpected loss = probability x consequence = 0.04 x 250,000 = $10,000. Premiums exceed expected loss because they include insurer costs and profit; insurance buys certainty.
10ARetention means accepting the loss and paying it from internal funds; it suits frequent, low-severity losses.
Printed book page 130
Domain 3 · Competency 3.4

Risk Analysis: Identify, Rank and Monitor

CSP11 blueprint: Apply risk analysis process of identifying, ranking, and monitoring (e.g., disasters/emergency preparedness, fire prevention, occupational health, hazardous materials management/environmental compliance)

1. Definition

This competency applies the risk process across specific risk areas: disasters and emergencies (hazard vulnerability analysis), fire, occupational health exposures and hazardous materials/environmental compliance. Risks are identified, ranked so resources go to the highest risks first, and monitored over time.

2. Explanation in simple words

Make a list of everything that could go wrong across the site, score each one, fix the top of the list first, and keep the list current as things change.

3. Key points to remember

  • Ranking tools: risk matrix (likelihood × severity), risk priority number (FMEA), risk scores with tie-breaker rules (often higher severity first), expected loss (probability × cost), Pareto of loss data.
  • Disaster/emergency: Hazard Vulnerability Analysis (HVA) rates probability, human/property/business impact and preparedness; drives the emergency plan and business continuity priorities.
  • Fire risk: fuel load, ignition sources, occupancy, protection systems (sprinklers, detection), housekeeping, hot work; NFPA codes; insurer (FM Global) surveys.
  • Occupational health: exposure assessment by similar exposure groups (SEGs); compare exposure (e.g., 95th percentile) with OEL; exposure categories 0-4 (AIHA); prioritize sampling and controls.
  • Hazardous materials/environmental compliance: chemical inventory, quantities vs thresholds (EPCRA TPQ, PSM TQ, RMP), compatibility, storage limits, permits, spill potential; compliance calendar and audits.
  • Monitoring: risk register reviews, inspections, KPIs, audits, changes in operations (MOC), incident trends; re-rank after controls (residual risk).

4. Real work scenario

A chemical distributor ranks its risks: ammonia release (likelihood 2, severity 5 = 10), forklift collision (4 × 3 = 12), warehouse fire (2 × 5 = 10). The forklift risk is addressed first by score, and the company's tie-breaker rule ranks the two 10s by severity, so both get specific controls and quarterly monitoring.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Calculate every option's score; read any tie-breaker rule in the stem.
  • HVA = probability × impact adjusted for preparedness.
  • Exposure ranking questions compare the upper percentile with the OEL.

6. Every blueprint point (8 blueprint items + 2 more)

Identification (identifying risks across categories)BLUEPRINT

Turn diverse hazards into one defensible risk picture. ISO 31000 gives the process, ISO 45001 embeds it in the OHSMS, PSM adds process-safety rigor and NFPA 551 guides fire risk methods.

  • Tools: Business Impact Analysis, scenario analysis, What-If, bow-tie, ignition source surveys, exposure profiling, compatibility matrices.
  • Categories in the blueprint: disasters/emergency preparedness, fire prevention, occupational health, hazardous materials and environmental compliance.
  • Document assumptions and data sources so the analysis is defensible.

Ranking (risk ranking toolkit)BLUEPRINT

Rank risks consistently so leadership has a clear decision path.

  • Risk matrices (3x3, 5x5) for qualitative ranking; semi-quantitative Severity x Likelihood with calibrated scales.
  • Bow-tie with barrier health to weight controls by effectiveness and degradation.
  • LOPA for process scenarios with independent protection layers; consequence modeling for fire and release scenarios.
  • Define evaluation criteria in writing (ALARP thresholds, legal must-meets) per ISO 31000.
  • Mini example (guide): solvent wash line, S = 5, L = 2 -> 10; with weak IPL independence treat as high and escalate to engineering/substitution, not PPE.

Exam tip: Pick the answer that creates the clearest decision path: defined criteria, documented assumptions, verification steps.

Monitoring (leading and lagging indicators for risk)BLUEPRINT

Monitor so you know controls actually work, using leading indicators, lagging indicators, a risk register and management review.

  • Leading (PSM): overdue MOCs and PHAs, safety-critical maintenance backlog, bypassed interlocks, IPL proof-test compliance.
  • Leading (fire/IH/env): hot work permit quality, impairment counts and duration, dust housekeeping scores, % tasks with current exposure assessment, capture velocity checks, container defects, manifest errors.
  • Lagging: TRIR, recordables tied to specific hazards, small fires, near releases, regulatory NOVs, insurance findings.
  • Revalidation: PSM PHA at least every 5 years; fire risk after occupancy/process change; IH reassessment when tasks, materials or exposures change.
  • ANSI/ASSP Z16.1 covers safety and health metrics.

Disaster/emergency preparedness risk analysisBLUEPRINT

Identify natural, utility, cyber, supply chain and civil unrest threats and rank them for emergency planning.

  • Tools: Business Impact Analysis, scenario analysis, What-If, bow-tie for critical scenarios.
  • Example (guide): loss of power to critical ventilation - ranked high severity, moderate likelihood without redundancy; monitored by monthly generator tests, transfer switch checks and ERP drill pass rate.
  • ISO 45001 clause 8.2 (emergency preparedness and response).

Fire prevention risk analysisBLUEPRINT

Identify ignition sources, fuel loads, ventilation paths, occupancy and impaired protection; rank and monitor fire risk using NFPA 551 methods.

  • Tools: ignition source surveys, hot work reviews, combustible dust assessments, bow-tie linking ignition barriers and suppression.
  • NFPA 551 = guide for evaluating fire risk assessments; expects transparent assumptions, data sources and sensitivity analysis.
  • Example (guide): spray booth with flammable coatings - monitor weekly booth differential pressure, semiannual sprinkler tests, hot work audits.

Occupational health risk analysisBLUEPRINT

Identify chemical, physical, biological and ergonomic exposures, rank them against OELs and task frequency, and monitor trends.

  • Tools: exposure profiling, task inventory, SDS review, qualitative and quantitative IH sampling, control banding.
  • Example (guide): silica during maintenance chipping - compare samples to OELs, consider frequency and duration; monitor exposure trends, LEV capture velocity, training logs.
  • Keep IH data traceable for periodic review.

Exam tip: For IH questions, a sampling strategy plus the hierarchy beats a PPE-only plan.

Hazardous materials risk analysisBLUEPRINT

Identify storage incompatibilities, secondary containment gaps and transport errors, rank by release potential and monitor segregation and containment.

  • Tools: compatibility matrices, secondary containment calculations, DOT packaging checks.
  • Screen for PSM applicability (highly hazardous chemicals at threshold quantities).
  • Example (guide): acids and bases in a shared room with single-wall containment and a drain path = high risk; monitor segregation audits, containment inspections, spill drills.

Environmental compliance risk analysisBLUEPRINT

Identify waste misclassification, spill pathways and permit gaps, and enter compliance exposure into the integrated risk register.

  • Tools: cradle-to-grave waste mapping, SPCC alignment, waste stream error rates.
  • Leading indicators: manifest errors, SPCC drill performance; lagging: Notices of Violation (NOVs).
  • ISO 31000 register entries should include compliance exposure, not only safety consequences.

Communication that leads to action

Risk rankings are only useful when converted into prioritized, owned actions communicated to each audience.

  • Two-way communication (ISO 31000); tailor to executives, supervisors and operators.
  • Prioritized action plan with owners and due dates.
  • Link each high-priority item to the governing standard so the rationale is clear.

Exam tip: Process scenarios cue LOPA, IPL independence, MOC and revalidation; fire scenarios cue NFPA 551 documentation and sensitivity testing.

Risk vocabulary (assessment vs. analysis vs. evaluation)

The guide's memory table distinguishes closely related risk terms that are often used as distractors.

  • Risk assessment = identification + analysis + evaluation (overall process).
  • Risk analysis = understand nature and determine level of risk; risk estimation = assigning values to likelihood and consequence.
  • Risk evaluation = compare results with criteria to decide acceptability.
  • Risk characterization = description of nature and magnitude of risk, including uncertainties.
  • Risk treatment = selecting and implementing measures to modify risk; risk management = coordinated activities to direct and control an organization with regard to risk.

Exam tip: 'Which step compares risk to criteria?' = evaluation, not analysis.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 26 Risk Assessment and Management; Ch. 39 Emergency Management and Preparedness; Ch. 27 Hazardous Materials Management

  1. Alphabetical order
  2. Probability and impact, adjusted for existing preparedness and mitigation
  3. Cost of insurance only
  4. Number of past OSHA citations

  1. Category 1 - no action
  2. Category 2 - periodic review
  3. Category 4 - unacceptable; implement controls and confirm with monitoring
  4. The exposure cannot be categorized without medical data

  1. Install properly designed ventilation with explosion-proof (classified) equipment
  2. Upgrade worker PPE
  3. Train workers on paint handling
  4. Add more signage

3.4-Q4. Using Risk = Likelihood x Severity (1-5 scales): Hazard P = L3 x S4, Hazard Q = L4 x S3, Hazard R = L2 x S5. Policy says ties are broken by higher severity. Which hazard is addressed FIRST?

  1. P
  2. Q
  3. R
  4. P and Q equally

3.4-Q5. When ranking a facility's environmental compliance risks, an SPCC (Spill Prevention, Control, and Countermeasure) plan primarily addresses:

  1. OSHA safety standards
  2. Fire suppression codes
  3. Industrial hygiene sampling
  4. EPA oil spill prevention requirements

3.4-Q6. In emergency preparedness, regular backup generator tests and evaluation of emergency drill performance are examples of:

  1. Preventive barriers
  2. Risk monitoring activities
  3. Loss reduction
  4. Administrative risk ranking

3.4-Q7. A company incurred $800,000 in losses over the last 3 years. Assuming the same loss rate and no inflation, what are the expected losses over the next 10 years?

  1. $800,000
  2. $2,400,000
  3. $2,666,667
  4. $8,000,000

3.4-Q8. Risk profiling is BEST described as:

  1. Listing job steps and hazards for each task
  2. Assigning a numerical score to each risk
  3. Ranking risks by financial cost only
  4. Describing the types, levels and sources of risk an organization faces

3.4-Q9. A fire risk assessment of a distribution warehouse produces four findings. Which should receive the HIGHEST priority?

  1. Cardboard stored next to an office copier
  2. A closed sprinkler control valve in an area with high-piled plastic commodities and forklift battery charging
  3. One burned-out exit sign bulb in a well-lit corridor with a second marked exit
  4. A fire extinguisher whose monthly inspection tag is one week overdue

3.4-Q10. An EHS manager must rank hazardous materials risks across a facility's chemical inventory to prioritize controls and compliance work. Which approach is MOST appropriate?

  1. Rank each chemical by hazard class, quantity relative to regulatory thresholds and likelihood of release, then monitor changes through inventory updates and MOC
  2. Rank chemicals by purchase cost
  3. Rank chemicals in the order of the SDS binder
  4. Rank only the chemicals that have spilled in the past

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BAn HVA scores probability and human/property/business impact, then adjusts for preparedness and response capability to rank relative risk.
2CExposure categories compare the upper percentile with the OEL; above the OEL is Category 4, requiring controls and follow-up sampling.
3AAn engineering control that keeps vapor below the LEL and removes ignition sources addresses both the fire and the health hazard at the source.
4AP = 12, Q = 12, R = 10. P and Q tie, and the tie-breaker (higher severity) selects P. Read the whole stem for decision rules.
5DSPCC plans are required by EPA (40 CFR 112) for facilities storing oil above threshold quantities to prevent discharges to navigable waters.
6BTesting and drills verify that controls and response capability still work, which is the monitoring part of the risk process.
7CAnnual rate = 800,000 / 3 = $266,667; x 10 years = $2,666,667. Trap: 2.4 million multiplies by 3.
8DA risk profile is the organization-wide picture of its risks, used for strategic prioritization. Listing task steps is a JHA.
9BAn impaired sprinkler system over a high fuel load with an ignition source combines high likelihood and catastrophic severity; restore protection and use impairment procedures.
10ARisk ranking combines hazard severity, quantity (e.g., PSM or EPCRA thresholds) and likelihood, and must be monitored as inventory changes. Spill history alone misses latent risks.
