Printed book page 136
Domain 4 · Competency 4.1

Emergency Response Plans

CSP11 blueprint: Create, employ, and maintain an Emergency Response Plan (e.g., fire, severe weather, nuclear incidents, natural disasters, terrorist attacks, chemical spills, utilities systems, cyber security)

1. Definition

An Emergency Response Plan (ERP) or Emergency Action Plan (EAP) defines how an organization will protect people, property and the environment during emergencies. OSHA 1910.38 sets minimum EAP elements; 1910.120(q) covers emergency response to hazardous substance releases; NFPA 1600 (now NFPA 1660) covers emergency management and business continuity programs.

2. Explanation in simple words

Before the emergency happens, decide who does what, how people are warned, where they go, how you count them, and how you call for help. Then practice it and update it.

3. Key points to remember

  • OSHA 1910.38 EAP minimum elements: procedures for reporting fires and emergencies; evacuation procedures and exit route assignments; procedures for employees who remain to operate critical operations; procedures to account for all employees after evacuation; rescue and medical duties; names/titles of people to contact. Written plan required for > 10 employees; alarm system; training and review when the plan changes.
  • Development steps: hazard vulnerability analysis → resources and capabilities → plan writing (roles, alarms, evacuation/shelter, accountability, communication, coordination with outside agencies) → training → drills/exercises (tabletop, functional, full-scale) → critique → update.
  • Phases: prevention, mitigation, preparedness, response, recovery.
  • Priorities: life safety → incident stabilization → property/environmental conservation.
  • Severe weather: watch (conditions favorable - prepare) vs warning (occurring/imminent - act); tornado - interior, lowest-level shelter; lightning - 30/30 rule.
  • Nuclear/radiological: shelter in place, close HVAC, time-distance-shielding; potassium iodide only when directed (protects thyroid only).
  • Terrorism/CBRNE and active shooter: shelter in place for chemical/biological plumes; Run-Hide-Fight; NFPA 3000 (active shooter/hostile event).
  • Chemical spills: incidental release (cleaned by trained employees in the area) vs emergency response (HAZWOPER). HAZWOPER training: awareness, operations (8 h), technician (24 h), specialist, incident commander; site workers 40 h.
  • Utilities: power loss (generators, emergency lighting), gas leaks (evacuate, no switches), water loss (sprinkler impairment, fire watch). Cyber: incident response plan - contain/isolate, eradicate, recover from clean backups, notify; ICS/OT systems put in a safe state.

4. Real work scenario

A warehouse fire drill shows employees reached the assembly area quickly, but supervisors could not confirm whether three temporary workers were inside. The plan is updated with a shift roster and a headcount procedure for contractors and temps, and the next drill includes a 'missing person' scenario.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Accounting for employees after evacuation is a required EAP element and a common missing-element answer.
  • Watch vs warning is a classic vocabulary trap.
  • Drills test and improve the plan; the follow-up to a poor drill is training and plan updates.
  • Plan may be communicated orally only for employers with 10 or fewer employees.

6. Every blueprint point (8 blueprint items + 6 more)

ERP purpose and legal basis (OSHA 1910.38 EAP, NFPA 1600)

An Emergency Response Plan is a written, site-specific, living document that describes how the organization prepares for, responds to and recovers from emergencies. OSHA 1910.38 sets the minimum Emergency Action Plan; NFPA 1600 is the broader best practice that adds business continuity.

  • 1910.38 minimum elements: reporting fires/emergencies; evacuation procedures and exit route assignments; procedures for employees who remain to operate critical operations; accounting for all employees after evacuation; rescue and medical duties; names or job titles to contact for plan information.
  • Written plan required if more than 10 employees; 10 or fewer may communicate orally.
  • Distinctive employee alarm system required (1910.165).
  • Review the plan with each employee when the plan is developed or the employee is assigned, when responsibilities change, and when the plan changes.
  • Related: HAZWOPER 1910.120 (site-specific ERP), PSM 1910.119 (emergency planning). NFPA 1600 has been consolidated into NFPA 1660 (2024 edition) - the guide cites NFPA 1600.

Exam tip: Memorize the six 1910.38 minimum elements - a common 'which is NOT required' question.

Identifying and analyzing emergency risks

The ERP is built on a documented hazard identification, risk assessment and impact analysis covering all reasonably expected emergencies - natural, human-caused and biological.

  • 1910.38(a): plan must address emergencies reasonably expected in the workplace; NFPA 1600 requires hazard identification, risk assessment and business impact analysis.
  • Steps: hazard survey; review incident/loss/insurance records; consult employees, managers and local authorities; risk matrix; prioritize; document and review annually.
  • Natural (earthquake, flood, tornado, wildfire) -> evacuation/shelter; human-caused (fire, violence, utility failure, sabotage, cyber) -> lockdown/evacuation/notify; biological (pandemic, bioterrorism, contamination) -> hygiene, isolation, notify.

Exam tip: OSHA expects workplace-specific risk identification, not a generic template.

Elements of an ERP

A complete ERP contains defined purpose and scope, roles, chain of command and communications, risk summary, scenario-specific procedures, resources, contacts, training/drills and a review process.

  • Roles: Incident Commander, evacuation/floor wardens, first aiders, communications lead, all employees.
  • Chain of command names a successor if the IC is absent; backup contacts for redundancy.
  • Procedures: evacuation, lockdown, shelter-in-place, fire/explosion, medical, spills/releases, utility failure.
  • Resource inventory (extinguishers, spill kits, first aid, AEDs, radios) with locations and responsible persons; internal and external contact lists (fire, police, EMS, poison control, agencies, insurer, vendors).
  • Annual review and after-action reviews after drills or real events (best practice; required by NFPA 1600, not explicitly by 1910.38).

Preparing for emergencies (site readiness)

Preparation covers risk assessment, worksite security, evacuation routes and assembly points, shelter-in-place areas, life safety systems, medical/first aid, drills and training.

  • Security: controlled entry, badges, cameras, visitor sign-in/escort, cybersecurity for building controls.
  • Evacuation: marked and illuminated exits, posted maps, assembly points at a safe distance, primary and alternate routes (NFPA 101).
  • Shelter-in-place rooms: interior, stocked with supplies; used when evacuation is more dangerous (toxic release, tornado, violence).
  • Medical: first aid kits and trained responders; 1910.151 requires a trained first aider if no infirmary/clinic is in near proximity (OSHA interprets 3-4 minutes for life-threatening injuries); eyewash where corrosives are used.
  • Drills: vary scenarios and shifts; after-action review after each.

Exam tip: OSHA expects you to plan, equip, train and drill for all reasonably anticipated emergencies, not just fire.

Creating, implementing and maintaining the ERP

Development, implementation and maintenance are a continuous cycle: involve stakeholders, train everyone, drill, document and update.

  • Develop: involve management, employees, unions, contractors and external responders; base procedures on the risk assessment; include site details (floor plans, chemical inventory, people with disabilities).
  • Implement: initial training and refresher after updates; signage; redundant communication channels; document everything.
  • Maintain: drills at least annually and after major changes; revise after new hazards, lessons learned, staff/contact changes or regulatory updates; keep current copies accessible.
  • Documentation of training and drills is both best practice and proof of compliance.

Exam tip: The ERP is not just a document - it is a living process; 'write it and file it' answers are wrong.

FireBLUEPRINT

Fire plans focus on detection, alarm, evacuation, extinguishment by trained employees (if permitted) and accounting at assembly points.

  • References: OSHA 1910.38 (EAP), 1910.39 (fire prevention plan), 1910.157 (extinguishers), 1910.165 (alarms), NFPA 101, NFPA 72.
  • Alarms audible/visible, tested; automatic fire department notification where required.
  • Evacuation wardens assist and account for staff and visitors; roll call at assembly point.
  • Extinguisher use: PASS (Pull, Aim, Squeeze, Sweep); monthly inspection, annual maintenance.
  • Drills at least annually (best practice), more often in high-risk sites.

Severe weatherBLUEPRINT

Severe weather plans cover tornadoes, hurricanes, floods, blizzards and extreme heat/cold with monitoring, shelter and shutdown procedures for hazards relevant to the location.

  • Tornado: interior rooms away from windows, lowest floor; tornado drills; supplies (water, radios, first aid).
  • Hurricane/flood: pre-arranged shutdown and evacuation, secure loose items, protect openings.
  • Extreme heat/cold: monitor advisories, hydration/cooling or warming stations.
  • Alerts: NOAA weather radios, text alerts; tornado watch = conditions favorable, warning = tornado sighted or on radar - take shelter.

Nuclear incidentsBLUEPRINT

Plans for releases of radioactive material from nuclear plants, medical/industrial sources or weapons; required for facilities near nuclear plants and coordinated with NRC, FEMA and local agencies.

  • Shelter-in-place: designated rooms, seal openings, shut down HVAC if needed.
  • Evacuation routes and transport coordination; follow official advisories.
  • Radiation monitoring (dosimeters); potassium iodide (KI) protects only the thyroid from radioactive iodine and is taken only on official instruction.
  • Protective principles: time, distance, shielding.
  • Planning zones around nuclear plants: about 10-mile plume exposure and 50-mile ingestion pathway zones (FEMA/NRC).

Natural disastersBLUEPRINT

Earthquakes, floods, wildfires and similar events need location-specific procedures, supplies and post-event hazard checks.

  • Earthquake: drop, cover and hold on; secure heavy items; afterward check for injuries, gas leaks and fires before re-entry.
  • Flood: move critical equipment to higher ground, shut down utilities, use flood maps.
  • Wildfire: defensible space, clear combustibles, monitor air quality, respirators for smoke, rapid evacuation.
  • Kits: water, food, radios, flashlights, batteries, PPE.

Terrorist attacks and active shooterBLUEPRINT

Deliberate attacks and active shooter events require lockdown, mass notification and Run-Hide-Fight training, coordinated with law enforcement.

  • References: OSHA General Duty Clause, DHS guidance, NFPA 3000 (Active Shooter/Hostile Event Response).
  • Run, Hide, Fight (DHS) - escape if possible, hide if not, fight only as a last resort.
  • Lockdown procedures, silent alarms, mass notification.
  • Bomb threats: keep caller talking, use a bomb threat checklist, do not use radios/cell phones near a suspected device.
  • Post-incident medical, psychological and legal support; tabletop and live drills with law enforcement.

Chemical spillsBLUEPRINT

Chemical spill plans cover immediate evacuation and isolation, containment by trained personnel only, decontamination, medical support and regulatory notification.

  • Evacuate, activate alarms, shut down sources only if safe.
  • Only trained responders with proper PPE clean up (HAZWOPER 1910.120(q) for emergency response; incidental spills handled by trained area employees).
  • Spill kits, neutralizers, booms; decontamination of people and areas; eyewash/showers.
  • Notify authorities when a reportable quantity is released (CERCLA RQ -> National Response Center; EPCRA 304 -> state/local).
  • Document: incident report, root cause, regulatory notifications.

Exam tip: Untrained employees evacuate and report; they do not attempt cleanup of a non-incidental release.

Utility systems failuresBLUEPRINT

Loss of power, water or gas can create safety hazards and stop critical systems, so plans address safe shutdown, backup power and safe restoration.

  • Procedures to safely shut down and restart equipment; inspect before re-energizing.
  • Emergency lighting and backup power (NFPA 110 generators; NFPA 101 requires 90 min emergency lighting).
  • Alternative communications (radios, cell phones with backup power); alternative water for drinking and sanitation.
  • Gas leak: evacuate immediately, eliminate ignition sources, call utility provider.

CybersecurityBLUEPRINT

Cyber incidents can disable safety controls, alarms and communications. There is no OSHA cyber standard, but NFPA 1600 and DHS recommend cyber response planning for safety and operational systems.

  • Train employees to recognize phishing, unusual activity and outages; report quickly.
  • Activate IT/cyber incident response team and plan.
  • Secure offsite backups and restoration protocols.
  • Maintain safety monitoring and alarms via manual backups; alternate notification methods if digital systems fail.
  • Industrial control systems guidance: ISA/IEC 62443, NIST Cybersecurity Framework.

Pandemic and bioterrorism

Biological emergencies need prevention, detection, communication, medical response, coordination with public health and law enforcement, and business continuity for high absenteeism.

  • Pandemic = global outbreak; epidemic = cases above expected in a community or region.
  • Pandemic controls: hygiene, ventilation/barriers (engineering), staggered shifts/remote work (administrative), PPE; symptom reporting and return-to-work criteria; cross-training.
  • Bioterrorism: recognize suspicious packages/illness clusters, isolate area, notify public health, law enforcement and FBI; decontamination; rumor control point.
  • References: OSHA General Duty Clause, 1910.1030 (BBP), 1910.120, CDC/NIOSH guidance, NFPA 1600.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 39 Emergency Management and Preparedness, p. 870; Ch. 4 Regulations (HAZWOPER)

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

4.1-Q1. A radiological release is expected from a nearby nuclear power plant and officials advise sheltering in place. Which action is MOST appropriate?

  1. Open windows to dilute any contamination entering the building
  2. Have all employees take potassium iodide to protect against all radiation
  3. Shelter indoors, shut down HVAC and close openings, and take potassium iodide only if officials instruct it
  4. Drive employees through the plume to reach a shelter farther away

4.1-Q2. During a night shift, a fire breaks out in a section of a warehouse. Several employees evacuate safely, but one forklift operator is unaccounted for. The plan identifies assembly points and alarm systems but has no accountability procedure. What element is missing from the emergency plan?

  1. Communication procedures with insurance carriers
  2. Coordination procedures with environmental agencies
  3. Post-incident counseling for employees
  4. A system for accounting for all employees after evacuation

4.1-Q3. The National Weather Service issues a TORNADO WARNING for your county. What should the facility do?

  1. Move personnel immediately to designated storm shelters or interior refuge areas
  2. Continue normal work but monitor the weather radio
  3. Evacuate everyone to their vehicles
  4. Wait until the tornado is visible before acting

4.1-Q4. A 1-liter bottle of a known solvent breaks in a lab. Trained lab staff in the immediate area can safely absorb it with a spill kit. Under HAZWOPER 1910.120(q), this is:

  1. An emergency response requiring a HAZMAT team
  2. An incidental release that may be cleaned up by trained employees in the immediate area
  3. A release that automatically requires EPA notification
  4. An uncontrolled release requiring evacuation of the building

4.1-Q5. A toxic gas plume is drifting toward a residential area at a steady 10 mph. It takes 15 minutes to mobilize and complete evacuation of the at-risk population. What is the MINIMUM distance the plume must be from the area when the evacuation order is given to ensure safe clearance?

  1. 1.5 miles
  2. 2.5 miles
  3. 3 miles
  4. 4 miles

4.1-Q6. What is the MOST important step after conducting an emergency drill?

  1. Repeat the same drill immediately until all employees pass
  2. Send the drill results to OSHA for recordkeeping
  3. Conduct an after-action review and document improvement areas
  4. Dismiss employees early as a reward for participation

4.1-Q7. During a drill evaluation, observers note confusion about who should contact external emergency responders. Which plan element MOST likely needs improvement?

  1. Command hierarchy and communication chain
  2. Evacuation route signage
  3. Number and placement of fire extinguishers
  4. Sprinkler system activation pressure

4.1-Q8. A ransomware attack encrypts the building automation and process control servers at a chemical plant during operations. According to the facility's emergency response plan, what should be done FIRST?

  1. Pay the ransom quickly to restore operations
  2. Restore the servers from backup while they remain connected to the network
  3. Wait for the IT vendor to diagnose the problem before taking any action
  4. Isolate affected systems from the network and activate the cyber incident response plan, placing processes in a safe state

4.1-Q9. Employees report a strong odor of natural gas in an office building after nearby utility work. What is the BEST immediate action?

  1. Turn on exhaust fans to ventilate the building
  2. Locate the leak by checking appliances and connections
  3. Evacuate the building without operating electrical switches, then call the gas utility and 911 from a safe location
  4. Have employees stay at their desks while maintenance investigates

4.1-Q10. A receptionist receives a telephone bomb threat. According to a typical emergency response plan, what should the receptionist do?

  1. Hang up immediately and continue working
  2. Keep the caller talking, record details using a bomb threat checklist, and notify security and police per the plan
  3. Personally search the building for suspicious packages
  4. Announce over the public address system that there is a bomb in the building

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CBuildings provide shielding and closing HVAC limits infiltration. KI only blocks thyroid uptake of radioiodine and is taken only on official instruction.
2DOSHA 1910.38(c) requires procedures to account for all employees after evacuation; without it, missing workers may not be identified for rescue.
3AA warning means a tornado is occurring or imminent: shelter now in interior, lowest-level, windowless areas. Trap: treating a warning like a watch (prepare only).
4BReleases that employees in the immediate area can absorb or control without significant safety or health risk are incidental, not emergency responses under 1910.120(q).
5BDistance = speed x time = 10 mi/h x (15/60) h = 2.5 miles. Trap: forgetting to convert minutes to hours.
6CDrills exist to test and improve the plan; the after-action review captures weaknesses and assigns corrective actions. OSHA does not collect drill results.
7AThe plan must assign clear roles, including who notifies outside responders; confusion here is a chain-of-command and communication gap, not a hardware issue.
8DContainment comes first: isolate infected systems to stop spread and move the process to a safe state. Restoring while still connected risks reinfection.
9CUtility gas leaks present an explosion hazard: evacuate, avoid ignition sources such as switches and phones, and report from a safe distance.
10BBomb threat procedures emphasize capturing caller details (checklist) and notifying authorities, who decide on search or evacuation; panic announcements and self-searches are inappropriate.
Printed book page 143
Domain 4 · Competency 4.2

Disaster Response and Recovery

CSP11 blueprint: Describe the elements in disaster response and recovery (e.g., incident command, business continuity, contingency plans)

1. Definition

Disaster response and recovery covers how an organization organizes the emergency response (Incident Command System), keeps critical functions running or restores them (business continuity), and prepares alternatives for foreseeable disruptions (contingency plans).

2. Explanation in simple words

During a disaster one person is in charge and everyone knows their role (ICS). Afterwards, the business needs a plan to get the most important work running again fast (business continuity).

3. Key points to remember

  • Incident Command System (ICS/NIMS): Incident Commander; Command Staff - Safety Officer, Public Information Officer, Liaison Officer; General Staff sections - Operations, Planning, Logistics, Finance/Administration.
  • ICS principles: common terminology, modular organization, management by objectives, Incident Action Plan (prepared by Planning), manageable span of control (3-7, ideally 5), unity of command, unified command for multi-agency incidents, formal transfer of command with briefing, accountability, integrated communications.
  • Business continuity: business impact analysis (BIA) identifies critical functions and impacts; Recovery Time Objective (RTO) = maximum acceptable downtime; Recovery Point Objective (RPO) = maximum acceptable data loss; strategies include alternate sites, backup suppliers, cloud backups, mutual aid.
  • Standards: NFPA 1600/1660, ISO 22301 (business continuity management systems), FEMA guidance.
  • Contingency planning: 'what-if' alternatives for supply disruption, utility loss, staffing shortages, pandemic; pre-arranged contracts; succession plans.
  • Recovery: damage assessment, restoration, employee assistance (EAP/counseling), insurance claims, lessons learned and plan updates. Disaster recovery (IT) focuses on systems and data.

4. Real work scenario

A regional power outage hits during peak production. The facility's generators run life-safety systems, but IT and supply links are down. The business continuity plan activates: critical orders move to a sister plant, data are restored from cloud backups within the 4-hour RTO, and the logistics section of the ICS arranges fuel deliveries for the generators.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Planning Section prepares the IAP; Logistics provides resources; Operations does the tactical work; Finance tracks costs and claims.
  • Safety Officer, PIO and Liaison are Command Staff, not sections.
  • RTO = time; RPO = data loss.
  • Unified Command keeps each agency's authority while sharing objectives.

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 5 more)

Incident Command System (ICS)BLUEPRINT

ICS is the standardized, scalable on-scene management system within the National Incident Management System (NIMS). OSHA HAZWOPER 1910.120(q) requires an ICS for emergency response to hazardous substance releases, led by the senior emergency response official.

  • Five major functions: Command, Operations, Planning, Logistics, Finance/Administration (Intelligence/Investigations may be added).
  • Command staff: Public Information Officer, Safety Officer (authority to stop unsafe acts), Liaison Officer.
  • Span of control: 3 to 7 subordinates, optimum 5.
  • Principles: common terminology, modular organization, unity of command, unified command for multi-agency incidents, Incident Action Plan, integrated communications.
  • The Incident Commander is responsible for everything until delegated; the IC position always exists.

Exam tip: Safety Officer is command staff (reports to IC), not general staff; unified command = several agencies sharing command, not several ICs giving separate orders.

Business continuityBLUEPRINT

Business continuity keeps critical functions running or restores them quickly after a disruption. It is driven by a Business Impact Analysis (BIA) and covered by NFPA 1600 (now NFPA 1660) and ISO 22301.

  • BIA identifies critical processes, dependencies and the impact of disruption over time.
  • RTO (recovery time objective) = target time to restore a function; RPO (recovery point objective) = maximum tolerable data loss (how far back backups must go); MTPD = maximum tolerable period of disruption (RTO must be shorter).
  • Strategies: alternate sites (hot, warm, cold), backup suppliers, data backups, cross-training, remote work.
  • Benefits: less downtime and financial loss, preserved reputation, insurance and regulatory compliance.
  • ISO 22301 = business continuity management systems (requirements).

Exam tip: A BIA comes before choosing continuity strategies.

Contingency planningBLUEPRINT

Contingency plans are pre-arranged 'what if' alternatives for specific disruptions - backup resources, alternate procedures and triggers for using them.

  • Examples: mutual aid agreements with neighboring plants or fire departments, pre-qualified restoration contractors, backup generators, alternate communications.
  • Define activation triggers, decision authority and resources in advance.
  • Test through tabletop exercises and update after use.

Phases of emergency management

Emergency management is a cycle of mitigation (and prevention), preparedness, response and recovery.

  • Mitigation: reduce the impact before an event (elevating equipment above flood level, seismic bracing).
  • Preparedness: plans, training, drills, equipment, agreements.
  • Response: protect life, stabilize the incident, protect property and environment (life safety -> incident stabilization -> property conservation).
  • Recovery: restore operations, repair, return to normal, lessons learned.

Exam tip: 'Before the event, reducing impact' = mitigation; 'plans and drills' = preparedness.

HAZWOPER emergency response (1910.120(q))

Employers whose employees respond to hazardous substance releases need a written emergency response plan, ICS, training by role and medical surveillance for HAZMAT team members.

  • Training levels: First Responder Awareness (recognize, notify), Operations (8 hr, defensive containment), Hazmat Technician (24 hr, offensive stopping of release), Specialist (24 hr), On-Scene Incident Commander (24 hr plus competencies).
  • Annual refresher training or demonstrated competency.
  • Employers who evacuate all employees and do not respond may use a 1910.38 EAP instead of a (q) ERP.
  • Buddy system and backup personnel required in danger areas; positive-pressure SCBA in unknown atmospheres.

Exam tip: Defensive (contain from a distance) = Operations level; plugging/patching the leak = Technician.

Exercises and drills

Exercises test plans, train people and reveal gaps; results feed corrective actions.

  • Types (FEMA HSEEP): discussion-based - seminar, workshop, tabletop, game; operations-based - drill, functional exercise, full-scale exercise.
  • Tabletop: discussion of a scenario in a conference room, low cost.
  • Functional: tests coordination and command/communications without moving people and equipment to the field.
  • Full-scale: real-time deployment with multiple agencies; most realistic and costly.
  • Follow every exercise or real event with an after-action review and improvement plan.

Recovery and after-action review

Recovery restores safe operations and captures lessons learned so the plan improves.

  • Re-entry only after hazards are assessed (structural, electrical, gas, atmosphere) and authorities clear the site.
  • Restoration contractors, damage assessment, insurance claims and documentation.
  • Psychological support (EAP, critical incident stress management) for affected workers.
  • After-action review: what was planned, what happened, why, what to change; update ERP, training and resources.

Crisis and emergency communications

Clear, redundant communication protects people during response and protects the organization's reputation afterward.

  • Mass notification and redundant channels (PA, text, email, radios).
  • Single designated spokesperson / Public Information Officer; consistent, accurate, timely messages.
  • Pre-scripted messages and contact lists; rumor control.
  • Notify regulators as required (e.g., OSHA fatality within 8 hours, inpatient hospitalization, amputation or eye loss within 24 hours).

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 39 Emergency Management and Preparedness, p. 870

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1DCommand staff report directly to the IC: Safety, Public Information and Liaison Officers. Section chiefs (Operations, Planning, Logistics, Finance/Admin) form the general staff.
2APlanning collects and evaluates information, tracks resource status, and prepares the IAP. Operations executes it; Logistics obtains resources.
3CRPO is how much data you can afford to lose (drives backup frequency); RTO is how long a process can be down. Swapping the two is the classic trap.
4BThe BIA identifies time-critical functions, their downtime tolerance and dependencies, which sets recovery priorities, RTOs and RPOs.

6DUnity of command means each responder reports to only one supervisor. Span of control (3-7, ideally 5) limits how many people one supervisor manages.
7CLogistics provides facilities, services and materials (food, fuel, communications, equipment). Finance/Admin tracks costs, Planning tracks resource status.
8BContingency planning pre-arranges backups and alternate sites so critical records can be restored quickly; rebuilding or waiting delays recovery.
9DBCP activation prioritizes restoring the time-critical functions ranked in the BIA; financial, insurance and review activities follow.
10AResponse protects life and stabilizes the incident; recovery and continuity restore critical systems and business functions after stabilization.
Printed book page 148
Domain 4 · Competency 4.3

Fire Prevention, Protection and Suppression

CSP11 blueprint: Identify key components of fire prevention, protection, and suppression systems

1. Definition

Fire prevention removes or controls fuel, oxygen and ignition sources so fires do not start; fire protection detects fires and protects occupants and property (alarms, compartmentation, egress); suppression systems control or extinguish fires (sprinklers, standpipes, special agents, extinguishers).

2. Explanation in simple words

Stop fires starting (housekeeping, hot-work permits, safe storage), find them fast (detectors and alarms), and put them out or keep them small (sprinklers and extinguishers) while people get out.

3. Key points to remember

  • Fire triangle/tetrahedron: fuel, heat, oxygen + chemical chain reaction. Dust explosion pentagon adds dispersion and confinement.
  • Fire classes: A (ordinary combustibles), B (flammable liquids/gases), C (energized electrical), D (combustible metals), K (cooking oils/fats). Lithium-ion battery fires: large amounts of water for cooling; thermal runaway.
  • Flammable liquids (OSHA/GHS): Category 1 FP < 73.4 °F and BP ≤ 95 °F; Category 2 FP < 73.4 °F and BP > 95 °F; Category 3 FP 73.4-140 °F; Category 4 FP 140-199.4 °F. Cabinet limit 60 gal Cat 1-3 (120 gal Cat 4), max 3 cabinets per fire area; storage rooms 6 air changes/hour; bonding and grounding during transfer.
  • LFL/UFL: flammable range; work limit typically < 10% LFL. Flash point, autoignition temperature, vapor density (> 1 heavier than air).
  • Hot work (NFPA 51B, OSHA 1910.252): permit, PAI (permit authorizing individual), fire watch during and at least 30 min after, combustibles 35 ft away or covered.
  • Detection: heat (fixed temperature, rate-of-rise), smoke (ionization - fast flaming; photoelectric - smoldering), flame (UV/IR - fast, open areas).
  • Sprinklers (NFPA 13; ITM per NFPA 25): wet pipe (most common), dry pipe (freezing areas), pre-action (sensitive areas), deluge (high-hazard, all heads open). Q = K√P. Keep 18 in. clearance below deflectors.
  • Portable extinguishers (1910.157): monthly visual inspection, annual maintenance, hydrostatic testing per type; training annually if employees are expected to use them; PASS (pull, aim, squeeze, sweep).
  • Special agents: CO₂ (no residue, asphyxiation hazard), clean agents (FM-200, Novec 1230), dry chemical, foam (Class B), wet chemical (Class K).
  • Combustible dust: NFPA 652/660 dust hazard analysis (DHA), housekeeping (avoid compressed air), explosion venting/isolation/suppression, Class II electrical classification; secondary explosions cause most damage.

4. Real work scenario

A food plant's corn-starch dust collector is indoors with no explosion protection, and dust layers have built up on rafters. The safety professional orders a dust hazard analysis, moves the collector outdoors with explosion venting and isolation valves, starts a housekeeping schedule using vacuum systems approved for combustible dust, and bans compressed-air cleaning.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Know which extinguisher class matches the fire: kitchen grease = K; energized panel = C (no water).
  • Fire watch: 30 minutes after hot work (NFPA 51B).
  • Prevention vs protection: housekeeping prevents; explosion venting and sprinklers protect.
  • Sprinkler K-factor and flammable storage limits are favorite numbers.

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 13 more)

Fire preventionBLUEPRINT

Fire prevention keeps fires from starting by controlling ignition sources and fuel loads (NFPA 1 Fire Code). OSHA 1910.39 requires a fire prevention plan when an OSHA standard calls for one.

  • 1910.39 plan contents: list of major fire hazards with proper handling/storage, potential ignition sources and their control, type of fire protection equipment for each hazard; names/job titles responsible for maintaining equipment and for controlling fuel source hazards; housekeeping procedures.
  • Written if more than 10 employees; oral if 10 or fewer; review with employees upon assignment.
  • Controls: hot work permits, housekeeping, electrical maintenance, static control, segregation of incompatibles, smoking rules.
  • Employers must provide a workplace free of recognized fire hazards (General Duty Clause).

Exam tip: Prevention = stop ignition (frequency). Sprinklers and alarms are protection, not prevention.

Fire protectionBLUEPRINT

Fire protection is the systems and methods that detect, control or extinguish a fire and protect occupants (NFPA 72, NFPA 13, NFPA 101). It includes active systems (alarms, sprinklers, extinguishers) and passive features (fire-rated construction).

  • Active: needs detection/activation - sprinklers, extinguishers, alarm and detection systems, clean agent, foam, standpipes; needs ongoing ITM (NFPA 25, 72; OSHA 1910.157).
  • Passive: built-in, no action needed - fire-rated walls/doors/floors (NFPA 80, 101), fire dampers, smoke barriers, fireproofing on steel, firestopping, compartmentation, intumescent paint.
  • Layered approach: engineering (sprinklers, alarms, ventilation), administrative (prevention plans, permits, drills), protective equipment.
  • Annual program review and adjustment after audits, new hazards or code changes.

Exam tip: Fire doors propped open defeat passive protection - a frequent inspection finding.

Fire suppressionBLUEPRINT

Suppression systems control or extinguish fire, from portable extinguishers to automatic sprinklers and special agent systems.

  • Wet pipe: water in pipes, most common, heated areas.
  • Dry pipe: pressurized air, for freezing areas (unheated warehouses, garages).
  • Deluge: all heads open, high-hazard areas (aircraft hangars, chemical processing).
  • Preaction: requires detection signal before water enters pipes (double interlock = two signals); data centers, museums, libraries.
  • Occupancy hazard classes for sprinkler design: light (offices), ordinary (warehouses, manufacturing), extra (flammable liquids); hydraulically calculated (NFPA 13).

Exam tip: Water-damage-sensitive area = preaction; freezing = dry pipe; rapid total flooding of water = deluge.

Fire science: triangle, tetrahedron, pentagon and phases

Fire is rapid exothermic oxidation (combustion). Removing any element of the fire model stops it.

  • Triangle: fuel, oxygen (~21% in air), heat. Tetrahedron adds the chemical chain reaction (interrupted by dry chemical and halogenated agents).
  • Dust explosion pentagon: fuel, oxygen, ignition, dispersion, confinement.
  • Deflagration = subsonic flame front (flash fire); detonation = supersonic.
  • Fire phases: incipient, growth, fully developed, decay.
  • Incomplete combustion produces more CO and soot.

Flash point, fire point, autoignition and flammable limits

Key properties that classify liquids and define when vapors can ignite.

  • Flash point: lowest temperature at which a liquid gives off enough vapor to form an ignitable mixture with an ignition source (closed-cup method for OSHA).
  • Fire point: vapors sustain burning for at least 5 s; typically about 10-30 deg C above flash point.
  • Autoignition temperature (AIT): ignites with no external ignition source (NFPA 497, SDS).
  • LEL/UEL: below LEL too lean, above UEL too rich; flammable range lies between. Gasoline about 1.4%-7.6%.
  • Hot work not allowed where atmosphere is within flammable range; confined space entry limit is 10% LEL.

Exam tip: Flash point = vapors CAN ignite; fire point = fire KEEPS burning; AIT = no ignition source needed.

Heat transfer in fires

Fire spreads by conduction, convection and radiation; understanding them predicts fire spread.

  • Conduction: through solids by direct contact (steel beam through a fire wall ignites the other side).
  • Convection: movement of hot gases/liquids (smoke rising through stairwells, shafts, ducts to upper floors).
  • Radiation: electromagnetic (infrared) waves, no medium needed (burning building ignites a neighbor across an alley).

Exam tip: Spread to upper floors via shafts = convection; across a gap to another building = radiation.

Flammable and combustible liquid classification and storage (NFPA 30 / OSHA 1910.106)

Liquids are classed by flash point and boiling point; the lower the class number, the stricter the storage and handling.

  • Class IA: FP < 73 deg F, BP < 100 deg F (ethyl ether). IB: FP < 73, BP >= 100 (acetone, gasoline, toluene). IC: FP 73-100 (xylene, turpentine).
  • Class II: 100-140 deg F (diesel, mineral spirits); IIIA: 140-200 (the guide lists kerosene here, but kerosene is usually Class II, FP about 100-150 deg F); IIIB: >= 200 (vegetable oil, glycerol).
  • OSHA 1910.106 now uses GHS Categories 1-4 (Cat 4 = FP > 140 to 199.4 deg F); NFPA 30 still uses classes.
  • Cabinets: no more than 60 gal of Category 1-3 (Class I/II) or 120 gal of Category 4/Class III per cabinet; max 3 cabinets in one fire area.
  • Storage rooms: 1-hour (or 2-hour) fire rating, self-closing doors, curbs/sumps; ventilation at least 1 cfm/ft2 of floor area (NFPA 30); keep vapors below 10% LEL.
  • Bond and ground containers when transferring Class I liquids (NFPA 77).

Exam tip: Know how to classify a liquid from flash point and boiling point and then pick the control.

Electrical and static ignition sources

Faulty wiring, overloaded circuits, arcing and static discharge are leading ignition sources.

  • Grounding connects to earth; bonding equalizes potential between conductive objects - both used for flammable liquid transfer (NFPA 77).
  • Static controls also: humidity control, anti-static flooring, non-sparking tools.
  • Hazards: overloaded circuits, damaged insulation, loose connections (arcing), unlisted equipment, misused extension cords.
  • Controls: AFCIs, explosion-proof equipment in classified locations, infrared thermography, NFPA 70E program.
  • Electrical fires: Class C agents (CO2, dry chemical); de-energize before using water. Heat detectors are better than smoke detectors in dusty electrical rooms.

Hot work safety (NFPA 51B / OSHA 1910.252)

Welding, cutting, brazing, grinding and soldering are major ignition sources; outside designated hot work areas they require a permit.

  • Permit: date, location, nature of job, authorization signature, fire watch assigned, precautions checklist.
  • Move combustibles at least 35 ft away or protect with fire-resistant covers/shields.
  • Fire watch during work and at least 30 min after (OSHA 1910.252); NFPA 51B (2019 and later editions) requires at least 1 hour after, followed by further fire monitoring - the guide states 30 minutes.
  • Test atmosphere with LEL detection in enclosed or poorly ventilated areas.
  • Fire watch must know how to use extinguishers and sound the alarm; designated permanent hot work areas preferred.

Exam tip: Combustibles that cannot be moved 35 ft must be covered - and a fire watch is required.

Combustible dust

Finely divided solids that present a fire or explosion hazard when dispersed in air - even materials not normally flammable (sugar, flour, aluminum, wood).

  • Stages: accumulation, dispersion, confinement, ignition, primary explosion, secondary explosion (secondary usually causes most damage and deaths).
  • NFPA 652 requires a Dust Hazard Analysis (DHA); NFPA 654 prevention; OSHA uses General Duty Clause and the Combustible Dust NEP (CPL 03-00-008).
  • Housekeeping trigger: dust layer over 1/32 in. (paper clip thickness) over 5% of floor area; vacuum with approved equipment, avoid dry sweeping and compressed air.
  • Electrical: Class II locations; Group E metal, F carbonaceous, G grain/organic; Div 1 normal operations, Div 2 abnormal.
  • Tests: Kst (deflagration index; St1 0-200, St2 200-300, St3 > 300 bar-m/s), MIE, MEC, go/no-go screening.
  • Engineering: explosion venting (NFPA 68), isolation and inerting (NFPA 69), spark detection; cases: Imperial Sugar 2008 (14 deaths).

Exam tip: Secondary explosion prevention = housekeeping of settled dust.

Fire detection and alarm systems (NFPA 72)

Detection provides early warning, starts evacuation and can trigger suppression and shutdowns.

  • Ionization smoke detectors: fast-flaming fires; photoelectric: smoldering fires.
  • Heat detectors: fixed temperature (e.g., 135 or 190 deg F) or rate-of-rise; used where smoke detectors false-alarm (kitchens, dusty areas).
  • Flame detectors: UV fastest (also triggered by welding arcs), IR for hydrocarbon fires, UV/IR dual for accuracy.
  • Gas detectors alarm before LEL is reached.
  • FACP supervises power and circuits; notification appliances meet ADA/NFPA 72; mass notification per NFPA 72 Ch. 24; records available to the AHJ.
  • Interlocks: sprinklers, gas shutoff, HVAC smoke dampers, emergency power (NFPA 110).

Exam tip: Smoldering fire = photoelectric; flaming fire = ionization; dusty room = heat detector.

Portable fire extinguishers (NFPA 10 / OSHA 1910.157)

Extinguishers are classed by fire type and must be selected, located, inspected and maintained for the expected hazard.

  • Classes: A ordinary combustibles; B flammable liquids/gases; C energized electrical; D combustible metals (dry powder); K cooking oils (wet chemical, potassium acetate).
  • Max travel distance: A 75 ft; B 30 or 50 ft depending on rating; D 75 ft; K 30 ft; C based on the A or B hazard.
  • Monthly visual inspection; annual maintenance (records kept 1 year or until next maintenance).
  • Hydrostatic test every 5 years (water, CO2, wet chemical) or 12 years (dry chemical) - the guide lists '6-year and 12-year'; 6 years is the internal maintenance interval for stored-pressure dry chemical.
  • Training on initial hire and annually if employees are expected to use them.

Special suppression systems

Gaseous and foam systems protect areas where water is unsuitable.

  • Clean agents (NFPA 2001): FM-200 (HFC-227ea), Novec 1230, inert gases (Inergen, Argonite) - no residue, non-conductive; for data centers, electrical rooms, museums.
  • CO2 (NFPA 12): smothers by displacing oxygen; lethal at design concentrations - pre-discharge alarm, delay, lockout for entry; use in normally unoccupied areas (transformers, generators).
  • Foam (NFPA 11, 16): AFFF and AR-AFFF for Class B fires (hangars, fuel racks); alcohol-resistant foam for polar solvents. PFAS-containing AFFF is being phased out.
  • Clean agent inspection: semiannual cylinder weight/pressure checks and functional tests without discharge.

Sprinkler inspection, testing and maintenance (NFPA 25)

Water-based systems must be inspected and tested on fixed schedules to remain reliable.

  • Monthly: control valves (sealed/locked) and gauges (dry, preaction, deluge).
  • Quarterly: alarm devices (waterflow, supervisory).
  • Annually: visual sprinkler inspection, main drain/flow tests.
  • Every 5 years: internal pipe inspection.
  • Sprinkler head testing: dry sprinklers every 10 years; standard-response heads at 50 years then every 10; fast-response at 20 years then every 10.
  • Keep at least 18 in. clearance below sprinkler deflectors; do not hang items from piping.

Exam tip: Closed control valve = most common reason sprinklers fail - valves must be supervised or locked open.

Segregation and separation

Separate incompatible or flammable materials and control building design and ventilation to prevent fires and limit their spread.

  • Oxygen cylinders separated from fuel-gas cylinders or combustibles by 20 ft or a 5-ft-high noncombustible barrier with at least 1/2-hour fire rating (1910.253).
  • NFPA 400 segregation by hazard class (oxidizers away from flammables); NFPA 30 Ch. 9 container storage; 1910.1450 lab segregation procedures.
  • Separate full and empty containers; store chemicals below eye level; secondary containment; keep fire department access clear.
  • Outdoor storage: minimum distances to buildings, property lines and public ways per NFPA 30 tables.

Fire safety housekeeping

Good housekeeping reduces fuel load, prevents ignition and keeps exits and fire equipment accessible (1910.22, NFPA 1, 101, 13).

  • Remove trash, scrap and oily rags daily; oily rags in covered metal containers.
  • Keep aisles, exits, extinguishers, alarms and electrical panels clear (36 in. working clearance at panels per NEC).
  • No storage in stairwells, exits or mechanical rooms; maintain 18 in. below sprinklers.
  • Written checklists, assigned duties, documentation and surprise audits.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 12 Fire Prevention and Protection, p. 350

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

4.3-Q1. A safety professional notes that a Class BC extinguisher has been placed near the range hood of a deep fat fryer in the cafeteria. The manager says a Class BC extinguisher is fine for a kitchen. Who is correct?

  1. The manager, because a BC agent both cools grease and displaces oxygen
  2. The safety professional, because one extinguisher never holds enough agent for a fryer fire
  3. Neither, because a Class A water extinguisher should be used
  4. The safety professional, because a Class K extinguisher is required for cooking oil and fat fires

4.3-Q2. A combustible dust explosion requires which two elements in addition to the fire tetrahedron (fuel, heat, oxygen, chain reaction)?

  1. Fuel and confinement
  2. Dispersion and confinement
  3. Dispersion and ignition
  4. Oxygen and pressure

4.3-Q3. Which detector is MOST appropriate for fast-developing flammable-liquid fires in an open process area?

  1. Optical flame detector (UV/IR)
  2. Rate-of-rise heat detector
  3. Photoelectric smoke detector
  4. Fixed-temperature heat detector

4.3-Q4. A contractor will weld near a combustible wood-framed wall. According to NFPA 51B, what is the BEST course of action before starting hot work?

  1. Cover the wall with a plastic tarp to prevent damage
  2. Keep a fire extinguisher nearby and start welding immediately
  3. Move combustibles at least 35 feet away or protect them with approved fire-resistant shields or blankets
  4. Post a 'Caution: Hot Work in Progress' sign and proceed

4.3-Q5. When nearby combustible materials cannot be removed or guarded during welding, a fire watch must be maintained for at least how long after the work is completed?

  1. 15 minutes
  2. 30 minutes
  3. 45 minutes
  4. 90 minutes

4.3-Q6. Which NFPA standard specifies the inspection, testing, and maintenance requirements for water-based fire protection systems such as sprinklers?

  1. NFPA 25
  2. NFPA 13
  3. NFPA 72
  4. NFPA 70E

4.3-Q7. An employee uses a portable fire extinguisher on an office fire, but the flames continue to grow. What is the MOST appropriate next action?

  1. Move flammable materials away from the fire
  2. Open windows to allow smoke to escape
  3. Retrieve a larger extinguisher from another floor
  4. Sound the fire alarm and evacuate immediately

4.3-Q8. A renovated attic is being fitted with fire detection. The space is dusty, has wide temperature swings, and no HVAC. What should the safety engineer recommend?

  1. Install combination smoke/CO detectors
  2. Defer installation until the dust settles
  3. Install heat detectors instead of smoke detectors
  4. Calibrate the smoke detectors with open-flame testing

4.3-Q9. Which element was added to the traditional fire triangle to form the fire tetrahedron?

  1. Dispersion
  2. Chemical chain reaction
  3. Confinement
  4. Vaporization

4.3-Q10. What is the required inspection and maintenance frequency for portable fire extinguishers under OSHA 1910.157 and NFPA 10?

  1. Weekly visual check only
  2. Semi-annual inspection only
  3. Inspection every 3 years
  4. Monthly visual inspection and annual maintenance

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1DNFPA 10 requires Class K (wet chemical) extinguishers for cooking media; the agent saponifies and cools hot oil to prevent reignition. Water on grease causes violent spattering.
2BThe dust explosion pentagon adds dispersion (a dust cloud) and confinement to fuel, ignition and oxygen. Fuel, ignition and oxygen are already part of the base elements.
3AUV/IR flame detectors respond in seconds to radiant energy and work outdoors, where smoke and heat dissipate before reaching smoke or heat detectors.
4CNFPA 51B and OSHA 1910.252 require removal of combustibles within 35 ft or protection with fire-resistant guards, plus a permit and fire watch. Plastic tarps are combustible.
5BOSHA 1910.252(a)(2)(iii) requires the fire watch for at least 30 minutes after hot work ends to detect smoldering fires. NFPA 51B (2019+) extends this to 60 minutes, so read what the question cites.
6ANFPA 25 covers ITM of water-based systems; NFPA 13 covers sprinkler installation and design, NFPA 72 fire alarms, NFPA 70E electrical safety.
7DPortable extinguishers are for incipient fires only. If the fire grows, the employee should leave, alert others and let trained responders take over.
8CDust and environmental extremes cause nuisance alarms and failures in smoke detectors; heat detectors are suited to dirty or unconditioned spaces.
9BThe tetrahedron adds the uninhibited chemical chain reaction to fuel, heat and oxygen; agents such as dry chemical and halon work by interrupting it. Dispersion and confinement belong to the dust pentagon.
10DOSHA 1910.157(e) requires monthly visual inspection and annual maintenance checks; hydrostatic testing occurs at longer intervals (5 or 12 years).

Domain 4 · Competency 4.4

Hazardous Materials Transportation and Security

CSP11 blueprint: Prepare procedures for the safe transportation and security of hazardous materials

1. Definition

Hazardous materials transportation is regulated by the U.S. DOT Hazardous Materials Regulations (49 CFR 171-180) and internationally by the UN Model Regulations, IMDG Code (sea), IATA DGR (air) and ADR (road, Europe). Security requirements address theft, sabotage and terrorism risks (49 CFR 172 Subpart I security plans; DHS CFATS for facilities).

2. Explanation in simple words

Before hazmat moves, you must classify it, package it correctly, label and placard it, prepare shipping papers, train the people involved and keep it secure so it cannot be stolen or misused.

3. Key points to remember

  • Nine hazard classes: 1 explosives, 2 gases, 3 flammable liquids, 4 flammable solids, 5 oxidizers/organic peroxides, 6 toxic/infectious, 7 radioactive, 8 corrosives, 9 miscellaneous.
  • Shipper responsibilities: classify, select UN-specification packaging, mark (proper shipping name, UN number), label, prepare shipping papers with emergency response information and 24-h telephone number, placard information, certify.
  • Placards (172.504): Table 1 materials (e.g., Division 1.1-1.3, 2.3, 4.3 dangerous when wet, 6.1 PIH, 7 radioactive yellow III) - any quantity; Table 2 materials - when the aggregate gross weight is 1,001 lb or more.
  • Shipping papers must be within the driver's immediate reach (or in the door pocket when out of the vehicle).
  • Hazmat employee training (172.704): general awareness, function-specific, safety, security awareness, in-depth security (if a security plan is required) - within 90 days of hire and at least every 3 years.
  • Emergency Response Guidebook (ERG): first responders' guide - initial isolation and protective action distances; SDS gives detailed chemical information.
  • Security plans: personnel security (background checks), unauthorized access controls, en-route security (routes, stops, GPS tracking).
  • Other modes: IMDG Code (sea), IATA (air), pipelines (PHMSA).

4. Real work scenario

A plant ships drums of flammable solvent weighing 1,200 lb in total by truck. The shipping coordinator prepares shipping papers with the UN number and emergency phone, applies Class 3 labels, provides Class 3 placards because the aggregate weight exceeds 1,000 lb, and confirms that the driver has hazmat training and keeps the papers within reach.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Table 1 = placard any quantity; Table 2 = 1,001 lb aggregate.
  • Hazmat training every 3 years; new employees within 90 days.
  • ERG is for first responders; SDS for detailed chemical data.
  • Class 8 = corrosive; Class 2 = gases; Class 3 = flammable liquids.

6. Every blueprint point (2 blueprint items + 4 more)

Hazmat transportation (DOT 49 CFR 171-180)BLUEPRINT

DOT's Hazardous Materials Regulations govern classification, packaging, marking, labeling, placarding, shipping papers, training and incident reporting for hazmat in commerce - from loading through transit to unloading.

  • Nine hazard classes: 1 explosives, 2 gases (2.1 flammable, 2.2 non-flammable, 2.3 toxic), 3 flammable liquids (FP <= 140 deg F / 60 deg C), 4 flammable solids, 5 oxidizers (5.1) and organic peroxides (5.2), 6 toxic (6.1) and infectious (6.2), 7 radioactive, 8 corrosives, 9 miscellaneous.
  • Packing groups: I great danger, II medium, III minor.
  • Shipping paper basic description order: UN ID number, proper shipping name, hazard class, packing group; plus 24-hour emergency response phone number.
  • Placards: any quantity of Table 1 materials (e.g., 1.1-1.3, 2.3, 4.3, 7 Yellow-III); Table 2 materials when aggregate gross weight is 1,001 lb or more.
  • PHMSA (DOT) enforces; FMCSA covers motor carrier rules; incidents reported by phone to the National Response Center immediately (within 12 hours) and in writing on DOT Form 5800.1 within 30 days.

Exam tip: DOT applies during transport; EPA RCRA applies to hazardous waste management and disposal; OSHA HazCom applies in the workplace.

Hazmat securityBLUEPRINT

Hazmat security prevents theft, sabotage and terrorist misuse of hazardous materials in transport and at facilities.

  • DOT security plans (49 CFR 172.800, Subpart I) required for higher-risk shipments (e.g., placarded quantities, Division 1.1-1.3, select agents, large bulk toxic inhalation hazards).
  • Plan must address personnel security, unauthorized access and en-route security, based on a security risk assessment.
  • Facility security: access control, inventory reconciliation, cameras, lighting, background checks, cybersecurity of process controls.
  • DHS CFATS (6 CFR 27) covered high-risk chemical facilities; its statutory authority lapsed in July 2023. EPA RMP (40 CFR 68) and PSM still apply.
  • Integrate security planning with emergency response planning.

DOT hazmat employee training (49 CFR 172.704)

Every hazmat employee (anyone who loads, unloads, packages, marks, prepares shipping papers or transports hazmat) must be trained, tested and certified.

  • Content: general awareness/familiarization, function-specific, safety, security awareness, and in-depth security training if a security plan is required.
  • Initial training within 90 days of employment or job change (may work under direct supervision meanwhile).
  • Recurrent training at least every 3 years; records kept for the preceding 3 years and 90 days after employment ends.
  • Driver training also required for those operating motor vehicles carrying hazmat.

Exam tip: DOT = every 3 years; contrast with HAZWOPER annual refresher.

Marking, labeling, placarding and the Emergency Response Guidebook (ERG)

Communication of hazard during transport lets responders identify materials quickly.

  • Labels go on packages (4-in. diamonds); placards go on vehicles, bulk containers and rail cars (10.8-in. diamonds) on all four sides.
  • UN/NA number displayed on placards or orange panels for bulk shipments.
  • ERG: yellow pages (ID number index), blue pages (material name index), orange pages (response guides), green pages (initial isolation and protective action distances for toxic inhalation hazards).
  • Shipper certifies shipment is properly classified, described, packaged, marked and labeled.
  • CHEMTREC and other services can provide the required 24-hour emergency response number.

Exam tip: Green ERG pages = isolation and protective action distances.

Packaging and loading/segregation in transport

Packaging must be authorized for the material and packing group, and incompatible classes must be segregated in vehicles.

  • UN specification packaging is performance-tested (drop, stack, leakproofness) and marked with the UN packaging code.
  • Segregation table (49 CFR 177.848) prohibits loading certain classes together (e.g., oxidizers with flammables, cyanides with acids).
  • Secure packages against movement; bulk containers need closures and venting as specified.
  • Hazardous waste shipments also need an EPA uniform hazardous waste manifest (cradle-to-grave tracking).

Hazmat transportation incident response

Transport incidents require isolation, identification and notification before action.

  • Approach from upwind, uphill and upstream; isolate the area; identify the material from placards, shipping papers (in the cab within reach of the driver) and the ERG.
  • Use ERG initial isolation distance first, then protective action (evacuate or shelter in place).
  • Only trained HAZWOPER responders take offensive action.
  • Report to NRC and DOT as required; CERCLA reportable quantities trigger federal notification.

Exam tip: Shipping papers are kept within the driver's reach (or in the door pocket) so responders can find them.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 27 Hazardous Materials Management, p. 616; Ch. 32 Site Security, p. 731

  2. To regulate the discharge of pollutants from ships
  3. To set standards for ship construction and design
  4. To provide requirements for safely transporting dangerous goods by sea

4.4-Q10. Under DOT hazmat training rules (49 CFR 172.704), which training must ALL hazmat employees receive in addition to general awareness and function-specific training?

  4. Incident commander training

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1A49 CFR 177.817 requires shipping papers within the driver's immediate reach (or in the door pouch), and required placards must be displayed before transport continues.
2CDOT Class 2 = gases (2.1 flammable, 2.2 non-flammable, 2.3 toxic). Class 1 = explosives, Class 3 = flammable liquids, Class 8 = corrosives.
3B1910.120(q)(6)(iii): HAZMAT technicians need at least 24 hours of training equal to the operations level plus specific competencies. 40 hours applies to general site workers at cleanup sites.
4ALevel D is a work uniform with no respiratory protection and minimal skin protection. Level C adds an air-purifying respirator; B adds SCBA; A is fully encapsulating.
5DWith an unknown material and symptoms already present, untrained workers must evacuate; only trained responders with proper PPE should identify and control the release.
6C49 CFR 172.504: Table 1 materials (e.g., Division 1.1-1.3 explosives, poison inhalation hazards) need placards in any quantity; Table 2 materials at 1,001 lb or more aggregate gross weight.
7BThe ERG gives generic guidance by UN/NA number and guide page (isolation distances, fire and spill response). The SDS gives product-specific detail.
8DThe IMDG Code (IMO) governs sea transport of dangerous goods; IATA DGR covers air, and 49 CFR covers US ground transport.
9ADOT security plans must include an assessment of transportation security risks and measures for personnel security, unauthorized access and en route security, plus security training.
10CAll hazmat employees need general awareness, function-specific, safety and security awareness training, with recurrent training at least every 3 years. In-depth security training applies when a security plan is required.

Domain 4 · Competency 4.5

Workplace Violence Prevention

CSP11 blueprint: Implement a workplace violence prevention program

1. Definition

Workplace violence is any act or threat of physical violence, harassment, intimidation or other threatening disruptive behavior at the work site. A prevention program combines management commitment, worksite analysis, hazard prevention and control, training and recordkeeping/evaluation (OSHA 3148 guidelines; ANSI/ASIS/SHRM WVPI standard).

2. Explanation in simple words

Violence at work usually has warning signs. Assess where and how it could happen, design the workplace to deter it, train people to recognize and report warning signs, respond quickly to threats and support those affected.

3. Key points to remember

  • Types: Type I criminal intent (robbery, no relationship - leading cause of workplace homicide), Type II customer/client/patient (healthcare, social services), Type III worker-on-worker, Type IV personal relationship (domestic violence spilling into work).
  • Risk factors: handling cash, working alone or at night, contact with the public, delivering services in community settings, high-crime areas, guarding valuables.
  • Program elements: written policy (zero tolerance, no retaliation for reporting), threat assessment team (HR, security, legal, EHS, management), worksite analysis and security survey, controls, training, reporting, post-incident response (medical and psychological support, EAP, investigation), evaluation.
  • Controls: engineering (access control, lighting, cameras, panic alarms, drop safes, barrier windows, metal detectors), administrative (two-person staffing, cash-handling limits, visitor policies, de-escalation procedures), CPTED (crime prevention through environmental design).
  • Warning signs: threats, talk of weapons or violence, intimidation, escalating anger, sudden behavior change, isolation, substance abuse. Aggression continuum: anxiety → intimidation → crisis → violence (stages vary by model).
  • Response: report concerns immediately to supervisor/HR/security; for an imminent threat, call security/911 and follow emergency procedures (Run-Hide-Fight for active shooter). Do not confront or physically intervene.
  • Security concepts: deter, detect, delay, respond.

4. Real work scenario

A former employee sends emails hinting that people 'will understand' his anger. The threat assessment team reviews the messages the same day, notifies security and local police, updates the visitor and badge system, briefs reception staff with a photo, and offers EAP support to the targeted supervisors.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Immediate response to threats: report to HR/security; do not ignore, confront or forward to coworkers.
  • Know the four violence types and the main risk factors.
  • Engineering vs administrative: metal detectors and drop safes = engineering; weapon bans and visitor rules = administrative.
  • Post-incident: medical and psychological support first, then investigation and review of controls.

6. Every blueprint point (1 blueprint items + 5 more)

Workplace violence prevention programBLUEPRINT

A written program that commits management, involves employees, analyzes the worksite, controls hazards, trains staff and records and evaluates incidents. OSHA has no specific standard but enforces under the General Duty Clause (directive CPL 02-01-064).

  • OSHA program elements (OSHA 3148): management commitment and employee participation; worksite analysis; hazard prevention and control; safety and health training; recordkeeping and program evaluation.
  • Written policy: zero tolerance, clear reporting procedures, no reprisal for reporting.
  • Threat assessment team (HR, security, safety, legal, management, EAP) to assess and manage threats.
  • California requires most employers to have a written Workplace Violence Prevention Plan (Labor Code 6401.9, from July 2024).
  • Consensus standard: ANSI/ASIS/SHRM WVPI.1 (workplace violence and active assailant prevention, intervention and response).

Exam tip: Management commitment and employee involvement come first; 'install cameras' alone is not a program.

Types of workplace violence

Workplace violence is classified by the relationship between the perpetrator and the workplace (NIOSH / UIIPRC typology).

  • Type I criminal intent: no legitimate relationship (robbery) - most workplace homicides.
  • Type II customer/client: patients, customers, students, inmates - most common in healthcare and social services.
  • Type III worker-on-worker: current or former employee.
  • Type IV personal relationship: domestic violence that spills into the workplace.

Exam tip: Patient assaulting a nurse = Type II; ex-spouse attacking at work = Type IV.

Risk factors and high-risk occupations

Certain job conditions increase the likelihood of violence and should drive the worksite analysis.

  • Risk factors: exchanging money with the public, working alone or in small numbers, late night/early morning hours, high-crime areas, guarding valuables, contact with unstable or volatile persons, alcohol served, delivering passengers/goods/services.
  • High-risk sectors: healthcare and social assistance, retail/late-night retail, taxi/rideshare, law enforcement and corrections.
  • Worksite analysis: records review (OSHA 300, incident and security reports), employee surveys, security audits.

Engineering and administrative controls for workplace violence

Apply the hierarchy: design out opportunity, then procedures, training and staffing.

  • Engineering: controlled access, badge systems, bullet-resistant barriers, good lighting, cameras, panic/duress alarms, drop safes and limited cash, curved mirrors, furniture arrangement preventing entrapment, escape routes.
  • Administrative: adequate staffing, no working alone at night, visitor management, cash-handling limits, check-in procedures for field staff, flagging of violent patients/clients.
  • Training: recognizing warning signs, de-escalation, reporting, emergency procedures, Run-Hide-Fight.

Warning signs and threat assessment

Most perpetrators show observable concerning behavior beforehand; a trained team assesses and manages threats.

  • Warning signs: verbal threats, intimidation, fascination with weapons or prior violent incidents, sudden behavior changes, substance abuse, severe stress, paranoia, violation of protective orders.
  • Threat assessment: gather facts, assess credibility and capability, decide interventions (EAP referral, discipline, security, law enforcement, protective orders).
  • Treat all threats seriously and document them.

Exam tip: Report and assess threats early - waiting for an overt act is the wrong answer.

Active shooter response and post-incident response

Employees must know what to do during an attack and the organization must support victims afterward.

  • DHS Run, Hide, Fight; when police arrive, keep hands visible and follow commands.
  • NFPA 3000 (ASHER) - integrated active shooter/hostile event preparedness, response and recovery with unified command.
  • Post-incident: medical care, report and investigate, critical incident stress debriefing, EAP/counseling, legal support.
  • Record injuries from workplace violence on the OSHA 300 log if recordable criteria are met; evaluate the program after each incident.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 32 Site Security, p. 731

4.5-Q1. A hospital emergency department nurse is assaulted by a patient's relative. Which type of workplace violence is this?

  1. Type I - criminal intent (no relationship)
  2. Type II - customer/client/patient
  3. Type III - worker-on-worker
  4. Type IV - personal relationship

4.5-Q2. An employee reports that a co-worker has been making aggressive comments about supervisors and slammed equipment after a performance review. The company has a written workplace violence prevention program. What should the supervisor do FIRST?

  1. Report the concern to HR or the safety/threat assessment team for immediate assessment under the program
  2. Ignore the behavior until it escalates into a physical incident
  3. Confront the worker publicly to discourage further aggression
  4. Tell employees to avoid the worker but take no formal action

4.5-Q3. A recently terminated employee is sending hostile, vaguely threatening emails to your team, saying he might do something to 'make everyone understand.' How should the team respond?

  1. Reply to explain the reasons for his termination
  2. Ignore the emails, assuming he will cool down
  3. Forward the emails to coworkers to gather opinions
  4. Report the emails to security and human resources as soon as possible

4.5-Q4. Before construction begins, management convenes architects, safety experts, police, and fire officials to design security into a new facility (lighting, sight lines, controlled entrances). Which approach is this?

  1. Safe construction through environmental design
  2. Planned protection through environmental engineering
  3. Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design (CPTED)
  4. Planned construction through environmental engineering

4.5-Q5. In a workplace violence prevention program, which element BEST demonstrates management commitment?

  1. Installing CCTV cameras without any policy updates
  2. A written zero-tolerance policy signed by top leadership and communicated to all employees
  3. Conducting unannounced drills only
  4. Providing counseling only after incidents

4.5-Q6. What is the MOST appropriate post-incident action following a workplace violence event?

  1. Provide medical and psychological support, then investigate and review controls
  2. Return operations to normal immediately
  3. Focus only on legal reporting requirements
  4. Suspend all security staff involved

4.5-Q7. Which four basic security concepts should be included in a facility security program?

  1. Deter, detect, train, respond
  2. Read, react, identify, respond
  3. Deter, detect, delay, dial
  4. Deter, detect, delay, respond

4.5-Q8. According to NSC data, what is the greatest risk factor for homicide at work?

  1. A disgruntled coworker
  2. A random shooter
  3. Robbery
  4. A recently fired worker

4.5-Q9. What can a company do to help employees avoid the bystander effect if an active shooter enters the workplace?

  1. Permit employees to carry concealed firearms
  2. Train employees in advance on what to do (e.g., run, hide, fight) and how to report
  3. Establish a clear hierarchy of communication
  4. Develop stress management programs

4.5-Q10. A worksite analysis of a 24-hour convenience store shows that most incidents occur late at night during robberies. Which control is MOST effective at reducing this risk?

  1. Require employees to work faster during night shifts
  2. Post a zero-tolerance policy in the break room
  3. Limit cash on hand with a drop safe, keep windows clear for visibility, and provide bright lighting with posted signs
  4. Provide annual training only for day-shift workers

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BType II involves customers, clients, patients, students or their families and is most common in healthcare. Type I is criminal intent, such as robbery by a stranger.
2AWarning signs should be reported early so a trained team can assess the threat; public confrontation can escalate and ignoring it misses the chance for prevention.
3DVeiled threats from a former employee are a recognized risk factor; preserve the emails and report them to security and HR for threat assessment and protective measures.
4CCPTED uses natural surveillance, access control, territorial reinforcement and maintenance to reduce crime opportunities; it is the security analog of prevention through design.
5BOSHA's program elements begin with management commitment and employee participation, shown by a visible, leadership-endorsed written policy backed by resources.
6AOSHA guidance calls for immediate care, trauma counseling and debriefing, followed by investigation and corrective action to prevent recurrence.
7DLayered security aims to deter attempts, detect intrusions, delay the intruder long enough for a response, and respond.
8CMost workplace homicides are Type I, committed during robberies, which is why late-night retail and taxi work are high-risk. Coworker violence is far less frequent.
9BPrior training and drills reduce freezing and diffusion of responsibility, so people act quickly instead of waiting for others.
10CEngineering and administrative controls that reduce the reward and raise visibility (drop safes, low cash, clear sight lines, lighting) are proven robbery deterrents. A policy alone does not deter outsiders.
