Domain 5 · Competency 5.1

Environmental Protection and Pollution Prevention

CSP11 blueprint: Describe environmental protection and pollution prevention programs (e.g., spill containment, abatement, best practices)

1. Definition

Environmental protection programs prevent or control releases to air, water and land. Pollution prevention (P2) reduces pollution at its source rather than treating it after it is created (Pollution Prevention Act of 1990). Key U.S. laws include the Clean Air Act, Clean Water Act (NPDES, SPCC), RCRA, CERCLA, EPCRA and TSCA.

2. Explanation in simple words

It is cheaper and safer not to make pollution in the first place. When you must store oils or chemicals, put a second container around them so a leak cannot reach drains, soil or rivers.

3. Key points to remember

  • P2 hierarchy: source reduction → recycling/reuse → treatment → disposal (last resort).
  • SPCC (40 CFR 112): facilities with > 1,320 gal aggregate aboveground oil storage capacity (containers ≥ 55 gal) or > 42,000 gal underground that could discharge to navigable waters; secondary containment for the largest container plus freeboard for precipitation; plan certified by a PE (or self-certified if qualified); annual training for oil-handling personnel.
  • Secondary containment design: 110% of the largest tank is a common rule; RCRA container storage: 10% of total volume or 100% of the largest container, whichever is greater.
  • Clean Water Act: NPDES permits for point-source discharges; stormwater (SWPPP), construction site erosion and sediment controls (silt fence, inlet protection).
  • Clean Air Act: NAAQS for six criteria pollutants (CO, lead, NO₂, ozone, PM, SO₂); NESHAP for hazardous air pollutants; Title V operating permits; abatement equipment - baghouses (particulates), cyclones, electrostatic precipitators, wet scrubbers (acid gases), thermal oxidizers (VOCs), carbon adsorption.
  • EPCRA (SARA Title III): emergency planning (Section 302 threshold planning quantities of extremely hazardous substances), release notification (304), Tier II inventory reporting (311/312), Toxics Release Inventory (313). CERCLA (Superfund): cleanup of contaminated sites and release reporting of reportable quantities (RQ).
  • Best practices: inventory minimization, closed-loop systems, leak detection and repair, housekeeping, spill kits and drills, environmental aspects/impacts review (ISO 14001).

4. Real work scenario

A trucking terminal stores three 1,000-gallon diesel tanks. Its aggregate capacity (3,000 gal) exceeds 1,320 gal, so it needs an SPCC plan regardless of how much fuel is actually in the tanks. Double-walled tanks, a curbed fueling pad draining to an oil-water separator, and annual spill-response training are added.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • SPCC applicability is based on storage CAPACITY, not the amount on hand.
  • Source reduction beats recycling, treatment and disposal.
  • Match law to topic: EPCRA = community right-to-know; CERCLA = Superfund; RCRA = hazardous waste cradle to grave; TSCA = chemical inventory (PCBs, asbestos, lead).

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 9 more)

Spill containmentBLUEPRINT

Spill containment prevents the uncontrolled release of oil, fuel, chemicals or waste into soil, water or air. It combines primary containment (tanks, drums, pipes), secondary containment (berms, double walls, spill pallets), floor and drain protection, and procedures and training.

  • Spills come from overfilling, equipment failure, corrosion, improper handling and transfer accidents.
  • Primary containment = the container holding the material under normal conditions; must be sound and compatible with contents.
  • Secondary containment = catches a primary failure: concrete/earthen berms, double-walled tanks, spill pallets, portable trays.
  • Floors in spill-prone areas: chemical-resistant, non-slip coating, sloped to sumps; storm drains covered or plugged during transfers.
  • Overfill prevention: high-level alarms, automatic shutoff valves, backup float systems (tank filling is a common spill source).

Exam tip: Containment is a system (design + technology + procedures + training), not just a berm.

SPCC rule (40 CFR 112)

EPA's Spill Prevention, Control and Countermeasure rule requires a written plan at non-transportation facilities that store oil above threshold amounts and could reasonably discharge to navigable waters.

  • Thresholds: more than 1,320 gal aggregate aboveground oil storage (counting containers 55 gal or larger) or more than 42,000 gal completely buried.
  • Plan includes site diagrams, drain maps, containment, inspection checklists, emergency contacts, training records.
  • Certified by a Professional Engineer and reviewed at least every 5 years (qualified facilities with 10,000 gal or less may self-certify).
  • Oil-water separators and secondary containment are typical SPCC controls.

Exam tip: SPCC is about OIL (EPA, water protection); HAZWOPER is about worker protection during response.

Secondary containment sizing (calculation)

Containment volume is sized from the largest container and the total stored volume. The guide states the rule as 110% of the largest container or 10% of total volume, whichever is greater.

  • RCRA container storage (40 CFR 264.175): 10% of total volume or 100% of the largest container, whichever is greater - the guide states 110%.
  • SPCC: hold the largest container plus freeboard for precipitation; 110% of the largest tank is the common design practice for outdoor dikes.
  • Example: four 55-gal drums = 220 gal total; 10% = 22 gal; largest = 55 gal (110% = 60.5 gal) -> size for the larger value (55-60.5 gal).
  • Example: 500-gal diesel tank -> 110% = 550 gal minimum; a 1,100-gal berm complies.
  • Dike volume = L x W x H (ft3) x 7.48 gal/ft3; subtract volume displaced by other tanks inside the dike.

Exam tip: Always compute BOTH values (percent of largest and percent of total) and choose the greater.

Spill response equipment and readiness

Spill kits must be located near potential spill points, matched to the hazard, and fully stocked.

  • Oil-only kits: hydrophobic pads/socks (repel water). Universal kits: water-based and non-aggressive chemicals. Hazmat kits: acids, bases, unknowns.
  • Contents: pads, socks, pillows, neutralizers, gloves/goggles/aprons, disposal bags and labels, emergency contacts.
  • Absorbent must be compatible - oil absorbents may be unsuitable for strong acids.
  • SDS Section 6 (accidental release measures) gives spill guidance.

Exam tip: Wrong absorbent for the chemical (e.g., combustible absorbent on an oxidizer) is a classic distractor.

Spill-related regulatory frameworks

Several overlapping rules govern spill prevention and response.

  • RCRA tank rules (40 CFR 264/265 Subpart J): secondary containment, leak detection, daily inspections with records.
  • SWPPP (40 CFR 122.26): stormwater controls such as berms, drain protection and covers.
  • OSHA HAZWOPER 1910.120: tiered responder training (awareness, operations - defensive, technician - offensive/hands-on, specialist, incident commander).
  • OSHA HazCom 1910.1200: SDS Section 6 and label/SDS training.
  • NFPA 30: storage spacing, containment and drainage for flammable/combustible liquids; ISO 14001 clause 8.2: emergency preparedness including environmental releases.

Exam tip: Operations level = defensive actions from a safe distance; technician level = approach the release to stop it.

Abatement (pollution abatement overview)BLUEPRINT

Pollution abatement captures, treats or reduces pollutants before they reach air, water or soil, ideally at the point of generation. It uses engineering controls plus administrative procedures to meet CAA, CWA, NESHAP and SWPPP requirements.

  • Proactive - controls pollution at the source rather than reacting to effects.
  • Media: air emissions, water discharges, stormwater runoff, soil.
  • Benefits: legal compliance, lower operational risk, worker safety, public image, sustainability metrics.
  • Multistage control is common (e.g., cyclone pre-cleaner then baghouse).

Exam tip: Memory set: Baghouse = dust; Scrubber = acid gas; VRU = VOC; Separator = oil; pH system = acid/base; SWPPP = sediment.

Air regulations: CAA, NAAQS, Title V, NESHAP

The Clean Air Act authorizes EPA to regulate air pollutants from stationary and mobile sources.

  • NAAQS criteria pollutants: PM (PM10, PM2.5), SO2, NOx (NO2), CO, ozone, lead.
  • Title V operating permits (40 CFR 70) required for major sources.
  • New Source Review: BACT for new or modified major sources (PSD areas); LAER in nonattainment areas.
  • NESHAP (40 CFR 63) controls hazardous air pollutants (benzene, formaldehyde, methylene chloride) by industry sector using MACT.
  • Nonattainment area = region not meeting a NAAQS; stricter controls for ozone precursors (VOC, NOx).

Exam tip: BACT = new/modified sources under NSR; MACT = HAPs under NESHAP.

Air pollution control devices

Device selection depends on pollutant type (particulate vs gas), particle size, industry and permit limits.

  • Baghouse (fabric filter): fine PM10/PM2.5, over 99% efficiency; watch bag rupture/bypass.
  • Wet scrubber: acid gases (SO2, HCl, HF) with alkaline liquid; needs mist eliminator; makes wastewater (CWA).
  • Dry scrubber/dry sorbent injection: acid gases with lime or sodium bicarbonate; no wastewater, lower cost, good for arid areas and retrofits; solids caught by baghouse/ESP.
  • Cyclone: coarse particles by centrifugal force, 70-90% for particles over 10 microns; pre-cleaner only.
  • Electrostatic precipitator (ESP): fine/submicron particles, up to 99.9%; affected by moisture and sticky particles.
  • Vapor recovery unit (VRU): VOCs at tank vents and loading; condenser, adsorber or compressor; required under NESHAP for bulk terminals.

Exam tip: Submicron particles -> ESP or baghouse; coarse only -> cyclone; acid gas -> scrubber; VOC -> VRU/carbon/thermal oxidizer.

Water pollution abatement (CWA, NPDES, pretreatment)

The Clean Water Act regulates discharges to waters of the U.S. Direct dischargers need NPDES permits; indirect dischargers to a POTW must meet pretreatment standards.

  • NPDES (40 CFR 122): effluent limits, monitoring, stormwater controls.
  • Pretreatment (40 CFR 403) for industrial users of municipal sewers; effluent guidelines 40 CFR 400-471.
  • Oil-water separator: oil rises and is skimmed, solids settle; sized for flow and oil type; required under SPCC/many NPDES permits.
  • pH neutralization: automated dosing (lime, caustic, acid) to typical discharge range 6.0-9.0.
  • Clarifiers/settling tanks: gravity removal of suspended solids, often with coagulants/flocculants.
  • Activated carbon: adsorbs trace organics, VOCs, chlorine, color; polishing step; monitor breakthrough; spent carbon may be hazardous waste.

Exam tip: Electroplating acidic rinsewater to a city sewer -> pH neutralization + industrial pretreatment permit.

Stormwater controls (SWPPP BMPs)

Stormwater Pollution Prevention Plans under 40 CFR 122.26 use best management practices to keep sediment and pollutants out of runoff.

  • BMPs: silt fences, retention/detention ponds, inlet protection (filter socks, drain baskets), vegetative swales and buffers.
  • Controls must be maintained; clogged filters and damaged fences are ineffective.
  • Visual monitoring and sampling during rain events are often required.
  • Prevent illicit discharges of chemicals or wash water to storm drains.

Soil pollution abatement

Soil abatement mitigates or removes hazardous substances (metals, hydrocarbons, pesticides, solvents, lead paint dust) under CERCLA, RCRA corrective action, TSCA and brownfield programs.

  • Excavation and off-site disposal: fast and definitive but costly; RCRA waste classification and DOT 49 CFR transport.
  • Stabilization/solidification: cement, fly ash or lime binders immobilize metals; does not remove contamination; verify with TCLP (Method 1311).
  • Bioremediation: microbes degrade hydrocarbons; needs oxygen, nutrients, proper pH; months to years (land farming, biopiles).
  • Soil vapor extraction: vacuum removes VOCs from dry permeable soil; off-gas treatment required (CAA); often paired with air sparging.
  • Phytoremediation: plants (sunflowers, willows, mustard) for low-level metals; cheap but slow.

Exam tip: Heavy metals -> stabilization; VOCs in dry soil -> SVE; fuel spills -> bioremediation; urgent/near receptors -> excavation.

Best practices in pollution preventionBLUEPRINT

Best practices combine the pollution prevention hierarchy with administrative controls: plans, training, inspections, compatibility checks and drills.

  • Pollution Prevention Act 1990 hierarchy: source reduction > recycling > treatment > disposal/release.
  • Train spill responders per HAZWOPER with hands-on drills; awareness training for all employees.
  • Weekly inspection of tanks, hoses, valves and containment (best practice); log findings and track corrective actions.
  • Keep drain valves on containment closed and locked; inspect rainwater before release.
  • Integrate into an ISO 14001 EMS for continual improvement.

Exam tip: Source reduction (prevent at origin) beats any end-of-pipe abatement on 'BEST' questions.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 34 Environmental Management, p. 754; Ch. 33 Sustainability

  2. 60.5 gallons

  2. To capture or reduce pollutants before they are released to the environment
  3. To clean up chemical spills after release

  1. Silt fences and sediment traps
  2. HEPA filtration
  3. Baghouse filters
  4. Cyclone separators

5.1-Q6. Which action ranks HIGHEST in the EPA pollution prevention hierarchy?

  1. Treating waste before discharge
  2. Recycling spent solvent off-site
  3. Disposing of waste in a permitted landfill
  4. Changing the process to use a water-based coating that produces no hazardous waste

5.1-Q7. Which air pollution control device is MOST suitable for removing coarse particles (greater than 10 microns), often as a pre-cleaner?

  1. Baghouse filter
  2. Electrostatic precipitator
  3. Cyclone separator

5.1-Q8. Under the Clean Air Act, what type of permit is required for major stationary emission sources?

  1. RCRA Part B permit
  2. Title V operating permit
  3. CERCLA clearance certificate
  4. NPDES permit

  1. Pretreatment
  2. Solidification
  3. Recycling
  4. Filtration

5.1-Q10. Under EPCRA Section 302, what is a 'threshold planning quantity' (TPQ)?

  1. The minimum quantity of any chemical that can be stored without reporting
  2. The quantity of an extremely hazardous substance (EHS) on site that triggers emergency planning notification
  3. The maximum amount of any chemical that may be released without consequence
  4. The quantity of waste a company must dispose of yearly to avoid EPA oversight

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CThe SPCC rule (40 CFR 112, issued under the Clean Water Act) applies to non-transportation facilities with over 1,320 gal aboveground (or 42,000 gal buried) oil capacity that could reach navigable waters.
2A40 CFR 112.8(c)(2): containment for the entire capacity of the largest single container plus freeboard for precipitation (commonly designed as 110%).
3DContainment must hold 10% of total volume or 100% of the largest container, whichever is greater: 10% x (20 x 55 = 1,100) = 110 gal, which exceeds 55 gal. Trap: using only the largest container.
4BAbatement means controlling or reducing emissions and discharges (e.g., scrubbers, filters, treatment) before release; cleanup after release is remediation.
5AStormwater BMPs such as silt fences, sediment basins and inlet protection keep sediment out of runoff. The other choices are air pollution controls.
6DThe Pollution Prevention Act hierarchy is source reduction > recycling > treatment > disposal. Eliminating the waste at the source is best.
7CCyclones use centrifugal force and are efficient and cheap for large particles; baghouses and ESPs are used for fine particulates.
8BTitle V operating permits consolidate all CAA requirements for major sources. NPDES permits cover point-source water discharges under the CWA.
9AIndustrial users must pretreat wastewater to meet POTW and CWA pretreatment standards; pH adjustment is a common pretreatment step.
10BWhen an EHS is present at or above its TPQ, the facility must notify the state and local emergency planning bodies and take part in local emergency planning.
Printed book page 173
Domain 5 · Competency 5.2

Hazardous Materials Management

CSP11 blueprint: Identify procedures used to manage hazardous materials (e.g., GHS classification system, storage and handling, policy, security, hazardous waste storage and disposal)

1. Definition

Hazardous materials management covers the whole life of hazardous chemicals at a site: purchasing approval, hazard classification and communication (GHS/HCS), safe storage and handling, security, and the storage and disposal of hazardous waste under RCRA.

2. Explanation in simple words

Know what chemicals you have, label them clearly, store incompatible ones apart, keep them secure, and when they become waste, follow the RCRA rules from the moment the waste is generated until final disposal.

3. Key points to remember

  • GHS/HCS 2012 (1910.1200, updated 2024): classification into physical, health (and environmental) hazard classes and categories; labels with product identifier, signal word (Danger or Warning), hazard statements, precautionary statements, pictograms, supplier information; 16-section SDS (Sec. 2 hazard ID, Sec. 7 handling and storage, Sec. 8 exposure controls/PPE, Sec. 10 stability and reactivity).
  • Nine HCS pictograms (eight OSHA-required): flame, flame over circle (oxidizer), exploding bomb, gas cylinder, corrosion, skull and crossbones (acute toxicity severe), exclamation mark (irritant, sensitizer, less severe), health hazard (carcinogen, mutagen, reproductive, respiratory sensitizer), environment (not required by OSHA).
  • Category 1 is the most severe in GHS (opposite of NFPA 704, where 4 is most severe).
  • Written HazCom program: chemical inventory list, labeling, SDS access, training (initially and when new hazards are introduced), non-routine tasks, contractor communication.
  • Storage and handling: segregation by compatibility (oxidizers away from flammables, acids away from bases, water-reactives dry), secondary containment, ventilation, flammable cabinets, quantity limits, inspections.
  • Security: chemical security (CFATS), inventory control, locked storage, access lists.
  • RCRA hazardous waste: listed (F - non-specific sources such as spent solvents; K - specific industries; P - acutely hazardous discarded commercial chemicals; U - toxic discarded commercial chemicals) or characteristic (ignitable, corrosive, reactive, toxic - TCLP).
  • Generator categories: VSQG ≤ 100 kg/month; SQG > 100 to < 1,000 kg/month (180 days storage, 270 if > 200 miles); LQG ≥ 1,000 kg/month or > 1 kg acute (90 days). Satellite accumulation up to 55 gal (1 qt acute). Containers closed, labeled 'Hazardous Waste' with hazard indication and accumulation start date; weekly inspections; uniform hazardous waste manifest; LQG annual training.

4. Real work scenario

An inspector finds unlabeled spray bottles of a solvent cleaner and containers of waste solvent with no dates. The safety professional labels the bottles with GHS workplace labels, adds the solvent to the chemical inventory, dates the waste containers with the accumulation start date, moves them to the central accumulation area, and schedules weekly inspections.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • SDS Section 8 = PPE and exposure limits; Section 2 = hazard identification.
  • GHS Category 1 = most hazardous.
  • Generator category thresholds and accumulation times are high-yield numbers.
  • Flame over circle = oxidizer.

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 8 more)

GHS classification systemBLUEPRINT

The UN Globally Harmonized System standardizes chemical classification, labels and SDSs. OSHA adopted it in the Hazard Communication Standard, 29 CFR 1910.1200 (2012, updated 2024).

  • Three hazard groups: physical, health, environmental (OSHA does not enforce environmental classes).
  • Each hazard class is split into categories; Category 1 is the most severe (Flammable Liquid Cat 1 is worse than Cat 3).
  • Physical: flammables, gases under pressure, explosives, oxidizers, pyrophorics, self-heating.
  • Health: acute toxicity, skin corrosion, respiratory sensitization, carcinogenicity, reproductive toxicity, STOT.
  • EPA uses hazard categories for EPCRA Tier II; DOT uses its own 9 classes; NFPA 704 is a separate 0-4 system (4 = most severe).

Exam tip: GHS: Category 1 = MOST hazardous. NFPA 704: 4 = MOST hazardous. The numbers run opposite.

GHS labels: signal words, pictograms and statements

A shipped-container label has six elements: product identifier, signal word, pictogram(s), hazard statements, precautionary statements and supplier information.

  • Only two signal words: Danger (severe) and Warning (less severe); only one per label - Danger overrides.
  • Nine pictograms (red diamond border); OSHA requires eight - the environment pictogram is not mandatory in U.S. workplaces.
  • Flame = flammables; flame over circle = oxidizers; exploding bomb = explosives/self-reactives; gas cylinder = gases under pressure; corrosion = skin burns/metal corrosion; skull and crossbones = acute toxicity (fatal/toxic); health hazard = carcinogen, mutagen, respiratory sensitizer; exclamation mark = irritant, skin sensitizer, narcotic effects.
  • Hazard statements are standardized and cannot be altered (e.g., 'May cause cancer').
  • Precautionary statements give handling, storage, PPE and emergency instructions.

Exam tip: Respiratory sensitizer = health hazard pictogram; skin sensitizer = exclamation mark.

Safety Data Sheets (16 sections)

The SDS replaced the MSDS and must follow a fixed 16-section format. Employers must keep SDSs readily accessible to workers on every shift and update them when new hazard information arrives.

  • 1 Identification, 2 Hazard identification, 3 Composition, 4 First aid, 5 Fire-fighting, 6 Accidental release, 7 Handling and storage, 8 Exposure controls/PPE.
  • 9 Physical/chemical properties, 10 Stability and reactivity, 11 Toxicological information.
  • 12 Ecological, 13 Disposal, 14 Transport, 15 Regulatory - included for GHS consistency but not enforced by OSHA.
  • 16 Other information, including date of last revision.
  • Electronic access is acceptable if there are no barriers to immediate access.

Exam tip: Spill guidance = Section 6; PPE and exposure limits = Section 8; incompatibles = Section 10.

Storage of hazardous materialsBLUEPRINT

Storage must prevent fires, toxic releases and reactions by using proper cabinets/rooms, segregation, ventilation and containment, under OSHA 1910.106, NFPA 30/400/45 and RCRA.

  • Flammable cabinets (NFPA 30/OSHA): max 60 gal Class I/II (120 gal Class III) per cabinet; labeled 'FLAMMABLE - KEEP FIRE AWAY'; doors closed; away from ignition sources.
  • Corrosives: chemically resistant (polyethylene or epoxy-coated) cabinets with trays; acids and bases separate.
  • Toxics/poisons: locked, access-restricted, inventoried.
  • Compressed gas cylinders: upright, capped, secured; full and empty separated; oxygen and fuel gas separated by 20 ft or a 5-ft-high, 1/2-hour fire-rated wall (1910.253).
  • Inside storage rooms for flammables: ventilation of at least six air changes per hour; explosion-proof electrical where required.

Exam tip: Oxygen cylinders stored next to acetylene with no 20 ft / 5 ft wall separation is a classic violation.

Segregation of incompatible materials

Incompatible chemicals must be physically separated so a leak cannot cause fire, explosion or toxic gas.

  • Acids away from bases; oxidizers away from flammables/combustibles.
  • Cyanides never near acids (hydrogen cyanide gas); hypochlorite away from acids (chlorine gas).
  • Water-reactives isolated from sinks, sprinklers and humid areas.
  • Segregate with separate cabinets, distance or fire-rated barriers - not just different shelves of one cabinet.
  • Check SDS Section 10 and compatibility charts.

Handling and transfer of hazardous materialsBLUEPRINT

Transfers are high-risk; controls focus on static, splashes and container breakage.

  • Bond and ground flammable liquid transfers to prevent static ignition (NFPA 77).
  • Use closed dispensing systems (pumps, nozzles) instead of open pouring.
  • Never carry breakable containers by hand - use carriers, spill trays and carts.
  • Select PPE from the SDS; eyewash/shower within 10 seconds travel (ANSI Z358.1) where corrosives are handled.
  • Containers kept closed, compatible and in good condition; secondary containers labeled.

Exam tip: Bonding equalizes potential between containers; grounding drains charge to earth - both are needed for Class I transfers.

Security of hazardous materialsBLUEPRINT

Hazmat security protects against intentional misuse - theft, sabotage or terrorism - through access control, physical barriers, information protection and emergency integration.

  • Access control: authorized trained staff only, key cards/PINs/biometrics, sign-in logs, restricted-area signage.
  • Physical: locked cabinets/cages, steel doors, fencing, CCTV, motion detectors, lighting, intrusion alarms.
  • Bulk tanks: locked hatches and remote fill points, screened vents, fenced dikes, tamper-evident seals.
  • Protect inventory data: limit database access, secure Tier II reports and chemical maps, no public posting of tank contents.
  • Plan for theft, sabotage and suspicious items; drill with police, fire and LEPC.

Exam tip: Insider threat and missing inventory scenarios: first secure/control access and investigate, then report.

CFATS (DHS Chemical Facility Anti-Terrorism Standards)

CFATS covered facilities holding screening threshold quantities of Chemicals of Interest (e.g., ammonium nitrate, chlorine, hydrogen peroxide).

  • Top-Screen survey submitted to DHS; high-risk facilities assigned Tiers 1-4 (Tier 1 = highest risk).
  • Tiered facilities implement a Site Security Plan: perimeter security, personnel vetting, cybersecurity, incident response coordination.
  • Note: CFATS statutory authority lapsed in July 2023; verify current status. EPA RMP and DOT security plans still apply.
  • DOT security plans (49 CFR 172 Subpart I) apply to shippers/carriers of certain hazmat quantities.

Hazardous materials policy and program

A written, management-approved hazmat program defines how chemicals are acquired, stored, used and disposed of, integrating HazCom, EPA emergency planning and NFPA codes.

  • Chemical inventory: names, quantities, container sizes, locations, linked SDSs; supports EPCRA Tier II (40 CFR 370) and emergency planning.
  • Policy elements: procurement controls, labeling, handling/PPE, spill and first aid, storage assignments, inspections.
  • SOPs for receiving, transfers, disposal/decontamination, equipment cleanup.
  • HazCom training at initial assignment and whenever a new hazard is introduced; document it.
  • Inspections (weekly/monthly) for container condition, labels, containment, expired/unknown materials. Note: the guide cites 3-year retention under 1910.1020, but exposure records under 1910.1020 must be kept 30 years.

Exam tip: 'When is retraining required?' - when a NEW hazard is introduced, not when a new product with the same hazards arrives.

DOT hazard classes (49 CFR 171-180)

DOT's Hazardous Materials Regulations govern hazmat in transport (loading, transit, unloading) using nine hazard classes that drive packaging, labels and placards.

  • 1 Explosives; 2 Gases (2.1 flammable, 2.2 non-flammable non-toxic, 2.3 toxic).
  • 3 Flammable liquids (flash point not over 60 C / 140 F); 4 Flammable solids (4.1, 4.2 spontaneously combustible, 4.3 dangerous when wet).
  • 5 Oxidizers (5.1) and organic peroxides (5.2); 6 Toxic (6.1) and infectious (6.2).
  • 7 Radioactive; 8 Corrosives; 9 Miscellaneous (lithium batteries, dry ice, magnetized materials).
  • Hazmat employees need DOT training (49 CFR 172.704) at least every 3 years.

Exam tip: GHS governs the workplace label; DOT governs the transport label/placard - they can legitimately differ on the same drum.

Hazardous wasteBLUEPRINT

Once a hazardous material is no longer usable and is discarded, it becomes waste governed cradle-to-grave by RCRA Subtitle C (40 CFR 260-273), with DOT for transport and HAZWOPER for workers.

  • Hazardous materials are regulated in use; hazardous waste is regulated after use or disposal.
  • Store on impervious surfaces with secondary containment, in central or satellite accumulation areas.
  • Containers: compatible, closed, undamaged, labeled 'Hazardous Waste', hazards indicated, accumulation start date (central areas).
  • Inspect container storage areas weekly; keep spill kits, PPE and emergency info nearby.
  • States (e.g., California DTSC) may be more stringent than federal RCRA.

Hazardous waste identification

Generators must determine whether a waste is hazardous by listing or by characteristic, using testing or process knowledge.

  • Ignitability D001: liquid flash point below 140 F (60 C).
  • Corrosivity D002: aqueous pH 2 or less, or 12.5 or more (or corrodes steel).
  • Reactivity D003: unstable, water-reactive, explosive, generates toxic gas.
  • Toxicity D004-D043: TCLP extract exceeds limits (e.g., lead, benzene, mercury).
  • Listed wastes: F (non-specific sources, e.g., F003 spent solvents), K (specific industries), P (acutely hazardous discarded chemicals), U (toxic discarded chemicals).

Exam tip: pH 2 or less OR 12.5 or more = corrosive (D002). pH 12 is NOT a characteristic hazardous waste.

Generator categories, manifests and TSDFs

Generator status depends on monthly quantity and sets accumulation time and paperwork.

  • VSQG: 100 kg/month or less (and 1 kg or less acute); no federal time limit, but 1,000 kg on-site cap.
  • SQG: more than 100 and less than 1,000 kg/month; 180 days (270 if TSDF is over 200 miles); 6,000 kg cap.
  • LQG: 1,000 kg/month or more (or over 1 kg acute); 90 days; contingency plan and annual training.
  • Ship to a permitted TSDF via registered transporter (EPA ID) with the Uniform Hazardous Waste Manifest (EPA Form 8700-22) / e-Manifest; keep copies 3 years.
  • Example: 1,200 kg/month spent solvent -> LQG -> 90-day limit.

Exam tip: The guide's table lists SQG as 100-1,000 kg; federal rule is more than 100 and less than 1,000 kg; 1,000 kg exactly is LQG.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 4 Regulations (HazCom/GHS comparison), p. 55; Ch. 27 Hazardous Materials Management, p. 616

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

5.2-Q1. A facility generates 600 kg of hazardous waste per month (no acute hazardous waste). What is its RCRA generator category?

  1. Very Small Quantity Generator
  2. Small Quantity Generator
  3. Large Quantity Generator
  4. Treatment, Storage and Disposal Facility

5.2-Q2. A supplier's SDS for a new solvent shows a flame pictogram and the hazard statement 'Flammable liquid, Category 2.' What is your FIRST action before allowing the material on site?

  1. Verify that proper flammable storage and ventilation controls are available
  2. Require the supplier to train all employees in SDS reading
  3. Approve the purchase since the supplier provided an SDS
  4. Have employees store it in the nearest open area

5.2-Q3. An international project site receives a drum of toluene marked only with US DOT hazard labels. A European subcontractor refuses to use it, citing labeling noncompliance. What is the MOST appropriate corrective action?

  1. Repackage the drum into smaller unlabeled containers
  2. Explain that DOT labels are sufficient for workplace use
  3. Provide an SDS only if a worker asks for it
  4. Apply a GHS label with the product identifier, pictograms, signal word, and hazard and precautionary statements

5.2-Q4. Under GHS, which elements are required on a shipped container label?

  1. Chemical formula, warnings, and pictograms only
  2. pH, boiling point, flash point, and pictograms
  3. Product identifier, pictogram, signal word, hazard statement, precautionary statement, and supplier information
  4. SDS number, pictograms, and training date

5.2-Q5. During an inspection, you find a drum of acid cleaner (corrosive pictogram) stored next to a pallet of bleach. What is your MOST urgent concern?

  1. The chemicals could react to release toxic chlorine gas
  2. The drum label is missing the manufacturer's phone number
  3. The pictogram is somewhat faded
  4. The bleach lacks a transport placard

5.2-Q6. An SDS for a parts-cleaning solvent lists: 'Keep away from heat/sparks/open flame. Use explosion-proof equipment.' In which SDS section are these precautionary statements found?

  1. Section 2 - Hazard Identification
  2. Section 4 - First-Aid Measures
  3. Section 7 - Handling and Storage
  4. Section 10 - Stability and Reactivity

5.2-Q7. A safety professional is reviewing an SDS before training employees. Which section contains personal protective equipment recommendations?

  1. Section 2 - Hazard Identification
  2. Section 4 - First-Aid Measures
  3. Section 8 - Exposure Controls/Personal Protection
  4. Section 10 - Stability and Reactivity

5.2-Q8. Which area may accumulate up to 55 gallons of non-acute hazardous waste at or near the point of generation without the full central storage requirements?

  1. Central Accumulation Area (CAA)
  2. Universal Waste Area
  3. Hazardous Storage Room
  4. Satellite Accumulation Area (SAA)

5.2-Q9. During a spill response, a new worker reaches for a container labeled with a flame-over-circle pictogram. What should the supervisor remind the worker?

  1. This symbol means the chemical is flammable
  2. This symbol identifies an oxidizer, which can cause or intensify a fire
  3. This symbol identifies explosive chemicals
  4. This symbol warns of corrosion of skin and metals

5.2-Q10. A facility stores ammonium nitrate and other theft-attractive hazardous materials. Which measure BEST addresses hazardous materials security?

  1. Store the materials near the loading dock for faster shipping
  2. Restrict access to authorized personnel, conduct regular inventory reconciliation, and report unexplained losses
  3. Rely on GHS labels to deter theft
  4. Keep the storage area unlocked so emergency responders can enter

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BVSQG: 100 kg/month or less; SQG: more than 100 and less than 1,000 kg/month; LQG: 1,000 kg/month or more (or more than 1 kg acute). 600 kg falls in the SQG range.
2APre-purchase review should confirm that storage (cabinets, quantity limits), ventilation and ignition controls exist before the material arrives.
3DDOT labels communicate transport hazards; workplace use requires GHS/HazCom labeling and an accessible SDS.
4CHazCom 1910.1200(f) requires product identifier, signal word, hazard statements, pictograms, precautionary statements, and the supplier's name, address and phone.
5AAcids mixed with sodium hypochlorite liberate chlorine gas; incompatible materials must be segregated in storage (SDS Sections 7 and 10).
6APrecautionary statements are label elements and are listed in Section 2 along with signal word, hazard statements and pictograms. Section 7 gives detailed handling practices.
7CSection 8 lists exposure limits (PEL, TLV), engineering controls and PPE.
8DSAAs at or near the point of generation, under operator control, may hold up to 55 gal (or 1 qt acute); containers must be closed and marked 'Hazardous Waste' with the hazards indicated.
9BFlame over circle = oxidizer; plain flame = flammable; exploding bomb = explosive; corrosion = skin and metal damage. Keep oxidizers away from combustibles.
10BHazmat security combines access control, inventory accountability and reporting of losses so diversion is prevented or detected quickly. Labels communicate hazards but do not secure them.

Domain 5 · Competency 5.3

Waste Management

CSP11 blueprint: Identify procedures used to manage waste (e.g., universal, recycling, spill clean-up, labeling, remediation)

1. Definition

Waste management covers how all wastes are characterized, segregated, labeled, stored, recycled, transported and disposed of, how spills are cleaned up, and how contaminated sites are remediated.

2. Explanation in simple words

Sort waste correctly, label it, recycle what you can, clean up spills safely, and fix contaminated soil and water using the right technology.

3. Key points to remember

  • Universal waste (40 CFR 273): batteries, pesticides (recalled), mercury-containing equipment, lamps (and aerosol cans federally since 2019). Simplified rules: label 'Universal Waste - [type]', accumulate up to 1 year, train handlers, send to a destination facility. Used oil is managed under 40 CFR 279, not as universal waste.
  • Other categories: non-hazardous solid waste (municipal landfill), medical/regulated waste (state rules, sharps containers), radioactive waste (NRC; decay-in-storage for short half-lives), e-waste.
  • Recycling and reuse: solvent reclamation, scrap metal, pallets; legitimate recycling vs 'sham recycling'.
  • Spill cleanup: assess (SDS, size, hazards) → protect people (evacuate if needed, PPE) → stop the source if safe → contain (dikes, absorbents, drain covers) → clean up and decontaminate → dispose of cleanup materials as waste → report if above a reportable quantity → review. Incidental vs emergency spills (HAZWOPER).
  • Labeling: hazardous waste labels ('Hazardous Waste', hazard indication, start date), DOT labels for transport, universal waste labels.
  • Remediation technologies: pump-and-treat (groundwater), soil vapor extraction (VOCs in unsaturated soil), air sparging, bioremediation, phytoremediation, excavation and disposal, capping/containment (does not remove contamination), in-situ chemical oxidation, monitored natural attenuation.
  • CERCLA: potentially responsible parties (strict, joint and several liability), National Priorities List; brownfield redevelopment; Phase I/II environmental site assessments.

4. Real work scenario

A facility generates spent fluorescent lamps, used batteries, used oil and waste paint. The safety professional sets up universal-waste containers for the lamps and batteries (labeled, dated, kept less than one year), sends used oil to a used-oil recycler under Part 279, and characterizes the paint waste as ignitable hazardous waste.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Used oil is NOT universal waste - a common 'which is NOT' trap.
  • Capping limits migration but does not remove contaminants.
  • Universal waste accumulation limit: one year.

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 3 more)

Waste management regulatory overview

Waste management covers identifying, segregating, labeling, storing and disposing of all waste streams (hazardous and non-hazardous) from operations, spills, expired chemicals and maintenance.

  • RCRA 40 CFR 260-273: definitions, generator rules, labeling, storage, manifests.
  • DOT 49 CFR 171-180: packaging, marking and transport of hazardous waste.
  • OSHA HAZWOPER 1910.120: workers handling hazardous waste or cleaning up releases.
  • State programs may be stricter than federal rules.
  • Non-hazardous solid waste falls under RCRA Subtitle D.

Universal wasteBLUEPRINT

Universal waste (40 CFR 273) is a streamlined category for common, lower-risk hazardous wastes so they are easier to collect and recycle.

  • Federal types: batteries, recalled/unused pesticides, mercury-containing equipment (thermostats, thermometers), lamps (fluorescent, HID), aerosol cans (added 2019); some states add electronics.
  • Label 'Universal Waste - [type]' (or 'Waste/Used [type]') and show accumulation start date.
  • Accumulation limit: 1 year.
  • Containers closed, intact and compatible; employees trained on handling and emergency procedures.
  • Universal waste does not count toward generator status.

Exam tip: Fluorescent lamps in a fiber drum labeled 'Universal Waste - Lamps' with a start date = compliant. 'Hazardous Waste' label is not required.

Recycling of industrial wasteBLUEPRINT

Recycling reduces disposal cost and liability while supporting sustainability, provided materials are kept clean and recyclers are legitimate.

  • Common streams: used oil (40 CFR 279), scrap metal, distillable solvents, cardboard and plastic drums.
  • Label containers (e.g., 'Used Oil'), avoid contamination, verify recycler licensing and keep documentation proving recycling.
  • Closed-loop recycling: material reclaimed and returned to the same process may be excluded from RCRA.
  • Used oil mixed with hazardous waste can become hazardous waste - keep streams separate.

Exam tip: Documentation proving material was actually recycled (not sham recycling) is the CSP's protection.

Spill cleanupBLUEPRINT

Spills must be handled quickly and safely using a formal response plan. HAZWOPER applies when cleanup involves an uncontrolled release of a hazardous substance.

  • Sequence: 1 recognize the material (SDS/label), 2 alert and evacuate (EAP), 3 select PPE, 4 contain (absorbents, booms, dikes), 5 clean and decontaminate/neutralize, 6 dispose of debris (often as hazardous waste), 7 document and report.
  • Incidental spills handled by trained area employees are not HAZWOPER emergency response.
  • Oil spill planning: SPCC (40 CFR 112).
  • Cleanup debris from a hazardous chemical spill is generally hazardous waste.

Exam tip: FIRST step questions: identify the material and protect people before containment.

Release reporting (CERCLA and EPCRA)

Releases of hazardous substances at or above a Reportable Quantity must be reported to the National Response Center.

  • CERCLA RQ release: notify the NRC immediately upon knowledge (the guide says within 24 hours).
  • The RQ is measured as the amount released within any 24-hour period.
  • EPCRA Section 304 also requires notice to state and local emergency planning bodies (SERC/LEPC).
  • Oil discharges producing a sheen on water are reportable under the CWA.
  • Document the release and follow-up written reports as required.

LabelingBLUEPRINT

Labeling is one of the most-cited EPA/OSHA areas; containers must be labeled, compatible and closed at all times.

  • Hazardous waste: 'Hazardous Waste', indication of hazards, EPA waste codes (e.g., D001) before shipment, accumulation start date, clear contents.
  • Universal waste: type and accumulation start date.
  • Used oil: 'Used Oil'.
  • Shipping: DOT marking, labels and placards plus the manifest.

Satellite accumulation areas

An SAA lets generators accumulate waste at or near the point of generation under reduced requirements.

  • Up to 55 gal of hazardous waste (or 1 qt liquid / 1 kg solid acute) per SAA.
  • Must be under control of the operator; containers closed, labeled 'Hazardous Waste' with hazard indication.
  • Once the limit is exceeded, excess moves to the central area within 3 consecutive days and is dated.
  • Central accumulation area clock (90/180 days) starts when waste enters it.

RemediationBLUEPRINT

Remediation cleans up contaminated soil, water or structures under CERCLA (Superfund) or RCRA corrective action.

  • Techniques: excavation, solidification/stabilization, bioremediation, soil vapor extraction, pump and treat (groundwater).
  • Phase I ESA: records review, site visit, interviews (no sampling; ASTM E1527).
  • Phase II ESA: sampling and analysis to confirm contamination.
  • Brownfield programs support redevelopment of contaminated land.
  • CERCLA liability is strict, joint and several, and retroactive for potentially responsible parties.

Exam tip: Phase I = no sampling; Phase II = sampling.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 34 Environmental Management, p. 754; Ch. 27 Hazardous Materials Management

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

5.3-Q1. A consultant proposes installing a cap over contaminated soil on company property. What is a key LIMITATION of capping as a remediation method?

  1. A cap increases water infiltration and speeds plume movement
  2. A cap makes the property unattractive
  3. A cap does not remove the contamination; it only minimizes exposure and migration
  4. A cap prevents any reuse of the property

5.3-Q2. Under 40 CFR 279, containers and tanks of used oil stored for recycling must be labeled:

  1. 'Used Oil'
  2. 'Hazardous Waste - Oil'
  3. 'Universal Waste - Oil'
  4. 'Recyclable Waste Only'

5.3-Q3. What marking is required on a container of universal waste lamps?

  1. 'Hazardous Waste - Lamps' with an EPA waste code
  2. 'Used Oil for Recycling'
  3. 'Flammable - Keep Fire Away'
  4. 'Universal Waste - Lamp(s)' (or 'Waste Lamps'/'Used Lamps'), with the accumulation start date demonstrated

5.3-Q4. A worker must segregate four waste streams into 55-gallon drums: wastewater, lithium metal, toluene, and halogenated hydrocarbons. What is the GREATEST concern?

  1. Mixing lithium metal and toluene
  2. Mixing lithium metal and water
  3. Mixing halogenated hydrocarbons and toluene
  4. Mixing wastewater and toluene

5.3-Q5. Volatile organic compounds are found in unsaturated (vadose zone) soil above the water table. Which remediation technology is MOST suitable?

  1. Pump and treat
  2. Phytoremediation of groundwater
  3. Capping only
  4. Soil vapor extraction

5.3-Q6. While inspecting a small facility, you find several containers labeled 'corrosive waste' in the central accumulation area with no dates. What is your BEST course of action?

  1. Notify the facility manager that the containers are noncompliant and must be marked with the accumulation start date
  2. Remove the containers immediately without telling anyone
  3. Have workers put on PPE and move the waste elsewhere
  4. Call the fire department to dispose of the waste

5.3-Q7. How long may a universal waste handler accumulate universal waste (e.g., spent lamps, batteries) on site?

  1. 90 days
  2. 180 days
  3. 1 year from the date it became a waste
  4. Indefinitely

5.3-Q8. Which action represents the LOWEST (least preferred) level of the waste management hierarchy?

  1. Redesigning a process to eliminate hazardous chemical use
  2. Disposing of hazardous waste in a permitted landfill
  3. Reusing scrap material in the same process
  4. Sending waste solvent off-site for recycling

5.3-Q9. Which characteristic identifies a waste as EPA hazardous waste code D001?

  1. Ignitability (e.g., liquid with flash point below 140 F / 60 C)
  2. Corrosivity (pH 2 or less, or 12.5 or more)
  3. Reactivity (unstable or explosive)
  4. Toxicity (TCLP metals such as lead or mercury)

5.3-Q10. Employees clean up a spill of a listed hazardous solvent using absorbent pads. How should the used absorbent be managed?

  1. In the regular trash once it has dried
  2. By rinsing it in a floor drain and reusing the pads
  3. As universal waste, since it came from a spill
  4. As hazardous waste, properly containerized, labeled, and disposed of through a permitted facility

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CCapping is containment: it reduces infiltration and direct contact but leaves contaminants in place, requiring long-term monitoring and maintenance.
2AUsed oil managed for recycling falls under the used oil standards, not hazardous waste rules; containers must be labeled with the words 'Used Oil'.
3D40 CFR 273 requires the universal waste marking and a way to show how long it has been accumulated (one-year limit).
4BLithium is a water-reactive metal that releases flammable hydrogen and heat on contact with water; waste segregation must keep reactives apart.
5DSVE applies a vacuum to the unsaturated zone to remove volatile contaminants. Pump and treat addresses groundwater, and capping does not remove contaminants.
6AGenerators must mark containers 'Hazardous Waste', indicate the hazards, and record the accumulation start date to track time limits (40 CFR 262.16/262.17).
7CUniversal waste may be accumulated up to one year (longer only if needed to collect quantities for proper recovery). 90 and 180 days are LQG and SQG hazardous waste limits.
8BHierarchy: source reduction > reuse/recycling > treatment > disposal. Landfill disposal is the last resort.
9AD001 = ignitable, D002 = corrosive, D003 = reactive, D004-D043 = toxicity characteristic.
10DSpill cleanup materials contaminated with a listed hazardous waste carry that listing (contained-in principle) and must be managed as hazardous waste. Absorbents are not a universal waste category.
Printed book page 185
Domain 5 · Competency 5.4

Sustainability

CSP11 blueprint: Determine sustainability principles and practices (e.g., supply chain; reduce, reuse, recycle)

1. Definition

Sustainability means meeting the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs (Brundtland, 1987). In business it is often framed as the triple bottom line - people, planet, profit - and includes supply-chain responsibility, resource efficiency and life-cycle thinking.

2. Explanation in simple words

Use less, reuse what you can, recycle the rest, and choose suppliers who also work safely and responsibly. Sustainability and safety support each other because waste, inefficiency and unsafe work all cost money and harm people.

3. Key points to remember

  • Waste hierarchy: refuse/reduce → reuse → recycle → recover (energy) → dispose.
  • Life-cycle assessment (ISO 14040/44): impacts from raw material extraction through manufacturing, use and end of life ('cradle to grave' or 'cradle to cradle').
  • Supply chain: supplier codes of conduct, audits, conflict minerals, responsible sourcing, diversified suppliers to reduce disruption risk, green procurement (recycled content, low-VOC materials, energy-efficient equipment).
  • Green building: LEED (Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design) - energy, water, materials, indoor environmental quality.
  • Sustainability reporting: GRI, SASB, integrated reporting; safety metrics (fatalities, TRIR) appear in sustainability reports.
  • Links to safety: substitution of less hazardous chemicals reduces both exposure and hazardous waste; energy efficiency reduces emissions; worker well-being is the 'social' pillar.

4. Real work scenario

A construction company changes its purchasing policy to require recycled concrete aggregate, low-VOC paints and LED lighting. The safety professional notes that low-VOC paint also reduces worker solvent exposure, and the switch is reported in both the safety and the sustainability report.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Source reduction is the best sustainability answer.
  • LEED = Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design.
  • Diversifying suppliers = supply-chain risk reduction.

6. Every blueprint point (4 blueprint items + 8 more)

Sustainability frameworks

Sustainability means operations that are environmentally sound, socially responsible and economically viable across the product life cycle and supply chain.

  • ISO 14001: EMS with legal compliance, continual improvement and a life-cycle perspective.
  • EPA: CAA, CWA, RCRA and the Pollution Prevention Act (source reduction first), green chemistry, LCA support.
  • OSHA: green jobs and renewable-technology hazards (solar, battery storage), IAQ, ergonomics.
  • Triple bottom line: people, planet, profit.

UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

The 17 UN SDGs are non-binding global goals; several align directly with EHS work.

  • Goal 3 Good health and well-being (injury/illness prevention).
  • Goal 6 Clean water and sanitation; Goal 7 Affordable clean energy.
  • Goal 9 Resilient infrastructure; Goal 12 Responsible consumption and production; Goal 13 Climate action.
  • Not legally binding - a voluntary vision.

Supply chain sustainabilityBLUEPRINT

Sustainable supply chain management extends EHS expectations upstream and downstream to suppliers, contractors and logistics partners.

  • Supplier audits cover environmental compliance (ISO 14001), labor practices (ILO, SA8000), resource efficiency and hazmat handling.
  • Conflict minerals (3TG: tin, tantalum, tungsten, gold) - Dodd-Frank Section 1502.
  • SA8000: social accountability standard - health and safety, working hours, compensation, freedom of association.
  • Integrate procurement into ISO 14001 clause 8.1 (operational planning and control).
  • Supply chain emissions = Scope 3.

Exam tip: Auditing a supplier's labor and safety conditions in a developing region -> SA8000.

Green procurement and responsible sourcing

Green procurement adds environmental and social criteria to purchasing, not just cost and availability.

  • Specify recycled content, low-VOC/non-toxic materials, minimal/reusable packaging.
  • Prefer vendors with ISO 14001 or CDP ratings and low-carbon logistics.
  • EPA Environmentally Preferable Purchasing (EPP) is a model program.
  • Example: solvents in reusable stainless drums cut hazardous waste about 40%.

ReduceBLUEPRINT

Reduce (source reduction) eliminates waste and pollution before it is created and is the most effective of the 3Rs.

  • Strategies: lean process design, energy audits, material substitution (water-based for solvent), Design for Environment.
  • Matches the top of the hierarchy of controls (eliminate/substitute).
  • Pollution Prevention Act: source reduction is the preferred strategy.

Exam tip: Upstream redesign that removes a hazardous input = 'Reduce'.

ReuseBLUEPRINT

Reuse keeps products and materials in service without significant reprocessing, cutting raw material demand and disposal volume.

  • Examples: reusable totes, refillable chemical drums, launderable rags/absorbents, upcycled scrap, internal asset recovery.
  • Safety check: reused containers must be clean, compatible and not degraded (especially after corrosives/flammables).
  • Example: switching to reusable PPE totes cut plastic waste 60% and procurement cost 30%.

RecycleBLUEPRINT

Recycling turns waste into new material; it ranks below reduce and reuse but is vital when those are not practical.

  • Examples: cardboard baling, e-waste management, metal recovery, closed-loop recycling.
  • Can lower hazardous waste quantities and generator status (e.g., solvent recycling).
  • Hierarchy: Reduce > Reuse > Recycle > Treat/energy recovery > Dispose.

Exam tip: If a question asks which 3R is BEST, choose Reduce.

Life cycle assessment (LCA)

LCA quantifies environmental impacts across every stage of a product's life, cradle-to-grave or cradle-to-cradle, per ISO 14040/14044.

  • Stages: raw material extraction, manufacturing, distribution/transport, use, end of life.
  • ISO 14040/14044 phases: goal and scope, inventory analysis (LCI), impact assessment (LCIA), interpretation.
  • Tools: SimaPro, GaBi, OpenLCA, EPA Sustainable Materials Management tools.
  • Differs from a waste audit or carbon footprint - it covers all impacts across all stages.

Life cycle costing and total cost of ownership

Life cycle costing (LCC) weighs purchase price against long-term environmental, social and operational costs.

  • Include disposal fees, emissions penalties, water charges, reputational and supply-continuity risk.
  • TCO can justify paying more for a durable, certified product to avoid fines, failures and injuries.
  • LCA measures environmental impact; LCC measures money.

Sustainable design and green engineering

Design for Environment (DfE) and green engineering build environmental and safety considerations into the earliest design stage.

  • Aligns with the EPA P2 hierarchy, ISO 14001 clause 6.1.2 (environmental aspects) and Prevention through Design (ANSI/ASSP Z590.3; the guide cites Z10).
  • DfE: fewer raw materials, easy disassembly and recycling, low energy/water use, less waste.
  • Energy-efficient equipment: VFDs, compressed-air leak repair, LED lighting with motion sensors (also improves visibility).
  • Modular equipment and non-toxic paints/adhesives reduce IAQ problems and hazardous waste.

Safer chemical substitution

Substitution improves worker safety and environmental performance at the same time when evaluated holistically.

  • Use low-VOC solvents/coatings; lower GHS hazard categories (remove sensitizers and carcinogens).
  • Avoid substances restricted under TSCA or EU REACH.
  • Tools: EPA Safer Chemical Ingredients List (SCIL), GreenScreen.
  • Check new waste profile, process compatibility and PPE needs before switching.

Exam tip: A substitute that introduces a new, worse hazard is not a valid improvement.

Sustainability metrics and KPIs

Sustainability progress is tracked with environmental, economic and social indicators and benchmarked against recognized programs.

  • Environmental: water per unit, GHG (CO2e), hazardous waste per product.
  • Economic: life-cycle cost, return on sustainability investment, resource productivity.
  • Social: injury rates, engagement in green initiatives, fair labor certifications.
  • Benchmarking tools: ENERGY STAR, EPA WasteWise, GRI.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 33 Sustainability and Sustainability Principles, p. 743

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

5.4-Q1. Which is the MOST effective step in the 'Reduce-Reuse-Recycle' hierarchy?

  1. Reuse
  2. Recycle
  3. Reduce
  4. Dispose

5.4-Q2. Which sustainability practice manages suppliers and vendors based on their ethical, safety, and environmental performance?

  1. Sustainable supply chain management
  2. Energy audits
  3. Waste minimization
  4. Pollution abatement

5.4-Q3. A construction company changes its purchasing policy to require recycled concrete aggregate, energy-efficient lighting, and low-VOC materials on all projects. Which sustainability principle does this BEST represent?

  1. Waste isolation
  2. Pollution control after production
  3. Compliance with local code minimums only
  4. Source reduction and sustainable material selection

5.4-Q4. A company replaces solvent-based paints with water-based coatings to lower VOC emissions. Which sustainability principle does this demonstrate?

  1. Reuse
  2. Source reduction (Reduce)
  3. Recycling
  4. Waste diversion

5.4-Q5. What is the primary focus of LEED certification?

  1. Sustainable building performance, such as energy efficiency and water conservation
  2. Building aesthetics and architectural style
  3. Minimizing short-term construction costs
  4. Urban zoning and development approvals

5.4-Q6. A Life-Cycle Assessment (LCA) evaluates:

  1. Worker performance over a career
  2. Product pricing over its sales life
  3. Regulatory compliance at a single facility
  4. Environmental impacts of a product from raw material extraction through disposal

5.4-Q7. A manufacturer replaces single-use cardboard shipping boxes with durable plastic totes that suppliers return for refilling. Which principle does this represent?

  1. Recycle
  2. Reuse
  3. Source reduction through product redesign
  4. Energy recovery

5.4-Q8. A company finds that most of its environmental footprint comes from raw materials made by overseas suppliers. What is the BEST first step to improve supply chain sustainability?

  1. Stop tracking supplier impacts because they are outside the company's control
  2. Switch all suppliers immediately regardless of cost or quality
  3. Establish a supplier code of conduct with sustainability criteria and assess suppliers against it
  4. Publish a sustainability statement without supplier engagement

5.4-Q9. The 'triple bottom line' approach to sustainability evaluates an organization's performance in which three areas?

  1. People, planet, and profit
  2. Quality, cost, and schedule
  3. Plan, do, and check
  4. Reduce, reuse, and recycle

5.4-Q10. A plant collects scrap aluminum trimmings, sends them to be remelted, and buys back the resulting ingots for production. This is BEST described as:

  1. Reuse
  2. Source reduction
  3. Landfill diversion by incineration
  4. Closed-loop recycling

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1CReducing eliminates waste and resource use at the source, so no handling, reprocessing or disposal is needed; recycling still consumes energy.
2ASustainable supply chain management sets supplier codes of conduct, screens and audits suppliers, and extends EHS expectations beyond the company's own sites.
3DSpecifying recycled, efficient and low-toxicity inputs at procurement reduces impacts before they occur (green purchasing).
4BSubstituting a less hazardous input prevents the pollution from being generated at all, which is source reduction.
5ALEED (Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design) rates buildings on energy, water, materials, indoor environmental quality and site sustainability.
6DLCA is a cradle-to-grave (or cradle-to-cradle) analysis of materials, energy, emissions and waste across all life-cycle stages (ISO 14040/14044).
7BUsing the same item again for its original purpose without reprocessing is reuse. Recycling would break the material down to make new products.
8CSetting clear expectations, then assessing and engaging suppliers, is the foundation of supply chain sustainability; ignoring or making unfounded claims (greenwashing) is weak.
9AThe triple bottom line measures social (people), environmental (planet) and economic (profit) performance, a precursor to ESG reporting.
10DThe material is reprocessed (remelted) and returned to the same product system, which is closed-loop recycling, a circular economy practice. Reuse would involve no reprocessing.
Printed book page 190
Domain 5 · Competency 5.5

Environmental Issues and ESG

CSP11 blueprint: Describe the impact of environmental issues (e.g., aging infrastructure, asbestos, air pollution, climate change, environmental, social, and governance)

1. Definition

This competency covers major environmental issues that affect organizations and communities - deteriorating infrastructure, legacy hazards such as asbestos and lead, air pollution, climate change - and ESG (Environmental, Social and Governance) frameworks used by investors and stakeholders to judge corporate performance.

2. Explanation in simple words

Old pipes, tanks and buildings can leak or contain hazardous materials; dirty air and a changing climate affect health and operations; and investors now look at how companies manage environment, people and governance, not just profit.

3. Key points to remember

  • Aging infrastructure: corroded pipelines, tanks, sewers and bridges increase release and failure risk; manage with risk-based inspection, integrity management programs and planned replacement.
  • Asbestos: friable vs non-friable; asbestosis, mesothelioma, lung cancer; OSHA PEL 0.1 f/cc (8-h TWA), excursion limit 1.0 f/cc (30 min); building survey before renovation/demolition; NESHAP notification 10 working days before work when RACM thresholds are exceeded (260 linear ft, 160 ft², 35 ft³); removal with containment, negative pressure, wet methods and HEPA; never dry-sweep suspect material.
  • Lead: renovation (EPA RRP rule for pre-1978 housing), OSHA lead standards (PEL 50 µg/m³, action level 30 µg/m³).
  • Air pollution: criteria pollutants, ozone, PM2.5, VOCs; health effects (respiratory, cardiovascular); indoor air quality links.
  • Climate change: greenhouse gases (CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, fluorinated gases); scope 1 (direct), scope 2 (purchased energy), scope 3 (value chain) emissions; physical risks to operations (heat stress, flooding, severe weather) and transition risks (regulation, markets).
  • ESG: Environmental (emissions, waste, water), Social (worker safety and health, human rights, community), Governance (board oversight, ethics, transparency). Frameworks: GRI, SASB/ISSB, TCFD. Safety performance is a core 'S' metric.

4. Real work scenario

Before renovating a 1960s office, the safety professional orders an asbestos survey. It finds 400 linear feet of friable pipe insulation, so the company files a NESHAP notification 10 working days ahead, hires a licensed abatement contractor using negative-pressure containment, and performs clearance air sampling before re-occupancy.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Any suspect insulation in an old building: stop, do not sweep, assume asbestos until tested.
  • Scope 2 = purchased electricity.
  • ESG 'S' includes occupational safety and health.

6. Every blueprint point (5 blueprint items + 8 more)

Aging infrastructureBLUEPRINT

Many facilities run past their design life; pipelines, tanks, structures and electrical systems 40+ years old face corrosion, fatigue and overload, raising failure risk under modern demands and extreme weather.

  • Common failures: corroded USTs/transfer lines (40 CFR 280 requires leak detection, corrosion protection, secondary containment for new USTs).
  • Fire sprinklers: scale-clogged galvanized pipe; inspect/test/maintain per NFPA 25; design to NFPA 13.
  • Structures: rusted fasteners, rotted supports (1910 Subpart D).
  • Electrical: degraded insulation, overloaded panels, no arc-flash labels, not lockable (1910.147, NFPA 70E).
  • HVAC: decayed ducts, mold - review ASHRAE 62.1.

Exam tip: Aging infrastructure is multi-disciplinary: compliance, life safety and business continuity.

Inspecting and managing aging assets

Risk-based inspection and asset management prioritize work by consequence and likelihood of failure.

  • Risk-based inspection: API 580/581.
  • NDT: ultrasonic thickness, radiography, magnetic particle, dye penetrant.
  • Infrared thermography (electrical hot spots, bearings), vibration and acoustic monitoring.
  • ISO 55000 asset management; CMMS, SCADA, structural health monitoring sensors.
  • Administrative: aging infrastructure register, retrofit timelines in capital plans, pre-task checks. Example: ultrasonic testing of tanks over 20 years old after a corrosion leak.

Exam tip: Wall thinning from corrosion -> ultrasonic thickness testing.

AsbestosBLUEPRINT

Asbestos is a group of fibrous silicate minerals once used in insulation, pipe wrap, flooring, roofing, gaskets and fireproofing. Disturbed fibers cause mesothelioma, asbestosis and lung cancer decades later; there is no known safe level.

  • OSHA PEL 0.1 f/cc 8-hr TWA; excursion limit 1.0 f/cc over 30 minutes (1910.1001; construction 1926.1101).
  • Above the PEL: regulated areas, respirators, training, signage, medical surveillance.
  • Buildings built before 1981 - presume thermal insulation and surfacing materials are ACM unless tested.
  • Latency of 20-40+ years; smoking multiplies lung cancer risk.

Exam tip: Exceeding the excursion limit (1.0 f/cc for 30 min) is a violation even if the 8-hr TWA is under 0.1.

Asbestos regulations, abatement and disposal

Several agencies regulate asbestos during work, demolition, schools and transport.

  • EPA NESHAP (40 CFR 61 Subpart M): survey before renovation/demolition; notify 10 working days before work involving at least 260 linear ft, 160 sq ft or 35 cu ft of regulated ACM (demolition always needs notice).
  • AHERA (schools): inspections, management plans, re-inspection every 3 years (surveillance every 6 months).
  • Controls: licensed contractors, negative-pressure enclosures, wet methods, glove bags, HEPA vacuums, decon units.
  • Clearance: PCM or TEM air sampling; often under 0.01 f/cc.
  • Waste: wetted, double-bagged in 6-mil leak-tight bags, labeled, transported to approved landfill with records.

Exam tip: Disturbed suspect ceiling insulation: FIRST stop work, evacuate and isolate the area, then sample.

Other legacy contaminants (lead, PCBs, USTs)

Legacy contaminants in pre-1980s buildings become hazardous when disturbed during maintenance, renovation or demolition.

  • Lead-based paint: OSHA 1926.62 (PEL 50 ug/m3, action level 30); EPA RRP rule for target housing.
  • PCBs (old transformers, ballasts, caulk): TSCA regulated.
  • Leaking underground storage tanks: 40 CFR 280.
  • Failure to manage can bring civil penalties, criminal prosecution or fatal exposures.

Air pollutionBLUEPRINT

Industrial air pollution affects worker health, the community, regulatory standing and ESG scores.

  • Combustion: NOx and SO2 (acid rain and smog precursors).
  • Particulate matter: PM2.5 penetrates deep into lungs (diesel exhaust, grinding).
  • VOCs from paints, solvents and fuels form ground-level ozone.
  • Fugitive dust may carry silica or heavy metals.
  • Health effects: short-term irritation, headaches; long-term asthma, bronchitis, cardiovascular disease, lung cancer.

Air quality regulation, reporting and monitoring

EPA, OSHA and states regulate air emissions and exposures.

  • CAA: NAAQS, NSR/PSD permits, Title V permits when potential to emit exceeds major-source thresholds; state SIPs may be stricter.
  • Title V reports semiannually with CEMS data for key sources.
  • TRI (EPCRA 313): annual Form R when a listed chemical exceeds 25,000 lb manufactured/processed or 10,000 lb otherwise used (the guide cites only 10,000 lb).
  • Penalties: the guide states up to $50,000/day; inflation-adjusted CAA civil penalties now exceed $100,000 per day per violation.
  • Monitoring: fence-line stations, DustTrak (PM), PID (VOCs), MIRAN/infrared analyzers.

Exam tip: PID measures VOCs; DustTrak measures particulate - know which instrument fits the pollutant.

Climate changeBLUEPRINT

Climate change is now a safety, health, operational and regulatory issue: hotter summers, extreme weather, shifting disease vectors and utility disruptions.

  • Heat stress for outdoor and hot indoor work; OSHA heat National Emphasis Program (launched 2022; the guide says 2023).
  • Extreme weather: floods, hurricanes, wildfires disrupt facilities and supply chains; EAPs (1910.38) must reflect new risks.
  • Vectors: ticks and mosquitoes spreading (Lyme, West Nile, dengue).
  • Grid strain and blackouts affect ventilation, cooling and communications; floods can release chemicals.
  • ISO 14001 (2024 amendment) requires considering climate change in context of the organization; TCFD for financial disclosure.

Heat illness prevention

A heat illness prevention program is expected under the General Duty Clause and the NEP.

  • Water: about 1 quart per hour (1 cup every 15-20 minutes).
  • Rest and shade on work-rest cycles based on heat index or WBGT (NIOSH RELs, ACGIH TLVs).
  • Acclimatization: new and returning workers ramp up over 7-14 days (20% first day rule).
  • Train workers and supervisors on symptoms; emergency plan for heat stroke (medical emergency - cool immediately, call 911).
  • Humidity raises heat index even at moderate air temperature.

Exam tip: Most heat deaths occur in the first days on the job - acclimatization is the key control.

Climate resilience and emergency planning

Facilities adapt to climate risk through resilience audits, updated ERPs and forward-looking climate risk assessment.

  • ERP updates: long blackouts, wildfire smoke (N95s, HVAC sealing), shelter-in-place for hurricanes/floods, resilient communications, drills with local agencies.
  • Resilience: backup power (generators, UPS), drainage capacity, anchored rooftop equipment, fire-resistant materials, supplier redundancy.
  • Climate risk assessment: identify regional trends (NOAA, IPCC), overlay on critical assets, score and control, add to aspects register or BCP.
  • Use ISO 31010 risk techniques; results inform capital projects and insurance.

ESG (Environmental, Social and Governance)BLUEPRINT

ESG evaluates how responsibly and sustainably an organization operates; it affects investment, insurance and public trust. Safety performance feeds every pillar.

  • Environmental: GHG emissions, resource efficiency, compliance record (fines, spills).
  • Social: worker health and safety (TRIR, DART, near misses), DEI, community engagement and environmental justice.
  • Governance: transparency and reporting, regulatory compliance, whistleblower protection, ethics.
  • A strong safety culture underpins the Social score.

GHG scopes and ESG reporting frameworks

ESG data is reported through recognized frameworks, and emissions are split into three scopes.

  • Scope 1: direct emissions from owned sources (boilers, fleet, forklifts, refrigerants).
  • Scope 2: indirect emissions from purchased electricity, steam, heat.
  • Scope 3: other value chain emissions (suppliers, transport, product use).
  • Frameworks: GRI, SASB (now under IFRS/ISSB), CDP, TCFD.
  • Management systems supporting ESG: ISO 14001, ISO 45001.

Exam tip: Purchased electricity = Scope 2; supplier emissions = Scope 3.

ESG KPIs

CSPs prove value by supplying measurable indicators for each pillar.

  • Environmental: % GHG reduction per revenue unit, tons diverted from landfill, water intensity.
  • Social: TRIR/DART trends, % workforce trained, safety perception survey results.
  • Governance: near misses reported, % audit findings closed on time, inspections without citations.
  • KPIs may link to bonuses, public reports and stock value.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 34 Environmental Management, p. 754; Ch. 33 Sustainability; Ch. 4 Regulations (asbestos)

  2. 1.0 fiber/cc as an 8-hour TWA
  3. 0.05 fiber/cc as an 8-hour TWA
  4. 0.1 fiber/cc as an 8-hour TWA

5.5-Q8. In ESG frameworks, the 'S' (Social) pillar MOST directly includes which CSP-related metric?

  2. TRIR and DART incident rates

5.5-Q9. A plant has 40-year-old underground process piping with no inspection history. What is the BEST approach to manage environmental release risk?

  1. Wait for a leak to identify weak sections
  2. Apply risk-based inspection: prioritize segments by likelihood and consequence of failure and inspect or replace accordingly
  3. Replace all piping immediately regardless of cost
  4. Increase insurance coverage

5.5-Q10. Electrostatic precipitators (ESPs) are primarily used to control air pollution by:

  1. Separating oil from water
  2. Neutralizing acid gases
  3. Removing fine particulates from exhaust gas using an electric field

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1ANESHAP (40 CFR 61 Subpart M) requires written notice 10 working days in advance when regulated ACM meets or exceeds 260 linear ft, 160 sq ft, or 35 cu ft.
2CRegulated asbestos removal relies on engineering controls (negative-pressure enclosure, HEPA, wet methods) supplemented by proper respirators; fans spread fibers.
3DOSHA prohibits sanding asbestos-containing flooring outright; floor care must use wet methods and low-abrasion pads at low speeds.
4BISO 14001 supports E, worker safety and culture support S, and board-level reporting and management systems support G.
5AScope 1 = direct emissions from owned sources (boilers, fleet); Scope 2 = indirect emissions from purchased energy; Scope 3 = other value-chain emissions.
6COver time corrosion, fatigue and wear thin walls and weaken joints, leading to leaks; mechanical integrity programs address this.
7DOSHA's asbestos PEL is 0.1 f/cc (8-hr TWA) with an excursion limit of 1.0 f/cc over 30 minutes. Trap: confusing the excursion limit with the PEL.
8BWorker health and safety performance is a core Social metric; GHG, energy and waste metrics are Environmental.
9BRisk-based inspection and integrity management focus resources where failure is most likely and most harmful. Waiting is reactive; insurance transfers cost but not the hazard.
10CESPs charge particles and collect them on plates, achieving high efficiency for fine particulates at power plants and smelters.
Domain 6

Occupational Health and Applied Science

10% of the exam · 6 competencies
6.1Occupational Exposure: Anticipate, Recognize, Evaluate, Control
Anticipate, recognize, evaluate, and control occupational exposures by implementing techniques for measurement, sampling, and analysis (e.g., hazardous chemicals, SDS, radiation, noise, biological hazards, heat/cold, indoor air quality, ventilation, nanoparticles, combustible dust, heat systems, high pressure, silica, powder and spray applications, blasting, molten metals, hot work, cold and heat stress, laser)
6.2Public Health and Epidemiology
Understand principles of public health as applicable (i.e., fundamentals of epidemiology, infectious disease, risk factors, statistics to interpret data)
6.3Toxicology
Apply toxicology principles to create exposure control plans and develop risk mitigation plans (e.g., using sampling equipment, symptoms of an exposure, LD50, LC50, mutagens, carcinogens, teratogens, ototoxins)
6.4Ergonomics and Human Factors
Evaluate principles related to ergonomics and human factors (e.g., visual acuity, body mechanics, lifting, vibration, anthropometrics, fatigue management)
6.5Chemistry: Containment Volume and Storage Requirements
Apply chemistry principles to calculate required containment volumes and hazardous materials storage requirements
6.6Physics: Energy, Weight, Force and Stress
Apply core concepts in physics (e.g., forms of energy, weights, forces, stresses)
Printed book page 197
Domain 6 · Competency 6.1

Occupational Exposure: Anticipate, Recognize, Evaluate, Control

CSP11 blueprint: Anticipate, recognize, evaluate, and control occupational exposures by implementing techniques for measurement, sampling, and analysis (e.g., hazardous chemicals, SDS, radiation, noise, biological hazards, heat/cold, indoor air quality, ventilation, nanoparticles, combustible dust, heat systems, high pressure, silica, powder and spray applications, blasting, molten metals, hot work, cold and heat stress, laser)

1. Definition

Industrial hygiene is the science of anticipating, recognizing, evaluating and controlling workplace health hazards (chemical, physical, biological and ergonomic) that can cause illness or impaired well-being. Evaluation compares measured exposures with occupational exposure limits (OSHA PELs, ACGIH TLVs, NIOSH RELs).

2. Explanation in simple words

Expect where exposures will be, spot them, measure how much people breathe, hear or absorb, compare that with the safe limit, and then reduce it - using engineering controls first.

3. Key points to remember

  • Exposure limits: PEL (OSHA, legal), TLV (ACGIH, guideline, updated annually), REL (NIOSH); TWA (8-h), STEL (15-min), Ceiling (never exceed), IDLH (immediately dangerous to life or health). Action level usually ½ PEL.
  • Sampling: personal samples in the breathing zone; active (pump, calibrated before/after) vs passive (diffusion badges); media - filters (particulates), sorbent tubes (vapors), impingers; direct-reading instruments (4-gas meters, PIDs). Sample volume = flow rate × time. Concentration = mass ÷ volume.
  • Chemicals: ppm = mg/m³ × 24.45 ÷ MW (25 °C, 1 atm). TWA = Σ(C × T) ÷ 8. Mixtures with additive effects: Σ(C/T) must be ≤ 1.
  • Noise (1910.95): PEL 90 dBA (5-dB exchange rate); action level 85 dBA (50% dose) triggers hearing conservation program - monitoring, annual audiometric testing, hearing protectors, training, records. Standard threshold shift = average 10 dB shift at 2,000, 3,000 and 4,000 Hz. T = 8 ÷ 2^((L − 90)/5). Adding sources: L = 10 log Σ10^(L/10); doubling distance −6 dB (point source).
