  • Radiation: ionizing (alpha - stopped by paper; beta - plastic/aluminum; gamma/X-ray - lead/concrete; neutron - water/hydrogen-rich materials). ALARA with time, distance (inverse square law I₂ = I₁(d₁/d₂)²), shielding. Dosimeters measure cumulative dose. OSHA/NRC occupational limit 5 rem (50 mSv)/year whole body. Half-life: A = A₀(½)^(t/T½). Non-ionizing: UV, IR, RF/microwave, lasers.
  • Lasers (ANSI Z136.1): Class 1, 2, 3R, 3B, 4; Class 3B and 4 require a Laser Safety Officer, nominal hazard zone, interlocks, eyewear with correct optical density.
  • Heat stress: WBGT outdoors = 0.7 NWB + 0.2 GT + 0.1 DB; indoors = 0.7 NWB + 0.3 GT. Heat stroke (core > 104 °F, confusion, may stop sweating) is a medical emergency; heat exhaustion (heavy sweating, weakness). Acclimatization: new workers 20% day 1, +20%/day. Cold stress: hypothermia, frostbite, trench foot; wind chill.
  • Ventilation: Q = V × A; V = 4005√VP; TP = SP + VP; local exhaust (capture at source - preferred for toxics) vs general/dilution ventilation (low-toxicity, low-volume, dispersed sources). Dilution Q = 403 × 10⁶ × SG × ER × K ÷ (MW × C). Air changes per hour = Q × 60 ÷ volume.
  • Respirators (1910.134): written program, medical evaluation, fit test (annually for tight-fitting), training; APFs - half-mask 10, full-face APR 50, PAPR loose hood 25, SAR/SCBA pressure-demand full face 1,000/10,000; MUC = PEL × APF; atmosphere-supplying respirators for IDLH or oxygen deficient (< 19.5%).
  • Biological hazards: bloodborne pathogens (1910.1030 - exposure control plan, universal precautions, hepatitis B vaccine within 10 working days), Legionella (cooling towers), molds, TB (BSL-3 labs).
  • Indoor air quality: CO₂ as a ventilation indicator (> ~700 ppm above outdoor), ASHRAE 62.1, sources (VOCs, mold, outdoor air intake location).
  • Specific hazards: respirable crystalline silica (PEL 50 µg/m³, AL 25; wet methods, LEV; Table 1 for construction), combustible dust (DHA, housekeeping), nanoparticles (high surface area, HEPA effective), abrasive blasting (substitute non-silica media, blast enclosures, Type CE respirators), molten metal (moisture → steam explosions, aluminized PPE), welding fumes (manganese, hexavalent chromium from stainless steel, zinc → metal fume fever), high-pressure systems (stored energy, injection injuries).

4. Real work scenario

Workers polishing stainless steel complain of sinus irritation. The hygienist first reviews SDSs, then takes task-based personal breathing-zone samples for hexavalent chromium and nickel. Results exceed the action level, so a downdraft table with local exhaust is installed, respirators are used while it is built, and follow-up sampling confirms exposures below the action level.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Always start with SDS review and hazard recognition, then personal breathing-zone sampling.
  • Noise and radiation calculations (dose, adding dB, inverse square, half-life) are high-yield.
  • Local exhaust is preferred over dilution ventilation for toxic contaminants.
  • Mixture exposure can exceed the limit even if each chemical is individually below its own limit.

6. Every blueprint point (22 blueprint items + 7 more)

OSHA hazard categories

Workplace health hazards are grouped into four categories: physical, chemical, biological and ergonomic. Recognizing the category tells you which standards, sampling methods and controls apply.

  • Physical: noise, vibration, heat/cold, ionizing and non-ionizing radiation, electricity, pressure.
  • Chemical: solvents, metals, dusts (silica, asbestos), gases, corrosives - governed by HazCom 1910.1200 and substance-specific standards.
  • Biological: bacteria, viruses, fungi, parasites, prions/toxins - e.g., Bloodborne Pathogens 1910.1030.
  • Ergonomic: force, repetition, awkward posture, contact stress, vibration - no OSHA ergonomics standard; enforced via the General Duty Clause 5(a)(1).

Exam tip: IH sequence: anticipate, recognize, evaluate, control (plus confirm). Recognition comes before measurement.

Occupational exposure limits (PEL, TLV, REL, STEL, Ceiling, IDLH)

Exposure limits set the airborne concentration most workers can tolerate without adverse effect. Know who publishes each and whether it is enforceable.

  • PEL = OSHA, legally enforceable (1910.1000 Z-tables and substance standards); many date from 1971 and are outdated.
  • TLV = ACGIH, advisory, updated yearly; REL = NIOSH, recommended (10-h TWA basis); WEL = UK; IOELV = EU.
  • TWA = 8-h average; STEL = 15-min average, max 4 times/day, at least 60 min apart; Ceiling (C) = never exceed.
  • Action level (AL) is usually 1/2 the PEL and triggers monitoring and medical surveillance.
  • IDLH (NIOSH) = concentration from which escape within 30 min is possible without irreversible effects; used for respirator selection (e.g., CO IDLH 1,200 ppm).

Exam tip: 'Legally enforceable limit' = PEL; 'best practice' or 'most protective' usually = TLV or REL.

MeasurementBLUEPRINT

Measurement quantifies exposure with direct-reading instruments or integrated sampling so it can be compared with an exposure limit. Instruments must be appropriate for the agent, calibrated and used by a competent person.

  • Direct-reading: multigas meters (O2, LEL, CO, H2S), PID for VOCs, FID, electrochemical sensors, colorimetric detector tubes, real-time aerosol monitors.
  • Combustible gas indicators are calibrated to a specific gas (e.g., methane or pentane); readings for other gases need a response/correction factor.
  • Bump test before each day's use; full calibration per manufacturer; pre- and post-calibrate sampling pumps (accept if within about 5%).
  • Noise: sound level meter (Type 1 precision, Type 2 general survey); dosimeter for personal dose; octave band analyzer for frequency content.
  • Heat: WBGT monitor; radiation: GM counter (survey), TLD/film badge (personal dose).

Exam tip: Direct-reading = immediate decision (entry, alarm); integrated personal sampling = compliance with a TWA.

SamplingBLUEPRINT

Air sampling collects contaminant from the worker's breathing zone over a known air volume. The strategy - who, when, how long, how many - matters as much as the method.

  • Personal breathing-zone samples (within about a 1-ft radius of nose/mouth) are required for compliance; area samples characterize sources.
  • Media: charcoal/sorbent tubes for vapors (about 0.01-0.2 L/min), filters/cassettes for particulates and metals (about 1-4 L/min), cyclone for respirable dust (10-mm nylon about 1.7 L/min), PCM cassette for asbestos fibers, bags/canisters for grab samples.
  • Concentration = mass collected / air volume. Example: 0.5 mg at 2.0 L/min for 240 min = 480 L = 0.48 m3, so C = 0.5/0.48 = 1.04 mg/m3.
  • Full-shift single sample or consecutive samples are preferred over grab samples for TWA comparisons; STEL samples are 15 min.
  • Include field blanks (commonly 10% of samples, at least 1-2 per set); sample similar exposure groups (SEGs) and the maximum-risk employee first.
  • Other types: bulk samples (identify material, e.g., asbestos in insulation), wipe/surface samples (lead, beryllium, hygiene), biological monitoring (BEIs).

Exam tip: Unit trap: 1 m3 = 1,000 L. Forgetting this gives answers off by 1,000.

AnalysisBLUEPRINT

Analysis turns sample results into decisions: the lab quantifies the agent, and the safety professional compares the result with the limit using sound statistics.

  • Lab methods: gas chromatography (organics), AAS/ICP (metals), X-ray diffraction (crystalline silica), PCM (asbestos fibers, TEM to confirm type), gravimetric (total/respirable dust). Use an AIHA-accredited lab and NIOSH/OSHA methods.
  • OSHA compliance test: Y = result/PEL; UCL = Y + SAE, LCL = Y - SAE. UCL <= 1 compliance; LCL > 1 violation; otherwise 'possible overexposure'.
  • Example: result 105 ppm, PEL 100 ppm, SAE 0.10 -> Y = 1.05, LCL = 0.95 -> possible overexposure, not a citable violation.
  • Exposure data are usually lognormal: describe with geometric mean (GM) and geometric standard deviation (GSD) and judge the 95th percentile against the OEL.
  • Extended shifts (Brief and Scala): RF = (8/h) x ((24 - h)/16). 12-h shift: RF = (8/12)(12/16) = 0.5, so a 100 ppm limit becomes 50 ppm.
  • AIHA exposure categories compare the 95th percentile with the OEL (e.g., <10% highly controlled ... >100% poorly controlled).

Exam tip: One result just under the PEL does not prove compliance - variability means more samples or a lower AL are needed.

TWA and mixture calculations

The 8-hour time-weighted average combines different concentrations over a shift; mixtures of chemicals with similar effects are assessed with the additive formula.

  • TWA = (C1T1 + C2T2 + ... + CnTn) / 8 h. Unsampled time is assumed zero only if no exposure is known to occur.
  • Example: 3 h at 50 ppm, 2 h at 100 ppm, 3 h at 20 ppm -> (150 + 200 + 60)/8 = 51.25 ppm.
  • Additive mixture: Em = C1/L1 + C2/L2 + ... + Cn/Ln; Em > 1 means the mixture limit is exceeded.
  • Example: 50 ppm (L 100) + 40 ppm (L 100) + 15 ppm (L 50) -> 0.5 + 0.4 + 0.3 = 1.2 -> exceeds.
  • Use the additive formula only for agents with the same target organ/effect (e.g., CNS-depressant solvents); independent effects are judged separately.

Exam tip: Always divide by 8 h for an 8-hr TWA, even if the worker was exposed for only 6 h.

ChemicalsBLUEPRINT

Chemical hazards include toxic, flammable, corrosive and reactive substances in gas, vapor, mist, fume, dust or fiber form. OSHA's Hazard Communication standard (1910.1200, aligned with GHS) is the core program.

  • HazCom elements: written program, chemical inventory, labels, SDSs, employee training (before initial assignment and when a new hazard is introduced).
  • Shipped-container label elements: product identifier, signal word, hazard statements, pictograms, precautionary statements, supplier name/address/phone.
  • Signal words: 'Danger' (more severe) or 'Warning'; only one is used on a label.
  • Physical forms: gases/vapors (molecular), aerosols - dusts (mechanical), fumes (condensed metal vapor, < 1 um), mists (liquid droplets), fibers.
  • Substance-specific standards (1910.1001-1053) add AL, exposure monitoring, regulated areas, medical surveillance and hygiene rules (e.g., lead, benzene, Cr(VI), silica).

Exam tip: If a question gives a new chemical, the first step is to obtain and review the SDS.

SDS (Safety Data Sheet)BLUEPRINT

The SDS is the 16-section GHS document from the manufacturer that describes a chemical's hazards and safe handling. It must be readily accessible to workers on every shift.

  • Sections: 1 Identification, 2 Hazard(s), 3 Composition, 4 First aid, 5 Fire-fighting, 6 Accidental release, 7 Handling and storage, 8 Exposure controls/PPE, 9 Physical/chemical properties, 10 Stability and reactivity, 11 Toxicological, 12-15 Ecological, Disposal, Transport, Regulatory (not enforced by OSHA), 16 Other (revision date).
  • Section 8 = PEL/TLV and PPE; Section 10 = incompatibilities; Section 11 = LD50/LC50 and carcinogenicity; Section 9 = flash point, vapor pressure, vapor density.
  • Employers can rely on the supplier's SDS; electronic access is fine if no barriers exist (power outage backup).
  • 1910.1020 treats SDSs (or a record of chemical identity, where used and when) as exposure records kept 30 years.

Exam tip: 'Which SDS section lists incompatible materials?' = Section 10 (handling/storage segregation guidance is in 7).

RadiationBLUEPRINT

Radiation is energy emitted as particles or waves. Ionizing radiation (alpha, beta, gamma/X-ray, neutrons) can remove electrons and damage DNA; non-ionizing radiation (UV, visible, IR, microwave, RF) mainly causes heating, burns and eye damage.

  • Shielding: alpha - paper/skin (internal hazard if inhaled/ingested); beta - plastic, glass, aluminum (use low-Z first to limit bremsstrahlung); gamma/X-ray - lead, concrete, water; neutrons - water, polyethylene, concrete.
  • Protection = time, distance, shielding (ALARA). Dose = dose rate x time: 50 mrem/h for 30 min = 25 mrem.
  • Inverse square law: I1 x d1^2 = I2 x d2^2. Example: 100 mR/h at 2 ft -> at 10 ft: 100 x (2/10)^2 = 4 mR/h. Doubling distance cuts intensity to 1/4.
  • Half-life: A = A0 x (1/2)^(t/T). 100 mCi of I-131 (T = 8 d) after 24 d -> 12.5 mCi.
  • Non-ionizing: UV - photokeratitis (welder's flash), skin cancer, cataracts; IR - cataracts, burns (glassblowers, furnace work); RF/microwave - tissue heating.
  • Regulators: NRC (licensed nuclear material, 10 CFR 20), states (Agreement States), OSHA 1910.1096 (other sources, e.g., X-ray machines), EPA/DOE environmental and defense.

Exam tip: Inverse square law applies to point sources; for 'move from 3 ft to 6 ft' the answer is always 1/4 the original intensity.

Radiation units, dose limits and area classifications

Radiation quantities have traditional and SI units, and dose limits apply to workers, lens of eye, skin/extremities and the public.

  • Activity: curie (Ci) / becquerel (Bq; 1 Ci = 3.7 x 10^10 Bq). Exposure: roentgen (R). Absorbed dose: rad / gray (1 Gy = 100 rad). Dose equivalent: rem / sievert (1 Sv = 100 rem).
  • Dose equivalent = absorbed dose x quality (weighting) factor: alpha 20, neutrons 5-20, beta/gamma/X-ray 1.
  • Annual occupational limits (NRC 10 CFR 20): 5 rem (50 mSv) whole body TEDE, 15 rem lens of eye, 50 rem skin/extremities; public 0.1 rem (1 mSv); declared pregnant worker 0.5 rem over the pregnancy. The guide labels these OSHA limits; OSHA 1910.1096 itself uses 1.25 rem per calendar quarter whole body.
  • Areas (NRC): Radiation Area > 5 mrem/h at 30 cm; High Radiation Area > 100 mrem/h at 30 cm; Very High Radiation Area > 500 rad/h at 1 m.
  • Monitoring: TLD/OSL badges and film badges measure accumulated dose; GM counters survey contamination.
  • Overexposure reporting under 1910.1096: immediate/24-h notification depending on dose, written report within 30 days.

Exam tip: Gray/rad = energy absorbed; sievert/rem = biological effect. Pick rem/Sv when the question mentions 'biological damage'.

Radiation emergency response

Radiation incidents follow life-safety-first priorities, isolation, notification and ALARA-based protection of responders.

  • Rescue the injured first if safe - medical care outranks decontamination for serious injuries.
  • Isolate and post the area; notify NRC/state, DOE or EPA as applicable, and OSHA if limits are exceeded.
  • Responders use time, distance, shielding and worker rotation; wear dosimeters and respirators for airborne alpha/beta contamination.
  • Personal decon: remove clothing (removes about 90% of contamination), wash with mild soap and water, do not abrade skin.
  • Potassium iodide (KI) blocks thyroid uptake of radioactive iodine only; shelter-in-place if evacuation is unsafe.
  • Contaminated PPE is radioactive waste; store until decayed per half-life or ship to licensed disposal.

Exam tip: 'First action' at a radiation spill with an injured worker = life-safety rescue/medical care, then control contamination.

NoiseBLUEPRINT

Noise is unwanted sound; prolonged exposure above 85 dBA destroys cochlear hair cells and causes permanent noise-induced hearing loss (NIHL). OSHA 1910.95 sets the PEL and Hearing Conservation Program triggers.

  • OSHA PEL 90 dBA 8-h TWA (5-dB exchange rate); Action Level 85 dBA (dose 50%) triggers the HCP; impulse noise should not exceed 140 dB peak.
  • NIOSH REL and ACGIH TLV: 85 dBA 8-h with 3-dB exchange rate (more protective).
  • Human hearing 20-20,000 Hz; most sensitive 1,000-4,000 Hz; NIHL first appears as a notch at 3,000-6,000 Hz (classic 4 kHz notch).
  • dBA weighting mimics the ear and is used for compliance; dBC/peak (flat) for impulse and low-frequency noise.
  • Instruments: SLM for area spot checks, integrating SLM for variable noise, dosimeter for personal dose (compliance).

Exam tip: PEL 90 vs AL 85: at 87 dBA TWA the employer is under the PEL but must run a hearing conservation program.

Noise calculations (dB addition, dose, distance)

Decibels are logarithmic, so sound levels cannot be added arithmetically. Dose and TWA calculations follow OSHA 1910.95 Appendix A.

  • Adding levels: L = 10 log10(sum of 10^(Li/10)). Two equal sources = +3 dB (90 + 90 = 93 dB); 90 + 95 = 96.2 dB.
  • Shortcut: difference 0-1 dB add 3; 2-3 add 2; 4-9 add 1; 10+ add 0 to the higher level.
  • OSHA allowed time T = 8 / 2^((L - 90)/5): 90 dBA 8 h, 95 4 h, 100 2 h, 105 1 h, 110 0.5 h, 115 0.25 h max (85 dBA = 16 h). NIOSH: T = 8 / 2^((L - 85)/3).
  • Dose D = 100 x (C1/T1 + C2/T2 + ...). Example: 2 h at 95 (T 4), 4 h at 90 (T 8), 2 h at 85 (T 16) -> D = 100 x (0.5 + 0.5 + 0.125) = 112.5%.
  • TWA = 16.61 x log10(D/100) + 90. D = 112.5% -> 90.85 dBA; D = 200% -> 95 dBA; D = 50% -> 85 dBA.
  • Distance (point source, free field): L2 = L1 - 20 log10(d2/d1); drops 6 dB per doubling. 100 dB at 3 ft -> 88 dB at 12 ft.

Exam tip: Halving exposure time under OSHA allows +5 dB; under NIOSH only +3 dB. Do not mix exchange rates.

Hearing conservation program and hearing protectors

1910.95(c)-(o) requires a hearing conservation program when the 8-h TWA reaches 85 dBA. Engineering and administrative controls are required above 90 dBA; HPDs are interim/last line of defense.

  • Elements: monitoring, audiometric testing, HPDs at no cost (mandatory over 90 dBA or with an STS), training annually, recordkeeping.
  • Baseline audiogram within 6 months of first exposure at/above AL (1 year with mobile van, HPDs required meanwhile); 14 h without workplace noise before baseline; annual audiograms thereafter.
  • Standard threshold shift (STS) = average change of 10 dB or more at 2,000, 3,000 and 4,000 Hz in either ear; OSHA-recordable if also 25 dB or more above audiometric zero. Notify employee in writing within 21 days.
  • NRR derating (OSHA App. B, dBA): effective level = TWA - (NRR - 7). OSHA FOM also applies a 50% safety factor: TWA - (NRR - 7)/2. Example: 100 dBA, NRR 29 -> 100 - 11 = 89 dBA.
  • Records: noise exposure measurements 2 years; audiograms for duration of employment.
  • Hearing loss types: conductive (outer/middle ear, often treatable), sensorineural (cochlea/nerve - NIHL, permanent), mixed, central (brain). TTS is temporary; PTS and presbycusis (age) are permanent.

Exam tip: Calculation trap: subtract 7 from NRR before derating when the exposure is in dBA.

Biological hazardsBLUEPRINT

Biohazards are microorganisms, their toxins and biological materials that cause infection, allergy or poisoning. Exposure is significant in healthcare, labs, agriculture, animal handling, sewage and waste work.

  • Categories: bacteria (TB, anthrax, Legionella), viruses (HBV, HCV, HIV, rabies, influenza), fungi (Aspergillus, Histoplasma, Candida), parasites (malaria, Giardia), prions and toxins (CJD, botulinum, aflatoxin).
  • Bloodborne Pathogens 1910.1030: exposure control plan reviewed annually, universal/standard precautions, engineered sharps, HBV vaccine offered within 10 working days at no cost (signed declination if refused), annual training, sharps injury log, post-exposure evaluation.
  • BBP records: medical records duration of employment + 30 years; training records 3 years.
  • Biosafety levels: BSL-1 teaching labs (E. coli K-12); BSL-2 clinical labs, Class II BSC (Salmonella, HBV, HIV samples); BSL-3 negative pressure, HEPA exhaust (TB, SARS-CoV-2, Coxiella); BSL-4 positive-pressure suits, isolated building (Ebola, Marburg).
  • Controls: elimination/substitution, engineering (BSCs, negative-pressure rooms, ventilation), vaccination, administrative (screening), PPE (N95 or higher for airborne agents).

Exam tip: Match the containment clue: 'negative pressure' = BSL-3; 'full-body positive-pressure suit' = BSL-4.

Heat/coldBLUEPRINT

The thermal environment (heat or cold) is assessed by measuring air temperature, humidity, air movement and radiant heat, together with work rate and clothing. The WBGT index is the standard heat-stress metric.

  • Outdoor with sun: WBGT = 0.7 NWB + 0.2 GT + 0.1 DB. Indoor/no solar load: WBGT = 0.7 NWB + 0.3 GT (NWB = natural wet bulb, GT = globe, DB = dry bulb).
  • Example: NWB 25 C, GT 40 C, DB 30 C -> outdoor WBGT = 17.5 + 8 + 3 = 28.5 C; indoor = 17.5 + 12 = 29.5 C.
  • Compare WBGT (adjusted for clothing) with ACGIH TLV/Action Limit tables by metabolic rate and work-rest regimen (e.g., continuous moderate work, acclimatized TLV about 28 C).
  • Heat index (temperature + humidity) is used for outdoor work planning; OSHA relies on the General Duty Clause and a heat NEP (a federal heat rule was proposed in 2024).
  • Cold: wind chill (air temperature + wind speed) drives frostbite risk; ACGIH cold TLV aims to keep core temperature at or above 36 C (96.8 F).

Exam tip: NWB carries 70% of the WBGT weight - humidity and evaporative cooling dominate heat stress.

Heat/cold stressBLUEPRINT

Heat and cold illnesses range from mild skin conditions to life-threatening emergencies. Recognize the signature symptom and the correct first response.

  • Heat rash - blocked sweat ducts; heat cramps - sodium/potassium loss (electrolyte drinks); heat syncope - blood pooling in legs (lie down, elevate legs).
  • Heat exhaustion - heavy sweating, nausea, rapid pulse, core temperature below 104 F (40 C); reversible with rest, cooling and fluids.
  • Heat stroke - core above 104 F (40 C), confusion/seizures, often hot dry skin; MEDICAL EMERGENCY: call EMS and cool rapidly (cold-water immersion).
  • Prevention: acclimatization (NIOSH: no more than 20% of usual exposure on day 1 for new workers, increasing about 20%/day), water about 1 cup every 15-20 min, shade, work-rest cycles, buddy system.
  • Cold: hypothermia (core below 95 F / 35 C, shivering then confusion), frostbite (frozen tissue, pale waxy skin), chilblains (non-freezing, red itchy patches), trench/immersion foot (cold and wet, 32-60 F), reduced dexterity from vasoconstriction.

Exam tip: Confusion/altered mental state + heat = heat stroke, regardless of whether the worker is still sweating.

Indoor air qualityBLUEPRINT

IAQ problems arise from inadequate ventilation, indoor sources (VOCs, mold, combustion products) and outdoor air intake contamination. Investigations combine occupant surveys, walkthroughs and targeted measurements.

  • Tools: CO2 monitoring (surrogate for ventilation adequacy), direct-reading CO/VOC/particle meters, smoke tubes and anemometers for airflow, culture plates and spore traps for mold.
  • CO2 above about 1,000 ppm (or more than about 700 ppm above outdoor, ~400 ppm) suggests inadequate outdoor air.
  • Sick building syndrome (SBS): non-specific symptoms (headache, irritation, fatigue) that improve after leaving the building; no single cause.
  • Building-related illness (BRI): a diagnosable illness from an identified contaminant (Legionnaires' disease, hypersensitivity pneumonitis, CO poisoning); OSHA-recordable if work-related.
  • ASHRAE 55 = thermal comfort (about 68-76 F, RH about 30-60%); ASHRAE 62.1 = ventilation for acceptable IAQ.
  • Moisture control is key to mold; remediate water damage within 24-48 h.

Exam tip: SBS vs BRI: 'symptoms disappear when they leave' = SBS; 'confirmed diagnosis linked to a contaminant' = BRI.

VentilationBLUEPRINT

Ventilation controls airborne contaminants either by diluting them with clean air (general/dilution ventilation) or by capturing them at the source (local exhaust ventilation, LEV). LEV is preferred for toxic contaminants and point sources.

  • Q = V x A (cfm = fpm x ft2). Example: 12-in duct (A = 0.785 ft2) at 3,000 fpm -> Q = 2,356 cfm.
  • Air changes per hour: ACH = Q (cfm) x 60 / room volume (ft3). Example: 2,000 cfm in 50 x 40 x 10 ft room (20,000 ft3) -> 6 ACH.
  • Plain-opening hood capture (DallaValle): Q = V(10X^2 + A); flanged hood Q = 0.75V(10X^2 + A). Example: 100 fpm at X = 1 ft, A = 0.5 ft2 -> Q = 1,050 cfm.
  • Velocity from velocity pressure (standard air): V = 4,005 x sqrt(VP in. w.g.); VP = 1 in. -> 4,005 fpm.
  • Dilution: Q = (403 x 10^6 x SG x ER x K)/(MW x C), ER in pints/min evaporated, K = mixing safety factor 1-10; only for low-toxicity contaminants with dispersed sources.
  • LEV components: hood, ductwork, air cleaner, fan, exhaust stack; provide make-up air; typical dust transport velocity about 3,500-4,500 fpm. ASHRAE 62.1 and ACGIH Industrial Ventilation manual are references.

Exam tip: Capture velocity falls off roughly with the square of distance - moving the hood closer is the cheapest improvement.

NanoparticlesBLUEPRINT

Engineered nanoparticles are smaller than 100 nm. Their very high surface-area-to-mass ratio makes them more reactive and lets them reach the alveoli and possibly the bloodstream.

  • Sources: nanotech labs, coatings/paints, electronics, drug delivery, carbon nanotubes/nanofibers, 3D-printing powders.
  • Hazards: lung inflammation and fibrosis, possible cancer, cardiovascular effects (translocation), skin/eye irritation; long-term toxicity data are limited - apply precautionary controls.
  • NIOSH RELs: carbon nanotubes/nanofibers 1 ug/m3 (respirable elemental carbon); ultrafine TiO2 0.3 mg/m3.
  • Controls: enclosure and LEV with HEPA filtration (HEPA captures nanoparticles efficiently by diffusion), wet methods, HEPA vacuums, no dry sweeping or compressed air, respirators with P100 filters.
  • References: NIOSH Approaches to Safe Nanotechnology; ISO/TR 12885.

Exam tip: Mass-based limits under-represent nano hazards - surface area and particle count matter more.

Combustible dustBLUEPRINT

Finely divided combustible solids can deflagrate when dispersed in air in a confined space. Secondary explosions from accumulated dust disturbed by a primary event cause most deaths.

  • Dust explosion pentagon: fuel, oxygen, ignition, dispersion, confinement (fire triangle + 2).
  • Materials: wood, grain/sugar/flour, plastics, coal, metals (aluminum, magnesium), pharmaceuticals.
  • Standards: NFPA 652 (fundamentals, Dust Hazard Analysis required), 654 (general), 61 (agricultural/food), 484 (metals), 664 (wood); OSHA Combustible Dust NEP; 1910.272 grain handling.
  • Kst (deflagration index) classes: St 1 up to 200, St 2 201-300, St 3 above 300 bar-m/s; higher = more violent.
  • Housekeeping: remove accumulations (historic NFPA 654 benchmark 1/32 in. over 5% of floor area); use approved vacuums; avoid compressed-air blowdown; inspect hidden surfaces.
  • Engineering: dust collection, explosion venting (NFPA 68), suppression/isolation (NFPA 69), Class II hazardous location equipment, bonding/grounding, spark detection.

Exam tip: The most effective prevention is controlling dust accumulation (housekeeping/containment), not just ignition control.

Heat systems (boilers, steam, thermal fluids, furnaces)BLUEPRINT

Process heating systems store large amounts of thermal and pressure energy. Failures cause burns, scalds, fires and boiler or furnace explosions.

  • Boilers and pressure vessels: ASME Boiler and Pressure Vessel Code, National Board inspection, state jurisdictional inspections.
  • Safeguards: safety relief valves, low-water fuel cutoffs, pressure/temperature limits, burner management systems and flame safeguards (NFPA 85/86), purge cycles before ignition.
  • Steam: scalding, water hammer, flashing condensate; lock out and depressurize/cool before opening; use proper steam-rated hoses.
  • Thermal fluid (hot oil) systems: leaks into insulation can autoignite below the fluid's normal autoignition temperature.
  • Controls also include insulation/guarding of hot surfaces (burn threshold about 140 F/60 C contact), heat-resistant PPE and trained operators.

Exam tip: Low-water condition in a boiler: never add cold water to a hot dry boiler - shut down the burner and let it cool.

High pressure (compressed air, hydraulics, gas cylinders, pressure testing)BLUEPRINT

High-pressure systems release stored energy violently through hose whip, projectiles, injection injuries and vessel rupture.

  • Compressed air for cleaning must be reduced below 30 psi with chip guarding and PPE (1910.242(b)); never use on skin or clothing.
  • Hydraulic/high-pressure fluid injection injuries look minor but are surgical emergencies; never locate leaks with hands - use cardboard.
  • Cylinders (1910.101, CGA P-1): store upright, secured, valve caps on; oxygen 20 ft from fuel gases or separated by a 5-ft, 1/2-hour fire-rated barrier; move with carts, not by rolling or lifting by caps.
  • Pressure testing: hydrostatic is preferred over pneumatic because gas stores far more energy; establish exclusion zones.
  • Whip checks on air hoses; relief valves on receivers; LOTO includes bleeding stored pressure.
  • Water jetting (very high pressure) requires trained operators, deadman controls and specific PPE.

Exam tip: Pneumatic test vs hydrostatic test: hydrostatic is safer because liquids are nearly incompressible.

SilicaBLUEPRINT

Respirable crystalline silica (quartz, cristobalite) from cutting, grinding, drilling, blasting and foundry work causes silicosis, lung cancer, COPD, kidney and autoimmune disease.

  • OSHA 1910.1053 / 1926.1153: PEL 50 ug/m3 8-h TWA; action level 25 ug/m3. NIOSH REL 50 ug/m3; ACGIH TLV 25 ug/m3 (stricter, best practice).
  • Construction: follow Table 1 control methods (water, LEV, respirators) or do exposure assessment; competent person and written exposure control plan required.
  • Medical surveillance: general industry - exposure at/above AL for 30+ days/yr; construction - required respirator use 30+ days/yr; exams every 3 years.
  • Prohibited: dry sweeping and compressed-air cleaning where alternatives exist.
  • IARC Group 1 carcinogen; 'pneumoconiosis + autoimmune disease' points to silica.
  • Controls: substitution (non-silica abrasives), wet methods, LEV/vacuum shrouds, enclosed cabs, respirators.

Exam tip: Best practice limit = ACGIH TLV 25 ug/m3; enforceable limit = OSHA PEL 50 ug/m3.

Powder/spray applications (spray finishing, powder coating)BLUEPRINT

Spray painting and powder coating create flammable vapors, combustible dusts and toxic or sensitizing aerosols (isocyanates, Cr(VI) primers, solvents).

  • Standards: OSHA 1910.107 (spray finishing) and 1910.94(c) (spray booth ventilation); NFPA 33.
  • Booth interior and exhaust plenum are hazardous (classified) locations; no open flames or spark sources within 20 ft of spraying unless separated.
  • Keep vapor below 25% of LFL; exhaust fans interlocked with spray equipment; clean filters and residue regularly.
  • Electrostatic equipment and powder coating require bonding/grounding of parts and workers; powder coatings are combustible dusts.
  • Health: isocyanate (2-part urethane) asthma - supplied-air respirators often required; hexavalent chromium mist in aircraft painting.
  • Controls: high-transfer-efficiency (HVLP) guns, water-based coatings, enclosure, PPE (respirators, coveralls, gloves).

Exam tip: 'Aircraft painting + chromate primer' = hexavalent chromium; 'auto-body clearcoat + asthma' = isocyanates.

Blasting (abrasive blasting)BLUEPRINT

Abrasive blasting cleans and prepares surfaces by propelling media at high pressure. It exposes workers to silica and toxic metals, noise, high-pressure injury and fire/explosion hazards.

  • Respirable silica (silicosis, lung cancer) - substitute non-silica media (steel grit, garnet, crushed glass, slag - check metals content).
  • Toxic metals from coatings: lead, cadmium, chromium, beryllium.
  • Noise often exceeds 100 dBA; high-pressure stream can inject or lacerate (deadman controls on nozzles).
  • OSHA 1910.94(a): blast cleaning enclosures with exhaust; abrasive-blasting respirators (Type CE supplied-air) for operators inside enclosures or where silica/toxic dust exists.
  • Dust-laden confined spaces raise fire/explosion risk; ground nozzles and hoses to prevent static.
  • Explosive blasting (demolition, quarrying) is separate: 1926 Subpart U and 1910.109 - blaster in charge, misfire procedures, radio-frequency precautions.

Exam tip: First control for silica in blasting is substitution of the abrasive, not a better respirator.

Molten metalsBLUEPRINT

Foundry, smelting, casting and welding operations combine thermal, chemical and explosion hazards.

  • Burns from splashes and spills; aluminized/FR clothing, face shields, spats and leggings.
  • Steam explosions: molten metal contacting water or moisture (wet scrap, tools, molds, floors) expands violently - preheat and dry everything that contacts the melt.
  • Metal fumes: zinc/copper - metal fume fever; manganese - neurotoxic (manganism); Cr(VI) and others - respiratory/systemic toxicity.
  • Infrared radiation damages eyes (cataracts) and skin; use IR-rated eyewear and shielding.
  • Radiant heat causes heat stress; apply WBGT monitoring, work-rest cycles and cooling.
  • Additional: silica in foundry sand, CO from cupolas, noise, crane and ladle handling.

Exam tip: Most catastrophic molten-metal incidents involve water/moisture - the key preventive control is keeping everything dry.

Hot workBLUEPRINT

Hot work is any welding, cutting, brazing, grinding or other spark- or flame-producing work. It requires a permit system, fire prevention measures and control of welding fumes and radiation.

  • Standards: OSHA 1910.252 (and 1926 Subpart J), NFPA 51B. Prefer moving the work to a designated safe area.
  • Remove combustibles within 35 ft or protect them with covers/shields; cover floor and wall openings.
  • Fire watch during work and at least 30 min after (OSHA); NFPA 51B (2019 and later) requires 60 min followed by additional monitoring.
  • Permit authorizing individual inspects the area and issues a written permit; test atmosphere for flammables in confined spaces and around tanks (do not weld on containers that held flammables until cleaned/purged/inerted).
  • Health: welding fumes (Mn, Cr(VI) from stainless, Zn from galvanized), ozone, NOx, UV (welder's flash) - use LEV and correct filter shade numbers.
  • Cylinders, hoses and flashback arrestors inspected; separate oxygen and fuel gas in storage.

Exam tip: Where the work can be relocated to a shop, that beats any permit or fire-watch answer.

LasersBLUEPRINT

Lasers emit coherent, highly collimated optical radiation that can damage the eye (retina for visible/near-IR, cornea for UV/far-IR) and skin. ANSI Z136.1 classifies lasers by hazard.

  • Classes: 1 and 1M safe (1M unless viewed with optics); 2 visible, protected by blink reflex (0.25 s); 2M hazardous with optics; 3R low risk; 3B direct and specular beam eye hazard; 4 eye, skin, fire and diffuse-reflection hazard.
  • A Laser Safety Officer (LSO) is required for Class 3B and 4.
  • Engineering controls first: beam enclosures, interlocks, key switches, beam stops, shutters; administrative: SOPs, training, 'Laser On' illuminated signs, restricted access, nominal hazard zone (NHZ).
  • Eyewear must match the laser's wavelength and have adequate optical density: OD = log10(H0/MPE). Example: exposure 10 W/cm2, MPE 0.001 W/cm2 -> OD = log10(10,000) = 4.
  • Class 4 lasers also create fire and laser-generated air contaminants (plume) - need FR materials and smoke evacuation.

Exam tip: The blink reflex protects only against Class 2 visible beams - never assume it protects against 3B or 4.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 6 Particulates and Gases, p. 228; Ch. 8 Industrial Hygiene Air Sampling, p. 273; Ch. 9 Ventilation, p. 294; Ch. 10 Noise, p. 314; Ch. 11 Biological Hazards, p. 332; Ch. 13 Thermal Stressors, p. 367; Ch. 14 PPE, p. 385; Ch. 28 Radiation Safety, p. 636

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

6.1-Q1. A worker's acetone exposure over an 8-hour shift was: 2 h at 300 ppm, 3 h at 200 ppm, and 3 h at 100 ppm. What is the 8-hour TWA?

  1. 187.5 ppm
  2. 202 ppm
  3. 163 ppm
  4. 221 ppm

6.1-Q2. A worker is exposed to 95 dBA for 2 hours, 90 dBA for 4 hours, and 85 dBA for 2 hours. What is the worker's OSHA noise dose?

  1. 81.25%
  2. 100%
  3. 92%
  4. 112.5%

6.1-Q3. A noise dosimeter reports a daily dose of 200% (OSHA criteria). What is the equivalent 8-hour TWA? (TWA = 16.61 log10(D/100) + 90)

  1. 90 dBA
  2. 93 dBA
  3. 95 dBA
  4. 100 dBA

6.1-Q4. A jackhammer produces 98 dB at 3 ft. Using the inverse square law (L2 = L1 - 20 log(d2/d1)), what is the approximate sound level at 12 ft?

  1. 86 dB
  2. 88 dB
  3. 92 dB
  4. 94 dB

6.1-Q5. A radiography crew performs weld inspections with a gamma source. A helper normally works 4 ft from the source but steps back to 12 ft during exposures. How does the radiation intensity at 12 ft compare with that at 4 ft?

  1. 1/2 as much
  2. 1/9 as much
  3. 1/3 as much
  4. The same amount

6.1-Q6. Outdoors in direct sun, a heat stress survey records: natural wet bulb 25 C, globe 40 C, dry bulb 30 C. What is the WBGT? (Outdoor: WBGT = 0.7 NWB + 0.2 GT + 0.1 DB)

  1. 29.5 C
  2. 28.5 C
  3. 31.7 C
  4. 25.0 C

6.1-Q7. Under OSHA's respirable crystalline silica standard, what are the PEL and action level (8-hour TWA)?

  1. PEL 100 ug/m3; AL 50 ug/m3
  2. PEL 50 ug/m3; AL 25 ug/m3
  3. PEL 25 ug/m3; AL 10 ug/m3
  4. PEL 10 mg/m3; AL 5 mg/m3

6.1-Q8. A metal finishing shop uses a nickel-containing polishing compound. Workers complain of sinus irritation during buffing. No prior exposure data exists and ventilation effectiveness is unknown. What is the MOST appropriate first step in the exposure assessment?

  1. Conduct full-shift dosimetry on all workers in the department
  2. Rely on SDS information to estimate exposures without sampling
  3. Assume exposures are below limits because symptoms are mild
  4. Perform task-based personal air sampling on workers performing the buffing activity

6.1-Q9. Ralph is assigned to sweep a dusty floor and asks for a filtering facepiece respirator (dust mask). Dust levels are well below regulatory limits. The supervisor refuses because Ralph has not been fit tested. Was the supervisor correct?

  1. Yes, anyone wearing a tight-fitting respirator must be medically evaluated and fit tested prior to wearing one.
  2. Yes, Ralph should not have asked for the respirator due to budget constraints.
  3. No, fit testing is not required in this specific case.
  4. No, the safety supervisor should have performed a fit test and issued the respirator.

6.1-Q10. An employee with facial hair that prevents a half-mask seal is assigned a powered air-purifying respirator (PAPR) with a loose-fitting hood. Which type of fit testing is required?

  1. Fit testing is not required
  2. Medical fit testing
  3. Qualitative fit testing
  4. Quantitative fit testing

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1ATWA = sum(C x T)/8 = (600 + 600 + 300)/8 = 187.5 ppm. Trap: averaging the three concentrations without time weighting gives 200 ppm.
2DAllowed times (OSHA 5-dB rule): 95 dBA = 4 h, 90 dBA = 8 h, 85 dBA = 16 h. D = 2/4 + 4/8 + 2/16 = 0.5 + 0.5 + 0.125 = 112.5%, which exceeds the PEL.
3CTWA = 16.61 x log10(200/100) + 90 = 16.61 x 0.301 + 90 = 95 dBA. With a 5-dB exchange rate, doubling the dose adds 5 dB; 93 dBA is the 3-dB (NIOSH) trap.
4AInverse square law for a point source: L2 = L1 - 20 log(d2/d1) = 98 - 20 log(12/3) = 98 - 12 = 86 dB. Each doubling of distance drops about 6 dB.
5BRadiation intensity follows the inverse square law: I2/I1 = (d1/d2)^2 = (4/12)^2 = 1/9. Trap: 1/3 assumes intensity drops linearly with distance.
6BWBGT = 0.7(25) + 0.2(40) + 0.1(30) = 17.5 + 8 + 3 = 28.5 C. Trap: 29.5 C uses the indoor/no-solar-load formula (0.7 NWB + 0.3 GT).
7BOSHA 1910.1053/1926.1153 set the respirable crystalline silica PEL at 50 ug/m3 and the action level at 25 ug/m3 (8-h TWA). The old 100 ug/m3 value is the trap.
8DWith symptoms, no data, and unknown ventilation, the first step is task-based personal breathing-zone sampling of the buffing workers. Department-wide dosimetry is unfocused; SDS data alone cannot quantify exposure.
9CVoluntary use of a filtering facepiece where exposures are below limits falls under 1910.134 Appendix D: give the user Appendix D information; fit testing and medical evaluation are not required.
10AFit testing under 1910.134 applies to tight-fitting facepieces. A loose-fitting PAPR hood does not need a facial seal, so no fit test is required (medical evaluation is still required).
Printed book page 210
Domain 6 · Competency 6.2

Public Health and Epidemiology

CSP11 blueprint: Understand principles of public health as applicable (i.e., fundamentals of epidemiology, infectious disease, risk factors, statistics to interpret data)

1. Definition

Epidemiology is the study of the distribution and determinants of health-related events in populations and the application of that study to control health problems. Public health principles help the safety professional recognize disease patterns, infectious disease risks and risk factors in the workforce.

2. Explanation in simple words

Epidemiology asks who gets sick, where, when and why. Comparing exposed and unexposed groups shows whether a workplace exposure raises the risk of disease.

3. Key points to remember

  • Measures: incidence (new cases ÷ population at risk over time), prevalence (all existing cases ÷ population at a point/period), attack rate, mortality rate.
  • Study designs: cohort (follows exposed vs unexposed forward; relative risk), case-control (starts with cases vs controls, looks back at exposure; odds ratio), cross-sectional (snapshot; prevalence), ecological, randomized controlled trial.
  • Relative risk RR = incidence exposed ÷ incidence unexposed (RR > 1 = increased risk). Odds ratio OR = (a × d) ÷ (b × c). Attributable risk = Ie − Iu.
  • Causation criteria (Bradford Hill): strength, consistency, specificity, temporality (exposure before disease), dose-response, plausibility, coherence, experiment, analogy. Correlation is not causation; watch for confounding and bias (e.g., healthy worker effect).
  • Infectious disease chain of infection: agent → reservoir → portal of exit → mode of transmission (contact, droplet, airborne, vector, common vehicle) → portal of entry → susceptible host. Break the chain with vaccination, hygiene, ventilation, isolation, PPE.
  • Occupational infectious risks: bloodborne pathogens (HBV, HCV, HIV), TB, Legionella, zoonoses (brucellosis from animals/milk, anthrax, histoplasmosis), pandemic influenza/COVID-19 (pandemic plans).
  • Risk factors: modifiable (smoking, obesity, inactivity) vs non-modifiable (age, genetics); occupational vs non-occupational; Total Worker Health (NIOSH) integrates protection and health promotion.
  • Screening tests: sensitivity (true positive rate), specificity (true negative rate).

4. Real work scenario

A plant notices several cases of a rare respiratory disease. A cohort analysis shows 30 cases per 1,000 among workers exposed to a metalworking fluid versus 10 per 1,000 among unexposed workers - a relative risk of 3.0. The fluid system is found contaminated with bacteria; the system is cleaned, a biocide program and mist collectors are installed, and new cases stop.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Cohort → relative risk; case-control → odds ratio.
  • Incidence = new cases; prevalence = existing cases.
  • Temporality (exposure before disease) is the one causation criterion that is required.

6. Every blueprint point (4 blueprint items + 3 more)

EpidemiologyBLUEPRINT

Epidemiology studies the distribution (who, where, when) and determinants (why) of disease in populations and applies that knowledge to control health problems. In occupational health it links exposures to outcomes such as cancer or hearing loss.

  • Functions: prevention of disease, measuring frequency (incidence and prevalence) and describing distribution by person, place and time.
  • Descriptive studies generate hypotheses; analytic studies (cohort, case-control) test them.
  • Cohort study: start with exposed and unexposed groups and follow for disease (prospective or retrospective/historical); gives incidence and relative risk.
  • Case-control study: start with cases and controls and look back at exposures; good for rare diseases; gives odds ratio.
  • Cross-sectional: snapshot of prevalence; ecological: group-level data (ecological fallacy risk).
  • Bradford Hill causation criteria: strength, consistency, specificity, temporality (must have), dose-response, plausibility, coherence, experiment, analogy.

Exam tip: Rare disease, limited budget, look back at exposure = case-control. Following workers forward = cohort.

Infectious diseaseBLUEPRINT

Infectious disease spreads through the chain of infection. Breaking any link - at the agent, reservoir, transmission route or host - prevents spread.

  • Chain of infection: infectious agent, reservoir, portal of exit, mode of transmission, portal of entry, susceptible host.
  • Modes: contact (direct/indirect), droplet (large particles, about 3-6 ft), airborne (droplet nuclei - TB, measles), vehicle (food/water - Hep A), vector (ticks, fleas, mosquitoes).
  • Standard precautions apply to all patients; transmission-based precautions add contact, droplet or airborne measures (airborne infection isolation rooms: negative pressure, 12+ ACH, N95 or higher).
  • Host defenses: vaccination (HBV, Hep A, rabies pre-exposure, Tdap), post-exposure prophylaxis (HBIG, rabies PEP, TIG).
  • Outbreak response: case definition, confirm diagnosis, count cases, describe by person/place/time, hypothesize, test, control, communicate.
  • Pandemic planning ties to business continuity: absenteeism planning, ventilation, PPE stockpiles, remote work.

Exam tip: TB transmission is airborne (droplet nuclei), not surface contact - controls are ventilation, isolation and respirators.

Risk factorsBLUEPRINT

A risk factor is any attribute or exposure that increases the probability of disease or injury. Public-health thinking considers agent, host and environment together (the epidemiologic triangle).

  • Host factors: age, sex, genetics, immune status, pregnancy, pre-existing disease (diabetes, asthma), lifestyle (smoking, alcohol, obesity).
  • Agent factors: toxicity, infectivity, dose, duration; environmental factors: temperature, ventilation, crowding, sanitation, work organization.
  • Modifiable (smoking, PPE use, exposure level) vs non-modifiable (age, genetics).
  • Confounder: a factor linked to both exposure and outcome (e.g., smoking in a lung-cancer study of miners) - control by matching, stratification or adjustment.
  • Effect modification: smoking multiplies asbestos lung-cancer risk (synergy); healthy worker effect makes worker mortality look lower than the general population.

Exam tip: When a study ignores smoking in a lung disease outcome, the weakness is confounding.

Statistics (epidemiologic measures)BLUEPRINT

Public-health statistics express how often disease occurs and how strongly it is associated with an exposure.

  • Incidence rate = new cases / population at risk over a time period; prevalence = existing cases / total population at a point in time. Prevalence roughly = incidence x duration.
  • Relative risk RR = incidence exposed / incidence unexposed. Example: 30/1,000 vs 10/1,000 -> RR = 3. RR = 1 no association; > 1 increased risk.
  • Attributable risk = Ie - Iu = 0.03 - 0.01 = 20 per 1,000 exposed.
  • Odds ratio (case-control) = ad/bc. Example: exposed cases 40, unexposed cases 60, exposed controls 20, unexposed controls 80 -> (40 x 80)/(20 x 60) = 2.67.
  • SMR = observed deaths / expected deaths x 100. 15 observed vs 10 expected = 150 (50% excess).
  • Screening: sensitivity = TP/(TP + FN); specificity = TN/(TN + FP). 95% CI = mean +/- 1.96 x SD/sqrt(n); if a CI for RR/OR includes 1 the result is not statistically significant (p >= 0.05).

Exam tip: Odds ratio comes from case-control studies; relative risk comes from cohort studies.

Zoonotic and occupational bacterial diseases

Several bacterial diseases are acquired from animals, animal products, soil or water in specific occupations. Exam questions pair an occupation and a signature symptom.

  • Anthrax (Bacillus anthracis): wool, hides, livestock; cutaneous = black eschar (95% of cases); inhalation = 'woolsorter's disease' (most lethal); not person-to-person; CDC Category A agent.
  • Leptospirosis (Leptospira): animal urine in water/soil - rice-field, sewage, flood workers; severe form (Weil's disease) = jaundice + kidney failure.
  • Brucellosis (Brucella): meat packing, dairy, unpasteurized milk; 'undulant fever'; no human vaccine - pasteurization is key.
  • Q fever (Coxiella burnetii): inhalation of aerosols from animal birth products; pneumonia + hepatitis; chronic = endocarditis; BSL-3.
  • Psittacosis (Chlamydia psittaci): bird droppings/feather dust - pet shops, poultry; atypical pneumonia.
  • Tularemia (rabbit/deer-fly fever), plague (Yersinia pestis; rodent-flea-human; pneumonic form spreads person-to-person), tetanus (Clostridium tetani; lockjaw; Td booster every 10 years), cat scratch fever (Bartonella), RMSF (Rickettsia; tick-borne; rash starts at wrists/ankles).

Exam tip: Category A bioterrorism agents in the guide: anthrax, plague, tularemia.

Viral and fungal occupational diseases

Viral and fungal infections in the workplace follow distinct transmission routes that determine the control.

  • Hepatitis A: fecal-oral (food handlers, daycare, sewage); self-limiting, no chronic infection; vaccine available.
  • Hepatitis B: blood/body fluids (needlesticks, splashes); can become chronic (cirrhosis, liver cancer); 3-dose vaccine; HBIG + vaccine post-exposure. HBV survives on surfaces up to 7 days.
  • Hepatitis C and HIV: bloodborne; no vaccine; needlestick transmission risk roughly HBV 6-30% (unvaccinated) > HCV about 1.8% > HIV about 0.3%.
  • Rabies: bite/saliva of dogs, bats, raccoons; hydrophobia; nearly always fatal once symptoms appear - immediate wound washing and PEP.
  • Orf (sheep/goats - painful hand nodules); Histoplasmosis (bird/bat droppings - demolition, bridge work); Aspergillosis (construction dust, compost - HEPA in hospitals during renovation); Candidiasis (opportunistic, moist PPE, ICU).
  • Legionella: aerosols from cooling towers, hot water systems; prevented by water management programs (ASHRAE 188).

Exam tip: Hep A = fecal-oral and acute only; Hep B = bloodborne and can be chronic.

Total Worker Health

Total Worker Health (NIOSH) integrates protection from work-related hazards with promotion of injury and illness prevention and worker well-being.

  • Hazard prevention comes first; health promotion supplements, never replaces, controls.
  • Addresses work organization: scheduling, stress, fatigue, workload, supervision.
  • Integrates programs such as fitness, nutrition, smoking cessation, mental health and financial well-being.
  • Participatory approach - workers help design programs; evidence-based evaluation.
  • Reference: NIOSH Fundamentals of Total Worker Health Approaches.

Exam tip: Question stresses 'integration of OSH with health promotion' = Total Worker Health.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 11 Biological Hazards, p. 332; Ch. 15 Statistics for the Safety Professional, p. 408

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

6.2-Q1. Which statement correctly distinguishes incidence from prevalence?

  1. Incidence counts all existing cases; prevalence counts new cases
  2. Incidence counts NEW cases over a period; prevalence counts ALL existing cases at a point or period in time
  3. They are the same
  4. Prevalence applies only to infectious disease

6.2-Q2. In a cohort study, 30 of 1,000 exposed workers and 10 of 1,000 unexposed workers develop a disease. What is the relative risk?

  1. 0.33
  2. 2.0
  3. 3.0
  4. 20

6.2-Q3. In a case-control study, 40 cases were exposed and 60 were not; 20 controls were exposed and 80 were not. What is the odds ratio?

  1. 0.38
  2. 1.5
  3. 2.0
  4. 2.67

6.2-Q4. A cohort study reports a relative risk of 1.4 for solvent exposure and liver disease, with a 95% confidence interval of 0.8 to 2.4. What is the BEST interpretation?

  1. The association is not statistically significant at the 0.05 level because the interval includes 1.0
  2. Solvent exposure is proven to cause a 40% increase in liver disease
  3. Solvent exposure is protective against liver disease
  4. Solvent exposure reduces liver disease risk by 40%

6.2-Q5. Foundry workers show a higher lung cancer rate than office workers, but the foundry workers also smoke at a much higher rate. In this study, smoking is BEST described as a:

  1. Outcome variable
  2. Primary exposure
  3. Random sampling error
  4. Confounding variable

6.2-Q6. At the start of a year, 25 of a plant's 400 workers already have occupational dermatitis. During the year, 15 new cases occur. What is the annual incidence rate per 100 workers at risk?

  1. 3.75
  2. 4.0
  3. 10.0
  4. 6.25

6.2-Q7. What is the primary route of transmission for Tuberculosis (TB)?

  1. Airborne droplets
  2. Skin contact
  3. Blood transfusion
  4. Contaminated water

6.2-Q8. Which indoor contaminant is primarily responsible for Legionnaires' disease outbreaks?

  1. Asbestos
  2. Molds
  3. Legionella bacteria in cooling towers and water systems
  4. Ozone

6.2-Q9. Brucellosis is commonly known as:

  1. Undulant fever
  2. Blackwater fever
  3. Weil's disease
  4. Rabbit fever

6.2-Q10. Which occupation is at highest risk of Leptospirosis infection?

  1. Electricians
  2. Rice-field workers
  3. Office staff
  4. Painters

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BIncidence = new cases over a period among those at risk (measures risk); prevalence = all existing cases at a point or period (measures burden).
2CRR = incidence in exposed / incidence in unexposed = (30/1,000)/(10/1,000) = 3.0. Exposed workers are three times as likely to develop the disease.
3DOR = (a x d)/(b x c) = (40 x 80)/(60 x 20) = 3,200/1,200 = 2.67. Case-control studies yield odds ratios; cohort studies yield relative risks.
4AA 95% CI for a ratio that includes 1.0 (no effect) means the result is not statistically significant. Trap: reading the point estimate of 1.4 as proof of causation.
5DA confounder is linked to both the exposure group and the outcome and can distort the apparent association. It must be controlled by stratification, matching or adjustment.
6BIncidence = new cases / population at risk = 15/(400 - 25) = 15/375 = 4.0 per 100. Trap: 3.75 wrongly includes existing cases in the denominator; 10.0 counts all 40 cases (prevalence).
7ATuberculosis (Mycobacterium tuberculosis) spreads by inhaling airborne droplet nuclei expelled when an infected person coughs or sneezes, so controls focus on airborne isolation and respirators.
8CLegionella pneumophila grows in poorly maintained warm-water systems such as cooling towers and is spread by inhaled aerosols. Water management programs are the main control.
9ABrucellosis, spread through contact with infected animal tissues and fluids (meat packers, dairy and abattoir workers), is called undulant fever because the fever comes and goes. Weil's disease is leptospirosis and rabbit fever is tularemia.
10BLeptospira spreads through water and soil contaminated with animal urine, so rice-field, sewer and farm workers are at highest risk.
Printed book page 216
Domain 6 · Competency 6.3

Toxicology

CSP11 blueprint: Apply toxicology principles to create exposure control plans and develop risk mitigation plans (e.g., using sampling equipment, symptoms of an exposure, LD50, LC50, mutagens, carcinogens, teratogens, ototoxins)

1. Definition

Toxicology is the study of the adverse effects of chemical, physical and biological agents on living organisms. The dose makes the poison (Paracelsus): the effect depends on dose, route of entry, duration and frequency of exposure and individual susceptibility.

2. Explanation in simple words

Every chemical can be harmful at a high enough dose. Know how it gets into the body, what it attacks, how much it takes, and the signs that someone has been exposed - then plan controls to keep the dose low.

3. Key points to remember

  • Routes of entry: inhalation (most important occupationally), skin absorption, ingestion, injection.
  • Dose-response: NOAEL/LOAEL, threshold; LD50 (lethal dose for 50% of test animals, mg/kg, oral/dermal) and LC50 (lethal concentration in air, ppm or mg/m³, over a stated time); lower LD50/LC50 = more toxic. Most OELs are derived from animal toxicology studies.
  • Acute (short-term, high exposure, immediate effects) vs chronic (long-term, low exposure, delayed effects); local vs systemic effects.
  • Interactions: additive (1 + 1 = 2), synergistic (1 + 1 = 5, e.g., asbestos + smoking), potentiation (0 + 1 = 3, non-toxic agent increases another's effect), antagonism (1 + 1 < 2).
  • Target effects: carcinogens (IARC Group 1 = carcinogenic to humans, 2A probably, 2B possibly, 3 not classifiable), mutagens (change DNA), teratogens (birth defects), reproductive toxicants, sensitizers (allergic reaction after repeated exposure - isocyanates, epoxies), neurotoxins, hepatotoxins (liver), nephrotoxins (kidney), ototoxins (hearing - solvents like styrene, toluene; lead, CO), asphyxiants (simple - displace O₂, e.g., N₂, CO₂; chemical - interfere with O₂ use, e.g., CO, H₂S, cyanide), irritants.
  • Particulate lung diseases: silicosis (silica), asbestosis, siderosis (iron oxide), byssinosis (cotton dust), black lung/anthracosis (coal), berylliosis; fibrosis = scarring of lung tissue.
  • Symptoms of exposure: solvents - dizziness, headache, narcosis, defatting dermatitis; CO - headache, cherry-red skin; H₂S - rotten egg odor then olfactory fatigue; metal fume fever (zinc) - flu-like.
  • Exposure control plan: identify agents and SEGs, sampling strategy and equipment, compare with OELs, controls by hierarchy, medical surveillance, training, reassessment.

4. Real work scenario

A furniture plant introduces a new isocyanate-based coating. The safety professional reviews the SDS (respiratory sensitizer, carcinogen category 2), sets up personal sampling with the correct coated filters, installs a ventilated spray booth, adds supplied-air respirators for spraying, starts medical surveillance for sensitization, and trains workers to report wheezing early.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Lower LD50/LC50 = MORE toxic.
  • Synergism vs potentiation is a common definition trap.
  • Teratogen = birth defects; mutagen = DNA changes; carcinogen = cancer; ototoxin = hearing.
  • IARC Group 1 = known human carcinogen.

6. Every blueprint point (9 blueprint items + 6 more)

Routes of entry and dose

Toxic substances enter by inhalation, ingestion, skin/eye absorption or injection. The effect depends on the dose that reaches the target organ.

  • Inhalation is the most common and significant occupational route (gases, vapors, dusts, fumes, mists).
  • Ingestion from poor hygiene (lead dust on hands/food); absorption through intact skin for lipid-soluble chemicals (solvents, pesticides, phenol - 'Skin' notation); injection by needlesticks or high-pressure fluid.
  • Dose = concentration x time (Haber's rule); Paracelsus: 'the dose makes the poison'.
  • Inhaled dose = C x breathing volume x time (x retention). Light work about 10 m3 per 8-h shift: 2 mg/m3 x 10 m3 = 20 mg inhaled.
  • Resting minute ventilation about 5 L/min (guide: 4.3-5.7 L/min) and tidal volume about 500 mL; work raises both.
  • Acute vs chronic exposure and effects; local (at contact site) vs systemic (distributed to target organs).

Exam tip: 'Most significant occupational route of entry' = inhalation.

Dose-response (NOAEL, LOAEL, threshold)

Dose-response describes how the magnitude of effect changes with dose. Thresholds and no-effect levels are used to set exposure limits.

  • Threshold: dose below which no effect occurs (assumed for most non-carcinogens; not for genotoxic carcinogens).
  • NOAEL = highest dose with no observed adverse effect; LOAEL = lowest dose with an observed adverse effect (always above NOAEL).
  • Limits are derived from NOAEL/LOAEL divided by uncertainty (safety) factors, e.g., 10 for animal-to-human, 10 for human variability.
  • Example (guide): benzene animal NOAEL 10 ppm and LOAEL 25 ppm, but OSHA PEL 1 ppm and NIOSH REL 0.1 ppm after safety factors and cancer data.
  • Potency: a steeper curve or lower ED50 means more potent; TD50/ED50 relate toxic to effective dose (therapeutic index).

Exam tip: NOAEL is the regulatory benchmark; LOAEL is used when no NOAEL could be determined.

LD50BLUEPRINT

LD50 is the dose that kills 50% of a test-animal population by a non-inhalation route (oral, dermal, injection), expressed in mg/kg of body weight. Lower LD50 means higher acute toxicity.

  • Units mg/kg body weight; always state species and route (e.g., oral rat).
  • GHS acute oral categories: Cat 1 <= 5, Cat 2 > 5-50, Cat 3 > 50-300, Cat 4 > 300-2,000, Cat 5 > 2,000-5,000 mg/kg. Dermal: 50 / 200 / 1,000 / 2,000 / 5,000.
  • Examples (guide): sodium cyanide oral LD50 about 5.7 mg/kg (Cat 1/2 border); caffeine about 190 mg/kg (Cat 3); table salt about 3,000 mg/kg (Cat 5 range).
  • Signal words: Cat 1-2 'Danger', Cat 3-4 'Warning'; skull-and-crossbones for Cat 1-3, exclamation mark for Cat 4. OSHA HazCom does not adopt Cat 5.
  • Scale-up: lethal dose for a 70-kg person at LD50 50 mg/kg = 3,500 mg (3.5 g) - animal data extrapolate only approximately.

Exam tip: Keywords oral, dermal, injection, mg/kg = LD50. A chemical with LD50 4 mg/kg is MORE toxic than one with 400 mg/kg.

LC50BLUEPRINT

LC50 is the airborne concentration that kills 50% of test animals over a stated exposure time (usually 4 h), used for gases, vapors, dusts, mists and fumes.

  • Units: ppm (gases/vapors) or mg/L (vapors, dusts/mists); exposure duration must be stated.
  • GHS inhalation (gases, ppmV, 4 h): Cat 1 <= 100, Cat 2 <= 500, Cat 3 <= 2,500, Cat 4 <= 20,000.
  • Lower LC50 = more toxic. Example (guide): solvent with LC50 500 ppm (4-h rat) and LD50 200 mg/kg oral.
  • LC50 is an acute measure; it says nothing about chronic, carcinogenic or reproductive effects.
  • Related: LCLo / LDLo = lowest reported lethal concentration/dose; TCLo = lowest toxic concentration.

Exam tip: Keyword 'inhalation' or 'airborne' = LC50, not LD50.

IDLH and acute exposure guidance

IDLH values (NIOSH) mark atmospheres that pose an immediate threat to life, irreversible health effects or impaired escape. They drive respirator selection and emergency planning.

  • IDLH basis: escape within 30 minutes without escape-impairing or irreversible effects (e.g., CO 1,200 ppm, benzene 500 ppm, H2S 50 ppm).
  • Oxygen-deficient atmospheres (< 19.5%) are treated as IDLH under 1910.134 unless the employer shows otherwise.
  • IDLH entry requires pressure-demand full-facepiece SCBA (APF 10,000) or supplied-air with auxiliary escape SCBA, plus standby persons outside.
  • Emergency planning also uses ERPGs (AIHA) and AEGLs (EPA) for community exposure tiers.
  • H2S causes olfactory fatigue at about 100 ppm - smell is not a warning.

Exam tip: Any unknown concentration or IDLH atmosphere = SCBA (pressure-demand), never an air-purifying respirator.

Exposure controlBLUEPRINT

Exposure control applies the hierarchy of controls to chemical, physical and biological agents; respirators are the last line of defense and require a full program.

  • Order: elimination, substitution, engineering (enclosure, isolation, LEV, wet methods), administrative (rotation, scheduling, hygiene, training), PPE.
  • Respiratory protection program (1910.134): written program, program administrator, medical evaluation before fit testing, annual fit test, training, cartridge change schedule, voluntary-use provisions.
  • APFs: half-mask APR 10; full-facepiece APR 50; loose-fitting PAPR 25; full-face PAPR 1,000; supplied-air pressure-demand full face 1,000; SCBA pressure-demand 10,000.
  • Maximum use concentration MUC = APF x PEL. Example: half-mask APF 10, PEL 50 ppm -> MUC 500 ppm (but never above IDLH).
  • Required protection factor = measured concentration / PEL: 300 ppm with PEL 25 ppm needs APF >= 12 -> full-face APR (50).
  • Personal hygiene: no eating/drinking in work areas, change rooms, showers (lead, arsenic, cadmium).

Exam tip: Hazard factor = C/PEL; select the respirator whose APF is at least that number.

Sampling (biological monitoring and toxicological exposure assessment)BLUEPRINT

Toxicological sampling measures how much of an agent the body has absorbed from all routes. Biological monitoring complements air sampling, especially when skin absorption or ingestion matters.

  • Biological Exposure Indices (BEIs, ACGIH) = reference values in blood, urine or exhaled air corresponding to exposure at the TLV.
  • Examples: blood lead (OSHA medical removal at 60 ug/dL or average of last 3 >= 50 ug/dL in general industry; 50 ug/dL in construction), urinary cadmium and beta-2 microglobulin, urinary metabolites (hippuric acid for toluene, phenol/S-PMA for benzene), carboxyhemoglobin for CO, cholinesterase for organophosphates.
  • Sample timing matters: end of shift, end of work week, or prior to shift depending on the agent's half-life.
  • Medical surveillance (pre-placement, periodic, exit) is required by substance standards (lead, cadmium, benzene, silica, asbestos, beryllium).
  • Exposure and medical records retained duration of employment + 30 years (1910.1020); employees have access within 15 working days.

Exam tip: Air sampling misses skin absorption and ingestion - biological monitoring captures total dose.

Exposure symptoms (signature effects of common agents)BLUEPRINT

Many exam questions describe a worker's symptoms and occupation and ask for the likely agent. Learn the signature effects.

  • Benzene - bone marrow suppression, aplastic anemia, leukemia (IARC Group 1). Lead - anemia, wrist/foot drop, slowed reflexes, kidney damage, hypertension, reproductive harm.
  • Arsenic - skin lesions (hyperpigmentation, keratosis) + peripheral neuropathy; skin/lung/bladder cancer. Mercury - tremor, erethism, Minamata disease (methylmercury).
  • Cadmium - acute pulmonary edema; chronic kidney damage (proteinuria) and osteomalacia (Itai-itai). Cr(VI) - nasal septum perforation ('chrome holes'), skin ulcers, lung cancer.
  • Manganese - Parkinson-like manganism in welders (mask face, micrographia). Zinc/copper - metal fume fever (flu-like, resolves 24-48 h). Cobalt (carbide tool grinding) - occupational asthma.
  • Formaldehyde - eye/airway irritation, nasopharyngeal cancer (mortuaries, labs). Antimony - GI effects (diarrhea, vomiting). Aluminum - pulmonary fibrosis.
  • CO - headache, cherry-red skin, hypoxia; H2S - rotten eggs then olfactory paralysis; cyanide - cellular asphyxiant; n-hexane - peripheral neuropathy; methanol - blindness.

Exam tip: Read the occupation clue: 'welding stainless' = Cr(VI) or Mn; 'battery plant' = lead or cadmium; 'galvanized' = zinc.

Pneumoconioses and occupational lung disease

Pneumoconioses are lung diseases caused by inhaled mineral or organic dust. Some are fibrotic and disabling; others are benign.

  • Silicosis - crystalline silica (nodular fibrosis, autoimmune link, lung cancer). Asbestosis - asbestos (also mesothelioma and lung cancer; synergistic with smoking).
  • Berylliosis (chronic beryllium disease) - granulomas that mimic sarcoidosis; sensitization test (BeLPT); P100 filtration; aerospace, electronics, ceramics, nuclear.
  • Coal workers' pneumoconiosis ('black lung'); siderosis - iron oxide (welders), benign/non-fibrotic.
  • Byssinosis ('brown lung') - cotton, flax, hemp dust; chest tightness on the first day back after a break ('Monday fever').
  • Hypersensitivity pneumonitis (farmer's lung - moldy hay); occupational asthma from isocyanates, cobalt, flour, latex.

Exam tip: 'Cotton dust' = byssinosis; 'iron oxide, benign' = siderosis; 'granulomas, aerospace' = berylliosis.

Target organ toxicity (STOT)

Toxicants often act on a specific organ. GHS calls this specific target organ toxicity (single or repeated exposure).

  • Nephrotoxins (kidney): lead, cadmium (proximal tubule, proteinuria), mercury, halogens, ethylene glycol (oxalate crystals), high-dose NSAIDs.
  • Hepatotoxins (liver): carbon tetrachloride (fatty liver, centrilobular necrosis), chloroform, vinyl chloride (angiosarcoma), acetaminophen overdose (NAPQI), ethanol, aflatoxin (Group 1 liver carcinogen).
  • Neurotoxins: lead (cognition, neuropathy), methylmercury (Minamata disease), arsenic (nerve conduction), n-hexane, organophosphates (cholinesterase inhibition), manganese.
  • Hematotoxins: benzene (marrow), CO (carboxyhemoglobin), nitrites/aniline (methemoglobinemia); pulmonary toxins: silica, asbestos, NO2, phosgene (delayed edema).
  • Sensitizers (skin/respiratory): isocyanates, nickel, chromium, epoxy, latex - once sensitized, very low exposure triggers reaction.

Exam tip: Ethylene glycol = oxalate crystals; CCl4 = liver; cadmium = kidney - classic exam pairs.

Chemical interactions (additive, synergistic, potentiation, antagonism)

When two or more chemicals are present, their combined effect may differ from each acting alone.

  • Additive: 2 + 2 = 4 - e.g., toluene + xylene CNS depression (basis of the mixture formula).
  • Synergistic: 2 + 2 > 4 - smoking + asbestos multiplies lung-cancer risk (roughly 50-90x combined vs about 5x asbestos alone and 10x smoking alone).
  • Potentiation: 0 + 2 > 2 - isopropanol (non-hepatotoxic) increases CCl4 liver toxicity.
  • Antagonism: 4 + 4 < 8 or 3 + (-2) = 1 - chelating agents binding heavy metals, antidotes.
  • Ototoxic interaction: solvents (toluene, styrene) + noise cause more hearing loss than either alone.

Exam tip: 'One chemical has no effect alone but increases the other's toxicity' = potentiation, not synergism.

MutagensBLUEPRINT

Mutagens damage DNA and cause heritable genetic changes. Germ-cell mutations can pass to offspring; somatic mutations can lead to cancer.

  • GHS germ cell mutagenicity: Category 1A (known human evidence), 1B (presumed), 2 (suspected).
  • Examples: benzene (bone marrow, leukemia), ethylene oxide (sterilizers), vinyl chloride (liver angiosarcoma), ionizing radiation.
  • Tests: Ames test (bacterial reverse mutation) screens for mutagenicity; many mutagens are also carcinogens.
  • Control as non-threshold agents: minimize exposure (ALARA), closed systems, substitution.

Exam tip: Keywords 'DNA damage' and 'heritable' = mutagen; 'fetus during pregnancy only' = teratogen.

CarcinogensBLUEPRINT

Carcinogens cause or promote cancer. Several bodies classify them by strength of evidence; only OSHA's list is legally enforceable.

  • IARC: Group 1 carcinogenic (asbestos, benzene, silica, arsenic, formaldehyde, diesel exhaust since 2012), 2A probably (glyphosate, night-shift work), 2B possibly (RF-EMF, gasoline engine exhaust, chloroform), 3 not classifiable (caffeine). The guide lists Group 4 'probably not' (caprolactam) and diesel as 2A; IARC removed Group 4 in 2019 and diesel exhaust has been Group 1 since 2012.
  • ACGIH: A1 confirmed human, A2 suspected human, A3 confirmed animal (unknown human relevance), A4 not classifiable, A5 not suspected.
  • NTP Report on Carcinogens: 'known to be' vs 'reasonably anticipated to be' human carcinogens.
  • OSHA regulates specific carcinogens with substance standards (asbestos, benzene, formaldehyde, Cr(VI), vinyl chloride, coke oven emissions, 13 carcinogens standard 1910.1003).
  • GHS carcinogenicity Category 1A/1B (Danger, health hazard pictogram) and 2 (Warning).
  • Latency is long (10-40 years for asbestos-related cancers); most are treated as non-threshold.

Exam tip: Group 1 / A1 / 'known' = confirmed in humans; 2A = probable; 2B = possible. Enforceable = OSHA.

TeratogensBLUEPRINT

Teratogens harm the developing embryo or fetus during pregnancy, causing birth defects; unlike mutagens, their effects are not passed to later generations.

  • Examples: thalidomide (limb malformations), ethanol (fetal alcohol syndrome), methylmercury (fetal brain), lead, ionizing radiation, some anticancer drugs and anesthetic gases.
  • Most sensitive period: organogenesis, roughly weeks 3-8 of gestation - often before pregnancy is known.
  • Reproductive toxicants also include agents affecting fertility (lead, DBCP, ethylene glycol ethers).
  • Program approach: hazard communication, exposure minimization for all workers (not exclusion of women), NRC declared-pregnant-worker limit 0.5 rem over the pregnancy.

Exam tip: Fetal protection policies that bar women from jobs are illegal (UAW v. Johnson Controls, 1991) - control the exposure instead.

OtotoxinsBLUEPRINT

Ototoxic chemicals damage the cochlea or auditory nerve and can cause hearing loss on their own or worsen noise-induced loss.

  • Solvents: toluene, styrene, xylene, ethylbenzene, n-hexane, trichloroethylene.
  • Asphyxiants: carbon monoxide, hydrogen cyanide; metals: lead, mercury, organotins.
  • Combined solvent + noise exposure produces greater-than-additive hearing loss.
  • OSHA/NIOSH (2018 SHIB) recommend audiometric testing for workers exposed to ototoxicants even below 85 dBA; ACGIH notes ototoxic agents in the noise TLV.
  • Controls: reduce both noise and chemical exposure; hearing protectors do not prevent ototoxic damage from inhaled/absorbed chemicals.

Exam tip: Painter exposed to toluene + noise with unexpected hearing loss = ototoxic synergy.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 7 Toxicology, p. 251; Ch. 6 Particulates and Gases, p. 228

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

6.3-Q1. Chemical A has a 4-hour rat inhalation LC50 of 50 ppm; Chemical B has an LC50 of 5,000 ppm. Which is correct?

  1. B is more acutely toxic because its number is larger
  2. A is more acutely toxic by inhalation
  3. LC50 measures carcinogenic potency
  4. Neither can be compared without an LD50

6.3-Q2. A chemical has an oral LD50 of 4 mg/kg. What is its GHS acute toxicity category?

  1. Category 1
  2. Category 2
  3. Category 3
  4. Category 4

6.3-Q3. Isopropyl alcohol, which is not itself hepatotoxic, greatly increases the liver toxicity of carbon tetrachloride. What is this interaction called?

  1. Additive
  2. Synergistic
  3. Antagonistic
  4. Potentiation

6.3-Q4. Smoking combined with asbestos exposure increases lung cancer risk far beyond the sum of the individual risks. What interaction is this?

  1. Additive
  2. Antagonistic
  3. Synergistic
  4. Potentiation

6.3-Q5. A worker is exposed to acetone at 300 ppm (TLV 500 ppm) and MEK at 100 ppm (TLV 200 ppm). Both affect the same target organs (additive). Is the mixture limit exceeded?

  1. No - each is below its own TLV
  2. Yes - the sum of ratios is 1.1
  3. No - the sum of ratios is 0.8
  4. Yes - the sum of ratios is 2.0

6.3-Q6. What does a Group 1 classification by the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) indicate about a substance?

  1. The substance is not classifiable as to its carcinogenicity to humans.
  2. The substance is probably not carcinogenic to humans.
  3. The substance is possibly carcinogenic to humans.
  4. The substance is carcinogenic to humans.

6.3-Q7. A welder cutting galvanized steel develops fever, chills, a metallic taste and muscle aches several hours after the shift. Symptoms resolve within two days. Which fume is the MOST likely cause?

  1. Iron oxide
  2. Manganese
  3. Tin oxide
  4. Zinc oxide

6.3-Q8. Area noise levels average 82 dBA (below the 85 dBA action level), yet several workers in a fiberglass boat lay-up area that uses styrene resin show standard threshold shifts. What is the MOST likely contributing factor?

  1. Normal age-related hearing loss only
  2. An uncalibrated audiometer
  3. Ototoxic styrene exposure acting together with noise
  4. Overuse of hearing protection

6.3-Q9. Section 11 of an SDS states that a solvent caused malformations in the offspring of pregnant test animals but showed no germ-cell mutations. How is this solvent BEST classified?

  1. Teratogen
  2. Mutagen
  3. Sensitizer
  4. Carcinogen

6.3-Q10. An industrial hygienist must measure a painter's 8-hour exposure to toluene vapor. Which sampling method is MOST appropriate?

  1. Activated charcoal sorbent tube on a low-flow personal pump in the breathing zone
  2. Mixed cellulose ester (MCE) filter cassette
  3. PVC filter with a cyclone pre-selector
  4. Area grab sample taken at the room exhaust fan

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BLC50 is the air concentration lethal to 50% of test animals over a stated time. A LOWER LC50 means GREATER acute inhalation toxicity, so A (50 ppm) is more toxic.
2AUnder GHS, acute oral toxicity Category 1 is LD50 <= 5 mg/kg, so 4 mg/kg is Category 1 (the most severe). Lower category numbers mean higher toxicity.
3DPotentiation: a substance with no toxicity of its own (for example isopropanol) increases another's toxicity (carbon tetrachloride liver damage): 0 + 2 > 2. Synergism requires both agents to be toxic.
4CSynergism: two toxic agents together produce an effect much greater than the sum of their individual effects. Smoking plus asbestos multiplies lung cancer risk.
5BFor additive mixtures, Em = C1/T1 + C2/T2 = 300/500 + 100/200 = 0.6 + 0.5 = 1.1. Because this exceeds 1.0, the mixture limit is exceeded even though each chemical alone is below its TLV.
6DIARC Group 1 = carcinogenic to humans (sufficient human evidence). 2A = probably, 2B = possibly, 3 = not classifiable.
7DInhaling freshly formed zinc oxide fume (for example when welding galvanized steel) causes metal fume fever: flu-like fever and chills that resolve within 24-48 h.
8COtotoxins such as styrene, toluene, lead and carbon monoxide can damage hearing on their own and add to the effect of noise, causing hearing loss below noise limits.
9AA teratogen causes birth defects in a developing embryo or fetus. A mutagen alters genetic material (DNA), and a carcinogen causes cancer. Exposure control plans should address pregnant workers.
10AOrganic vapors such as toluene are collected on sorbent media (charcoal tube or passive badge) in the breathing zone. Filters collect particulates, and a cyclone is for respirable dust.
Printed book page 224
Domain 6 · Competency 6.4

Ergonomics and Human Factors

CSP11 blueprint: Evaluate principles related to ergonomics and human factors (e.g., visual acuity, body mechanics, lifting, vibration, anthropometrics, fatigue management)

1. Definition

Ergonomics (human factors engineering) is the science of fitting the job, equipment and environment to the worker's capabilities and limitations to prevent musculoskeletal disorders (MSDs), reduce error and improve performance.

2. Explanation in simple words

Design the work to fit the person, not the person to fit the work. Reduce force, awkward postures, repetition, vibration and fatigue, and design controls and displays that people can see and use without mistakes.

3. Key points to remember

  • MSD risk factors: force, repetition, awkward/static postures, contact stress, vibration, cold. Common MSDs: carpal tunnel syndrome (median nerve at wrist), lateral epicondylitis ('tennis elbow'), De Quervain's tenosynovitis (thumb-side wrist), rotator cuff injuries, low back pain, trigger finger.
