  • Ergonomics program elements: management commitment, employee involvement, job analysis (RULA, REBA, NIOSH lifting equation, Strain Index, Snook tables), controls (engineering first), training, medical management, evaluation.
  • Lifting: Revised NIOSH equation RWL = 51 × HM × VM × DM × AM × FM × CM (HM = 10/H in inches); LI = load ÷ RWL. Best lifting zone between knuckle and shoulder height, close to the body. Back belts are not proven effective (NIOSH).
  • Neutral posture: wrists straight, elbows close to body at about 90°, shoulders relaxed, knees about hip height, feet flat, lumbar support; monitor top at or slightly below eye level about an arm's length away.
  • Vibration: hand-arm (HAVS, Raynaud's phenomenon - white finger) and whole-body (low back, vehicles); A(8) = a × √(T ÷ 8); controls - low-vibration tools, reduced exposure time, warm hands.
  • Anthropometrics: body dimensions by percentile; design reach for the 5th percentile (small) user, clearance for the 95th percentile (large) user, adjustability for the 5th-95th range.
  • Visual acuity and displays: Snellen 20/40 = sees at 20 ft what normal sees at 40 ft; lighting levels, glare control, contrast; display/control compatibility (population stereotypes - clockwise to increase).
  • Human factors: human error types (slips, lapses, mistakes, violations), error-proofing (poka-yoke), workload, situational awareness.
  • Fatigue management: shift length and rotation (forward rotation), circadian rhythm, sleep debt, fatigue risk management systems, rest breaks, limits on consecutive night shifts.

4. Real work scenario

Warehouse workers lift 40-lb cases from floor-level pallets to a 36-in. conveyor 300 times a shift and report back pain. A NIOSH analysis gives LI ≈ 2.0. The company installs pallet lift tables that keep the top layer at waist height and a vacuum lift assist for heavy items; the LI falls below 1.0 and back complaints drop.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Engineering redesign beats training, stretching and back belts in ergonomics questions.
  • LI = load ÷ RWL; LI > 1 means increased risk.
  • Reach → 5th percentile; clearance → 95th percentile.
  • Know where MSDs occur: epicondylitis = elbow; De Quervain's = thumb side of wrist; Raynaud's = fingers (vibration/cold).

6. Every blueprint point (6 blueprint items + 3 more)

Visual acuityBLUEPRINT

Visual acuity is the sharpness of vision needed for accurate, safe work. Lighting, glare, contrast, display design and the aging workforce all affect it.

  • Snellen 20/20 = normal acuity; aging (presbyopia) reduces near focus and increases light needs - a 60-year-old needs about 2-3 times the light of a 20-year-old.
  • IES recommended illuminance (guide): offices/VDT 300-500 lux (30-50 fc); drafting 750-1,000 lux; labs 500-1,000; precision assembly/inspection 1,000-2,000 lux (100-200 fc); active warehouses 200-300 lux.
  • 1 footcandle = 10.76 lux. Illuminance from a point source follows the inverse square law: E = I/d^2.
  • Glare: direct (light source in field of view) and reflected (screen, glossy surfaces); control with diffusers, indirect lighting, screen positioning perpendicular to windows.
  • Controls: task lighting, adjustable contrast, anti-glare screens, regular vision testing, prescription safety eyewear.
  • OSHA 1926.56 sets minimum illumination for construction (e.g., 5 fc general construction areas).

Exam tip: Increasing overall room light can worsen screen glare - task lighting and positioning are often the better answer.

Body mechanicsBLUEPRINT

Good body mechanics keep the body in a neutral posture - straight spine, elbows close to the body, wrists straight - and minimize twisting, bending, overhead work and long reaches.

  • Neutral posture minimizes muscle effort and joint stress; awkward postures multiply the force on the spine and tendons.
  • Spinal disc compression rises sharply with load distance from the body (moment arm); L5/S1 is the most stressed disc. NIOSH action limit for disc compression is 3,400 N (770 lb); 6,400 N is the maximum permissible limit.
  • Use power zone: between mid-thigh and shoulder height, close to the body.
  • Pushing is preferred over pulling; avoid twisting - turn with the feet.
  • Controls: adjustable work heights, tool redesign, training on posture, job design to reduce static and repetitive positions.

Exam tip: A load held 20 in. from the spine stresses the back far more than the same load held at 10 in. - reduce horizontal distance.

Lifting (NIOSH lifting equation)BLUEPRINT

The revised NIOSH Lifting Equation computes a Recommended Weight Limit (RWL) for two-handed manual lifts; the Lifting Index compares the actual load to the RWL.

  • RWL = LC x HM x VM x DM x AM x FM x CM, with load constant LC = 51 lb (23 kg).
  • HM = 10/H (in.) [25/H cm], H 10-25 in.; VM = 1 - 0.0075|V - 30| (in.); DM = 0.82 + 1.8/D (in.); AM = 1 - 0.0032A (degrees); FM from frequency/duration table; CM from coupling (good/fair/poor) table.
  • Lifting Index LI = load / RWL. LI <= 1 nominal risk; LI > 1 increased risk; LI > 3 high risk for most workers.
  • Example: H = 20 in. (HM 0.5), V = 30 in. (VM 1.0), D = 10 in. (DM 1.0), A = 0, FM = CM = 1 -> RWL = 25.5 lb. A 30-lb box -> LI = 1.18 (increased risk).
  • Biggest lever: horizontal distance - halving H from 20 to 10 in. doubles the RWL.
  • Equation does not apply to one-handed, seated, kneeling, team lifts, high-speed lifting or unstable loads; consider Snook/Liberty Mutual tables or ACGIH lifting TLV.

Exam tip: If V is above 30 in. or below, VM drops; any multiplier is <= 1, so the RWL can never exceed 51 lb.

VibrationBLUEPRINT

Vibration transmitted to the hands (hand-arm) or whole body damages vessels, nerves and the spine. OSHA has no vibration PEL; ACGIH TLVs and ISO standards apply.

  • Hand-arm vibration (HAV) about 8-1,000 Hz (guide: 50-1,000 Hz) from grinders, chainsaws, jackhammers -> HAVS / vibration white finger (Raynaud's phenomenon): blanching in cold, numbness, loss of dexterity.
  • Whole-body vibration (WBV) about 0.5-80 Hz from trucks, forklifts, mining and construction vehicles -> low back pain, spinal degeneration, fatigue.
  • ACGIH (guide values): HAV TLV 5 m/s2 A(8), action level 2.5 m/s2; WBV about 0.5-1.0 m/s2 A(8). ISO 5349 = HAV; ISO 2631 = WBV.
  • Measured with accelerometers (frequency-weighted acceleration, m/s2).
  • Controls: low-vibration tools, tool maintenance/balancing, isolation mounts and suspension seats, limiting exposure time, keeping hands warm and dry, lighter grip; anti-vibration gloves have limited value.

Exam tip: 'White fingers in winter + power tools' = HAVS; 'truck driver back pain' = WBV.

AnthropometricsBLUEPRINT

Anthropometrics is the measurement of human body dimensions used to design workplaces, tools and equipment that fit the user population.

  • Common design range: 5th percentile female to 95th percentile male (about 90-95% of users).
  • Reach distances and control forces: design for the smallest/weakest (5th percentile female).
  • Clearances (doorways, aisles, headroom) and tool handles/openings: design for the largest (95th percentile male).
  • Work surfaces and seats: make adjustable across the 5th-95th range; standing work height about elbow height (lower for heavy work, higher for precision).
  • Designing for the 'average' person fits almost nobody; use adjustability and universal design.

Exam tip: Reach = smallest user; clearance = largest user; height = adjustable.

FatigueBLUEPRINT

Fatigue is reduced physical and mental capability caused by long hours, shift work, inadequate sleep, heavy or monotonous work. It increases errors, slower reactions and incidents.

  • Hazards: night and rotating shifts, extended overtime, long commutes, sleep disorders, circadian low point roughly 2-6 a.m.
  • About 17-19 hours awake impairs performance comparably to a BAC of about 0.05%; 24 hours about 0.10%.
  • IARC classifies night shift work as Group 2A (probably carcinogenic).
  • Controls: fatigue risk management system, forward-rotating shifts (day -> evening -> night), limit consecutive nights and 12-h shifts, guaranteed rest days, micro-breaks, task rotation, fatigue monitoring.
  • Transport: DOT/FMCSA hours-of-service and NRC 10 CFR 26 fatigue rules for regulated industries.

Exam tip: 'Night shift' in a question should make you think of both performance decline and IARC 2A.

Musculoskeletal disorders and risk factors

Work-related MSDs (cumulative trauma disorders) are injuries to muscles, tendons, nerves and joints that develop over time from repeated stress.

  • Primary risk factors: force, repetition, awkward/static posture; secondary: contact stress, vibration, cold, inadequate recovery time; personal factors: diabetes, obesity, pregnancy, arthritis.
  • Carpal tunnel syndrome = median nerve compression at the wrist: numbness/tingling in thumb, index, middle and half of ring finger, worse at night, thenar atrophy when chronic.
  • Others: tendinitis, tenosynovitis, De Quervain's (thumb), trigger finger, epicondylitis (tennis elbow), rotator cuff injury, low back pain.
  • Assessment tools: RULA, REBA, Strain Index, ACGIH HAL TLV, Snook tables, checklists, symptom surveys and OSHA 300 log trends.
  • Controls: engineering (tool redesign, lift assists, workstation adjustment, automation), administrative (job rotation, rest breaks, pacing), training on neutral posture, early medical management.

Exam tip: Early reporting and medical management is a core ergonomics-program element - it catches MSDs before they become lost-time cases.

Ergonomics program and workstation design

An effective ergonomics program follows a systematic process and is enforced in the US through the General Duty Clause and industry guidelines.

  • Elements: management commitment, employee involvement, job hazard analysis, hazard prevention/control, training, medical management, program evaluation.
  • Computer workstation: monitor about an arm's length away with the top at or slightly below eye level; elbows about 90 degrees; wrists neutral; feet flat or on a footrest; ANSI/HFES 100.
  • Human factors design principles: follow population stereotypes (clockwise/up = increase, red = stop), make controls distinguishable, design out error (poka-yoke), keep displays simple.
  • Human error types: slips and lapses (skill-based), mistakes (rule- or knowledge-based), violations (intentional) - each needs a different control.
  • OSHA has industry guidelines (poultry, nursing homes, retail grocery, shipyards) but no general ergonomics standard (the 2000 rule was repealed in 2001).

Exam tip: Retraining fixes knowledge-based mistakes, not slips - slips need design changes.

Fitness for duty and return to work

Fitness for duty confirms a worker can safely perform the job; return-to-work programs bring injured or ill workers back gradually and safely.

  • Fitness for duty covers medical fitness (vision, hearing, strength), substance use testing (DOT, NRC 10 CFR 26) and psychological fitness (fatigue, stress).
  • Evaluations must be job-related and consistent with business necessity (ADA); use essential functions from job descriptions.
  • Return to work: medical clearance, transitional/light duty, written work restrictions, coordination among employer, provider, insurer and employee, follow-up monitoring.
  • Early RTW reduces lost workdays, claim costs and disability duration; modified duty converts lost-time cases to restricted cases on the OSHA 300 log.

Exam tip: 'Worker capable of performing safely' = fitness for duty; 'gradual, monitored reintegration' = return to work.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 24 Ergonomics, p. 546; Ch. 13 Thermal Stressors (fatigue/heat)

7. Ten CSP-style practice questions

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

6.4-Q1. A forklift driver's vision test shows 20/40 in each eye. What does this mean?

  1. The driver sees at 40 ft what normal vision sees at 20 ft
  2. The driver sees at 20 ft what a person with normal vision sees at 40 ft
  3. The driver has double normal acuity
  4. The driver is legally blind

6.4-Q2. When designing a control panel, which anthropometric rule is MOST appropriate for REACH distances?

  1. Design for the 5th percentile (small) user so nearly everyone can reach
  2. Design for the 95th percentile male
  3. Design for the 50th percentile (average) user
  4. Anthropometrics do not apply to reach

6.4-Q3. In anthropometric design, clearances such as door height are based on which percentile?

  1. 5th percentile female
  2. 50th percentile male
  3. 95th percentile male
  4. 100th percentile male

6.4-Q4. For a lifting task the load weight is 70 lb and the NIOSH Recommended Weight Limit (RWL) is 20 lb. What is the Lifting Index (LI = LW/RWL)?

  1. 1.00
  2. 2.50
  3. 4.00
  4. 3.50

6.4-Q5. A NIOSH lifting analysis gives these multipliers: HM 0.625, VM 0.93, DM 0.91, AM 1.0, FM 0.88, CM 0.95. The load is 30 lb. What are the RWL and Lifting Index? (RWL = 51 lb x HM x VM x DM x AM x FM x CM)

  1. RWL 22.6 lb; LI 0.75
  2. RWL 22.6 lb; LI 1.33
  3. RWL 51 lb; LI 0.59
  4. RWL 30 lb; LI 1.00

6.4-Q6. At a fitness equipment plant, four back injuries this year involved lifting a 110-lb box from a 36-in-high conveyor to a 4-in-high pallet 36 in away. What is the BEST solution?

  1. Rotate the task so each worker lifts the product only two hours a day
  2. Lower the conveyor to 28 inches and ensure two workers lift the boxes
  3. Provide the worker with safe lifting training and implement a stretching program
  4. Provide a vacuum lift-assist device to lift the box to the pallet

6.4-Q7. On an assembly line, workers repeatedly reach more than 20 in in front of their bodies to grab parts from a supply bin, and several report shoulder fatigue late in the shift. Which improvement would MOST effectively reduce the shoulder strain?

  1. Increase job rotation so workers switch positions every two hours
  2. Provide anti-fatigue mats to reduce discomfort while standing
  3. Move the parts bin closer to the worker to reduce extended forward reaches
  4. Require workers to stretch before starting their shift

6.4-Q8. Hand-Arm Vibration Syndrome (HAVS) is also known as:

  1. Raynaud's Phenomenon / Vibration White Finger
  2. Siderosis
  3. Carpal Tunnel Syndrome
  4. Berylliosis

6.4-Q9. Which fatigue-management measure is MOST effective in reducing the risk of human error in shift work?

  1. Long shifts with fewer breaks
  2. Rotating shifts forward with regular rest days
  3. Fixed night shifts every week
  4. Increasing overtime hours

6.4-Q10. Workers in an assembly line are reporting back pain and discomfort. What initial step should you take to address potential ergonomic issues?

  1. Conduct an ergonomic assessment to identify risk factors contributing to discomfort.
  2. Provide training on proper lifting techniques and posture.
  3. Redesign the workstation layout to minimize awkward postures and repetitive motions.
  4. Implement job rotation to reduce the physical strain on workers.

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BSnellen 20/40 means the person must be at 20 ft to see what a person with normal vision sees at 40 ft, which is worse than normal. 20/200 is the usual legal-blindness threshold.
2AReach is designed for small users (5th percentile female) so about 95% of people can reach. Clearances are designed for large users (95th percentile male).
3CClearances such as door height and legroom use the largest expected user (95th percentile male) so nearly everyone fits. A 100th percentile does not exist in design practice.
4DLI = LW/RWL = 70/20 = 3.5. An LI above 1.0 means increased risk, and above 3.0 most workers are at high risk, so redesign is needed.
5BRWL = 51 x 0.625 x 0.93 x 0.91 x 1.0 x 0.88 x 0.95 = 22.6 lb; LI = 30/22.6 = 1.33 (above 1.0, so the task needs redesign). Trap: dividing RWL by load gives 0.75.
6DFour injuries and a 110-lb box need an engineering control. A vacuum lift assist removes the manual lift. Rotation and training are administrative controls that leave the hazard in place.
7CReducing the reach distance (moving the bin into the primary reach zone) removes the risk factor at its source. Rotation, mats and stretching do not change the reach.
8AHand-arm vibration syndrome (vibration white finger, a secondary Raynaud's phenomenon) comes from prolonged use of vibrating hand tools. It causes finger blanching, numbness and loss of dexterity.
9BForward-rotating shifts (day to evening to night) with regular rest days fit the circadian rhythm and limit cumulative fatigue. Long shifts, fixed nights and overtime increase fatigue and error.
10AThe FIRST step is an ergonomic assessment (job hazard / risk-factor analysis) to identify causes. Controls such as redesign or rotation should follow from what it finds.

Domain 6 · Competency 6.5

Chemistry: Containment Volume and Storage Requirements

CSP11 blueprint: Apply chemistry principles to calculate required containment volumes and hazardous materials storage requirements

1. Definition

Applied chemistry for the CSP covers the properties of chemicals (states of matter, molecular weight, vapor pressure, flash point, pH, reactivity), gas laws, concentration conversions and the calculations needed to size secondary containment and comply with storage limits.

2. Explanation in simple words

Know how chemicals behave - how fast they evaporate, whether vapors sink, whether they burn or react - and be able to calculate how big a dike must be and how much you are allowed to store.

3. Key points to remember

  • Containment volume: required volume = % × largest container (e.g., 110%); convert gallons to ft³ (÷ 7.48) or liters to m³ (÷ 1,000); wall height = volume ÷ floor area; subtract volume displaced by other tanks inside the dike.
  • Geometry: cylinder V = πr²h; rectangle V = L × W × H; 1 ft³ = 7.48 gal; 1 gal = 3.785 L; 1 m³ = 264.2 gal.
  • Gas laws (use absolute temperature and pressure): Boyle P₁V₁ = P₂V₂; Charles V₁/T₁ = V₂/T₂; Gay-Lussac P₁/T₁ = P₂/T₂; combined P₁V₁/T₁ = P₂V₂/T₂; ideal gas PV = nRT (R = 0.0821 L·atm/mol·K or 8.314 J/mol·K). K = °C + 273; °R = °F + 460; psia = psig + 14.7. 1 mole of gas = 24.45 L at 25 °C, 22.4 L at 0 °C.
  • Concentration: ppm = mg/m³ × 24.45 ÷ MW; % to ppm: 1% = 10,000 ppm; % LEL readings convert using the LEL (e.g., 10% of methane LEL 5% = 0.5% = 5,000 ppm).
  • pH = −log[H⁺]; 7 neutral; < 7 acid; > 7 base/caustic; each unit is a 10× change. Neutralization of strong acids and bases is highly exothermic.
  • Properties: vapor density (> 1 heavier than air, collects low), specific gravity (< 1 floats on water), vapor pressure (higher = evaporates faster), flash point, boiling point, LEL/UEL, autoignition temperature.
  • Storage requirements: flammable cabinets (60 gal Cat 1-3, 120 gal Cat 4, max 3 per fire area), container size limits by category, storage rooms (ventilation 6 ACH), separation of compressed gas oxygen and fuel gas cylinders by 20 ft or a 5-ft-high ½-hour fire-rated barrier, maximum allowable quantities per control area (IFC/NFPA 400), incompatibility segregation.
  • Balanced equations and chemical change (e.g., Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂; rusting is a chemical change).

4. Real work scenario

A dike must hold 110% of the largest of three tanks (10,000, 8,000 and 5,000 gal). Required volume = 11,000 gal ÷ 7.48 = 1,470.6 ft³. The dike floor is 40 × 30 ft (1,200 ft²), so the minimum wall height is 1.23 ft before allowing for the other tanks' displacement; the engineer specifies 1.6 ft to include displacement and freeboard.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Containment questions use the LARGEST tank, not the total, unless the rule says otherwise.
  • Gas-law problems require absolute temperature and pressure - a frequent trap.
  • 1% = 10,000 ppm.
  • Higher vapor density means vapors flow to low points (pits, trenches).

6. Every blueprint point (2 blueprint items + 4 more)

Containment-volume calculationsBLUEPRINT

Secondary containment must hold the release from the largest container plus allowances. Calculate the required volume, then size the dike or berm.

  • Rule set: SPCC (40 CFR 112) - capacity of the largest container plus freeboard for precipitation (110% is the common design value); RCRA container areas (40 CFR 264.175) - 10% of total volume or 100% of the largest container, whichever is greater (the guide states 110% of the largest). NFPA 30 dikes - at least the capacity of the largest tank.
  • Conversions: 1 ft3 = 7.48 gal; 1 gal water = 8.34 lb; 1 m3 = 1,000 L = 264.2 gal.
  • Rectangular dike volume = L x W x H. Cylinder volume = pi x r^2 x h.
  • Example: largest tank 10,000 gal -> 110% = 11,000 gal / 7.48 = 1,470.6 ft3. Dike 30 ft x 25 ft (750 ft2) -> wall height = 1,470.6/750 = 1.96 ft (about 2 ft, plus freeboard).
  • Example: ten 55-gal drums (550 gal total) -> 10% = 55 gal vs largest 55 gal -> 55 gal required under RCRA (110% rule gives 60.5 gal).
  • Subtract volume displaced by other tanks/foundations inside the dike below the wall height.

Exam tip: Always use the LARGEST container (not the sum) for the 100/110% rule, then compare with 10% of the total.

Hazardous-material storage requirementsBLUEPRINT

Storage requirements limit quantities, separate incompatibles, control ignition sources and provide containment, ventilation and fire protection.

  • OSHA 1910.106 flammable liquid categories: Cat 1 FP < 73.4 F and BP <= 95 F; Cat 2 FP < 73.4 F and BP > 95 F; Cat 3 FP 73.4-140 F; Cat 4 FP 140-199.4 F. NFPA classes: IA, IB, IC (FP < 100 F), II (100-140 F), IIIA (140-200 F), IIIB (>= 200 F).
  • Flammable storage cabinets: max 60 gal Cat 1-3 or 120 gal Cat 4 per cabinet; no more than 3 cabinets in one fire area (more allowed in sprinklered areas per NFPA 30). Safety cans <= 5 gal with flame arrester and spring-closing lid.
  • Outside a room or cabinet in one area: max 25 gal Cat 1 in containers, 120 gal Cat 2-4 in containers, 660 gal Cat 2-4 in one portable tank (1910.106(e)).
  • Segregate incompatibles: oxidizers from flammables, acids from bases and cyanides/sulfides, water-reactives away from water/sprinklers; NFPA 400 hazardous materials code; SDS Sections 7 and 10.
  • Compressed gases: upright, secured, caps on; oxygen 20 ft from fuel gases or 5-ft half-hour barrier. Peroxide formers (ethers): date on receipt/opening, test or dispose per schedule.
  • Bond and ground when transferring Class I liquids; ventilation and Class I Div 2 electrical in storage rooms; NFPA 704 diamond (0-4, 4 = most severe; opposite of GHS where Cat 1 is most severe).

Exam tip: NFPA 704: 4 = worst. GHS category: 1 = worst. Know which scale the question uses.

Concentration units and ppm to mg/m3 conversion

Airborne concentrations of gases and vapors are expressed as ppm (by volume) or mg/m3 (by mass). Conversion uses the molar volume of an ideal gas.

  • mg/m3 = ppm x MW / 24.45; ppm = mg/m3 x 24.45 / MW (25 C, 1 atm; use 22.4 at 0 C).
  • Example: toluene (MW 92.14) at 100 ppm -> 100 x 92.14 / 24.45 = 376.9 mg/m3.
  • Example: 50 mg/m3 of acetone (MW 58.08) -> 50 x 24.45 / 58.08 = 21.0 ppm.
  • 1% by volume = 10,000 ppm; 1 ppm = 0.0001%. Example: LEL 1.2% = 12,000 ppm.
  • Particulates and fibers cannot be expressed in ppm - use mg/m3 or fibers/cc.
  • Molar volume adjusts with temperature/pressure: V = 24.45 x (T/298) x (760/P).

Exam tip: 24.45 is the magic number - if a question gives MW and asks to convert, this is the formula.

Flammability properties (flash point, LEL/UEL, vapor pressure, vapor density)

Fire and explosion hazard of a chemical is predicted from its physical properties on SDS Section 9.

  • Flash point: lowest temperature at which vapors ignite with an ignition source; fire point slightly higher; autoignition temperature ignites without a spark.
  • Flammable range LEL-UEL: gasoline about 1.4-7.6%, methane 5-15%, hydrogen 4-75%, acetylene 2.5-100% (wider range = more hazardous). Confined-space alarm at 10% LEL.
  • Mixture LEL (Le Chatelier): LEL_mix = 100 / (P1/LEL1 + P2/LEL2 + ...). Example: 50% methane (5%) + 50% propane (2.1%) -> 100/(10 + 23.8) = 2.96%.
  • Vapor pressure: higher = evaporates faster (more airborne exposure). Vapor density (air = 1): > 1 sinks to low areas (propane 1.55, H2S 1.19, gasoline vapors 3-4); < 1 rises (methane 0.55, hydrogen 0.07).
  • Specific gravity (water = 1): < 1 floats (most hydrocarbons - water spreads the fire); > 1 sinks. Weight = gallons x 8.34 x SG.
  • Water solubility determines whether water dilutes (alcohols) or spreads (hydrocarbons) a spill fire - use alcohol-resistant foam for polar solvents.

Exam tip: Vapor density > 1 means look for vapors at floor level, pits and trenches.

Gas laws and pressure

Gas laws predict how pressure, volume and temperature of a confined gas change. Always use absolute temperature and absolute pressure.

  • Combined gas law: P1V1/T1 = P2V2/T2. Boyle (T constant): P1V1 = P2V2. Charles (P constant): V1/T1 = V2/T2. Ideal gas: PV = nRT.
  • Absolute temperature: K = C + 273; Rankine R = F + 460. Absolute pressure: psia = psig + 14.7.
  • Example: cylinder at 2,000 psig and 70 F heated to 150 F -> P2 = 2,014.7 x (610/530) = 2,318.8 psia = 2,304 psig.
  • Liquid-to-gas expansion ratios are large (e.g., liquid nitrogen about 1:694, LPG about 1:270) - oxygen-deficiency and BLEVE hazards.
  • Water pressure head: 0.433 psi per ft of water; 1 atm = 14.7 psi = 33.9 ft of water = 760 mm Hg.

Exam tip: Classic error: using F or psig directly in gas law ratios - convert to absolute units first.

Corrosives, reactivity and pH

Corrosives destroy tissue and materials; reactive chemicals can release energy or toxic products when mixed, heated, shocked or exposed to air or water.

  • pH scale 0-14: < 7 acidic, > 7 basic; each unit is a 10-fold change. GHS skin corrosion Cat 1 typically pH <= 2 or >= 11.5. RCRA corrosivity characteristic: pH <= 2 or >= 12.5.
  • Bases (caustics) penetrate deeper than acids (liquefaction necrosis); hydrofluoric acid needs calcium gluconate first aid.
  • Eyewash/shower (1910.151(c); ANSI Z358.1): reachable within 10 s (about 55 ft), flush 15 min, tepid water 60-100 F.
  • Dangerous combinations: bleach + acid -> chlorine; bleach + ammonia -> chloramines; acid + cyanide -> HCN; acid + sulfide -> H2S; oxidizer + organic -> fire.
  • Reactive classes: water-reactive (sodium, calcium carbide), pyrophoric (spontaneously ignite in air), peroxide formers, polymerizers, oxidizers (NFPA 400 classes 1-4).
  • Exothermic runaway reactions are controlled by cooling, inhibitors, relief systems and MOC (link to process safety).

Exam tip: Always add acid to water, never water to acid ('Do what you oughta, add acid to water').

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 5 Math Review, p. 211; Ch. 6 Particulates and Gases, p. 228; Ch. 12 Fire Prevention (storage); Ch. 27 Hazardous Materials Management

  2. 10

  2. It is not hazardous below 100 F and can be stored with non-flammable liquids
  3. Flash point only matters during combustion, not storage

6.5-Q10. During an inspection, you find containers of nitric acid stored on the same shelf as containers of acetone. What should you do?

  4. Move the acetone to a flammable cabinet and segregate it from the nitric acid

QAnsWhy
1BRequired volume = 1.1 x 10,000 = 11,000 gal / 7.48 = 1,470.6 ft3. Height = 1,470.6 / (40 x 30) = 1.23 ft. Trap: 2.56 ft wrongly sums all three tanks.
2BTotal = 20 x 55 = 1,100 gal; 10% = 110 gal; largest container = 55 gal. The greater value is 110 gal. Trap: 55 gal applies only the largest-container rule.
3AV = pi r^2 h = 3.14 x 5^2 x 20 = 1,570 ft3 (about 11,750 gal at 7.48 gal/ft3). Trap: using the diameter (10) as the radius gives 6,280 ft3.
4DGas law calculations need absolute units: P2 = 2,014.7 psia x (600 R / 530 R) = 2,280.8 psia = about 2,266 psig. Trap: using F directly (140/70) doubles the pressure to 4,000 psig.

10DNitric acid is a strong oxidizer and acetone is a flammable organic, so they are incompatible and can react violently. Segregate them: put acetone in a flammable cabinet and acids in a separate corrosive cabinet.

Domain 6 · Competency 6.6

Physics: Energy, Weight, Force and Stress

CSP11 blueprint: Apply core concepts in physics (e.g., forms of energy, weights, forces, stresses)

1. Definition

Applied physics (mechanics, hydrostatics and electricity) explains how energy causes injury and how structures, rigging and equipment resist loads. The CSP exam tests calculations of force, energy, velocity, pressure, stress and electrical quantities.

2. Explanation in simple words

Injuries happen when energy reaches the body faster than it can absorb it. Physics lets you calculate how fast something falls, how hard it hits, how much force a sling or pressure vessel carries, and whether a part is strong enough.

3. Key points to remember

  • Forms of energy: kinetic (KE = ½mv² - doubling speed quadruples energy), potential (PE = mgh), elastic (springs), thermal, chemical, electrical, radiant; energy is conserved (PE converts to KE in a fall).
  • Newton's laws: 1st - inertia (unsecured loads keep moving when a vehicle stops); 2nd - F = ma; 3rd - action-reaction. Weight W = mg (g = 9.81 m/s² or 32.2 ft/s²). 1 N = 1 kg·m/s².
  • Kinematics: v = √(2gh); d = ½gt²; v² = v₀² + 2ad; stopping distance = reaction distance (v × t) + braking distance (v² ÷ 2a). Momentum p = mv; impact force ≈ change in momentum ÷ time.
  • Friction: F = μN; static friction > kinetic friction (harder to start moving); slip resistance measured by coefficient of friction with a tribometer.
  • Stress and strength: stress σ = F ÷ A (psi or Pa); factor of safety = ultimate strength ÷ working stress (or breaking strength ÷ working load); tension, compression, shear, bending, torsion; fatigue failure under cyclic loading.
  • Pressure: P = F ÷ A, so F = P × A (force on a vessel door = pressure × area); hydrostatic P = ρgh (water: 0.433 psi per ft; 62.4 lb/ft³).
  • Mechanical advantage: levers, pulleys (effort = load ÷ number of supporting rope parts), inclined planes.
  • Electricity: Ohm's law V = IR; power P = VI = I²R; series and parallel resistance; current (not voltage) causes injury.
  • Work = force × distance; power = work ÷ time (1 hp = 550 ft·lb/s = 746 W).

4. Real work scenario

A 10-lb wrench falls 64 ft from a tower. Using v = √(2gh) = √(2 × 32.2 × 64) ≈ 64 ft/s, it hits the ground at about 44 mph with KE = ½ × (10/32.2) × 64² ≈ 636 ft·lb - far beyond what a hard hat is rated to absorb. The safety professional requires tool tethers and an exclusion zone below.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Keep units consistent (pounds-mass vs slugs; convert lb to slugs by ÷ 32.2 when using F = ma in English units).
  • Doubling speed quadruples kinetic energy - why speed control matters.
  • Force on a door or plate = pressure × area (use radius, not diameter, in πr²).
  • Static friction > kinetic friction explains 'hard to start, easy to keep moving'.

6. Every blueprint point (4 blueprint items + 2 more)

Energy (kinetic and potential)BLUEPRINT

Energy is the capacity to do work; injuries occur when energy is released uncontrolled and transferred to the body. Kinetic energy depends on mass and the square of velocity; potential energy on weight and height.

  • KE = 1/2 m v^2 (joules with kg and m/s; ft-lb with slugs and ft/s, where mass in slugs = weight lb / 32.2).
  • PE = m g h = W x h. Example: 10-lb tool dropped 50 ft -> PE = 500 ft-lb; 1 J = 0.738 ft-lb.
  • Velocity after a free fall: v = sqrt(2gh). 16 ft -> sqrt(2 x 32.2 x 16) = 32.1 ft/s (time t = sqrt(2h/g) = 1.0 s).
  • Doubling speed quadruples KE: 3,000-lb car (93.2 slugs) at 44 ft/s (30 mph) -> KE = 0.5 x 93.2 x 44^2 = about 90,200 ft-lb; at 60 mph about 361,000 ft-lb.
  • Other forms: electrical, chemical, thermal, pressure, radiant - all must be controlled in LOTO (energy isolation and stored energy release).
  • Haddon's energy-release theory: prevent the build-up, reduce the amount, prevent release, separate in time/space, put barriers, modify surfaces, strengthen the target.

Exam tip: Speed matters more than mass for vehicle crash energy because velocity is squared.

WeightBLUEPRINT

Weight is the force of gravity on a mass: W = m x g. In US units the pound is a force, so weight in lb divided by g gives mass in slugs.

  • W = m g; g = 9.81 m/s2 = 32.2 ft/s2. Example: 100 kg -> 981 N; 1 kg weighs about 2.2 lb; 1 lb = 4.448 N.
  • Mass is constant; weight changes with gravity (and with acceleration - an accelerating hoist feels heavier).
  • Liquid weight: water 8.34 lb/gal, 62.4 lb/ft3; other liquids = 8.34 x SG. Example: 55 gal of liquid with SG 1.2 = 55 x 8.34 x 1.2 = 550 lb (plus drum).
  • Steel about 490 lb/ft3; concrete about 150 lb/ft3 - used to estimate rigging loads (weight = volume x density).
  • Center of gravity: the point where the weight acts; for lifting, the hook must be directly above the CG to avoid swing/tilt.

Exam tip: When rigging, estimate weight from dimensions and density if it is not marked - never guess.

ForceBLUEPRINT

Force changes an object's motion: F = m x a. Force questions on the CSP exam include accelerations, impacts, fall arrest, friction and rigging tension.

  • Newton's 2nd law F = m a; units N (kg m/s2) or lb (slug ft/s2). Example: 2,000-kg forklift decelerating at 3 m/s2 -> 6,000 N.
  • Impulse-momentum: F x t = m x delta-v; increasing stopping time or distance reduces force (energy absorbers, airbags).
  • Fall arrest average force: F = W(h + d)/d. Example: 220-lb worker, 6-ft free fall, 3.5-ft deceleration -> 220 x 9.5 / 3.5 = 597 lb average (OSHA max arresting force 1,800 lb with harness).
  • Friction: F = mu x N. Walking surfaces: static COF about 0.5 commonly cited as slip-resistant (ANSI A1264.2).
  • Pressure-force: F = P x A. Example: 1,500 psi on a 4-in2 hydraulic piston -> 6,000 lb.
  • Stopping distance: d = v^2 / (2a). 60 mph (88 ft/s) braking at 0.7 g (22.5 ft/s2) -> 172 ft, plus reaction distance (1.5 s x 88 = 132 ft).

Exam tip: Shorter stopping distance = higher impact force: that is why a 2-ft free fall on a positioning device is limited.

StressBLUEPRINT

Stress is internal force per unit area in a material: stress = F/A. Comparing working stress with material strength gives the safety factor used in rigging, scaffolds and structures.

  • Stress (psi or Pa) = force / cross-sectional area. Example: 2,000 lb on a 0.5-in2 rod -> 4,000 psi.
  • Types: tensile (pulling), compressive (pushing), shear (sliding), bending and torsion.
  • Strain = change in length / original length; Young's modulus E = stress/strain (elastic region). Beyond the yield point deformation is permanent; ultimate strength = failure.
  • Safety factor = ultimate (breaking) strength / working load. Wire rope 5:1 -> 10,000-lb breaking strength gives WLL 2,000 lb. Scaffolds: 4 times intended load; guardrail top rail 200 lb.
  • Fatigue failure: repeated cyclic stress below yield can crack metal over time - inspect cranes, hooks, pressure vessels.

Exam tip: Doubling the rod diameter quadruples the area and cuts the stress to 1/4.

Rigging and sling angles

Sling leg tension increases as the angle from horizontal decreases. Rigging calculations combine weight, number of legs and sling angle.

  • Leg tension T = (W / n) / sin(angle from horizontal). Load angle factor = 1/sin: 90 deg 1.000, 60 deg 1.155, 45 deg 1.414, 30 deg 2.000.
  • Example: 2,000-lb load, 2-leg bridle at 60 deg -> each leg 1,000 x 1.155 = 1,155 lb; at 30 deg each leg 2,000 lb.
  • Sling angles below 30 deg from horizontal are not recommended (ASME B30.9).
  • Choker hitch reduces rated capacity (about 75-80% of vertical); basket hitch with vertical legs = 2 x vertical capacity.
  • Assume only 2 legs carry the load on a 3- or 4-leg bridle unless an equalizer is used.

Exam tip: Flatter sling = more tension. At 30 degrees, each leg carries the full load weight.

Work, power, torque and stability

Work and power quantify energy transfer and its rate; torque (moment) explains levers, wrenches, crane and forklift stability.

  • Work = force x distance (ft-lb or J). Power = work / time; 1 hp = 550 ft-lb/s = 746 W. Example: lifting 500 lb 10 ft in 5 s = 1,000 ft-lb/s = 1.82 hp.
  • Torque/moment = force x perpendicular distance. Example: 50 lb on a 2-ft wrench = 100 ft-lb.
  • Equilibrium: sum of moments about the fulcrum = 0. Forklift: load moment (load x distance from front axle) must not exceed counterweight moment - capacity drops as the load center moves out (e.g., rated at 24-in. load center).
  • Crane stability: tipping load and rated capacity charts (often 75% of tipping for crawlers, 85% for outriggers); capacity falls with longer radius.
  • Electrical: Ohm's law V = I x R; power P = V x I = I^2 x R. Example: 120 V across 1,000 ohm body resistance -> 0.12 A (120 mA) - potentially fatal.

Exam tip: Moving a forklift load forward beyond its rated load center reduces safe capacity even if the weight is unchanged.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 17 Mechanics, p. 454; Ch. 18 Hydrostatics and Hydraulics, p. 469; Ch. 16 Electrical Safety (Ohm's law); Ch. 5 Math Review

Answer all ten on paper first, then check the key. Score: ____ / 10   Date: __________   Retest: ____ / 10

6.6-Q1. A steel rod with a cross-sectional area of 0.5 in2 carries a tensile load of 2,000 lb. The material's ultimate strength is 60,000 psi. What is the working stress and factor of safety?

  1. 1,000 psi; 60
  2. 4,000 psi; 15
  3. 4,000 psi; 5
  4. 8,000 psi; 7.5

6.6-Q2. A 10-kg toolbox falls from a scaffold platform 2 m high. Ignoring air resistance, what is its approximate velocity just before it hits the ground? (v = sqrt(2gh), g = 9.8 m/s2)

  1. 6.3 m/s
  2. 4.5 m/s
  3. 2.2 m/s
  4. 9.8 m/s

6.6-Q3. A 1,500-kg vehicle travels at 10 m/s. What is its kinetic energy? (KE = 1/2 m v^2)

  1. 7,500 J
  2. 15,000 J
  3. 75,000 J
  4. 150,000 J

6.6-Q4. A 24-inch-diameter access door on a vessel is under 50 psig. What total force acts on the door?

  1. 1,200 lb
  2. 7,540 lb
  3. 22,620 lb
  4. 90,480 lb

6.6-Q5. A steel crane cable has a cross-sectional area of 150 mm2 and an allowable tensile stress of 400 MPa (1 MPa = 1 N/mm2). What is the maximum tensile force it can safely support? (F = stress x A)

  1. 4,000 N
  2. 60,000 N
  3. 600,000 N
  4. 6,000 N

6.6-Q6. A delivery truck is traveling through a jobsite at 15 mph when it strikes a pedestrian. If the same truck had been traveling at 30 mph, how would the impact energy compare?

  1. It would double
  2. It would increase by 1.5 times
  3. It would stay the same
  4. It would increase by four times

6.6-Q7. A 10-kg rubber block slides on a concrete floor (mu = 0.65). What is the friction force? (F = mu N; g = 9.8 m/s2)

  1. 33.7 N
  2. 43.7 N
  3. 53.7 N
  4. 63.7 N

6.6-Q8. A car accelerates uniformly from 10 m/s to 30 m/s over 10 seconds. How far does it travel? (a = (v2 - v1)/t; v2^2 - v1^2 = 2a d)

  1. 200 m
  2. 300 m
  3. 400 m
  4. 500 m

6.6-Q9. A worker says a heavy cart is hard to start moving but easier to keep moving. Which physics concept BEST explains this?

  1. Kinetic energy
  2. Static friction
  3. Gravitational potential
  4. Thermal expansion

6.6-Q10. A 10-lb wrench slips from a platform 20 ft above a worker who is wearing a hard hat. Which physical principle BEST explains why the wrench still poses a serious injury risk?

  1. Gravitational potential energy converting to kinetic energy
  2. Frictional resistance
  3. Conservation of momentum
  4. Electromagnetic interference

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BStress = F/A = 2,000/0.5 = 4,000 psi. Factor of safety = ultimate strength / working stress = 60,000/4,000 = 15.
2Av = sqrt(2gh) = sqrt(2 x 9.8 x 2) = sqrt(39.2) = 6.3 m/s. Mass does not affect free-fall velocity (it does affect impact energy).
3CKE = 1/2 mv^2 = 0.5 x 1,500 x 10^2 = 75,000 J. Because KE depends on v^2, speed control is critical in fleet and forklift safety.
4CArea = pi r^2 = pi x 12^2 = 452.4 in2; F = P x A = 50 psi x 452.4 = 22,620 lb. Trap: using the diameter as the radius gives 90,480 lb. Always depressurize before opening.
5BF = stress x area = 400 N/mm2 x 150 mm2 = 60,000 N (60 kN).
6DKE = 1/2 mv^2, so doubling speed from 15 to 30 mph raises impact energy by 2^2 = 4 times. Trap: assuming energy scales linearly (doubles).
7DFriction F = mu x N = 0.65 x (10 kg x 9.8 m/s2) = 0.65 x 98 N = 63.7 N.
8Aa = (30 - 10)/10 = 2 m/s2; distance = (v2^2 - v1^2)/(2a) = (900 - 100)/4 = 200 m. Check: average velocity 20 m/s x 10 s = 200 m.
9BStatic friction (resistance to starting motion) is greater than kinetic friction (resistance to continued sliding), so a cart takes more force to start than to keep moving.
10AThe 10-lb wrench's potential energy (mgh) at 20 ft becomes kinetic energy as it falls and can exceed what a hard hat is designed to absorb. Prevent drops with tool tethers, toe boards and barricades.

Printed book page 241
Domain 7 · Competency 7.1

Training Needs Assessment

CSP11 blueprint: Describe the needs assessment process to determine worker training, competencies, and qualifications

1. Definition

A training needs assessment (TNA) is a systematic process to identify gaps between current and required knowledge, skills and abilities (KSAs) and to decide whether training is the right solution. It defines the competencies and qualifications required for each job.

2. Explanation in simple words

Before you train, find out what people need to know and do, what they already know, and whether the problem is really a lack of skill. If they can already do the job but don't, more training will not fix it.

3. Key points to remember

  • Levels: organizational analysis (goals, regulations, incident trends), task/job analysis (what the job requires - task inventory, JHA), person analysis (who needs training - assessments, observation, performance data).
  • Data sources: injury/incident data, audits, regulatory requirements, JHAs, observations, interviews, surveys, focus groups, performance appraisals, new equipment/processes (MOC), task matrix.
  • Mager and Pipe performance analysis: 'Could they do it if their life depended on it?' If yes → not a skill deficiency (fix consequences, obstacles, feedback, equipment, supervision). If no → training, practice or job aids.
  • Competency = demonstrated ability to perform to a standard (knowledge + skills + behavior). Qualification = formal credential, certificate or license. Competent person (OSHA) = can identify hazards and has authority to correct them; qualified person = has a degree/certificate or extensive knowledge to solve problems.
  • Regulatory training requirements: e.g., forklift (evaluation every 3 years), HAZWOPER (40 h/24 h/8 h refresher annually), confined space, LOTO, HazCom (initially and when new hazards), respirators (annually), bloodborne pathogens (annually), hearing conservation (annually), crane operator certification.
  • Prioritize training needs by risk (severity and consequence of failure), legal requirements and number of workers affected.
  • Output: training plan with objectives, audience, methods, resources, schedule, evaluation.

4. Real work scenario

Forklift near-misses rise at a warehouse. Operators score 95% on written tests and perform inspections correctly when observed, but three trucks have broken backup alarms and the night-shift supervisor rarely checks pre-use inspections. The needs assessment concludes training is not the main fix: alarms are repaired and supervisors are made accountable for inspection checks.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • The FIRST step in designing training is the needs assessment.
  • If workers know how but don't do it, the answer is not more training.
  • Prioritize training by safety impact and consequences of failure.

6. Every blueprint point (3 blueprint items + 5 more)

Worker trainingBLUEPRINT

Worker training gives employees the knowledge and skills to recognize hazards and perform tasks safely. It is driven by regulatory requirements, task hazards, incidents and changes in the workplace, and it is only the right solution when the gap is a knowledge or skill gap.

  • OSHA 1926.21(b)(2): employer must instruct each employee in recognizing and avoiding unsafe conditions.
  • Training triggers: new hire, new task or equipment, process change (MOC), incident/near miss, audit finding, regulatory refresher.
  • Common refresher frequencies: HAZWOPER 8 h annually; forklift evaluation at least every 3 years; PSM refresher at least every 3 years; bloodborne pathogens annually; respirator annually.
  • ANSI/ASSP Z490.1 = accepted practices for SH&E training (needs assessment, design, delivery, evaluation, records).
  • Training is NOT the fix when the problem is equipment design, lack of resources or motivation - use engineering, PtD or management actions instead.

Exam tip: If a performance problem exists even though workers can do the task correctly, more training is the wrong answer.

CompetenciesBLUEPRINT

A competency is a demonstrated combination of knowledge, skills and abilities (KSAs) needed to perform a task safely. Training needs are defined by comparing the competency a job requires with what the worker currently has.

  • Knowledge: regulations, SOPs, hazard recognition.
  • Skills: equipment operation, emergency response, PPE use.
  • Abilities: physical/mental readiness, decision-making under stress.
  • Example: a scaffold inspector must know 1926.451, demonstrate inspection skills and be able to stop unsafe work.
  • ISO 45001 clause 7.2 requires the organization to determine and ensure worker competence and retain documented evidence.

Exam tip: Competence must be demonstrated, not just attended - a sign-in sheet proves attendance, not competency.

Qualifications (competent vs. qualified person)BLUEPRINT

OSHA uses two distinct roles. A competent person can identify existing and predictable hazards and has authority to take prompt corrective action; a qualified person has a recognized degree, certificate or professional standing, or extensive knowledge, training and experience, and can solve problems in the subject area.

  • Competent person: 29 CFR 1926.32(f) - hazard recognition + authority to correct/stop work; no degree required.
  • Qualified person: 29 CFR 1926.32(m) - degree/certificate/professional standing or extensive knowledge, training and experience.
  • Competent examples: scaffold inspector, trench supervisor (soil classification, daily inspection), fall protection supervisor.
  • Qualified examples: PE designing an anchorage or trench protective system, electrician on electrical systems, industrial hygienist.
  • Rule of thumb: qualified = designs; competent = inspects and corrects on site.

Exam tip: Key discriminator: AUTHORITY to take corrective action belongs to the competent person definition.

Training needs assessment (ADDIE Analyze phase)

A needs assessment identifies who must be trained, on what, and to what level before any course is designed. It is the Analyze step of ADDIE and answers who the learners are, their current skill level and what gaps exist.

  • Sources: JHA/JSA, incident and near-miss data, audit findings, regulatory requirements, new equipment/processes, worker and supervisor input.
  • Output: target audience, performance gaps, learning objectives, priorities.
  • Prioritize high-risk tasks first (confined spaces, electrical, work at heights).
  • Keywords 'needs analysis' or 'gap identification' = Analyze phase.

Exam tip: FIRST step in developing training = needs assessment (not choosing a method or writing slides).

Defining baseline competency

Baseline competency is the minimum level of KSAs a worker must demonstrate to do a task safely. Comparing baseline (current) competency against required competency reveals the training gap.

  • Steps: identify job tasks and hazards -> determine required KSAs -> assess current workforce competency -> define competency standards -> use the gap to build the needs assessment.
  • Assessing current competency: pre-training tests, interviews, on-the-job observation, performance data (KPIs, incident reports).
  • Standards used as benchmarks: OSHA/ANSI requirements, ISO 45001, company SOPs.
  • Example: forklift operators - certification plus skill evaluation at least every 3 years (1910.178(l)).

Exam tip: 'How do you decide whether workers need additional training?' = compare baseline (current) competency with required competency.

Tools for needs and competency assessment

Several practical tools link tasks to the skills needed and show where workers fall short.

  • Job Hazard Analysis: breaks a job into steps and links each to hazards and required skills.
  • Competency matrix: visual grid of required skills vs. employees, showing who is trained/qualified and when refreshers are due.
  • Pre-assessment tests: measure baseline knowledge.
  • Observation checklists: track safe behaviors vs. shortcuts.
  • Task analysis + interviews and surveys of workers and supervisors.

Human risk factors affecting performance

Training fails if human factors are ignored. Behavior, decision making, situational awareness, workload, risk perception and stress all determine whether workers apply what they learned.

  • Behavior: observable actions shaped by habits and culture - control with BBS and positive reinforcement.
  • Decision making: choices under pressure - scenario training, clear procedures, stop-work authority.
  • Situational awareness: perceive, understand, anticipate - hazard recognition training, checklists, pre-task briefings, fatigue management.
  • Workload: too much = fatigue/errors, too little = complacency - job rotation, breaks, staffing.
  • Risk perception: underestimating hazards leads to shortcuts - case studies, risk matrices.
  • Stress: reduces focus and memory - EAP, reasonable scheduling, stress management training.

Exam tip: Match the scenario: overwhelmed by demands = workload; anticipating hazards in dynamic settings = situational awareness; 'this low scaffold is not dangerous' = risk perception.

Who needs training: audience analysis

Different audiences need different content and depth: new hires, experienced workers, supervisors, managers, contractors and temporary workers.

  • Supervisors and managers need training on their responsibilities, leading by example and enforcing rules, not just hazard content.
  • Host and staffing agency share responsibility for temporary worker training; host provides site-specific training.
  • Consider language, literacy and culture - OSHA requires training in a manner and language workers understand.
  • Experienced workers still need training when tasks, equipment or rules change.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 19 Training, p. 483

7.1-Q1. What is the primary purpose of conducting a Training Needs Assessment (TNA)?

  1. To evaluate the effectiveness of past training programs
  2. To identify specific training needs based on current competencies and desired performance levels
  3. To assess the financial resources available for training
  4. To finalize the training schedule for the year

7.1-Q2. What is the primary purpose of a gap analysis in a training needs assessment?

  1. To count the number of employees requiring training
  2. To determine the profitability of training programs
  3. To identify discrepancies between current and desired performance levels
  4. To evaluate the effectiveness of past training programs

7.1-Q3. A needs assessment reveals more training needs than can be addressed in the available time. What is the BEST approach?

  1. Focus on the tasks that are performed most frequently
  2. Train employees on tasks they already excel at to gain quick wins
  3. Delay training until all needs can be addressed at once
  4. Prioritize tasks with the highest risk or consequence of failure

7.1-Q4. Baseline competency is BEST defined as:

  1. The minimum knowledge, skills, and abilities required to perform a job safely
  2. The maximum level of performance expected from a worker
  3. A supervisor's subjective rating of worker effort
  4. The average score on a post-training quiz

7.1-Q5. Which tool maps each employee's current skills against the competencies required for their role?

  1. Hazard control matrix
  2. Risk matrix
  3. Competency (skills) matrix
  4. Inspection checklist

7.1-Q6. Which statement BEST distinguishes a competency from a qualification?

  1. They are identical terms
  2. Competency is only knowledge; a qualification is only skill
  3. Qualifications are never required by OSHA
  4. A qualification is a formal credential (certificate, license); competency is the demonstrated ability to perform the task to the required standard

7.1-Q7. Which OSHA regulation contains the general definitions of 'competent person' and 'qualified person' for construction?

  1. 29 CFR 1910.120
  2. 29 CFR 1926.32
  3. 29 CFR 1910.146
  4. 29 CFR 1926.21

7.1-Q8. A construction supervisor identifies a cave-in hazard in a trench and immediately stops work until shoring is corrected. Under OSHA, the supervisor is acting as a:

  1. Competent person
  2. Qualified person
  3. Professional engineer
  4. Trainer

7.1-Q9. Under 29 CFR 1926.32(m), a qualified person is one who:

  1. Has jobsite experience but is not required to solve technical problems
  2. Has authority to stop work and take prompt corrective action
  3. By a recognized degree, certificate, or professional standing, or by extensive knowledge, training, and experience, has demonstrated the ability to solve problems relating to the subject matter
  4. Has completed basic PPE training under supervision

7.1-Q10. An incident trend shows operators skipping a startup checklist step. The plant manager asks the CSP to schedule retraining for all operators. What should the CSP do FIRST?

  1. Analyze whether the problem is caused by a lack of knowledge or skill, or by other factors such as time pressure, equipment design, or supervision
  2. Schedule retraining for all operators immediately
  3. Discipline the operators involved in the incidents
  4. Purchase an online refresher course on startup procedures

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BA TNA identifies the gap between current and required performance so training targets real needs. Evaluating past training is effectiveness evaluation, not needs assessment.
2CGap analysis compares actual performance (or KSAs) with the required standard; the difference defines what training must close.
3DTraining priorities follow risk: tasks whose failure could cause serious injury or loss come first. Frequency alone is a trap; a rare but critical task may matter more.
4ABaseline competency is the minimum KSA level required for safe performance; it is the standard against which current ability is compared.
5CA competency or skills matrix lists required competencies by role and shows who meets them, making training gaps visible. A risk matrix ranks hazards, not people's skills.
6DA credential shows something was earned; competency is proven ability to perform. A worker can hold a certificate yet still lack demonstrated competence on the job.
7B29 CFR 1926.32 (definitions) defines competent person (f) and qualified person (m). 1926.21 covers safety training and education; 1910.120 is HAZWOPER; 1910.146 is permit-required confined spaces.
8AAn OSHA competent person can identify existing and predictable hazards and has authorization to take prompt corrective measures. The key words are 'identify' plus 'authority to correct'.
9CThe qualified person is defined by demonstrated problem-solving ability (e.g., designing scaffolds or anchorages). Authority to stop work is the competent-person trap.
10AA needs assessment first determines whether a performance gap is actually a training problem. If operators know the step but skip it, the fix is a management or design issue, and retraining will not work.
Printed book page 246
Domain 7 · Competency 7.2

Training Program Development

CSP11 blueprint: Develop training programs with training materials to address various learning styles (e.g., presentation methods and tools)

1. Definition

Training program development turns needs into structured instruction: writing measurable learning objectives, selecting content, methods and media for different learners, creating lesson plans, instructor guides, trainee materials and job aids, and piloting the course. ADDIE (Analysis, Design, Development, Implementation, Evaluation) is the common instructional design model.

2. Explanation in simple words

Decide exactly what learners must be able to do at the end, then build lessons, activities and materials that get them there - using pictures, talk, reading and hands-on practice so everyone can learn.

3. Key points to remember

  • ADDIE: Analyze (needs, audience) → Design (objectives, assessments, sequence, methods) → Develop (materials, lesson plans, media, pilot) → Implement (deliver) → Evaluate (formative during, summative after).
  • Learning objectives (ABCD): Audience, Behavior (observable action verb), Condition, Degree (criterion). Avoid 'understand', 'know', 'be aware'.
  • Bloom's taxonomy (cognitive, low to high): Remember → Understand → Apply → Analyze → Evaluate → Create. Psychomotor and affective domains also exist.
  • Lesson plan contents: objectives, materials, time, content outline, activities, assessment, instructor notes - standardizes delivery and avoids omitting critical material.
  • Materials: instructor guide, participant workbook/handouts, job aids (checklists, flowcharts used on the job), visual aids, simulations, case studies.
  • Presentation design: 6 × 6 rule (about six lines, six words), large readable fonts, one idea per slide, images over text; lighting, room layout (U-shape for discussion), background noise low.
  • Accommodate diversity: literacy levels, language, generations (Gen Z prefers technology-based, interactive, short content), disabilities (captions, large print, audio description, braille), culture.
  • Pilot course with a representative sample to test content, timing and methods before full rollout; ethics - copyright/trademark compliance, test security, fairness and confidentiality.

4. Real work scenario

A plant needs confined-space attendant training. The developer writes the objective: 'Given a gas meter and entry permit, the attendant will verify atmospheric readings and record them with 100% accuracy.' The course combines a short lecture, a video of a real incident, a hands-on gas meter station and a mock rescue drill, and is piloted with one crew before rollout.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Good objectives are measurable and observable.
  • Lesson plans standardize the presentation.
  • Applying a formula in a new situation = Bloom's 'Apply'; designing a new program = 'Create'.
  • Pilot with a representative sample of the real audience.

6. Every blueprint point (4 blueprint items + 7 more)

Training materialsBLUEPRINT

Training materials are the lesson plans, slides, handouts, manuals, videos, job aids and assessments produced in the Develop phase of ADDIE. They must be accurate, current, job-specific and written at the audience's literacy level.

  • Develop phase outputs: lesson plans, slides, study guides, quizzes, case studies, videos, handouts.
  • ANSI Z490.1: materials should be clear, relevant and tied to measurable learning objectives.
  • Job aids (checklists, pictograms, flowcharts) support performance on the job after training.
  • Review and update materials after incidents, regulation changes and MOC.

Exam tip: 'Creating quizzes and handouts' = Develop phase, not Design.

Learning stylesBLUEPRINT

Learners differ in how they prefer to take in information; the VARK model groups them as visual, auditory, reading/writing and kinesthetic. Training should use a blend so every learner is reached.

  • VARK: Visual, Auditory, Reading/writing, Kinesthetic (detailed under 7.6).
  • The guide also lists expanded learner types: stress (learn under pressure), ease (calm settings), scribble (writing notes), trust (authority figures), teach (teaching others), copy (imitating models).
  • Kolb styles: diverging, assimilating, converging, accommodating.
  • Research gives limited support to matching instruction to a single preferred style; multimodal delivery is the safe design choice.

Exam tip: Best answer for a mixed workforce: use multiple methods/modalities, not one style.

Presentation methodsBLUEPRINT

Presentation methods are how content is delivered: lecture, demonstration, discussion, case study, role play, drill and simulation. The method should match the objective and learning domain.

  • Knowledge (cognitive) objectives: lecture, reading, discussion, quizzes.
  • Skill (psychomotor) objectives: demonstration then supervised practice, drills, simulation.
  • Attitude (affective) objectives: case studies, testimonials, discussion, leadership example.
  • Adults disengage in theory-heavy lectures - add real examples and hands-on exercises.

Exam tip: Hands-on skill (SCBA donning, extinguisher use) cannot be verified by lecture or written test - choose demonstration and practice.

Training toolsBLUEPRINT

Training tools are the aids and technology used to deliver and reinforce learning, from simple job aids to simulators.

  • Examples: projectors/slides, videos, props and actual equipment, mock-ups, LMS, VR/AR, simulators, polling/quiz apps, job aids.
  • Use real equipment or close mock-ups for skill training.
  • An LMS tracks completion, scores and due dates for records.
  • Engagement features: polls or quizzes every 10-15 minutes, short case studies, video clips of 2 minutes or less.

ADDIE instructional design model

ADDIE (Analyze, Design, Develop, Implement, Evaluate) is a systematic model for building training. Evaluation feeds back into analysis so the program continuously improves.

  • Analyze: needs assessment, audience, gaps.
  • Design: write learning objectives, choose methods, outline structure and schedule.
  • Develop: create materials, lesson plans, quizzes, videos.
  • Implement: deliver sessions, pre-assessments, interaction, post-assessments.
  • Evaluate: feedback surveys, pre vs. post test results, revise the program.

Exam tip: Selecting objectives or methods = Design; creating materials = Develop; delivering = Implement; survey after class = Evaluate.

Learning objectives

Objectives state what the learner will be able to do after training, in observable and measurable terms. They drive content selection and assessment design.

  • ABCD format: Audience, Behavior (action verb), Condition, Degree (standard).
  • Example: 'Given a lockout kit, the maintenance technician will apply LOTO to Press 4 following all 6 steps without error.'
  • Use measurable verbs (demonstrate, calculate, identify) - avoid 'understand', 'know', 'appreciate'.
  • Assessments must align with objectives (a skill objective needs a performance test).

Exam tip: If an objective cannot be observed or measured, it is poorly written.

Bloom's Taxonomy

A hierarchy of cognitive learning used to write objectives and design assessments, moving from recall up to creating new solutions.

  • Revised order (low to high): Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyze, Evaluate, Create.
  • Remember: list, define, state (recall exposure limits).
  • Understand: explain, summarize, interpret (explain the hierarchy of controls).
  • Apply: use in new situations (solve a fall clearance problem).
  • Analyze: compare, differentiate, break down (root causes from incident reports).
  • Evaluate: judge, assess, recommend (critique a fall protection plan). Create: design, develop (design a new safety program).

Exam tip: Recommending improvements = Evaluate; solving exposure calculations = Apply; designing a program = Create.

Domains of learning (cognitive, affective, psychomotor)

Effective safety training addresses the head (knowledge), heart (attitudes and values) and hands (physical skills).

  • Cognitive: knowledge and mental skills - quizzes, reading, written tests (memorizing exposure limits).
  • Affective: attitudes, values, motivation - discussions, testimonials, case studies, leadership example (believing PPE is essential).
  • Psychomotor: physical skill - hands-on practice, drills, simulation (fire extinguisher drills, safe lifting).
  • Bloom's taxonomy describes levels within the cognitive domain.

Exam tip: Changing a worker's belief that safety rules matter = affective domain.

Learner retention (learning pyramid)

The guide teaches that retention rises as learning moves from passive to active: reading 10%, hearing 20%, seeing 30%, hearing + seeing 50%, saying/talking 70%, saying + doing 90%.

  • Passive: reading, hearing, seeing. Active: discussing, practicing, teaching others.
  • Highest retention = talking + doing (role-plays, hands-on drills, teaching peers).
  • Use for high-risk training (confined space, LOTO): practice and explain-back.
  • Note: these exact percentages (Dale's Cone / learning pyramid) are not research-validated, but the principle that active learning beats passive learning is well supported.

Exam tip: 'Which method gives the highest retention?' = hands-on practice combined with explaining/teaching (talking + doing).

Disequilibrium in training

A deliberate state of cognitive challenge that disrupts what learners currently believe so they must restructure their thinking. Based on Piaget's constructivist theory.

  • Example: showing real incident case studies where skipping PPE caused severe injury disrupts the belief that shortcuts save time.
  • Example: VR confined-space emergency simulation forces learners to reconsider procedures.
  • Benefits: builds critical thinking, helps unlearn unsafe habits, deepens retention by engaging emotion and problem solving.

Exam tip: Presenting an incident that challenges workers' current practices = disequilibrium.

Presentation slide design

Slides should clarify, not decorate. The guide gives practical rules for count, visuals, fonts, color and density.

  • Count: about 25-30 slides for 60 minutes; 8-12 for a 15-20 minute session.
  • Visuals: a relevant image or diagram every 2-3 slides; use pictograms (e.g., GHS) rather than text descriptions.
  • Fonts: sans-serif; titles 28-32 pt or larger, body text 20-24 pt or larger, footnotes at least 16 pt.
  • Contrast: dark on light or light on dark; avoid red-green combinations (color blindness).
  • 6 x 6 rule: max 6 lines, max 6 words per line; bullets not sentences; one key idea per slide.
  • Accessibility: readable on small screens, alt text for shared slides, consistent templates.

Exam tip: Presentation-effectiveness answers emphasize clarity, engagement and less text/more practice.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 19 Training, p. 483

Answer key and explanations

QAnsWhy
1BIn Design, objectives are written and instructional strategies and delivery methods are selected. Producing the actual materials comes later, in Develop.

9DJob aids (checklists, flowcharts, pocket cards) support correct performance on the job and reduce reliance on memory, especially for infrequent or critical tasks.
10ALesson plans contain objectives, content, methods, materials, and assessment. Marketing is not part of instructional design. Watch for the NOT.

Domain 7 · Competency 7.3

Continuous Improvement of Training

CSP11 blueprint: Describe how to implement training programs utilizing the Continuous Improvement model

1. Definition

Continuous improvement applies the PDCA (Plan-Do-Check-Act) cycle to training: plan the program, deliver it, check results through evaluation data, and act on findings to revise content, methods and delivery - so training stays current, effective and aligned with organizational goals.

2. Explanation in simple words

Training is never 'done'. Deliver it, measure whether it worked, fix what didn't, and run it again better. Keep updating it when equipment, rules or incidents change.

3. Key points to remember

  • PDCA for training: Plan (needs, objectives, design) → Do (deliver, pilot) → Check (evaluate: reactions, test scores, observations, incident trends) → Act (revise content, retrain, update materials, standardize improvements).
  • Formative evaluation: during development and delivery (pilot feedback, module checks) to improve. Summative evaluation: after completion, judges overall effectiveness.
  • Triggers for revision: new regulations/standards, MOC (new equipment or processes), incident investigations showing knowledge gaps, audit findings, poor evaluation results, learner feedback.
  • Implementation success factors: management support, alignment with organizational goals and culture, clear objectives, scheduling to minimize production impact (opportunity cost), resources (rooms, equipment, trainers), records.
  • Feedback loops: post-training reports, interim reports, trainer debriefs, refresher schedules, lessons learned database.
  • Remediation: coaching or extra practice for trainees who do not meet objectives (support, not punishment).

4. Real work scenario

After a new HazCom course, post-tests average 92%, but on-the-job observations three months later show only 60% of workers can find SDS Section 8 for PPE. The trainer adds a hands-on SDS scavenger hunt and quarterly 10-minute refreshers. The next observation cycle shows 90% proficiency.

5. CSP exam tips and traps

  • Check = evaluate results; Act = make improvements based on them.
  • Updating course materials should be driven first by current regulations and standards, not cost or popularity.
  • Formative = during; summative = after.

6. Every blueprint point (1 blueprint items + 6 more)

Continuous improvement of trainingBLUEPRINT

Training programs are improved through a closed loop: evaluate results, find gaps, revise content and delivery, then re-evaluate. Inputs include incidents, audits, worker feedback, assessment results and regulatory changes.

  • PDCA applied to training: Plan (needs/objectives), Do (deliver), Check (evaluate), Act (revise).
  • ADDIE Evaluate phase feeds back into Analyze.
  • Update triggers: incident or near miss, audit finding, poor test scores, new equipment/MOC, regulation change, worker feedback.
  • ANSI Z490.1 and ISO 45001 (clause 10.3) both require ongoing evaluation and improvement.
  • Track trends: repeated errors on the same quiz items signal a content or delivery problem.

Exam tip: If post-training observations show workers still not following a procedure, revise the training and look for non-training causes - do not just repeat the same course.

Kaizen (continuous improvement)

Kaizen is the Japanese philosophy of small, incremental, ongoing improvements made by everyone, rather than large one-time projects.

  • Guide's Kaizen cycle: make problems visible -> develop countermeasures -> determine root cause (5 Whys, fishbone) -> hypothesize solution -> test (pilot) -> implement -> standardize work.
  • Safety uses: employee improvement suggestions, near-miss learning, incremental ergonomic adjustments, continuous refinement of training.
  • Links to Domain 2 (culture, SMS), Domain 3 (risk controls) and Domain 7 (training effectiveness).

Exam tip: Employees proposing small weekly safety improvements = Kaizen.

Poka-yoke (error-proofing)

Poka-yoke, from Shigeo Shingo and the Toyota Production System, designs processes so errors cannot happen or are immediately obvious - reducing reliance on human memory and training alone.

  • Examples: plug that fits only one way, red/green scaffold tags, interlocked machine guards, keyed LOTO systems where keys cannot be removed until isolation is complete.
  • Benefits listed: preventing errors, waste reduction, cost reduction, satisfied customers, competitive advantage.
  • Two types: control (prevents the error) and warning (signals the error).
  • Aligns with prevention through design and the hierarchy of controls.

Exam tip: 'Prevent errors through design rather than relying on training' = poka-yoke.

Learning curves

A learning curve plots practice or time against performance. CSPs use it to judge training effectiveness, plan refresher training and predict performance.

  • Positive accelerating: slow start, then rapid improvement (SCBA use).
  • Negative accelerating (diminishing returns): rapid early gains, then levels off (basic PPE).
  • S-curve (sigmoid): slow -> fast -> slow (confined space entry skills).
  • Plateau: improvement stops (lack of challenge, motivation or poor methods).
  • Declining: performance drops from disuse or poor transfer = skill decay - fix with refreshers and drills.

Exam tip: 'Fast initial learning then plateau' = negative accelerating; 'slow, fast, slow' = S-curve.

Refresher training and skill decay

Skills that are rarely used decay over time, so periodic refreshers, drills and practice are scheduled to keep competence current.

  • Emergency skills (evacuation, rescue, spill response) decay fastest because they are rarely used - drill them regularly.
  • Retraining triggers (forklift example, 1910.178): unsafe operation, incident or near miss, poor evaluation, new truck type, changed workplace conditions.
  • Spaced repetition and short refreshers outperform a single long session.

Positive safety culture and training

Training only changes behavior when the culture supports it: open communication, psychological safety and emotionally intelligent leadership.

  • Open feedback: toolbox talks, anonymous reporting, leaders walking the floor and listening.
  • Psychological safety: workers can report hazards, admit mistakes and suggest ideas without fear of retaliation.
  • Emotional intelligence components: self-awareness, self-regulation, empathy, social skills.
  • Building culture: lead by example, involve workers (committees), recognize safe behavior, share investigation results, give continuous feedback.

Exam tip: Workers feel safe to report without fear = psychological safety; recognizing and managing emotions = emotional intelligence.

Training records and program review

Documentation supports compliance, competency tracking and improvement decisions.

  • ANSI Z490.1 records: trainee name/ID, course title, date, duration, trainer, assessment results, content/outline.
  • Use LMS or competency matrix data to spot overdue training and recurring weak topics.
  • Periodic program audits verify that training still matches hazards, regulations and job tasks.

Read more: Yates, Safety Professional's Reference and Study Guide (4th ed.): Ch. 19 Training, p. 483; Ch. 31 Safety Management System (PDCA)
